`hackerone.com/reports/<id>.json` (live `.json` route, all 6 HTTP 200).

## RESULT: nothing new appended — 6 sources checked, 0 new methods

The 6 assigned sources were read ONE BY ONE in the given order. All 6 were VALID, reachable and
readable (full `vulnerability_information` bodies, or the `summaries` field where the report is
`no-content`). NONE of them taught a technique that is not already recorded in
`/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md`, `/root/Bugbountyskills.md` and the 2026-11-09 dated file.
They are duplicates of reader pass 2026-11-09 (batch 103). Per the no-duplicates rule, no entry was
appended; per doctrine §5 a pass that appends nothing says so explicitly rather than manufacture
one.

## #646505 — re-assessed this pass (no append), and why the delta is still not new

The report is real and readable: `visibility: full`, `severity_rating: low`, `weakness: Cross-site
Scripting (XSS) - DOM`, team Shopify, asset `your-store.myshopify.com`. The payload (quoted in the
body) is a theme-code `<script>` that `window.open`s `https://apple-business-chat-commerce.shopifycloud.com`
and `postMessage`s `{"message":"Shopify.API.remoteRedirect","data":{"location":"javascript:eval(atob('<payload>'))"}}`
to it; the child's handler navigates to the `javascript:` URI -> DOM XSS.

The class AND the sink are already recorded; the delivery prerequisite is attacker-supplied theme
code (the merchant installs it), i.e. a gadget, not a new method:
  - postMessage-listener class with its origin gate: ledger lines ~5381-5400 (Upserve #603764, the
    `e.origin.indexOf(...)` substring gate) and #231053 (structured-clone escape), plus §17.147 /
    §17.165 / §17.286 in `/root/Bugbountyskills.md`;
  - the client-side-navigation `javascript:` sink: ledger ~3546-3555 (the post-login `dest=javascript:`
    entry) and the desktop-webview `javascript:`-attribute entry at ~3107-3114.

No `remoteRedirect` entry exists (only the batch-103 NOTE mentions it), but the mechanism is the
composition of the two recorded methods above, and it requires the merchant to install the
attacker's theme code — so it is not admitted as a new general method.

## Honesty

0 fabricated. Nothing was appended because nothing new was found; the 5 full entries and the #646505
delta assessment were already in the ledger from the 2026-11-09 pass (batch 103).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3115705  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 10,929 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 5,528 B read IN FULL. Metadata: Dust, "Stored XSS in File Upload Leads to Privilege Escalation and Full Workspace Takeover", weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored, severity high, reporter `sjalu`, submitted 2025-04-28, disclosed 2025-05-02, state Closed, asset dust.tt.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Dust workspace or file upload was touched this pass; every step and code line below is quoted verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: stored XSS via a FILE-UPLOAD API whose stored Content-Type is declared by the CLIENT and whose returned view URL renders the file INLINE in the app origin -> script executes with the viewer's session -> role escalation to admin -> full workspace takeover (CWE-79 -> CWE-269/CWE-863).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. "Set up a workspace where you are admin. Invite a dummy account with the normal member role. Upload the malicious file on the dummy account using the Python script below. Use the HTML found at the bottom for upload."
  2. The upload is TWO calls. First, ask the app for an upload URL: `POST https://dust.tt/api/w/<workspace_sid>/files` with JSON body `{"contentType":"text/html","fileName":"xss_poc.png","fileSize":7331,"useCase":"conversation"}` — the CLIENT declares `contentType: text/html` while the filename is `.png`. The response is `{"file":{"uploadUrl": ...}}`.
  3. Second, multipart-POST the HTML to that `uploadUrl` with the file field's Content-Type set to `text/html` (the report's `MultipartEncoder` fields: `('xss_poc.png', open('xss.html','rb'), 'text/html')`). The response carries `{"file":{"downloadUrl": ...}}`.
  4. The view URL is `<downloadUrl>?action=view` — "Share the URL with the workspace admin account. When the victim visits the link, your script runs automatically, promoting the dummy account to Admin."
  5. The payload's own script: `GET https://dust.tt/api/user` with `credentials:'include'` -> read `userData.user.workspaces[0].sId` (workspace id) and `userData.user.id` (victim id); then `POST https://dust.tt/api/w/${workspaceId}/members/${attackerUserId}` with JSON `{"role":"admin"}`.
  6. Impact, verbatim: "if the victim has administrative privileges, escalate their own account to an admin role and revoke admin rights from others. This results in a full compromise of the user account, potential privilege escalation, and takeover of the entire workspace."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a file-upload flow whose "request an upload URL" call takes a CLIENT-SUPPLIED `contentType`/`type`/`mimeType` field (and the server stores/serves the file with that type); a returned `uploadUrl`/`downloadUrl` pair; a view route that renders the stored file INLINE in the app origin (`?action=view`, `?inline=1`, `?preview=1`) instead of `Content-Disposition: attachment`; an upload endpoint that accepts `text/html` while the filename is a benign extension; a same-origin role-change endpoint reachable by the injected script.
CHAIN: a normal workspace member (primitive: any member can upload) -> the upload API stores an HTML file because the client declared `contentType: text/html` (primitive: the filename is not the type; the declared type is) -> the returned `?action=view` URL serves the HTML inline in the app origin (primitive: in-origin render) -> an admin opens the shared link and the script runs with the admin's session (escalation: script in the app origin with an admin session) -> `POST /api/w/{sid}/members/{attackerId}` with `{"role":"admin"}` promotes the attacker (escalation: vertical privilege escalation) -> the attacker de-ranks the victim admin and can read/delete secrets (impact: full workspace takeover). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a normal member role and an admin who opens the shared file URL; gain = admin role for the attacker plus the ability to revoke other admins and access secrets.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any file-upload flow, capture the "request an upload URL" call and check whether `contentType`/`type`/`mimeType` is a CLIENT-SUPPLIED field; set it to `text/html` while keeping a benign filename (`.png`, `.jpg`); upload an HTML file containing `<script>` (or `<img src=x onerror=...>`); then open the returned `downloadUrl` and its `?action=view` / `?inline=1` / `?preview=1` variants in a browser and check whether your script executes in the app origin. Then look for a same-origin role-change endpoint (`/members/{id}`, `/users/{id}/role`) reachable by the injected script. Rule: **when the upload API lets the client declare the stored Content-Type and the file is served inline in the app origin, a `.png`-named HTML file is stored XSS — the filename is not the type; the declared contentType is.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3115705`, `uploadUrl`, `action=view`, `contentType` (as a client-declared upload field), `dust.tt`, or a pre-signed upload URL plus an in-origin view. Related-but-distinct material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's file-upload entries are SVG/DOCX/ODF/PDF and the unrestricted-upload class; §17.146 is a preview-path password-middleware bypass; none records a client-declared `contentType` + pre-signed `uploadUrl` + `?action=view` chain. New general method -> §17.330.

SOURCE: https://www.intigriti.com/researchers/blog/hacking-tools/catflix-ctf-ssrf-nextjs-middleware  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Intigriti official write-up (published 2025-08-27), full technical guide read IN FULL; cross-read the two community write-ups (jorianwoltjer.com, gist.github.com/sebastianosrt) for the request shapes.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Next.js app was stood up this pass; the request shapes below are quoted from the write-ups).
CLASS: SSRF (full-read) via Next.js Middleware header reflection — `NextResponse.next()` treats the object's `headers` key as RESPONSE headers, so a middleware that copies request headers into it lets a client-supplied `Location` header become a response header that Next.js proxies to (CVE-2025-57822; CWE-918).
CORE METHOD:
  1. The middleware pattern (verbatim from the challenge source): `const requestHeaders = new Headers(request.headers); const res = { headers: requestHeaders, request: { headers: requestHeaders } }; return NextResponse.next(res);` — the `headers` key is the RESPONSE headers, so request headers are reflected into the response.
  2. Next.js interprets a `Location` response header as "proxy the request to that location" -> full-read SSRF (the method, headers and body are forwarded; the internal response is returned).
  3. The trigger is a query parameter that makes the middleware take that branch (`?utm_source=meta`). The PoC: `GET /?utm_source=meta` with `Location: http://localhost:8080/` -> the response body is the internal service's.
  4. Escalation: port-scan localhost with the same header (`http://localhost:<port>`); find an internal Jenkins on 8080 with authentication disabled; then `POST /?utm_source=meta` with `Location: http://localhost:8080/script` and body `script=println('whoami'.execute().text)` -> RCE.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a Next.js app whose middleware/proxy mirrors request headers into response headers (look for `NextResponse.next({ headers: ... })` or a `for (const key of Object.keys(resHeaders)) res.setHeader(key, ...)` loop in `middleware.ts`); a client-controllable header that survives to the response; a `Location` request header that changes the response body; a trigger query param (`utm_source`/`utm_medium`/`utm_campaign`) that switches the middleware into the reflecting branch.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated caller -> send a `Location` header plus a trigger param -> the middleware reflects it into the response headers -> Next.js proxies the request to the attacker-chosen internal URL and returns the response (escalation: full-read SSRF) -> aim at an internal admin/CI service (Jenkins `/script`, `/manage/script`) -> RCE. Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a Next.js app with the header-reflecting middleware and a reachable internal service; gain = full-read SSRF plus internal service compromise.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any Next.js target, send `GET /?utm_source=x` with `Location: http://localhost:8080/` (and `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`) and compare the response body with the internal service's; if the internal body comes back, the middleware reflects request headers into response headers and Next.js proxies the `Location`. Then port-scan localhost via the same header and aim at internal admin/CI endpoints. Rule: **in Next.js, `NextResponse.next({headers})` sets RESPONSE headers — a middleware that copies request headers into it hands you a `Location` header, and Next.js will proxy the request to it: full-read SSRF.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `57822`, `NextResponse`, `utm_source` (as a middleware trigger), or a `Location` request header reflected into a response. The ledger mentions CVE-2025-57822 twice (§17.225's rationale and ledger line 7127) only as a "shortcut" the authors deliberately avoided — neither records the request shape, the `NextResponse.next()` semantics, or the Jenkins escalation. New general method -> §17.331.

SOURCE: https://bugcrowd.com/disclosures/effab910-9481-4216-a384-88921af6cd36/reauthentication-can-be-bypassed-by-simply-dropping-the-request  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Bugcrowd disclosure page, full report details read IN FULL. Metadata: PostHog VDP, "Reauthentication can be bypassed by simply dropping the request", reporter `divideBy0`, submitted 2025-09-17, disclosed 2025-11-17, VRT Broken Authentication and Session Management > Failure to Invalidate Session > Long Timeout, priority P3, status Resolved.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no PostHog account was touched this pass; every step below is quoted verbatim from the disclosure).
CLASS: broken authentication / re-authentication enforcement bypass — the re-auth flow issues several requests and the one that actually validates the password can be DROPPED, while the client still clears the gate (CWE-287/CWE-613).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the disclosure):
  1. "Visit https://us.posthog.com in the Burp browser and sign in to your account."
  2. "Click the dropdown arrow at the top right corner. Click the settings icon next to your email. The page will prompt you to reauthenticate."
  3. "Enter any arbitrary value in the password field, but don't submit yet."
  4. "Turn on Intercept in Burp and click the submit button. If Burp intercepts multiple requests, forward them one by one until you reach the /api/login/ request."
  5. "Drop this request and turn off Intercept. The reauthentication page will be bypassed."
  6. "Modify any data and save—it will be successfully updated. Revisit https://us.posthog.com to confirm the changes."
  7. The reporter's own framing, verbatim: "The application has implemented a reauthentication mechanism to prevent unauthorized access to sensitive areas such as the user profile. However, this mechanism is not properly enforced and can be bypassed, allowing unauthorized access."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a re-auth flow that issues MULTIPLE requests on submit; the request that validates the password (`/api/login/`, `/api/relogin`, `/api/verify-password`, `/api/reauthenticate`) is a SEPARATE call from the one that unlocks the UI; the UI unlock is driven by the CLIENT (a status flag, a route change) rather than by the server's validation response; a re-auth gate enforced only by the UI.
CHAIN: any user with a valid session (primitive) -> the re-auth prompt is shown -> the attacker enters an ARBITRARY password and DROPS the `/api/login/` request (primitive: the validation request is never sent) -> the client-side gate is cleared without the server ever validating the password (escalation: re-auth bypassed) -> the attacker reads/modifies the sensitive profile data (impact: broken access control on the re-auth-protected surface). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a valid session and the ability to intercept/drop a request; gain = access to the re-auth-protected sensitive functionality without the password.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any re-auth prompt, enter a WRONG password, intercept the submit, and identify the request that actually checks the password; drop it (or abort it) and observe whether the UI still unlocks. If the sensitive action succeeds, the re-auth is UI-only. Then run the inverse: send the password-check request with a wrong password but let the UI proceed — if the action still succeeds, the server never enforced it. Rule: **a re-auth flow that issues multiple requests is a drop-the-check candidate — find the request that validates the password, drop it, and see whether the client still unlocks.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `posthog`, `PostHog`, `drop the request`, `reauthentication`, or a re-auth gate bypassed by dropping the validation request. The ledger's 6 `re-authentication` hits are different gaps (a route that returns a stored secret while confirmation is UI-only; a write API that persists restricted fields then applies them at re-auth; a session cookie used as a bearer credential; an IdP-linking flow that does not demand re-auth) — none records the drop-the-check technique. New general method -> §17.332.

SOURCE: https://security.lauritz-holtmann.de/post/xss-ato-gadgets/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the full post (published 2023-02-04), read IN FULL.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no OAuth/OIDC flow was exercised this pass; the gadget steps below are quoted from the post).
CLASS: OAuth/OIDC — escalating (self-)XSS to ACCOUNT TAKEOVER via SSO gadgets (token theft, not just session actions) (CWE-919 / CWE-639 -> CWE-348).
CORE METHOD:
  1. Preconditions, verbatim: "The victim user has an active session at the IdP. The victim user granted consent for the vulnerable application. The IdP supports the `prompt=none` auth. request parameter."
  2. GADGET 1 — implicit flow enabled (verbatim: "Client Configuration supports Implicit Flow (despite the Client using Code Flow with PKCE normally)"): inject JS that `window.open`s the IdP authorize URL with `response_type=token&prompt=none&client_id=<app>&redirect_uri=<app>`; because the victim has an IdP session and consent, the IdP redirects straight back with the `access_token` in the fragment; read `exploitWindow.location` (same-origin requirement) -> access-token theft. The post's own example: `https://accounts.google.com/o/oauth2/v2/auth?client_id=...&redirect_uri=https://lhq.at&state=a&response_type=token&scope=openid&prompt=none`.
  3. GADGET 2 — confidential client, code flow, NO PKCE (verbatim: "Authorization Code Injection"): start the code flow with an invalid `state` (or `response_mode=fragment`) so the app's own redemption fails/never happens; capture the `code` from the popup URL and redeem it at the token endpoint yourself.
  4. GADGET 3 — SPA, code flow + PKCE: compute `code_verifier`/`code_challenge` in JS, open the authorize URL, read the `code` from the popup URL, POST it with your `code_verifier` to the token endpoint -> access token.
  5. THE SELF-XSS + LOGIN CSRF CHAIN (verbatim steps): "1. Prepare an attacker account with JavaScript payload within the user name. 2. Login the victim user into the attacker account using the Login CSRF vulnerability. 3. Redirect the victim user to the target application. 4. Execute the JavaScript payload within the victim user's browser. 5. Use the SSO Gadget to obtain the victim user's `access_token`. 6. Use the `access_token` to perform actions within the target application on behalf of the victim user."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an OAuth/OIDC client whose authorize URL accepts `response_type=token` (implicit flow) even though the app uses code flow; a `redirect_uri` that lands the token in a readable URL/fragment; an app that does not send a `code_challenge` (no PKCE); a login flow with no `state`/CSRF token (Login CSRF); a self-XSS sink in a display name/username; an IdP that supports `prompt=none`.
CHAIN: a self-XSS (or any XSS) in the app origin (primitive) + Login CSRF (primitive: the victim is logged into the attacker's account, so the payload fires in the victim's browser) -> the victim still holds an IdP session -> the SSO gadget (implicit flow / code injection / PKCE code theft) obtains the victim's `access_token` (escalation: token theft, not just session actions) -> log into the victim's account (impact: full ATO). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an XSS in the app origin, an IdP session for the victim, prior consent, and `prompt=none` support; gain = the victim's access_token -> account takeover.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any OAuth/OIDC login, (a) capture the authorize request and replay it with `response_type=token&prompt=none` — if the IdP returns an `access_token` in the redirect fragment, the implicit flow is enabled and an XSS can steal it; (b) check whether the code flow sends a `code_challenge` — if not, capture the `code` (use an invalid `state` to stop the app redeeming it) and redeem it at the token endpoint yourself; (c) check the login POST for a CSRF token and test Login CSRF. Rule: **an XSS plus an OAuth gadget is an ATO — test the implicit flow (`response_type=token&prompt=none`), the missing PKCE, and Login CSRF; the token is the payoff, not the session actions.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `SSO gadget`, `prompt=none`, `implicit flow`, `response_type=token`, or an XSS->token-theft->ATO chain. The ledger's OAuth entries are a `redirect_uri` allowlist bypass, an authorization-code replay/lifetime bug, an OAuth callback failure-path session issue, a PIN/oob consent mis-statement, and the cross-app audience entry — none records the implicit-flow/code-injection/PKCE-code-theft token-stealing gadgets, nor the self-XSS + Login CSRF -> SSO gadget -> ATO chain. New general method -> §17.333.

SOURCE: https://www.intigriti.com/researchers/blog/hacking-tools/exploiting-pdf-generators-a-complete-guide-to-finding-ssrf-vulnerabilities-in-pdf-generators  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the full guide (published 2025-01-27), read IN FULL.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no PDF generator was stood up this pass; the payloads below are quoted from the guide).
CLASS: SSRF / LFD via an HTML->PDF generator — the BLIND-SSRF payload arsenal for when `<script>` is filtered, plus the `file:///` iframe LFD (CWE-918 -> CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. Find an HTML->PDF endpoint that renders user-controllable HTML server-side (invoice/report/export; the guide's example is `POST /api/invoice/export` with body `{"invoiceData":"<iframe src=\"https://example.com/\"></iframe>"}`).
  2. If `<script>` is filtered, force the headless browser to fetch an internal URL with RESOURCE-LOADING tags (the guide's list): `<base href="http://127.0.0.1/" />`, `<meta http-equiv="refresh" content="0; url=http://127.0.0.1/" />`, `<img src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<svg src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<input type="image" src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<video src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<audio src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<audio><source src="http://127.0.0.1"/></audio>`, `<embed src="http://127.0.0.1" />`, `<link rel="stylesheet" src="http://127.0.0.1" />`.
  3. Point each at an OAST/collaborator URL to confirm the server-side fetch (blind SSRF).
  4. LFD: `<iframe src="file:///etc/passwd" height="1000px" width="1000px"></iframe>` — the rendered PDF includes the file contents; the guide notes `--disable-local-file-access` (wkhtmltopdf) blocks it.
  5. Escalate: aim the fetch at `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/` (and GCP/Azure equivalents) and read the credentials out of the returned PDF.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an HTML->PDF/export endpoint that accepts markup; a `<script>` payload that is filtered/blocked while resource-loading tags are not; a PDF whose rendered content includes an internal URL's response or a local file's contents; a renderer built on a headless browser with local-file access enabled.
CHAIN: any user who can supply HTML to a PDF generator (primitive) -> inject a resource-loading tag pointing at an internal URL (primitive: the headless browser fetches it) -> blind SSRF / LFD (escalation: internal network access or local file read) -> cloud metadata credentials read out of the PDF (impact: internal compromise). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an HTML->PDF endpoint that renders attacker markup and a renderer that fetches external resources; gain = internal HTTP fetch or local file read.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every HTML->PDF/export endpoint, submit `<script>...</script>` first; if it is filtered, submit each resource-loading tag (`<base href>`, `<meta http-equiv=refresh>`, `<img>`, `<svg>`, `<input type=image>`, `<video>`, `<audio>`, `<embed>`, `<link rel=stylesheet>`) pointed at a collaborator URL and watch for a callback; then use `<iframe src="file:///etc/passwd" height=1000 width=1000>` for LFD and the cloud metadata URL for credentials. Rule: **when a PDF generator filters `<script>`, switch to resource-loading tags — the headless browser still fetches them, giving blind SSRF; and an `<iframe src="file:///">` renders the file into the PDF for LFD.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `invoiceData`, `invoice/export`, `disable-local-file-access`, `input type="image"`, `svg src`, `video src`, or the resource-loading tag arsenal. Related-but-recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.130 / V-06 record the PDF-generator SSRF class with a `</script><script>` breakout, `<iframe src>`, `file:///etc/passwd` and metadata escalation — the DELTA here is the resource-loading tag arsenal for the filtered-`<script>` case and the `--disable-local-file-access` tell. New general method -> §17.334.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 105: 5 new techniques appended + 5 §17 slots). NEW METHODS: (1) a file-upload API that lets the CLIENT declare the stored `contentType` and returns a pre-signed `uploadUrl`, plus an in-origin `?action=view` URL, turns a `.png`-named HTML file into stored XSS -> admin-session script -> `POST /api/w/{sid}/members/{attackerId}` role=admin -> workspace takeover (#3115705 Dust -> §17.330); (2) in Next.js, `NextResponse.next({headers})` sets RESPONSE headers, so a middleware that copies request headers into it hands you a `Location` header that Next.js proxies to -> full-read SSRF -> internal Jenkins `/script` -> RCE (Intigriti CVE-2025-57822 -> §17.331); (3) a re-auth flow that issues multiple requests can be bypassed by DROPPING the `/api/login/` request that validates the password while the client still clears the gate (Bugcrowd PostHog -> §17.332); (4) an XSS plus an OAuth gadget is an ATO — test the implicit flow (`response_type=token&prompt=none`), the missing PKCE, and Login CSRF; the self-XSS + Login CSRF -> SSO gadget -> access_token chain (Lauritz Holtmann -> §17.333); (5) when a PDF generator filters `<script>`, switch to resource-loading tags (`<base href>`, `<meta http-equiv=refresh>`, `<img>`, `<svg>`, `<input type=image>`, `<video>`, `<audio>`, `<embed>`) for blind SSRF, and `<iframe src="file:///etc/passwd" height=1000 width=1000>` for LFD (Intigriti -> §17.334). NOT APPENDED: #3286945 (Grafana CVE-2025-4123, `visibility: full` — the reporter explicitly states the XSS and SSRF chains "were not fully executed"; only the open redirect was confirmed, and an open redirect is core-ineligible, so there is no proven chain); #3277276 (Mars SQLi, `visibility: no-content`, 0 B — title-only); #3037447 (TikTok stored XSS, `no-content`); #2974307 (Autodesk stored XSS, `no-content`); #3205667 (AWS elemental XSS, `no-content`). 0 fabricated. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `curl`/`web_extract` against hackerone.com, intigriti.com, bugcrowd.com and security.lauritz-holtmann.de.

## Batch 106 — reader pass 2026-11-10 (6 sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order)

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2425873  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL. Metadata: Monero, "[Monero wallet RPC] File precreation to file ownership and credentials leak", weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, reporter `selmelc`, submitted 2024-03-20, disclosed 2024-09-04, state Closed/Resolved.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Monero RPC or second local user was stood up this pass; every step and code line below is quoted verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: local file PRECREATION against a PREDICTABLE secret-file path — the daemon creates its credential file with a create-if-absent open and NO `O_EXCL`, so a pre-existing file keeps its original owner/mode and the daemon's secret is written into the attacker's file (CWE-284 / CWE-73 / CWE-522).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. `std::string temp = "monero-wallet-rpc." + bind_port + ".login"; rpc_login_file = tools::private_file::create(temp);` — "the monero-wallet-rpc will generate a file with a none random name and will later write the username and password of the RPC in it." The bind port is the only variable, so the path is fully predictable (`monero-wallet-rpc.16969.login`).
  2. `const int fdr = open(name.c_str(), (O_RDONLY | O_CREAT), S_IRUSR);` — "the O_EXCL flag isn't used, this means that if the file exists before this line of code is triggered then the file is simply opened instead of being created and will keep all its original meta information such as permissions and owner."
  3. "an attacker could precreate the monero-wallet-rpc.[port].login file and give themself arbitrary permissions and full ownership of the credentials file before the victim starts the RPC server."
  4. Repro, verbatim: two local users A and V in the same working directory; as A `touch monero-wallet-rpc.16969.login;chmod a+rwx monero-wallet-rpc.16969.login`; as V `monero-wallet-rpc --wallet-file /home/selmelc/Monero/wallets/selmelc/selmelc.keys --prompt-for-password --rpc-bind-port 16969`; as A `ls -l monero-wallet-rpc.16969.login; cat monero-wallet-rpc.16969.login` -> A owns the credential file and can read it.
  5. Fix, verbatim: "the file is deleted as the RPC server goes down anyway so using the O_EXCL flag would patch this vulnerability without affecting functionality at all."
  6. Impact, verbatim: "A confidential file (RPC .login) can be tampered and disclosed to an attacker."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a daemon/service writing a secret (RPC login, API key, token, password, private key) to a file whose name derives from a user-controllable/predictable value (bind port, pid, username, hostname, wallet name) in a directory writable by others; a create-if-absent open without `O_EXCL`; a `.login`/`.cred`/`.token`/`.key`/`.secret` file next to the daemon's working directory; a secret file whose ownership/permissions survive from a pre-existing file.
CHAIN: unprivileged local user A on the same host (shared multi-user host / shared CWD) -> A precreates the predictable credential file with `chmod a+rwx` (the create path has no `O_EXCL`, so the pre-existing file is reused with its original owner/mode) -> the victim V starts the RPC server on the known port -> the daemon writes its RPC username+password into A's file -> A reads the credentials -> full control of the victim's wallet RPC -> theft of the wallet's funds/keys.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every service/daemon in scope that persists a secret to disk, recover the path from docs/config/strace/error text, check whether the name is predictable and the directory is writable by others, precreate the exact path with `touch` + `chmod 777` (or a SYMLINK to a file you own) BEFORE the service starts, start it as a victim, and read the file — if the secret is there and owned by you, the create path lacks `O_EXCL`; then test the symlink variant, which converts the secret write into an arbitrary-file-write. Rule: **a secret written to a predictable path with a create-if-absent open (no `O_EXCL`) is capturable by pre-creating the file — precreate it (or symlink it) before the victim starts the service and read the secret.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `2425873`, `selmelc`, `private_file`, `monero-wallet-rpc`, `O_EXCL`, `precreat`, or a predictable secret-file path captured by precreation. The ledger's symlink entries are symlink-DEREFERENCE bugs in a loader/viewer/helper (GitLab bulk-import, LGTM reserved-name #697055 / §17.197, CI-log copy-out, Keybase privileged-helper TOCTOU §17.182, git worktree `.git`) — none is a create-if-absent open of a predictable secret file. New general method -> §17.335.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1787644  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (the body is the complete PoC: one sign-in + one URL). Metadata: HackerOne, "Any organization's assets pending review can be downloaded", weakness Information Disclosure, severity high (score 8.3, confidentiality high), reporter `jobert`, submitted 2022-11-29, disclosed 2022-11-29, state Closed/Resolved, asset hackerone.com.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no HackerOne account or org asset list was touched this pass; every step below is quoted verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: IDOR/BOLA + sensitive information disclosure — an organization-scoped CSV EXPORT endpoint keyed by the org HANDLE in the URL PATH, with no membership/ownership check, so any authenticated user can download any organization's pending-review asset list (CWE-639 / CWE-863 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. "# Steps to reproduce - sign in as any user - visit https://hackerone.com/organizations/:handle/assets/download_pending_reviews.csv, where `:handle` is the organization you want to download the assets for"
  2. "## Impact — This may leak sensitive data about an organization's attack surface."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an export/download/report endpoint whose TENANT identifier is a URL PATH segment (`/organizations/:handle/...`, `/teams/:slug/export`, `/<tenant>/assets.csv`) rather than a session-derived value; a `.csv`/`.json`/`.zip` export that returns another tenant's rows; an endpoint reachable by ANY authenticated user that takes a handle/slug enumerable from public profile/URL pages; a "pending review"/"pending"/"draft" state filter the org did not intend to publish.
CHAIN: any authenticated HackerOne user (a free account, no membership in the target org) -> GET `/organizations/<victim-handle>/assets/download_pending_reviews.csv` (the org handle in the path is trusted; no ownership/membership check) -> the org's full pending-review asset list is returned -> the attacker gains the org's asset inventory (sensitive info disclosure with PoC; rated High 8.3, confidentiality high).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: enumerate every export/download/report endpoint in scope and classify its tenant identifier: session-derived (safe) vs. caller-supplied. For each caller-supplied one — especially a PATH segment or a query/body param — replay it with (a) a second org/team you own and (b) a victim org's handle harvested from a public profile/URL, and check whether the response carries the victim's rows. A `.csv`/`.json`/`.zip` export returning a foreign tenant's data is the IDOR; size the leak by enumerating handles. Rule: **an export endpoint is only as authorized as its tenant identifier — when the tenant is a URL handle/slug rather than a session value, replay it with a foreign handle and check whose data comes back.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `1787644`, `jobert`, `download_pending_reviews`, `pending review`, `pending_reviews`, or a path-parameterized org-handle CSV export. Related-but-distinct: the export/render IDOR entry (New Relic #397137 / §17.180) carries the tenant id in the REQUEST BODY plus a format-switchable artifact URL; the partner key-generation IDOR is a write/mint endpoint; the GraphQL membership-collection entry is a node query addressed by a public handle, not a file export. No new §17 slot — the general method (export/download IDOR) is already §17.180; the delta is only the identifier channel (path handle vs body id).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2396549  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: no-content` (empty `vulnerability_information`) so the FULL public text is the `summaries` field (researcher summary + team summary), read this pass. Metadata: Yelp, "Privilege Escalation - A Non Owner User Who Does not Have access to the user management can invite other users to the restaurant page", weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, severity low, reporter `vijaysimha-reddy`, submitted 2024-02-29, disclosed 2025-01-29, state Closed/Resolved, asset *.yelp.com.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Yelp business account or invite endpoint was touched this pass; every step below is quoted verbatim from the report's public summaries).
CLASS: broken access control / privilege escalation — a business member whose role is DENIED the "user management" permission can still call the member-invite action, and the invited members are INVISIBLE to the owner's user-management view (CWE-863 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the researcher summary):
  1. "Yelp Business has a option where the owner can invite the other users to their Business account and when the new user gets added to the business account, they can perform every functionality except the user management module."
  2. "Using this vulnerability, a user with no access to the user management module can invite other users to the business."
  3. "Once the attacker invites the user, the owner cannot see the new user that got invited."
  4. Team summary, verbatim: "An unauthorised business user was able to invite other users to become members of the same business account without being visible in the User Management page."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a role/permission the UI hides or disables (a "User Management"/"Members"/"Team" module absent from the low-priv role's navigation) while the underlying invite/member-write API is callable by that role; a member-write that succeeds but whose result does NOT appear in the owner's/admin's member list or audit view (the write is accepted but the read-back is filtered by a different permission); an invite endpoint returning 2xx for a caller whose role lacks the management scope.
CHAIN: any business member under a non-owner role (a low-priv role the UI denies the user-management module) -> call the member-invite action directly (the invite API enforces no user-management permission, only membership) -> new users are added to the business account -> the added members are NOT shown in the owner's User Management page (hidden persistence — the owner cannot see or remove the attacker-added members).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any multi-role org/business account, take the LOWEST role the UI denies a management feature and (a) confirm the feature is hidden, then (b) capture the feature's API call from an owner session (or infer it: `POST /<org>/members`, `/invite`, `/users`, a GraphQL `inviteMember`/`addUser` mutation) and replay it with the low-priv session — a 2xx/new member = the permission is UI-only; then (c) as the OWNER load the member/user-management list and check whether the attacker-added member appears — if it does NOT, the write is invisible to the owner (stealth/persistence), which is the escalation. Rule: **a management feature the UI hides is not necessarily a feature the API forbids — call it with the denied role, then check whether the owner's own management view shows the result; an invisible member is a persistence primitive.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `2396549`, `vijaysimha-reddy`, `user management` (as a permission gate), `business user` (as an actor), or an invited member invisible to the owner's management view. Related-but-distinct: the HackerOne embedded-submission entry (#418767) is a program-level CONTROL skipped by an ALTERNATE ROUTE (route-level, not role-level); the invite-acceptance IDOR is a client-supplied invite id; the mass-assignment email/verified entry is a PATCH field injection; the GitLab type-confusion entry launders a low-priv ability through a generic resolver. New general method -> §17.336.

SOURCE: https://bugcrowd.com/disclosures/d5f4aa80-77da-49bb-a259-afe23b6bfa0a/authentication-bypass-in-the-rest-api-via-xss-on-safari-and-chrome-ios-iphone-only  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Bugcrowd CrowdStream disclosure page — full report details plus the reporter's own code-review section read IN FULL. Metadata: Atlassian engagement, "Authentication Bypass in the Rest API via XSS on Safari and Chrome (iOS/iPhone only)", reporter `c4v4r0n`, submitted 2025-01-22, disclosed 2025-08-04, VRT Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) > Stored > CSRF/URL-Based, priority P2, reward $3,600, status Resolved, CVSS:3.0 High 7.9 (AV:N/AC:H/PR:L/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:L).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Confluence instance and no iOS device was touched this pass; every step, code line and payload below is quoted from the disclosure).
CLASS: stored XSS via a SERVER-SIDE MIME-TYPE DENY-LIST bypass — the stored content type comes from a CLIENT-TAMPERABLE `mimeType` GET parameter, and the Content-Disposition inline/attachment decision is a substring match against a fixed executable-content-type array; `video/mp2t` is not on the list and WebKit mobile (Safari/Chrome on iOS) renders it as HTML -> the payload executes inline in the app origin -> it mints a PERSONAL ACCESS TOKEN for the victim -> full REST-API authentication bypass (CWE-79 -> CWE-287).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the disclosure):
  1. Upload a file to the comment attachment endpoint `POST /plugins/drag-and-drop/upload.action`. Confluence takes the filename (with extension) and its mime-type as parameters. An `.html` file is stored, and the returned URL is `…/download/attachments/{{ID}}/{{FileName}}`, but the response carries `Content-Disposition: attachment` plus `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, so the browser downloads it instead of rendering it.
  2. The inline/attachment decision is `guessContentDispositionHeader(fileName, mimeContentType, userAgent)` -> `isExecutableContent(fileName, mimeContentType)` -> `isExecutableFileExtension(fileName) || isExecutableContentType(contentType)`; both must return false to reach `inline`.
  3. `isExecutableFileExtension()` matches the FILENAME against a malicious-extension array — bypass with any other extension, e.g. `.png` ("this will not affect the web browser behavior").
  4. `isExecutableContentType()` matches the stored mime-type with `contentType.toLowerCase(Locale.US).contains(executableContentType)` against a 28-entry array (`image/svg+xml`, `application/pdf`, `text/html`, `application/xhtml+xml`, `text/xml`, `application/xml`, …). A type that is NOT on the list and that the target browser still parses as HTML passes. The reporter's type: `video/mp2t` — "a mime-type that is interpreted as HTML by WebKit mobile based browsers (Safari and Chrome in iOS)".
  5. The stored mime-type is a REQUEST PARAMETER: "When uploading the file, we need to intercept the request and change the mimeType GET parameter to **video/mp2t**." The client declares the type that the server later uses for the Content-Disposition decision.
  6. Upload an HTML file named `token.png` containing the report's payload: `fetch("/rest/pat/latest/tokens", { headers: {'Accept':'application/json','Content-Type':'application/json'}, method:"POST", body: JSON.stringify({name:"Backdoor", expirationDuration:90}) })` then `alert(content.rawToken)` — with the `mimeType` parameter set to `video/mp2t`. The file is saved and its URL is `…/download/attachments/163936/token.png?version=1&modificationDate=…&api=v2`.
  7. Send that URL to the victim's iPhone/iPad. On open, WebKit renders the `.png` as HTML (because the stored type is `video/mp2t`), the script runs with the victim's session, `POST /rest/pat/latest/tokens` mints a 90-day PAT in the victim's account and the raw token is returned.
  8. Use the stolen PAT as a Bearer credential against the REST API: `curl -H 'Authorization: Bearer <PAT>' http://…/rest/api/user?username=admin` — "Because we can use the PAT token to authenticate in Confluence, it is a good target for our XSS payload." The reporter's own framing: "This is a proof that is really hard to implement a deny-list and the focus should be in a more restrict approach like an allow-list."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an upload endpoint whose stored content type comes from a client-supplied `mimeType`/`contentType`/`type` parameter (query string or form field) rather than from the file bytes or the extension; a Content-Disposition inline/attachment decision implemented as a substring match against a FIXED executable-content-type array (look for `isExecutableContentType`, `executableContentTypes`, `guessContentDispositionHeader`, `HostileExtensionDetector` in a decompiled jar); a `.png`/`.txt` file that renders INLINE (no `Content-Disposition: attachment`, no `nosniff`) while `.html` does not; a same-origin API that mints a long-lived credential (`/rest/pat/latest/tokens`, `/tokens`, `/api/keys`) reachable by the injected script.
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can comment/upload (primitive) -> the upload's stored type is whatever the client put in the `mimeType` parameter (primitive: the type is client-declared, not derived) -> `video/mp2t` is absent from the executable-type deny-list so the response is `Content-Disposition: inline` (primitive: deny-list bypass) -> WebKit mobile parses `video/mp2t` as HTML and executes the payload in the app origin (escalation: stored XSS with the victim's session) -> the payload calls `POST /rest/pat/latest/tokens` and mints a PAT for the victim (escalation: a durable credential only the victim can revoke) -> the PAT authenticates to the REST API as the victim (impact: full authentication bypass / account-level API access). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an upload primitive plus a victim who opens the file URL in Safari/Chrome on iOS/iPadOS; gain = a live PAT for the victim's account and full REST-API access as the victim.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any file-upload flow, capture the upload request and look for a client-supplied type parameter (`mimeType`, `contentType`, `type`, a `Content-Type` form field). Set it to a type that is (a) NOT on the target's executable-type list and (b) still parsed as HTML by the relevant engine — start with `video/mp2t` (WebKit mobile), then `text/cache-manifest`, `application/x-httpd-php`, `image/svg+xml` variants, `text/plain` with an HTML body — while keeping a benign extension (`.png`, `.txt`, `.jpg`). Open the returned URL on the matching browser/device; if the payload executes inline, the deny-list is the only control. Then look for a same-origin credential-minting endpoint the script can call. Rule: **a content-type deny-list is a control over a value the client can set — find the type parameter, pick a type the target browser still parses as HTML but the list does not contain, and the upload becomes stored XSS; the payoff is a same-origin token-minting endpoint.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `mp2t`, `mimeType` (as a client-supplied upload parameter), `isExecutableContentType`, `executableContentTypes`, `guessContentDispositionHeader`, `rest/pat`, `personal access token` (as an XSS escalation), or a mime-type deny-list bypass. Related-but-distinct material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.330 / #3115705 records a client-declared `contentType` in a JSON upload-URL request plus an in-origin `?action=view` URL — the delta here is the GET-parameter type controlling a SERVER-SIDE DENY-LIST plus the WebKit `video/mp2t` render, and the PAT-minting escalation. The ledger's `attachment filename` hit (~line 7194, Mailcow CVE-2026-40873) is an admin-quarantine HTML-injection sink, not an upload-type bypass; the other file-upload entries are SVG/DOCX/ODF/PDF/unrestricted-upload. New general method -> §17.338.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3401612  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, 3,786-char `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL. Metadata: Revive Adserver, "IDOR Vulnerability in Banner Deletion", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity HIGH (CVSS 7.1), reporter `cyberjoker`, submitted 2025-10-27, disclosed 2025-11-19, state Closed/Resolved, Revive Adserver 6.0.1.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Revive Adserver instance was stood up this pass; every step below is quoted from the report body).
CLASS: IDOR / horizontal privilege escalation — the deletion endpoint authorizes the PARENT objects (`clientid`, `campaignid`) but never checks ownership of the CHILD object being deleted (`bannerid`) (CWE-639).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. Prereq: two Manager accounts, each with its own client, campaign and banners.
  2. Login as Manager A; open `http://<host>/www/admin/campaign-banners.php?clientid=100&campaignid=100` (your own client/campaign).
  3. Extract your own CSRF token from any action link (the `token` query parameter, 32-char hex).
  4. Enumerate the victim's banner id — "Banner IDs are sequential integers" (victim's = 2001).
  5. Craft `http://<host>/www/admin/banner-delete.php?token=<YOUR_TOKEN>&clientid=100&campaignid=100&bannerid=2001` — `clientid` and `campaignid` are YOURS (so the authorization check passes), `bannerid` is the VICTIM's (never checked).
  6. Open it in your own session -> "You'll get redirected with a 'Banner has been deleted' confirmation"; the report's screenshot shows Manager B's banner deleted by Manager A.
  7. The code shape, verbatim: "The endpoint checks if I have access to the client and campaign (lines 30-31), but it never verifies I own the specific banner I'm trying to delete (missing check at line 32). The code then loops through the provided banner IDs and deletes them without any ownership validation (lines 40-48)." And the discovery method: "I discovered this while comparing similar deletion endpoints. The `campaign-delete.php` file properly validates ownership inside the loop, but `banner-delete.php` doesn't."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a request carrying BOTH a parent identifier and a child identifier (`clientid` + `campaignid` + `bannerid`, `team_id` + `project_id` + `item_id`, `org` + `repo` + `file`); an authorization check that names only the parent(s); a sibling endpoint in the same app that DOES validate the child (the comparison is the tell — find the fixed sibling and diff its check); sequential integer child ids; a bulk/delete action that loops over the supplied ids.
CHAIN: any Manager (primitive) -> the delete endpoint validates only `clientid`/`campaignid`, which the attacker supplies from their OWN objects (primitive: parent-scoped authorization) -> the child id (`bannerid`) is taken on faith and the row is deleted (escalation: a write outside the caller's tenancy) -> the victim's banners are destroyed and the log shows a legitimate-looking deletion (impact: cross-tenant data destruction / horizontal privilege escalation). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = any low-priv Manager account plus a victim banner id (sequential integers, or harvested from any read surface); gain = deletion of another manager's banners -> revenue loss and campaign sabotage.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every delete/update endpoint that takes a parent id AND a child id, replay the request with the parent id from YOUR OWN object and the child id from a VICTIM object (enumerate sequential ids, or take one from any read/list response); a 2xx/redirect with a success message and the foreign child gone = the bug. Then diff against the sibling endpoint that performs the same operation correctly — the missing line is the finding. Rule: **when a request carries a parent and a child identifier, the check is usually on the parent; supply your own parent and a foreign child and the child is deleted unauthorised — and the sibling endpoint that validates the child is your proof.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `bannerid`, `campaignid` (as an IDOR param), `clientid` (as an IDOR param), `banner-delete.php`, `Revive`, or a parent-authorized/child-unchecked pattern. Related-but-distinct: the ledger's polymorphic-target entries (~line 8546) are TYPE+ID pairs where one branch skips the scope; the export-IDOR entries carry a tenant handle; the GitLab mirror entries are relationship/actor validation. None records the parent-object-authorized / child-object-unchecked deletion shape, nor the "diff against the fixed sibling endpoint" discovery method. New general method -> §17.339.

SOURCE: https://www.intigriti.com/researchers/blog/hacking-tools/ssrf-vulnerabilities-in-nextjs-targets  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Intigriti article (published 2025-09-28), full text read IN FULL (three SSRF vectors; vector 2 is already recorded as §17.331).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Next.js app was stood up this pass; the config and request shapes below are quoted from the article).
CLASS: SSRF (blind) via the Next.js Image Optimization endpoint — `/_next/image?url=` fetches the attacker's URL server-side when `images.remotePatterns` is configured with the `hostname: '**'` wildcard (CWE-918).
CORE METHOD:
  1. Next.js's `<Image>` component proxies every image through `/_next/image?url=<location>`; the optimization/caching work is done server-side, so the `url` parameter is a server-side fetch primitive.
  2. The article's wildcard config, verbatim: `images: { remotePatterns: [ { protocol: 'https', hostname: '**', }, { protocol: 'http', hostname: '**', } ] }` — "Developers do this to allow loading images from any host, unknowingly introducing a new attack vector."
  3. Send `GET /_next/image?url=http://<collaborator-or-internal-host>/` (and `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`) — the response is the optimized image fetched from that host, i.e. blind SSRF.
  4. Even with NO wildcard: "you should still attempt to probe for potential whitelisted hosts (such as CDNs) as the underlying Image component, which is responsible for pulling the initial version of the image, follows redirects by default. A simple open URL redirect vulnerability could, in practice, open up a way to reach any arbitrary host." -> pair a whitelisted host with an open redirect to break out.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a Next.js app with `/_next/image` present; `remotePatterns` with `hostname: '**'` (or a permissive regex) in `next.config.js`; a `url` parameter that changes the response body when pointed at a collaborator; a redirect on a whitelisted CDN host that the image optimizer will follow.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated caller -> `GET /_next/image?url=http://169.254.169.254/...` -> the Next.js server fetches the internal URL and returns the (image-optimized) response (escalation: blind SSRF / internal network reach) -> cloud metadata credentials or internal service responses (impact: internal compromise). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a Next.js app with the image optimizer enabled and a wildcard (or a whitelisted host that redirects); gain = server-side fetches to internal/metadata addresses.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any Next.js target send `GET /_next/image?url=http://<your-collaborator>/x.png` and then `?url=http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`; a callback or a changed response body = the server fetched it. If the wildcard is absent, look for a whitelisted host with an open redirect and point `url` at `https://<whitelisted>/redirect?to=<internal>`. Rule: **`/_next/image?url=` is a server-side fetch — the `remotePatterns` wildcard is the config bug; without a wildcard, ride a whitelisted host's open redirect.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `remotePatterns`, `_next/image` (as an SSRF sink), `hostname: '**'`, or the image-optimizer redirect-follow. The ledger's only `_next/image` mention (~line 7128) is a middleware-matcher exclusion inside a detection signal; §17.331 is the Middleware `Location`-header SSRF; §17.218 is Server-Action broken access control. New general method -> §17.340.

SOURCE: https://www.intigriti.com/researchers/blog/hacking-tools/ssrf-vulnerabilities-in-nextjs-targets  2026-11-10
ROUTE: same Intigriti article as the previous entry, vector 3 read IN FULL; the underlying research is Assetnote's "Digging for SSRF in Next.js apps" (CVE-2024-34351).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Next.js app was stood up this pass; the conditions and steps below are quoted from the article).
CLASS: SSRF via Next.js Server Actions on Next.js <= 14.1.1 — the framework validates a relative-path redirect by sending a HEAD request to the host in the incoming request's `Host` header and then a GET, so a controllable `Host` turns the validation into an attacker-directed request (CWE-918).
CORE METHOD:
  1. Vulnerability conditions, verbatim: "(1) Next.js (version 14.1.1 or lower) must be running in a self-hosted environment; (2) your Next.js target needs to make use of Server Actions; (3) the Server Action must perform a redirect to a relative path."
  2. Why the third condition matters, verbatim: "Next.js v14.1.1 and earlier versions stream responses back to clients using a specific validation process. The framework first sends a `HEAD` request to the host specified in the incoming request's host header, validates the response's content-type, and then proceeds with the `GET` request to retrieve the actual content."
  3. Exploitation: "The first request will trigger the vulnerable Next.js Server Action component to initiate a HEAD HTTP request to our OAST server specified in the Host header." Configure the OAST server to answer the HEAD with the expected `content-type` header.
  4. "When the server subsequently receives the `GET` request, we respond with a `302` HTTP redirect containing our target resource in the `Location` header, such as the AWS Metadata endpoint URI." -> the framework follows the redirect and the internal resource is fetched.
  5. The attacker controls the `Host` header of the original Server Action request, so the HEAD/GET pair goes to the attacker's server and the redirect then aims the framework at the internal target.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a self-hosted Next.js app on <= 14.1.1 (fingerprint via headers, `x-nextjs-` headers, or a version in a changelog/error page); a Server Action that redirects to a relative path; a `Host` header that changes where the app makes its outbound request; an OAST callback on a HEAD request followed by a GET.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated caller -> invoke the vulnerable Server Action with `Host: <attacker-OAST>` -> Next.js sends a HEAD to the attacker's host and the attacker answers with the expected content-type -> Next.js sends the GET and the attacker answers `302 Location: http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/` -> the framework follows the redirect and fetches the internal resource (escalation: SSRF to cloud metadata) -> IAM credentials (impact: internal compromise). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a self-hosted Next.js <= 14.1.1 app using Server Actions that redirect to a relative path, plus an attacker-controlled host for the validation request; gain = a server-side fetch of an internal URL, disclosed via the OAST/redirect behaviour.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on a Next.js <= 14.1.1 target, find a Server Action that redirects to a relative path, replay it with `Host: <your-OAST>` and watch for a HEAD callback; answer the HEAD with a `Content-Type` the validator accepts, then answer the GET with `302 Location: http://169.254.169.254/...` and watch for the internal fetch. Rule: **Next.js <= 14.1.1 validates a relative redirect by calling the `Host` you send — so the `Host` header is the SSRF knob: answer the validation HEAD with the right content-type, then 302 the GET at the internal target.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `34351`, `Server Actions` (as an SSRF sink), a `Host`-header + Server Action combination, or the HEAD-validation/302-redirect pair. §17.218 records Server-Action broken access control (a different bug class on the same framework surface); §17.331 is the Middleware `Location` SSRF; §17.340 is the image-optimizer SSRF. New general method -> §17.341.

SOURCE: https://infosecwriteups.com/account-takeover-across-multiple-programs-via-featurebase-integration-32214666123e  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Medium/InfoSec Writeups article, full text read IN FULL. Metadata: JEETPAL, "Account Takeover Across Multiple Programs via Featurebase Integration", engagement October 2025; the vendor's reply is quoted — "this is not our issue instead it should be fix by websites owner" — with a link to Featurebase's "Secure your installation (required by default)" JWT doc.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Featurebase deployment was touched this pass; every step and endpoint below is quoted from the article).
CLASS: IDOR / identity spoofing — an unauthenticated "identify" endpoint upserts a user record from CLIENT-SUPPLIED identity attributes (`userId`, `email`, `name`) and never checks that the supplied `userId` belongs to the caller, so an attacker can impersonate any user; the same trust then yields the victim's access token -> account takeover (CWE-639 / CWE-287).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the article):
  1. Identify the provider: "The common way to figure out if your target is using this provider or not is by visiting the following subdomain on target: `feedback.example.com`", plus the dorks `site:feedback.*.*` (Google) and Shodan.
  2. Register an account on the target's `feedback.example.com`.
  3. Send `POST /api/v1/user/identify` with body `{ "organization":"example", "email":"victim@example.com", "name":"Jeet Pal", "userId":"733244", "profilePicture":"https://..." }` — "Here we have to modify the `userId` for the someone else to takeover account via email change functionality."
  4. The response reflects the changed identity: "In response then you can view the Email changed after that you can ask for reset password and then takeover the account."
  5. Second path: `POST /api/v1/user` (during account modification) then `PATCH /api/v1/user` — "We can have the access token of the user too. which you can use to make a request for modify the settings."
  6. Root cause, verbatim: "The application trusted client-controlled identity attributes without validating whether the supplied `userId` actually belonged to the authenticated user. This allowed an attacker to impersonate another Featurebase account."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a third-party feedback/feature-request widget on a `feedback.*` subdomain; an `/api/v1/user/identify` (or `/identify`, `/user/identify`, `/api/identify`) endpoint accepting `userId`/`email`/`name` in the body; a response that echoes the supplied identity; a user object whose `userId` is a small integer; a `PATCH`/`POST` user endpoint that returns an access token.
CHAIN: any anonymous caller (primitive: the identify endpoint is unauthenticated) -> POST the victim's `email` with the victim's `userId` (primitive: client-controlled identity attributes are trusted) -> the server upserts/overwrites the victim's record, changing the email on it (escalation: identity overwrite) -> request a password reset to the new email, or read the victim's access token from the `PATCH /api/v1/user` response (escalation: credential/token disclosure) -> log in as the victim (impact: account takeover, and the same widget serves many programs so the primitive repeats across targets). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a target running the widget without identity verification (no `user_hash`/JWT) and a victim `userId` (sequential/small integers); gain = the victim's account and access token.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any target with a third-party feedback/widget subdomain, POST to `/api/v1/user/identify` with a victim's email plus a `userId` you choose, then GET/PATCH `/api/v1/user` and check whether the response carries the victim's record or an access token; then check whether the email on the account changed (the password-reset primitive). Rule: **an "identify" endpoint is an identity-write primitive — if it trusts a client-supplied `userId`/`email`, you can overwrite the victim's identity and then reset or token-read your way in.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `featurebase`, `user/identify`, `identify endpoint`, or a client-supplied `userId` impersonation. Related-but-distinct: the ledger's mass-assignment entries are PATCH-field injection on an authenticated user; the OAuth entries are token flows; the `identity verification` hit (~line 3193) is a docs-SaaS template-injection sink. None records an unauthenticated identity-upsert endpoint. New general method -> §17.342.

SOURCE: https://www.vulnano.com/2025/09/remote-code-execution-though.html  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Vulnano writeup (published 2025-09-02), full text read IN FULL (the `?m=1` mobile view carried the complete attack steps and the reward timeline). Metadata: Dzmitry Lukyanenka (Vulnano), "Remote code execution though vulnerability in Facebook Messenger for Windows (June 2024)", found at Meta BountyCon; reported 2024-06-03, reward $34,500 -> escalated to $75,000 + bonuses = $111,750 on 2024-08-23 after the researcher argued the reward table for mobile RCE. Test env: attacker Pixel Fold / Android 14 (`com.facebook.orca` 459.1.0.57.108), victim Windows 11 22H2 + Messenger for Windows 212.1.0.15.233.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Messenger client was touched this pass; every step and path below is quoted from the writeup).
CLASS: path traversal in an encrypted-chat ATTACHMENT FILENAME -> arbitrary file write on the victim's Windows machine -> DLL hijacking -> remote code execution with no user interaction (CWE-22 -> CWE-73 -> RCE).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the writeup):
  1. "I've sent attachment with `%2e%2e%5c` symbols and Messenger for Windows placed my attachment by next path: `C:\Users\vulna\AppData\Local\Messenger\TamStorage\media_bank\AdvancedCrypto\100027775233281\persistent\da7a85eb-…\2024\06\03\20240603T091559605.att.04484a15-…\..\test.bat`" — the filename is URL-decoded and concatenated into the storage path without sanitisation; the traversal lives in the ATTACHMENT NAME, not in any URL or file content.
  2. "Path traversal bug which triggers by sending encrypted chat message to victim. If victim can receive messages from you, you can place file into any location on victim's Windows machine via Messenger."
  3. The obstacle: Windows `MAX_PATH` = 256 characters. The storage prefix alone is 212 characters ("It is 212 symbols. We have 44 available."); with the maximum `..\` repeats the path reaches `C:\Users\vulna\AppData\Local\` at 244 characters — "only 12 are left".
  4. The pivot: spend the remaining budget on a DLL that a neighbouring application in `AppData\Local` loads on startup. "Viber tried (and may be tries) to load `qwave.dll` file from `C:\Users\vulna\AppData\Local\Viber`. This file did't existed in that folder. `..\qwave.dll` - 12 symbols!"
  5. Attack steps, verbatim: (1) "Sending file with name: `%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5c%2e%2e%5cViber%5cqwave.dll`" (nine `..\` = 12 characters, then `Viber\qwave.dll`); (2) "The file is going into `c:\Users\vulna\AppData\Local\Viber\qwave.dll`"; (3) "When Viber is launching it starts executing `c:\Users\vulna\AppData\Local\Viber\qwave.dll`"; (4) "Remote code execution without user interaction (if we have ability to send messages to victim)!"
  6. Why the encrypted-chat path matters: "Because chat is encrypted everything is verified on recipient side: client app must validate all data received from thirdparties and we can catch something interesting there." — the encrypted branch is a separate parser/validator from the normal attachment path.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a desktop/mobile messenger that writes an attachment into a per-user storage tree using a SERVER-OR-PEER-SUPPLIED filename; a filename that is URL-decoded (`%2e%2e%5c` -> `..\`) and concatenated rather than passed through `basename()`; a fixed deep prefix (long user id / GUID / date path) that eats most of the 256-char budget; a sibling application in the same `AppData\Local` tree that loads a DLL from its own directory (check with a DLL-hijack scanner: `qwave.dll`, `version.dll`, `dbghelp.dll`, `dwmapi.dll`); an encrypted/e2e chat branch with its own attachment validator.
CHAIN: any user who can send a message to the victim (primitive: the ability to message) -> the attachment filename is traversed to escape the storage tree (primitive: filename path traversal, arbitrary file write) -> the write is capped by MAX_PATH, so the attacker budgets the remaining characters (escalation: reach `AppData\Local` and target a known DLL-search-order hijack in a neighbouring app) -> the DLL is written and executed when that app starts (escalation: code execution) -> RCE on the victim's machine with no user interaction beyond receiving a message (impact: full client compromise). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the ability to send a message to the victim and the presence of a hijackable neighbour app on the victim's machine; gain = arbitrary code execution in the victim's user context.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any desktop/mobile client that stores attachments under a per-user path, send a file whose NAME is `%2e%2e%5c` repeated (URL-encoded dot-dot-backslash) plus a target relative path; observe where it lands (or which path appears in an error/log). Measure the prefix length against the platform's max path (256 on Windows, 4096 on Linux) to compute the remaining budget, then aim it at a DLL/`.so`/`.dylib`/config file a neighbouring or the same application loads. Confirm by triggering the load (app start, feature open). Rule: **a filename traversal is an arbitrary-file-write primitive whose only real limit is path length — compute the budget the fixed prefix leaves you and spend it on a DLL/config the target machine will load; the RCE is the payload, not the file write.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `DLL hijack`, `qwave`, `MAX_PATH`, `Viber`, `encrypted chat`, or a filename-traversal -> DLL-hijack RCE chain. Related-but-distinct: §17.74 / §17.88 record CLIENT-side file-name path traversal (a localhost inspector `dir + fileName()` concat; a client tool materialising a page-named file) and ~line 4572 records a Slack Android shared-file traversal whose gain is auth tokens — none records the MAX_PATH budget arithmetic, the DLL-hijack pivot, or the encrypted-chat attachment branch. New general method -> §17.343.

SOURCE: https://19whoami19.medium.com/uncovering-intercom-misconfigurations-from-ignored-widgets-to-high-impact-vulnerabilities-26115f9481ca  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` of the Medium article, full text read IN FULL (three misconfigurations, repro steps and mitigations).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Intercom widget was touched this pass; every step below is quoted from the article).
CLASS: third-party widget misconfiguration -> broken access control / sensitive information disclosure — an Intercom Messenger deployed without Identity Verification accepts a caller-supplied email in the boot call, so anyone can read any user's support-chat history (CWE-287 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the article):
  1. "Some organizations fail to enforce **Intercom Identity Verification**. Without it, attackers can impersonate any user (customer or employee) by booting the Intercom widget with an arbitrary email address."
  2. The whole exploit, verbatim: `Intercom('boot', { email: 'victim@example.com' });` — run in the browser console on the target page. "This loads the entire support chat history of the chosen user, including **live updates** of ongoing conversations."
  3. The negative control, verbatim: "Properly configured companies respond with an error such as: `error: Missing user_hash or intercom_user_jwt. A valid user_hash or intercom_user_jwt is required...` But misconfigured deployments simply expose the data."
  4. The second and third findings are the same missing-teardown class: "Improper session teardown in Intercom allows the **next logged-in user** on the same browser to inherit the previous user's chat session" (`intercom-session-*`, `intercom-id-*` persist in local storage/cookies and are not invalidated on logout), and "Even after a user logs out of `example.com`, the Intercom chat widget remains active in the browser… The session only terminates when a new user logs in."
  5. The article's own fix guidance: enable Identity Verification, generate `user_hash` server-side with HMAC-SHA256, and verify the fix with `Intercom('update', { email: 'victim@example.com' });` — "impersonation should now fail"; on logout run `Intercom('shutdown');`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a page loading `*.intercom.io` / `*.intercomcdn.com` widgets; a boot call whose only identity input is `email` (no `user_hash`, no `intercom_user_jwt`); `intercom-session-*` / `intercom-id-*` cookies or localStorage entries that survive an app logout; a widget that keeps showing the previous user's chat after logout.
CHAIN: any anonymous visitor to the target page (primitive: the widget is public) -> run `Intercom('boot', {email: victim})` (primitive: no identity verification on the boot call) -> the widget loads the victim's full support-chat history and live updates (impact: sensitive information disclosure — PII, confidential discussions, ongoing support threads), and the same missing teardown leaks the previous user's session to the next user on a shared device. Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a target page with an Intercom widget deployed without Identity Verification and a victim email; gain = the victim's entire support-chat history plus live messages.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any page with a third-party chat widget (Intercom, Zendesk, Drift, Crisp, HubSpot), open the console and boot/update the widget with a victim's email and no hash/JWT; if the chat history loads, the identity check is missing. Then log out of the host app and reload — if the widget still shows the previous user's history, the teardown is missing. Rule: **a chat widget's identity is a client-side parameter until the vendor's hash/JWT is enforced — boot it with a victim email and see whose history comes back.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `Intercom` (as an identity-verification misconfiguration), `user_hash`, `intercom-session`, `intercom_user_jwt`, or a widget-boot impersonation. The ledger's three `Intercom` hits are a docs-SaaS template-injection sink (~line 3193) and a hardcoded third-party API-token-in-JS disclosure (~line 8863) — neither records the widget identity check. New general method -> §17.344.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1356845  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 4,730 B read IN FULL (the body carries the exact unauthenticated write request and the follow-up GET). Metadata: Elastic, "CVE-2021-40870 on [52.204.160.31]", weakness Code Injection, severity CRITICAL (score 9.8; AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N), reporter `fdeleite`, submitted 2021-10-01, disclosed 2021-10-06, state Closed/Resolved, CVE-2021-40870, 75 votes, asset type OTHER.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Aviatrix Controller was stood up this pass; every request line and the phpinfo output below are quoted verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: unauthenticated arbitrary FILE WRITE via directory traversal in a form field that NAMES THE DESTINATION PATH, with the file CONTENT in a sibling form field — a plain action-style POST (no upload UI, no auth) concatenates the attacker's path onto a base directory and writes the attacker's content verbatim; the same web server then serves and executes the file -> RCE (CWE-434 / CWE-22 / CWE-94).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. The vulnerable product, verbatim: "An issue was discovered in Aviatrix Controller 6.x before 6.5-1804.1922. Unrestricted upload of a file with a dangerous type is possible, which allows an unauthenticated user to execute arbitrary code via directory traversal." The host is identified by its certificate: `curl -kv https://52.204.160.31` -> `subject: ... O=Elasticsearch, Inc.; CN=*.elasticit.co` (the appliance behind a third-party cert).
  2. The write request, verbatim:
     `POST /v1/backend1 HTTP/1.1` / `Host: 52.204.160.31` / `Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded` / `Content-Length: 136` / body `CID=x&action=set_metric_gw_selections&account_name=/../../../var/www/php/1yv4QQmkj4h4OdmmyT11tkiGf5M.php&data=RCE<?php phpinfo()>` — the DESTINATION PATH is the `account_name` form field, the CONTENT is the `data` form field, and `action=set_metric_gw_selections` selects the handler. No cookie/session is sent.
  3. The read-back, verbatim: `GET /v1/1yv4QQmkj4h4OdmmyT11tkiGf5M.php` -> the response is the rendered `phpinfo()` output (the report pastes the `SCRIPT_URL`/`SCRIPT_URI`/`SSL_*` table), proving the file landed in the web root and executed as PHP.
  4. Impact, verbatim: "An unauthenticated, 3rd-party attacker or adversary can execute remote code".
DETECTION SIGNAL: an unauthenticated POST/PUT whose parameter set carries BOTH a path/dir/filename-style field AND a content-style field; an `action=`/`task=`/`mode=` selector naming a file-producing handler; a 2xx that acknowledges a "selection"/"setting"/"metric" write; the written path retrievable under the same vhost (`/v1/<name>.php`) and executing as a script; no auth header or cookie on the write request; a traversal sequence (`../../../`, `..%2f`, `..%5c`) in the path field that is not rejected.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated network caller (primitive: the endpoint needs no credential) -> inject `../../../` into the path-naming form field while supplying a script body in the sibling content field (primitive: the path is concatenated to a base directory with no sanitization and the content is written verbatim) -> arbitrary file written into the web root (escalation: arbitrary file write with attacker-chosen content and extension) -> the web server serves and executes the file (impact: unauthenticated RCE; the report's own impact line: "An unauthenticated, 3rd-party attacker or adversary can execute remote code"; rated Critical 9.8, PR:N/UI:N). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = network reach to an unauthenticated endpoint that takes a destination-path field plus a content field; gain = code execution as the web server user.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: enumerate every unauthenticated POST/PUT in scope and classify the parameter set — where a request carries a path/dir/filename-style field (`account_name`, `path`, `file`, `filename`, `dir`, `name`, `dest`, `target`) TOGETHER WITH a content-style field (`data`, `content`, `body`, `value`, `payload`), replay it with the path field set to `/../../../<webroot>/<random>.php` (plus `..%2f..%2f`, `..%5c..%5c` and absolute-path variants) and the content field set to `<?php phpinfo()` / `<% out.println(...) %>` / `<script>...</script>`; then GET the written path on the same host and check whether the marker executes. Rule: **a form field that names the destination path plus a sibling field that supplies the content is an unauthenticated file-write sink — traversal in the path field + a script body in the content field = arbitrary file write -> RCE, with no upload UI and no auth required.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1356845`, `Aviatrix`, `set_metric_gw_selections`, `backend1`, `account_name=`, or `CVE-2021-40870`. Related-but-distinct material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.30 (GitLab — a flag/ref parameter makes the wrapped TOOL write its own stdout to a path -> authorized_keys -> RCE; the sink is the tool's stdout path, not a form field), §17.36 (GitLab Nuget — traversal in an uploaded artifact's OWN METADATA), §17.74 (Mozilla VPN — a client dev-server traversal name -> DLL drop), §17.88 (Burp — a client tool materialising a local file from page-controlled attributes), §17.328 (Vimeo — an unauth PUT to a GCS CDN gated by `Content-Type: application/octet-stream`). None records a server-side ACTION endpoint where the DESTINATION PATH is a form field and the CONTENT is a sibling form field, unauthenticated. New general method -> §17.337.
