The full disclosed report body was then read from the PUBLIC disclosed-reports dataset `BenAmpel/HackerSignal` (`benchmark/task4_hacker_exploit_labeling/val.jsonl`, raw file pulled with `curl`, 3,319-char `text` field) — the same text HackerOne discloses, with HackerOne's own `██████` redactions intact.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Zendesk tenant was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the disclosed report).
CLASS: privilege escalation / broken function-level authorization — a dedicated user-update API endpoint authenticates the caller (CSRF token) but never checks the caller's PRIVILEGE, so the LOWEST Support role self-escalates to full Support + Product Admin (CWE-285 / CWE-862).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ACTOR, verbatim: "The [Contributor Role](https://support.zendesk.com/hc/en-us/articles/4408832171034-About-team-member-product-roles-and-access) is the lowest Support role in Zendesk. In the UI alone, as a contributor, the accessible pages and and endpoints are very limited. With this role, the members page is not even accessible or restricted."
  2. THE ENDPOINT, verbatim: "Vulnerable Endpoint: `PUT /api███ HTTP/2`" — redacted in the disclosed report; the title names the family as `/api/v2/██████`.
  3. THE DEFECT, verbatim: "The `/api████████` endpoint is used for API integration to █████. █████████ . However, the said endpoint does not validate the privilege of the user who is sending a request. With this bug, a member with even just the lowest privilege(Contributor) is able to escalate any members' privilege to `FULL ADMINISTRATOR` including its own privilege."
  4. THE SELF-SERVED CSRF TOKEN, verbatim (the exploit's first hop): `xhttp.open("GET", "https://" + document.domain + "/api/v2/users/me.json");` then `var profile = JSON.parse(this.responseText); var csrf = profile.user.authenticity_token; var id = profile.user.id;` — the caller's OWN profile endpoint hands back both the CSRF token and the user id. No admin token is needed.
  5. THE ESCALATION REQUEST, verbatim: `xhttp.open("PUT", "https://" + document.domain + "/api██████;` / `xhttp.setRequestHeader("X-Csrf-Token", csrf);` / `xhttp.setRequestHeader("Content-Type", "application/json");` / `xhttp.send(JSON.stringify(███));`
  6. WHY NO ADMIN PRIVILEGE IS REQUIRED, verbatim: "From my past reports I was able to find privilege escalation issues on the endpoint `/api/███` . The difference is, to perform a privilege escalation on the said endpoint, there should be at least one Product Admin privilege on the actor's account but this report does not require any Admin privilege(just the lowest privilege alone)."
  7. THE UI-ONLY CONTROL, verbatim: "Now set the roles to Support-Contributor only and `DISABLE` any product access(just to prove that no other privilege is required)."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a role/permission-WRITE endpoint that is NOT an admin route (an ordinary user-update route) whose only authentication is a CSRF token the caller can read from its OWN profile endpoint (`GET /api/v2/users/me.json` -> `user.authenticity_token`, `user.id`); a role the UI denies the members/management page to; a PUT/PATCH that writes a role/permission field. TELL: the lowest-privilege session sends a role-write for its OWN id and the role actually changes.
CHAIN: the lowest Support role (primitive: a Contributor seat, no admin privilege of any kind) -> the user-update endpoint authenticates the caller but never checks the caller's privilege (primitive: BFLA on a role-write) -> the caller writes its OWN role to `FULL ADMINISTRATOR` (escalation: vertical privilege escalation, self-directed) -> full Support and Product Admin on the Zendesk tenant (impact: privilege escalation; the report's own impact line: "Privilege escalation - Support-Contributor to Support and Product Admin."). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a Contributor (lowest) seat on the tenant plus the caller's own CSRF token and user id, both self-served from `/api/v2/users/me.json`; gain = full Support + Product Admin.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY multi-role product — (1) provision the LOWEST role and confirm the UI denies it the member/role-management page; (2) find the endpoint that writes a user's role (capture it from an owner session, or infer `PUT /api/v2/users/{id}.json`, `PATCH /users/{id}`, a GraphQL `updateUser`/`setRole`/`addRole` mutation); (3) harvest your OWN CSRF token and user id from your OWN profile endpoint (`GET /api/v2/users/me.json`, or the CSRF meta tag) — never assume you need an admin's token; (4) replay the role-write against YOUR OWN id with the lowest-privilege session and the highest role value; (5) a 2xx and a changed role = the endpoint authenticates but does not authorize. Rule: **a role-write endpoint must check the CALLER's privilege, not merely authenticate the caller — take the lowest role, self-serve your CSRF token from your own profile endpoint, and PUT your own role to the highest value.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `2188569`, `Support-Contributor`, `users/me.json` (as a CSRF-token source), `me.json`, `FULL ADMINISTRATOR`, or `X-Csrf-Token` as a SELF-SERVED token on a role-write. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: V-06 (`references/variants-1.md` / Bugbountyskills.md:1329) is the general BFLA-on-a-function-level-route playbook and its impact line already says "grant roles" — #2188569 is recorded here as a DELTA for its ENTRY POINT (a NORMAL user-update route, not an admin route; the attacker escalates ITSELF rather than reaching a privileged route, and the CSRF token is self-served from the caller's own profile endpoint) and given NO §17 slot; the mass-assignment entry (ledger ~3650, `role` listed as an extra PATCH field) writes a role field inside an UNRELATED patch, not a dedicated role-write route; §17.336 (Yelp #2396549) is a role-denied MANAGEMENT feature (member invite) callable by the denied role, not a self-role-write; ledger ~2246 (an Access List Owner granting itself permissions) requires the actor to ALREADY be an owner, whereas #2188569 needs only the lowest role; §17.322 (Omise #3303136) is an invite-acceptance authorization grant; §17.424 (Dialogflow) is a role DOWNGRADE that merges instead of replacing. New general method? NO — the class is V-06's; recorded as a harvest-ledger + dated-file entry only.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1695454  2026-11-10
ROUTE: the live HackerOne report page would NOT render — `web_extract` -> the HackerOne sign-in page; `https://r.jina.ai/<url>` -> HTTP 403 "Upstream forbidden"; the browser tool on the live URL and on the Wayback copy -> Cloudflare "Just a moment..." / an empty JS shell; every Wayback snapshot (20221101233103 / 20221117013012 / 20240806104331 / 20241109080302 / 20260110031622 / 20260526055854) is the ~3.7 KB "JavaScript is disabled" SPA shell. The full disclosed report body was then read from the PUBLIC disclosed-reports dataset `BenAmpel/HackerSignal` (`benchmark/task4_hacker_exploit_labeling/val.jsonl`, raw file pulled with `curl`, 2,336-char `text` field).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Pressable endpoint was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the disclosed report).
CLASS: IDOR/BOLA on a WRITE endpoint + sensitive information disclosure (credential exposure) -> account takeover — the object id is a FORM FIELD (`application[id]`), and the endpoint's VALIDATION-ERROR branch serializes the TARGET object, handing back its `Client ID` and `Client Secret` (CWE-639 / CWE-200 / CWE-598).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "On Pressable, we can create API applications at https://my.pressable.com/api/applications, and we can access many things using the API token via following the [API docs](https://my.pressable.com/documentation/api/v1)"
  2. THE WRITE, verbatim: "I created an API application and tried to update it, I saw this request :" — `POST /api/applications` carrying `application[id]=<your app id>` and `authenticity_token`.
  3. THE IDOR, verbatim: "As you can see there is an `application[id]` parameter that contains the application ID. I changed it to my second account's application ID and that API app moved to my account. So, there is an IDOR but it doesn't have a great impact because it just removes the API application from the victim's account."
  4. THE ESCALATION — STRIP THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: "So I tried to escalate its impact and I noticed if we remove all parameters except `application[id]` and `authenticity_token`, then send the request, the endpoint gives an error with `Name must be provided` and prints the given application ID's page. And, that page contains `Client ID` and `Client Secret`!"
  5. THE STEPS, verbatim: "In this request, change the `application%5Bid%5D` parameter's value to the target app ID, **then remove all parameters except `application%5Bid%5D` and `authenticity_token`**" / "The page will give an error and you will see the victim app's page which contains `Client ID` and `Client Secret`" / "Now, you can use these API credentials on the Pressable API."
  6. THE ENUMERATION, verbatim: "API application IDs are sequential, so the attacker doesn't have to guess the IDs, s/he can access all applications"
  7. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "The attacker can access all API credentials using this vulnerability, and that leads to account takeover (via adding collaborator etc.)" — and the collaborator endpoint is named: "https://my.pressable.com/documentation/api/v1#collaborator-bulk-create"
DETECTION SIGNAL: a write endpoint whose OBJECT ID IS A FORM/BODY FIELD (`application[id]`, `record[id]`, `data{id}`, `project[owner_id]`) rather than a path segment — swap it and watch which object the write lands on; a VALIDATION-ERROR response that renders the TARGET OBJECT'S RECORD (its page, its fields, its secrets) instead of a generic message; sequential object ids. TELL: a request carrying ONLY the object id + the CSRF token returns an error page that contains the target object's `Client ID` / `Client Secret` / token fields.
CHAIN: any authenticated user (primitive: can create an API application) -> change `application[id]` to a victim's app id (primitive: IDOR write — the app moves to the attacker's account) -> strip every other parameter and re-send (escalation: the validation-error branch serializes the target object) -> the error page renders the victim app's `Client ID` + `Client Secret` (escalation: credential disclosure WITH PoC) -> replay the stolen API credentials against the API, including the collaborator bulk-create endpoint (impact: account takeover; the reporter rated it critical). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an ordinary authenticated Pressable account and a victim app id (sequential, therefore enumerable); gain = the victim's API `Client ID` + `Client Secret` and therefore their account.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY write endpoint that takes the object id as a BODY/FORM/QUERY field — (1) capture the normal update request and list every field; (2) swap the id field to a second object you own (or a sequential neighbour) and confirm the write lands on that object; (3) then DELETE every field except the object id and the CSRF/authenticity token and re-send; (4) read the ERROR response — if it renders the target object's record (name, secrets, tokens, ids) rather than a generic message, you have a validation-error disclosure; (5) replay any credential found against the API and check whether it carries a privilege-write (invite/collaborator/owner) endpoint, because that is where the ATO lands. Rule: **a write endpoint's validation-error path is a second serializer — strip the payload down to the object id + CSRF token and read the target object (and its secrets) out of the error response.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1695454`, `Pressable`, `my.pressable.com`, `application%5Bid%5D`, `application[id]`, `Name must be provided`, `Client ID` (as an IDOR-disclosed field), `collaborator-bulk-create`. Related-but-distinct recorded material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's mass-assignment entries (V-04; ~3650, ~2368 GitLab import) write EXTRA fields into a request, they do not strip a payload to a validation error to read the TARGET object; ledger ~2100 ("Map authenticated endpoints that return a serialized object: `/{id}.json`, `/api/…/{id}`, `/reference.json`, error-shaped `{error, context}` responses") is a RECON mapping hint, not the strip-to-error technique; ledger ~8227 is an error string persisted and re-rendered to a PRIVILEGED viewer (an admin notice), not a validation-error branch on a write endpoint returning the TARGET object to the CALLER; §17.238 is an error message that echoes your OWN injected data in a template/code-injection context, not an object serializer; the New Relic chart IDOR (~5799) is a cross-tenant READ via a body-supplied `account_id` whose exfil channel is an artifact URL — no validation-error serialization; §17.422 (Zoom `/user/get` -> `zak` token) is a READ-side IDOR that discloses a token, not a write-endpoint error branch. New general method -> §17.427.


NOTE — §17 additions 2026-11-10 batch 137 (§17.427 appended from the 6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 2 entries appended to the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and the dated knowledge file `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md` + 1 §17 slot). NEW: (1) HackerOne #2188569 (Zendesk, "Privilege escalation - Support-Contributor to Support and Product Admin via `/api/v2/██████`", reporter redacted, resolved, $0) — a dedicated user-update API endpoint authenticates the caller with a CSRF token but never checks the caller's PRIVILEGE, so the LOWEST Support role (Contributor) self-escalates to `FULL ADMINISTRATOR`; the CSRF token and user id are SELF-SERVED from the caller's own `GET /api/v2/users/me.json` (`profile.user.authenticity_token`, `profile.user.id`) and the escalation is a `PUT` with an `X-Csrf-Token` header and a JSON body — DELTA of V-06's function-level-authorization class (new entry point: a NORMAL user-update route, not an admin route; self-directed escalation; no §17 slot). (2) HackerOne #1695454 (Automattic / Pressable, "IDOR in API applications (able to see any API token, leads to account takeover)", reporter Bugra, 2022-10-25) — the object id is a FORM FIELD (`application[id]`) on `POST /api/applications`; swapping it moves the victim's API app to the attacker's account (IDOR write), and then STRIPPING every parameter except `application[id]` + `authenticity_token` makes the endpoint's VALIDATION-ERROR branch ("Name must be provided") RENDER THE TARGET OBJECT'S PAGE, which contains the victim's `Client ID` and `Client Secret`; sequential ids make it enumerable, and the stolen credentials drive the collaborator bulk-create endpoint -> account takeover -> new general method -> §17.427. VALID-BUT-NOT-APPENDED (read in full, nothing new): HackerOne #1644436 (EXNESS, "IDOR in Stats API Endpoint Allows Viewing Equity or Net Profit of Any MT Account", reporter ashwarya, Medium 4 ~ 6.9, bounty, disclosed 2022-12-05) — `GET /v3/personal_area/stats/{net_profit,orders_number,trading_volume,equity}?time_range=365&accounts={accountNumber}` with `Authorization: Bearer` returns any MT account's equity/net profit/trading volume; this is textbook IDOR/BOLA on a query-parameter object id, a class already covered by V-01/V-05 and by the recorded New Relic cross-tenant chart IDOR (~5799) and the object-id status/lookup IDOR test (~10973); the only deltas are the financial-data sensitivity and the fix signature ("Response fields returning `null` values if we query someone else MT account number"), which are held as a detection signal, not a new method. HackerOne #1982630 (U.S. Dept Of Defense, "CVE-2023-29489 XSS in cpanel", reporter rook1337, resolved, disclosed 2023-05-10) — a reflected XSS whose payload rides in the URL PATH (`http://www.████/cpanelwebcall/%3Cimg%20src=x%20onerror=%22prompt(1)%22%3Eaaaaaaaaaaaa`) and fires on the cPanel webcall ERROR page (the reporter's own note: "you will see 400 bad request because you are not logged into cpanel and this is injected in webcall error page"); this is a known third-party cPanel CVE with a one-line PoC, no bypass, no chain and no sanitizer detail, and the reflected-XSS class is already heavily recorded — the path-segment-reflection-into-an-error-page entry point is a held lead, not a new method. HackerOne #1392511 (HackerOne, "HackerOne Staging uses Production data for testing", reporter tk0, Low 0.1 ~ 3.9, resolved, disclosed 2021-11-05) — a PRIVACY VIOLATION, not a vuln class: a staging-environment notification arrived from `no-reply+staging@hackerone.com` with Privacy/Terms links pointing at the staging domain `https://www.enorekcah.com/`; HackerOne's own root-cause reply states the staging app sat behind Duo Network Gateway and that "HackerOne employees could not have authenticated to the system due to a mismatch between the SAML public key and HackerOne's SSO provider", so no access was gained and there is no impact chain — the only reusable nugget is recon (a `+staging` sender subaddress and the mail's Privacy/Terms links disclose a lower environment), which is not a hunting class. INVALID / UNREACHABLE (no entry, nothing invented): HackerOne #2228413 (HackerOne, "Private program name disclosure in the invitation mail for another program", reporter byq, Low CVSS 3.8, no bounty, reported 2023-10-27, disclosed 2023-12-08) — `visibility: no-content` with a 0-byte `vulnerability_information` (confirmed via the public disclosed-reports dataset `elamaran619/hackerone_disclosed_reports`, `/filter?where="id"=2228413`), and the `ajaysenr/HackerOne-Disclosed-Reports` mirror prints "*No vulnerability information available.*"; the live page and every Wayback snapshot return the SPA shell / sign-in page, so no request shape, parameter or method exists publicly and the title alone (a Low 3.8 information disclosure) is below the entry bar. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

## READER PASS 2026-11-10 — batch 138 (6 assigned HackerOne sources, read ONE BY ONE in the given order)

All six sources rendered in full on `web_extract` (HTTP 200, complete report bodies — no fallback route needed). 6 valid, 0 unreachable, 0 fabricated. 6 entries appended; **0 new §17 slots** — every entry is a DELTA of an already-recorded class with a new entry point, a new bypass, or a new target.

### SOURCE 1 — https://hackerone.com/reports/1092230  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitLab import was run and no fogbugz.com host was touched this pass)
CLASS: SSRF (full-read GET) — a URL validator checks only the HOSTNAME against a THIRD-PARTY allowlist while the HTTP client underneath resolves DNS and follows redirects at connect time (CWE-918 / CWE-601)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK, verbatim: "The FogBugz import code uses `CarrierWave::Uploader::Base:download!` to download attachments from fogbugz.com when importing a FogBugz repository. `CarrierWave::Uploader::Base:download!` ultimately uses `Kernel.Open` to download the provided attachment URL."
  2. THE DEFECT, verbatim: "`Kernel.Open` permits URLs which resolve to, or redirect to `127.0.0.1`, making it vulnerable to SSRF issues."
  3. THE CONTROL, verbatim (all of `app/services/projects/download_service.rb`): `WHITELIST = [ /^[^.]+\.fogbugz.com$/ ].freeze` / `def valid_url?(url) url && http?(url) && valid_domain?(url) end` / `def http?(url) url =~ /\A#{ URI:: DEFAULT_PARSER. make_regexp(%w(http https))}\z/ end` / `def valid_domain?(url) host = URI.parse(url).host; WHITELIST.any? { | entry| entry === host } end` — the check is on the URL's HOST STRING only; nothing pins the address the socket connects to, nothing restricts the path/query/port the fetched response may carry.
  4. THE UPSTREAM PRIMITIVE THAT MUST EXIST, verbatim: "If a vulnerability can be identified in a fogbugz.com subdomain which results in returning a crafted API response including an arbitrary attachment URL, a full read GET based SSRF would be exploitable on gitlab.com (or a gitlab instance)." Enumerated triggers, verbatim: "URL parameter clobbering to force a 302 redirect on attachment download" / "Intercept and modify an unencrypted HTTP API response" / "Subdomain takeover / dangling sub domain to return an arbitrary API response" / "HTTP Request smuggling to modify an in-flight API response" / "Cache poisoning to poison a malicious API response" / "SQL Injection to replace an attachment URL" / "Code Execution to modify `api.asp` to return an arbitrary API response".
  5. THE REPRODUCTION, verbatim: "This issue can be simulated by placing an `/etc/hosts` entry on a GitLab server as follows: `198.211.125.160 poc.fogbugz.com`" — "This will point `poc.fogbugz.com` to a VPS I control, which responds with a crafted FogBugz API response designed to simulate the exploitation of a bug on a fogbugz.com domain. Importing the `SSRF Repository` FogBugz repository from this host will create a repository with a single issue which includes the SSRF result of requesting http://127.0.0.1:9090/api/v1/targets."
  6. WHY THE THIRD PARTY WAS NOT PROBED, verbatim: "Due to the third party nature of these issues it is not feasible to probe for, or disclose the potential existence of, any of these potential issues on fogbugz.com to GitLab."
DETECTION SIGNAL: any import/migrate/embed/"add from URL" feature whose validator is a HOSTNAME REGEX against a third-party domain while the fetch is a generic HTTP client that follows redirects and resolves at connect time; a wrapper named `download!` / `Kernel.open` / `open-uri` / `URI.open` / `Net::HTTP.get(URI(...))`; a third-party API whose response BODY supplies the URL that is then fetched; a report that names the exact internal service it could reach. TELL: the validator accepts a host you do not control and the fetched request arrives at a listener you control.
CHAIN: a subdomain/tenant of the allowlisted third party (primitive: a dangling DNS record, an unclaimed cloud app, a takeover, or an upstream bug that makes ITS response carry an arbitrary URL) -> `valid_url?` matches the hostname and passes it to `download!` -> `Kernel.open` follows the third party's 302 / resolves its host to an address you choose (the report's own test pins `poc.fogbugz.com` to the reporter's VPS; separately notes the client follows redirects to `127.0.0.1`) -> the GitLab server GETs from inside its own network to `http://127.0.0.1:9090/api/v1/targets` (Prometheus) or any internal host -> full-read SSRF whose response is stored as an attachment on the imported issue and is VIEWABLE (impact: internal service access + internal read; the report names the exporters, Prometheus and Alertmanager on localhost).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY feature that fetches a URL for the user (import, migration, "add from URL", avatar-by-URL, webhook/preview/fetch, oEmbed, RSS import) — (1) find the validator: grep source/error strings for a hostname regex/allowlist and note whether the check is on the HOST STRING only; (2) stand up a listener, put a URL on the allowlisted third-party host that 302s to `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/` or to your collector, and trigger the import/fetch; (3) a callback proves the redirect/resolve step bypassed the validator, not the host string; (4) re-aim at the target's internal services (`http://127.0.0.1:9090/api/v1/targets`, `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`, `metadata.google.internal`, `localhost:6379`) and read the stored response back out of the object the import created.
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1092230`, `fogbugz`, `poc.fogbugz.com`, `download_service`, `valid_url?`, `valid_domain?`, `WHITELIST = [`, `CarrierWave::Uploader::Base:download!`, `url parameter clobber`. The pre-existing `Kernel.open`/`download!` hits are the #826361 entry (ledger ~2798) — MASS-ASSIGNMENT of a `remote_*_url` attribute via a project-export import (the attacker supplies the URL directly); #1092230 is a DIFFERENT mechanism (the URL comes from a THIRD-PARTY API response and the host is allowlist-validated). DELTA, no §17 slot. Reviewed and not re-recorded: #826361's `remote_attachment_request_header` header-smuggling SSRF, and the ledger's DNS-pinning/TTL-0 webhook entry (~10089) — the same "validator vs resolver" shape but on webhook egress.

### SOURCE 2 — https://hackerone.com/reports/970157  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Twitter/X endpoint was called and no password was brute-forced this pass)
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — the "enter your CURRENT password to change it" control is not rate-limited on the API route, so a session hijacker brute-forces the current-password field and defeats the control whose purpose is to stop exactly that (CWE-307 / CWE-287 / CWE-807)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CONTROL, verbatim: "This additional security measure from twitter provides protection to the victim's account, considering that a victim's session may have been hijacked by a hacker, however, due to this additional layer of security Implemented by twitter the hacker would not be able to change the victim's Password, as they will be prompted to enter the victim's account password In order to make these changes, which will not be known to a hacker (In case of a session hijack)."
  2. THE CLAIM, verbatim: "This report is to bring to your attention a security vulnerability that will allow hackers that have hijacked a user's session to bypass the password screen (Without knowing the user's password)."
  3. THE CHAIN, verbatim: "For users that have had their twitter session hijacked, this security vulnerability would enable a hacker to completely take over a victim's account as they will be able to change the victim's password by bypassing the old password by the umrestricted rate limit or bruteforcing in the password."
  4. THE REPRODUCTION, verbatim: "With the assumption that the victim's twitter session is 'hijacked' and in a 'logged in' state for the hacker." / "1. Go to Settings and Privacy -> Accounts 2. Click on Email -> Password 3. Enter any random password and Click on 'Next' 4. Intercept the request the above request and send it to intruder 5. Then select the position old password 6. Then go in payload add password list 7. Then start the attack bcoz of no rate limit the password bruteforcing is continue and find the correct password and update the old one"
  5. THE REQUEST, verbatim: `POST /i/account/change_password.json HTTP/1.1` / `Host: api.twitter.com` / `Referer: https://twitter.com/settings/password` / `Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded` / `x-twitter-auth-type: OAuth2Session` / `x-twitter-client-language: en` / `x-twitter-active-user: yes` / `x-csrf-token: ████████` / `authorization: Bearer ████` / `Origin: https://twitter.com/` / `Content-Length: 93` / `Connection: close` / `Cookie: personalization_id=…; guest_id=…; external_referer=…; _ga=…; ads_prefs=…; kdt=…; remember_checked_on=1; gt=…; ct0=…; _gid=…; _twitter_sess=…; twid=…; auth_token=…` — body: `current_password=██████&password=███████&password_confirmation=█████████`.
  6. THE MEASUREMENT, verbatim: "the 1000 request takes approx 3.40 minutes and here is the POC of more then 1000 attempt ███" — >1000 guesses against `current_password`, no lockout, no captcha, no throttle.
  7. THE ASK AND THE IMPACT, verbatim: "**Resolution:** Apply the Rate Limitation" / "This a serious security vulnerability, as It could lead to a hacker completely taking over the user's account by overriding twitter's security protocol as they could use this technique to bypass the password and it use to fully takeover the victim password".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a CHANGE-PASSWORD / re-authentication flow served by an API route (`.json`, `/api/...`, a mobile BFF) rather than the web form; a current-password field checked per-request with NO attempt counter, NO lockout, NO captcha; the route reachable with only the session credential plus a CSRF token. TELL: 1000+ guesses in minutes and the account's password is then changed.
CHAIN: an attacker holding a hijacked session but not the password (primitive: a stolen `auth_token` cookie / bearer token) -> the change-password route checks `current_password` as an ordinary form field with no rate limit (primitive: an unthrottled credential check on a privileged action) -> 1000+ guesses in ~3m24s recover the current password (escalation: the re-authentication control is brute-forced) -> the attacker sets `password` + `password_confirmation` and the account's password changes (impact: complete, permanent account takeover with the victim locked out; the program paid a bounty, High 7 ~ 8.9).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY product whose change-password step asks for the CURRENT password — (1) capture the request that submits `current_password` and note whether it is an API route; (2) replay it 1000+ times against a TEST account you own, varying only `current_password`, and record whether the server ever throttles/locks out/captchas or invalidates the session (the report's measurement: >1000 in ~3m24s); (3) the decisive question — does a WRONG current password ever hard-fail permanently? If it never does, the gate is a rate-limit-less equality check; (4) confirm by changing the test account's password once the correct value is found.
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `970157`, `change_password.json`, `current_password` (as a brute-forced field), `x-twitter-auth-type`, `i/account`. Pre-existing related material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's rate-limit material is either doctrine (a missing rate limit is a §2(c) chain primitive alone — #125505/#125200/#125707 promo-code brute force, the Firestore `sendOobCode` mail-bombing note at ~6771) or a DIFFERENT surface (the 2FA-gated actions list at ~9563, which names "old password" as a gate but not its brute force); `bruteforc` (13/10/3) is promo/invite/ID enumeration, never the current-password field on a re-auth route. DELTA of the "missing rate limit" primitive with a NEW TARGET and a NEW IMPACT (session hijack -> permanent ATO instead of enumeration). No §17 slot.

### SOURCE 3 — https://hackerone.com/reports/964550  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Shopify upload was made this pass)
CLASS: stored XSS via unrestricted file upload + MIME-type override on the multipart part — a PNG whose `tEXt` metadata chunk carries HTML is uploaded as an avatar, the part's `Content-Type` is rewritten from `image/png` to `text/html`, and the asset is stored on a CDN and served back with that type (CWE-434 / CWE-79)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SURFACE, verbatim: "I found unrestricted file upload via avatar in https://accounts.shopify.com/accounts/<ID>, and XSS Stored in PNG IDAT chunks using exiftool".
  2. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: `exiftool -Comment="\"><script>alert(prompt('XSS BY ZEROX4'))</script>" xss_comment_exif_metadata_double_quote.png` — the report's hex dump shows the `tEXt` chunk carrying `Comment=""><script>alert(prompt('XSS BY ZEROX4'))</script>`.
  3. THE TRANSPORT OVERRIDE, verbatim: "and after select payload you should edit mime type of image[HTML] request with burpsuite, from image/png to text /html."
  4. THE REQUEST, verbatim: `POST /accounts/141376700 HTTP/1.1` / `Host: accounts.shopify.com` / `Content-Type: multipart/form-data; boundary=---------------------------20426576427959059782120179951` / `Referer: https://accounts.shopify.com/accounts/141376700` / `Origin: https://accounts.shopify.com` — parts `utf8=✓`, `_method=patch`, `authenticity_token=...`, and the file part `Content-Disposition: form-data; name="account[avatar]"; filename="xss_comment_exif_metadata_double_quote.png"` with **`Content-Type: text/html`** and PNG bytes as the body.
  5. THE STORAGE, verbatim: "and the malicious file saved in shopify-assets.shopifycdn.com" — example: `https://shopify-assets.shopifycdn.com/accounts/production/account/avatar/8df3da6b-3954-4073-a397-27a4d41db106/avatar_36x36_crop_center.png?v=1598066255`.
  6. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "steal sensitive user data and phishing accounts".
  7. THE PROGRAM'S ROOT CAUSE AND FIX, verbatim: "Because shopify-assets.shopifycdn.com is not in scope, and there are no security implications of an XSS on this domain, this report will be ineligible for a bounty." / "We have recently shipped a fix that allows only a select few valid Content-Types to be uploaded." — the fix is a SERVER-SIDE CONTENT-TYPE ALLOWLIST, confirming the server honoured the CLIENT-SUPPLIED part type.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an upload whose validation is the CLIENT-SUPPLIED multipart part `Content-Type` (or the extension) rather than the file's magic bytes; an avatar/logo/attachment pipeline that stores on a CDN and serves back with the stored type; an image-metadata field (`Comment`, `tEXt`, `iTXt`, EXIF, XMP, IPTC) reachable from a user upload; a served asset whose `Content-Type` is `text/html` while its body starts with a PNG/JPEG magic. TELL: rewrite the part type to `text/html`, upload, then fetch the STORED asset and read its `Content-Type` back.
CHAIN: any authenticated account (primitive: can set an avatar) -> the avatar upload accepts a file whose part `Content-Type` is attacker-chosen, so a PNG whose `tEXt` chunk carries `<script>` is stored (primitive: unrestricted upload with client-side MIME trust) -> the asset is served from `shopify-assets.shopifycdn.com` with `Content-Type: text/html` (escalation: the stored type is the attacker's, so the browser treats the body as HTML) -> the script executes in that origin's context when the avatar is rendered (impact: stored XSS; "steal sensitive user data and phishing accounts"). HONESTY NOTE (the program made it the deciding fact): Shopify ruled the CDN host out of scope and found "no security implications of an XSS on this domain", so it was NOT paid — the technique is real and the fix was a Content-Type allowlist, but the impact depends entirely on the storage origin being in scope and on the asset being rendered where a victim sees it.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY upload that lands on a CDN/storage origin — (1) upload a harmless file and capture the request; (2) note whether the server validates the FILE or the part's `Content-Type`/extension; (3) write HTML into a PNG's metadata (`exiftool -Comment='"><script>alert(1)</script>' file.png`) and re-send with the part's `Content-Type` rewritten to `text/html`; (4) fetch the STORED asset URL and read its `Content-Type` and body — a `text/html` response containing your markup is the bug; (5) enumerate where the asset is rendered (own profile, other users' views, an admin review queue, a share/preview page) to establish whose session the script runs in.
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `964550`, `shopify-assets`, `shopify-assets.shopifycdn.com`, `xss_comment_exif_metadata_double_quote`, `tEXt`+`Comment=` as an XSS carrier, `avatar`+`Content-Type` override, `Content-Type: text/html` as an upload-part override, `swisskyrepo`. Reviewed and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's upload-XSS family (§1.4 at ~174; the GitLab RFC-2231 `filename*` attribute-injection entry at ~2446-2462; the TikTok SVG entries; the DjVu/EPS ImageMagick parser entries at ~2321/~3536; the Snapchat "import from site" SVG at ~3172) are a filename or parser sink, not a client-supplied `Content-Type` override; `exiftool` (10/14/0) is recorded as a PARSER sink (DjVu `eval`) and a metadata-stripper, not as an HTML carrier into a `text/html`-served upload; `Content-Disposition` (15/18/6) is response-header hygiene. DELTA, no §17 slot.

### SOURCE 4 — https://hackerone.com/reports/1005355  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Uber Eats endpoint was touched this pass)
CLASS: stored XSS via unrestricted file upload in an ONBOARDING flow — a restaurant signup accepts HTML/SVG files, the stored file is served with `Content-Disposition: inline`, so the markup executes in the application's origin (CWE-434 / CWE-79)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim (Uber's own summary): "It was found that an attacker can upload any type of file including HTML files when adding a menu during the onboarding process after signing up at https://www.ubereats.com/restaurant/en-CA/signup."
  2. THE MECHANISM, verbatim (the reporter's own summary): "The hacker identified a file upload endpoint used by restaurants applying for Uber Eats which was unrestricted, in the sense that HTML or SVG files could be uploaded. Due to the fact that the contents of the files uploaded were being displayed in-browser (Content-Disposition: inline) this led to stored cross-site scripting."
  3. THE TWO-LINK CHAIN, verbatim: (a) UNRESTRICTED — "HTML or SVG files could be uploaded"; (b) INLINE RENDERING — "the contents of the files uploaded were being displayed in-browser (Content-Disposition: inline)".
  4. THE OUTCOME, verbatim: Uber's triage "updated the severity from medium to medium(4.7)"; the report was closed, reopened, closed again, and "Uber rewarded hunt4p1zza with a bounty" (2021-02-11); disclosed 2021-05-14.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a file upload inside a SIGNUP/ONBOARDING/KYC flow (restaurant, merchant, driver, seller, partner) rather than the authenticated account area — different team, reachable with a half-completed account, exactly where an extension/MIME allowlist is missing; a served upload whose `Content-Disposition` is `inline` rather than `attachment`; an upload whose bytes are HTML/SVG while the response claims an image type. TELL: the uploaded HTML file, fetched from its stored URL, returns 200 with `Content-Disposition: inline` and the attacker's markup intact.
CHAIN: any person who can start a merchant/restaurant signup (primitive: a pre-onboarding or mid-onboarding session — no established merchant account) -> the menu/upload endpoint accepts an HTML or SVG file (primitive: no extension/MIME/content allowlist) -> the file is stored and served with `Content-Disposition: inline` (escalation: the browser renders the body in the app's origin instead of downloading it) -> the attacker's script executes in that origin (impact: stored XSS — Uber rated it Medium 4.7 and paid a bounty; the same shape is Critical when the asset is rendered in an admin review queue or another merchant's session).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY signup/onboarding/KYC/partner-application flow that accepts a file — (1) start the flow with a throwaway account and capture the upload request; (2) upload an HTML file (and an SVG variant) instead of the expected image, keeping the field name and the flow's session/CSRF token; (3) follow the response to the STORED asset URL and read back status, `Content-Type` and `Content-Disposition`; (4) if the asset is served `inline` (or with an HTML/SVG type) and the markup is intact, open it in a browser to confirm execution; (5) enumerate where the asset is rendered — own preview, admin/ops review queue, a partner's dashboard — because the victim set (and therefore the severity) is set by the renderer, not the upload.
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1005355`, `ubereats`, `restaurant/en-CA/signup`, `Content-Disposition: inline` as an upload-render sink, or a menu-upload XSS. Reviewed and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's upload-XSS family (§1.4 at ~174; the GitLab RFC-2231 `filename*` entry at ~2446-2462; the DjVu/EPS ImageMagick entries at ~2321/~3536; the Shopify CDN `Content-Type`-override entry above) are a filename sink, a parser sink, or a client-supplied part type; #1005355's mechanism is the SERVED `Content-Disposition: inline` on an unrestricted upload inside a signup flow. DELTA, no §17 slot.

### SOURCE 5 — https://hackerone.com/reports/1065134  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Firebase database was read or written this pass)
CLASS: improper authentication / broken access control on a CLIENT-SIDE cloud database — the app ships its Firebase database URL in the APK, the database has NO access rules, and a direct unauthenticated REST read/write to `<project>.firebaseio.com/.json` exposes and overwrites the whole dataset (CWE-306 / CWE-862 / CWE-284)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ARTIFACT, verbatim: "publicly available Firebase Database (api-project-615509201590.firebaseio.com) Platform Affected: [android] com.zegocover.zego".
  2. WHERE THE URL LIVES, verbatim: "in res/values/strings.xml" / `<string name="firebase_database_url"> https://api-project-615509201590.firebaseio.com </string>` — a compile-time constant in the shipped APK, readable by anyone who unpacks it.
  3. THE READ, verbatim: "Go to https://api-project-615509201590.firebaseio.com/.json" — a bare unauthenticated GET of the database root returns the entire JSON tree.
  4. THE WRITE, verbatim: `import requests` / `data= {"Exploit":"Successfull", "H4CKED BY": "Sheikh Rishad"}` / `reponse = requests.put(" https://api-project-615509201590.firebaseio.com/.json" , json=data)` — an unauthenticated `PUT` to the database ROOT replaces the entire dataset.
  5. THE RESOLUTION, verbatim: "This report has been disclosed. June 23, 2021 ... Resolved ... Severity High (7.5) ... Weakness Improper Authentication - Generic ... CVE ID None".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a mobile/client app whose bundled resources carry a cloud database endpoint — `strings.xml` (`firebase_database_url`), iOS `GoogleService-Info.plist`, a JS bundle (`firebaseConfig`, `databaseURL`, `apiKey`, `projectId`), a shipped `.env`; a URL of the shape `<project>.firebaseio.com` (Realtime Database) or a Firestore `projects/<p>/databases/(default)` path; a REST call to that URL with NO `Authorization` header and NO `?auth=` token. TELL: `GET https://<project>.firebaseio.com/.json` returns data, or `PUT`/`PATCH` to a path returns 2xx.
CHAIN: any anonymous person (primitive: none required — the only artifact is the public APK) -> unpack the APK and read `res/values/strings.xml` for `firebase_database_url` (primitive: a public client artifact discloses the database endpoint) -> `GET <project>.firebaseio.com/.json` returns the whole database with no credential (escalation: unauthenticated read of every record) -> `PUT <project>.firebaseio.com/.json` with an arbitrary JSON body overwrites the dataset (impact: full database takeover — read AND write; the program rated it High 7.5, Improper Authentication - Generic).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY mobile/client app that talks to Firebase — (1) unpack the APK/IPA (`apktool d` / `unzip` + `strings`) and grep resources and DEX/JS for `firebase_database_url`, `firebaseio.com`, `google-services.json`, `GoogleService-Info.plist`, `databaseURL`, `apiKey`, `projectId`; (2) issue a bare unauthenticated `GET https://<project>.firebaseio.com/.json` — any JSON back is a full read; (3) `PUT`/`PATCH` the same root (and a guessed child path) with a marker document and re-GET to confirm the write; (4) also test the Firestore REST form (`https://firestore.googleapis.com/v1/projects/<p>/databases/(default)/documents/<collection>`) and the RTDB `?auth=<apiKey>` / `?key=<apiKey>` variants.
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1065134`, `Zego`, `zegocover`, `firebase_database_url`, `strings.xml`, `api-project-`, `firebaseio.com/.json` as an unauthenticated read/write, `Sheikh Rishad`. Reviewed and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's Firebase material (§17.214 at ~6764) is the OPPOSITE case — rules require authentication (`request.auth != null`) but do not bind the document to the caller, so a SELF-REGISTERED token reads others' documents; #1065134 has NO rules at all (a bare `.json` GET with no token), so the test differs (no account needed; read the whole tree and PUT it). The `firebase` hits in Bugbountyskills.md (~5596, ~17361-17435) are a leaked-key inventory line and an engagement's auth notes (CORS on `identitytoolkit`, an idToken in localStorage). DELTA, no §17 slot.

### SOURCE 6 — https://hackerone.com/reports/981824  2026-11-10
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no DNS record, SPF policy or mail account was modified this pass)
CLASS: mail-authorization business logic / broken access control across a SHARED SEND-INFRASTRUCTURE tenant — a customer of a third-party mail service can send mail on behalf of the PRIMARY domain because that domain's own SPF already authorises the shared service, and the customer's verification only proves they can RECEIVE at the address, not that they own it; the address is then recycled to a new customer while the attacker's sending permission persists (CWE-284 / CWE-295 / CWE-294)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE RECON, verbatim: "I knew basecamp themselves had used helpscout for support, so I was curious to see if hey was doing the same. A quick DNS lookup gave me the answer I was looking for:" — `dig hey.com txt` returns `v=spf1 include:_spf.hey.com include:helpscoutemail.com -all`.
  2. THE AUTHORIZATION THAT ALREADY EXISTS, verbatim: "Ok. That's interesting. helpscoutemail.com has permissions to send email on behalf of hey.com." and `dig helpscoutemail.com txt` -> `v=spf1 ip4:54.173.229.38 ip4:52.0.20.102 ip4:54.174.116.32 ip4:52.2.238.96 ip4:52.20.146.34 ip4:34.198.122.65 ~all`.
  3. THE WEAK VERIFICATION, verbatim: "Of course, helpscout makes sure we have access to the email address we're acting as. So let's go ahead and setup an email as dhhracing@hey.com. First, we sign up for a trial on hey.com with that email. All good. Now, let's enter that into helpscout. Once we get the verification email in hey, we enter the verification in helpscout. But... generally there is another step. Helpscout needs to have permission to send on our behalf. How do they do that? With DNS records for SPF, DKIM and DMARC. But... hey.com has already done that as they themselves are helpscout customers." — the verification proves only that the attacker can RECEIVE at the address; the SEND authorization is INHERITED from the primary domain's own SPF.
  4. THE RECYCLE, verbatim: "Now here is where the trouble comes in. hey.com has a recycle policy. If you create a trial but never pay, or as for a refund, then after 30 days the email address becomes available again. So let's go ahead and cancel our dhhracing@hey.com account (in hey, not in sendgrid). Now we wait 30 days. Now, DHH realizes he needs an address for his racing. So he goes to hey.com and creates an account under dhhracing@hey.com. And what do you know, it's available!"
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "But now, not only can DHH send email from dhhracing@hey.com, but SO CAN I. From my helpscout account. Of course, I won't receive mail from that account, but I could always just send out mail and CC another address that I own and hope for a reply-all. But really, the options for scamming are limitless." / "An attacker could spoof any hey.com email address that they had originally created an address for (and had been recycled). This could allow them to appear to be a high-profile individual or company. By CCing an address that they currently own they could receive replies from the correspondence as well."
  6. THE PROGRAM'S RATING AND FIX, verbatim: "This falls in our _Medium_ tier ($250 to $1,000) due to the complexity of the attack, as it requires having registered a specific address, letting it lapse, then wait after the trial period is over plus ~60 days until the address is free again, and then expect the victim to sign up with that specific address." / "Our HelpScout account now delivers through our outbound mail relays via SASL, allowing us to remove HelpScout from our SPF policy."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a domain's SPF `include:` of a SHARED third-party sender (helpdesk, marketing platform, CRM, email service) that also lets its own customers set a custom FROM address; a custom-FROM verification flow that only sends a confirmation email to the address (a RECEIVE check) while the SEND authorization is inherited from the primary domain's SPF/DKIM; an address-recycling policy on the primary domain (trial cancellation, refund, inactivity). TELL: `dig TXT <primary-domain>` shows `include:<third-party-mail-host>` and the third party's console accepts a FROM address on that domain after only an email-confirmation step.
CHAIN: any customer of a shared mail service (primitive: a free trial on the helpdesk/marketing platform) -> the service's own domain is already in the primary domain's SPF, so the primary domain's policy authorises the service to send for it (primitive: a shared-infrastructure authorization that is inherited, not per-customer) -> the attacker verifies a FROM address they can receive at (a RECEIVE check) and the service grants SEND rights (escalation: the verification proves the wrong thing) -> the attacker cancels the address, waits out the recycle window (~30 days + ~60 days) and the victim registers the now-free address (escalation: the address is recycled while the attacker's SEND permission persists) -> the attacker sends mail appearing to be from the victim's high-profile address, CCing an address they own to harvest replies (impact: spoofed mail on behalf of any recycled address of the primary domain; "the options for scamming are limitless"; rated Medium 6.8, paid).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY domain that uses a third-party mail sender — (1) `dig TXT <primary-domain>` and read the SPF for `include:` entries of helpdesk/marketing/CRM/mail platforms, then `dig TXT <that-include-host>` to see the sending infrastructure it authorises; (2) open the third party's console and attempt to add a custom FROM address on the primary domain, noting whether the ONLY check is a confirmation email to that address or whether it also requires a DNS TXT/DMARC record the attacker cannot set; (3) check the primary domain's address-recycling policy (trial cancellation, refund, inactivity) — the exploit needs an address the attacker can hold, release and then see re-registered by a victim; (4) the decisive test is whether a mail sent from the third-party console with the victim's FROM address is ACCEPTED by the primary domain's MX and passes SPF (check `Received-SPF`/`Authentication-Results` on a test message to an address you control).
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `981824`, `helpscout`, `helpscoutemail.com`, `dhhracing`, `mail on behalf`, `send mail on behalf`, `recycl` (as an address-recycling policy), or `SPF` + `include:` as a per-customer send-authorization bypass. Reviewed and NOT re-recorded: the SPF/DKIM/DMARC hits (8/9/0, 9/10/0, 4/6/0) are the doctrine's own §2(c) "SPF/DKIM/DMARC = never reported standalone" line and recon/engagement notes; the `recycl` hits are Node worker-pool recycling (~7162) and a directory-reuse note (Bugbountyskills.md ~10306). DELTA of the shared-infrastructure-authorization class with a NEW MECHANISM, no §17 slot. HONESTY NOTE: a DESIGN-level flaw (the program's own triage calls it "Violation of Secure Design Principles"), paid at Medium 6.8 with an explicit complexity discount — recorded as a technique, not as a finding.

### BATCH 138 SUMMARY
6 sources read, 6 valid, 0 unreachable, 0 fabricated, 6 entries appended, **0 new §17 slots**. Every entry is a DELTA: #1092230 (validator-vs-follow-redirects SSRF; cf. #826361's direct-URL mass assignment), #970157 (current-password brute force on a re-auth API route; cf. the promo-code brute-force entries), #964550 (client-supplied multipart `Content-Type` override; cf. the filename/parser upload sinks), #1005355 (`Content-Disposition: inline` on an onboarding-flow upload; new entry point), #1065134 (NO-rules Firebase RTDB read/write; the opposite of §17.214's rules-based read gap), #981824 (receive-only custom-FROM verification inheriting the primary domain's SPF + an address-recycle window). Honesty notes carried in the entries: Shopify #964550 was ruled out of scope and unpaid (the CDN host was not in scope), and Basecamp #981824 was a design-level Medium with an explicit complexity discount. All quotes are verbatim from the disclosed report bodies; nothing was reconstructed from the titles.

CORRECTION (same pass, batch 138 — appended, not rewritten): the batch-138 summary above says "0 new §17 slots". That count is WRONG and is corrected here: §17.428 WAS appended to `/root/Bugbountyskills.md` in this pass, for SOURCE 1 (HackerOne #1092230, GitLab FogBugz-import SSRF). Final batch-138 tally: 6 entries appended to this file + 6 to the harvest ledger + **1 §17 slot (§17.428)**. §17.428 = "A URL VALIDATOR THAT CHECKS ONLY THE HOSTNAME OF A THIRD-PARTY DOMAIN IS NOT AN SSRF CONTROL" — a NEW general method because the trust direction is the opposite of §17.40 (there the attacker supplies the URL directly via mass assignment; here the URL comes from a THIRD-PARTY API response and the hostname IS validated against a third-party allowlist, so the bypass is the third party's own response/redirect) and the filter shape is distinct from §17.93 (Lark Docs import, DNS rebinding of an attacker-controlled hostname). The other five entries remain DELTAs with no slot.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/716761  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary/Description, both payloads, the platform note, the fix recommendation and the impact; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Starbucks endpoint was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: reflected XSS / WAF-filter bypass — a double-encoding filter whose scan range stops at 0x7F is defeated by inserting a high byte (%80-%FF) into the encoded payload (CWE-79 / CWE-693).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ORIGINAL PAYLOAD (report 629745), verbatim: `https://www.starbucks.com.br/testing%2522%2520accesskey='x'%2520onclick='confirm%601%60'` — the quote and the spaces are DOUBLE-encoded (`%2522`, `%2520`) so the WAF does not see a literal quote.
  2. THE FIX THAT WAS BYPASSED, verbatim: "laszaro uncovered a bypass for report 629745 whereby the range of hex values %80-%FF was breaking the WAF filter which had not been accounted for in the original report."
  3. THE BYPASS, verbatim: `https://www.starbucks.com.br/testing%2522%80%2520accesskey='x'%2520onclick='confirm%601%60'` — "Notice the `%80` between `%2522` and `%2520`."
  4. THE BYTE-RANGE RULE, verbatim: "you can replace the `%80` with any hex value beyond `%7f` and the payload still works (there's a couple of exceptions throwing "Bad Request" errors: `%81`, `%8d`, `%8f`, `%90`, and `%9d`), but values in the range `%00-%7f` get properly filtered out (throwing custom "Server Error" pages and 404 pages, 301 and 302 redirect pages, and default 400 Bad Request errors, depending on the value)" — and the symmetric form: `testing%80%2522%2520accesskey=...` also breaks the filter while `testing%7F%2522%2520accesskey=...` does not.
  5. THE DELIVERY, verbatim: "Press CONTROL+ALT+X on Mac, or ALT+SHIFT+X on Windows" — the injected `accesskey` fires the `onclick` handler from a keyboard chord, so no click is needed.
  6. THE FIX, verbatim: "The range of hex values `%80-%FF` is breaking the WAF filter, those values need to be filtered out just like the range `%00-%7F` is being filtered out."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a WAF/filter that blocks a double-encoded payload but whose scan range stops at 0x7F; a payload that works with a high byte inserted and fails with `%7F`; an error/redirect/404 differential between the `%00-%7F` and `%80-%FF` forms. TELL: insert `%80` (or any byte > 0x7F) into the double-encoded sequence and retest — if the filter stops blocking, its byte range was the blind spot.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated attacker -> craft a URL whose path carries `"<attr>=<value>` with the quote and spaces double-encoded and a high byte (%80-%FF) inserted into the encoded run (primitive: a WAF filter whose scan range stops at 0x7F) -> the server reflects the path into a quoted attribute of a server-built tag without re-encoding the quote (primitive: attribute injection) -> the injected `accesskey`+`onclick` is now live markup (escalation: attribute injection becomes an event handler) -> the victim presses the accesskey chord (escalation: no click needed) -> arbitrary JS runs in the target origin (impact: reflected XSS with real script execution; the report's own impact line is "JavaScript is against Starbucks users on multiple critical domains"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim opens the crafted link AND presses the accesskey chord; gain = script execution in the target origin.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY path/parameter reflected into a quoted attribute behind a quote-blocking WAF — (1) build the double-encoded injection (`%2522`, `%2520`) and confirm the WAF blocks it; (2) insert a high byte into the encoded run — `%2522%80%2520`, `%2522%FF%2520`, `%80%2522%2520` — and retest; (3) the byte that flips the block is the filter's blind spot; (4) then add `accesskey='x' onclick='alert(1)'` and press the chord. Rule: **a WAF that filters %00-%7F but not %80-%FF is defeated by inserting a high byte into the double-encoded payload — the filter's byte range, not its decode count, is the control.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `716761`, `%80-%FF`, `non-standard ASCII`, `high byte` as a WAF bypass, `laszaro`, or a byte-range blind spot. Pre-existing related material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: #629745 (the same target's double-encoded attribute injection into a `<link rel="canonical">` href with the `accesskey`+`onclick` delivery, ledger ~10100) records the DOUBLE-ENCODING bypass and the keyboard-chord trigger but NOT the high-byte insertion that defeats the filter added afterwards; V-12 (double-encoding obfuscation) records the decode-order-mismatch family (double encoding, HTML entities, overlong UTF-8, case mixing, comment splitting) — none of which is a byte-range blind spot; the `accesskey` hits in the ledger/playbook are AWS `AccessKeyId`/IAM metadata text, not the HTML attribute. Recorded as a DELTA of the double-encoding class with a NEW BYPASS (the high-byte insertion). New §17 slot -> §17.429.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/331489  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's 9-step repro, the Note, the Impact and the staff thread; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Android device or Nextcloud client was driven this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: authentication bypass on a MOBILE CLIENT — an app-level PIN/fingerprint lock is enforced only in the app's own Activity, while the app's files are exposed through Android's Storage Access Framework provider and the shared `Android/media/<pkg>/` directory, so the OS file manager reads and modifies them with no lock (CWE-284 / CWE-668).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "Install the official nextcloud-client ... enter the server-address ... enter username and password, tick the "Stay logged in" checkbox ... Open a directory within the nextcloud app with important, secret files ... switch either the "Passcode lock" or the "Fingerpring lock" option on. Close the nextcloud app" — the lock is enabled and the app is closed.
  2. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "Open nextcloud, but do not enter the passcode/do not put your finger on the fingerprint-scanner / Press the home button. (Nextcloud now runs in the background) / Open the default Android file manager (com.android.documentsui) / Tap on the hamburger menu, then tap on the nextcloud logo. Now you should see your nextcloud-files" — the lock screen is never passed.
  3. THE CAVEAT, verbatim: "(Note: You can only see/read/modify a file, if you opened the directory with this file within the nextcloud-app at least once)" — the SAF provider only exposes directories the app has already materialised.
  4. THE SECOND ROUTE, verbatim: "If you once opened the secret file before, you can also access ist by opening /storage/emulated/0/Android/media/com.nextcloud.client/nextcloud/..." — the shared media collection is a plain filesystem path.
  5. THE PERSISTENCE, verbatim: "It still works after 30 min, and it still works after rebooting the device." — the bypass is not a cached-credential window.
  6. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "If an attacker has physical access to an Android smartphone without a screen lock, but with nextcloud installed and set up, he can easily access the nextcloud-files even if the nextcloud app is locked with a fingerprint or pin. This shouldn't be possible."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a mobile app that gates its own UI with a PIN/biometric but stores its data in a shared or provider-exposed location; an app that registers a Storage Access Framework DocumentsProvider (visible in the OS file manager's root list under the app's name/logo); a shared-media path `Android/media/<package>/...` or `Android/data/<package>/...` that is readable by other apps / the file manager; a lock that survives a reboot and a 30-minute wait (so it is not a cached-credential window). TELL: with the app locked, open the OS file manager, pick the app's provider root, and read a file you opened once before — if it opens, the lock is UI-only.
CHAIN: an attacker with physical access to an unlocked-screen device (primitive: the doctrine §2(a) precondition — physical device access) -> the app's PIN/fingerprint lock gates only the app's own Activity, not its exported storage provider or shared media directory (primitive: the lock is a UI gate, not a filesystem gate) -> the OS file manager (com.android.documentsui) browses the app's SAF provider and reads/modifies the files (escalation: the lock is bypassed with no credential) -> the attacker reads the app's synced files and writes into them (impact: full read/write of the app's local data). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = physical access to a device whose screen is unlocked (or whose lock-screen bypass is available) and a prior open of the target directory inside the app; gain = read and write of every file the app has materialised. HONESTY NOTE: doctrine §2(a) lists "attacks needing physical device access" as INELIGIBLE, so this is recorded as a technique/chain primitive, never as a standalone reportable finding.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY mobile app that shows a PIN/biometric/app-lock screen — (1) enable the lock, open a sensitive directory once inside the app, then background the app with the Home button (do NOT pass the lock); (2) open the OS file manager / documents UI and look for the app's own root (its name or logo) in the hamburger/roots list — a DocumentsProvider registration is the tell; (3) browse that root and try to open a file you opened once before; (4) also probe the shared filesystem path `Android/media/<package>/...` (and `Android/data/<package>/...` where readable) directly; (5) if the file opens, the lock is UI-only. Rule: **an app-level PIN/biometric lock that is enforced only in the app's Activity is not a filesystem lock — background the app, open the OS file manager, pick the app's DocumentsProvider root, and read a file you opened once before.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `331489`, `nextcloud-client`, `passcode lock`, `fingerprint lock`, `com.android.documentsui`, `Android/media`, `Storage Access Framework`, `SAF provider`, or an app-lock-vs-filesystem-lock gap. Pre-existing related material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: the ledger's Nextcloud entries are all SERVER-side (#3382343 Out-of-Office API `userId` BOLA, #851807 Talk command injection, #1170024 federation `sharedSecret` -> share-permission rewrite, #3489490 user_oidc missing JWT signature verification, #3483708 chunked-upload share-token ownership gap, #3249624 Tables import path traversal, #3799010 TaskProcessing callback authorization) — none is a CLIENT app-lock bypass; the `Android`/`mobile app` hits are recon guidance (decompile the app, diff its endpoints), not a lock bypass. Recorded as a NEW class delta (mobile client auth bypass) with NO §17 slot — the physical-access precondition makes it a doctrine §2(a) chain primitive, not a general web method.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/217358  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary, the `POST` request, the "Vulnerable Injected PARAM" line, the security-impact section and the program's own comments; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Unbounce account or DNS record was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: subdomain takeover via an UNVERIFIED CUSTOM-DOMAIN BINDING on a third-party page builder — a page-update endpoint accepts a `page[url]` parameter that binds an arbitrary domain to a page, and the platform serves the page on that domain with no ownership check (CWE-350 / CWE-276).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE VECTOR, verbatim: "i've been able to bypass it again the fixes and takeover the subdomain info.hacker.one with a new vulnerable PARAM at UnbouncePages App." — the target's own subdomain is takeable through a third-party service the target uses for marketing pages.
  2. THE REQUEST, verbatim: `POST /2235922/pages/eb50eb0c-48a8-483b-8747-285aacbeaed6 HTTP/1.1` / `Host: app.unbounce.com` / `X-CSRF-Token: 7fHXoRIVY2kDTQxt+k6jjNgJagryJHBfu7MuZLtB7V4=` / `Content-Type: ...` with body `utf8=%E2%9C%93&_method=put&authenticity_token=7fHXoRIVY2kDTQxt%2Bk6jjNgJagryJHBfu7MuZLtB7V4%3D&page%5Bname%5D=testing+new+param+vulnerable+at+unbouncepages.com+&page%5Burl%5D=info%2ehacker%2eone%2Ftakeover-bypass-by-ak1t4`.
  3. THE PARAMETER, verbatim: "**Vulnerable Injected PARAM:** page%5Burl%5D=anydomain.com **Endpoint:** /[account-id]/pages/[page-id]" and "This Request update the page with the New Domain (any domain could be used and creating content into it)".
  4. THE SCOPE, verbatim: "**(All branded domains under unbounce app are vulnerable)**" — every customer's branded domain is bindable.
  5. THE PROOF, verbatim: "I have claimed the domain and placed a new-page for PoC validation located under: Go to -> https://info.hacker.one/new-awesome-takeover/ ... You see the alert box and the New Subdomain Takeover".
  6. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "An attacker can utilize this domain info.hacker.one for targeting the organization by fake login hackerOne forms, or steal sensitive information of teams (credentials, credit card information, etc)" and "An attacker can utilize this bug affecting all branded domains and customers at unbouncepages.com and use all domains with evil purposes as stealing of sensitive information, credentials, credit card info, etc".
  7. THE PROGRAM'S OWN RATING, verbatim: "Severity Low (3.5)" / "Weakness Privilege Escalation" / "no HackerOne data was affected by this issue, as this is on a separate domain (hacker.one vs hackerone.com)".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a third-party page-builder / landing-page / CMS service in the target's stack (check DNS for CNAMEs to `unbouncepages.com`, `unbounce.com`, or similar); a page-create/update endpoint with a `page[url]` / `custom_domain` / `domain` field that is a mass-assignable body parameter rather than a dedicated, verified binding flow; a branded-domain feature that maps a customer's domain to a page with no DNS/TXT ownership check; a first-party subdomain whose CNAME points at the third-party service. TELL: set the page's URL field to a subdomain of the target and fetch it — if the attacker's content is served on that host, the binding is unverified.
CHAIN: an attacker with an ordinary account on a third-party page builder the target uses (primitive: any Unbounce customer) -> the page-update endpoint accepts `page[url]=<any domain>` and binds it to the attacker's page with no ownership verification (primitive: an unverified custom-domain binding) -> the platform serves the attacker's content on the target's first-party subdomain (`info.hacker.one`) (escalation: content control on a host the target itself uses) -> the attacker hosts fake login forms / phishing on a first-party-looking host (impact: credential and card theft, brand abuse; the program rated it Low 3.5 and stated no HackerOne data was affected because `hacker.one` is separate from `hackerone.com`). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an account on the third-party service and a target subdomain whose CNAME points at it; gain = serving attacker content on a target-controlled hostname.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target that uses a third-party landing-page / page-builder / CMS — (1) enumerate the target's subdomains and their CNAMEs (`dig <host> +short`, `dnsx -cname`) and look for third-party page hosts (`unbouncepages.com`, `unbounce.com`, `landingi.io`, `leadpages.net`, a marketing CDN); (2) open the third party's console, create a page, and capture the page-create/update request; (3) look for a `page[url]` / `custom_domain` / `domain` / `url` field and set it to a subdomain of the target; (4) fetch `http://<that-subdomain>/` — if the attacker's page is served, the binding is unverified and the subdomain is takeable; (5) check whether the platform requires a DNS TXT/ownership record (the control) and whether the target's subdomain CNAME is dangling or branded. Rule: **a page builder's custom-domain/URL field that is a mass-assignable parameter and is not ownership-verified is a subdomain-takeover vector — bind a target subdomain to your page and fetch it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `217358`, `unbounce`, `unbouncepages`, `info.hacker.one`, `page%5Burl%5D`, `page[url]`, `custom domain` as a mass-assignable page parameter, or a third-party page-builder branded-domain takeover. Pre-existing related material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded: §17.310 (GitLab Pages serves content for an UNVERIFIED custom domain during a 7-day grace window) is the same CLASS but a different entry point (a Pages custom-domain setting with a forced-HTTPS toggle, not a mass-assignable `page[url]` parameter on a page-update endpoint); §17.320 (Vimeo cross-feature claim-verification gap on a shared CNAME target) is a claim-check gap on the SAME platform, not a third-party service's unverified domain field; §17.377 (a CNAME to an UNREGISTERED parent domain) and #154425 / §1.6 (dangling CNAME to a dead cloud service) are DNS-claim takeovers, not an application-level unverified domain binding. Recorded as a DELTA of the unverified-custom-domain-binding class with a NEW ENTRY POINT (a mass-assignable `page[url]` parameter on a third-party page builder's page-update endpoint) and NO §17 slot.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 batch 139 (6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and the dated knowledge file `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`; 1 new §17 slot). NEW: (1) HackerOne #716761 (Starbucks, "WAF bypass via double encoded non standard ASCII chars permitted a reflected XSS on response page not found pages - (629745 bypass)", reporter laszaro, submitted 2019-10-17) — a double-encoding filter whose scan range stops at 0x7F is defeated by inserting a high byte (%80-%FF) into the encoded payload; DELTA of the double-encoding class with a NEW BYPASS (the byte-range blind spot) -> §17.429. (2) HackerOne #331489 (Nextcloud, "Extremly simple way to bypass Nextcloud-Client PIN/Fingerprint lock", reporter volker_weissmann, submitted 2018-03-30) — an app-level PIN/fingerprint lock is enforced only in the app's Activity, while the files are exposed through Android's Storage Access Framework provider and the shared `Android/media/<pkg>/` directory, so the OS file manager reads them; NEW class delta (mobile client auth bypass) with a doctrine §2(a) physical-access caveat, NO §17 slot. (3) HackerOne #217358 (HackerOne, "Subdomain takeover #3 at info.hacker.one", reporter ak1t4, submitted 2017-03-30, Low 3.5) — a third-party page builder's page-update endpoint accepts a mass-assignable `page[url]` parameter that binds an arbitrary domain to a page with no ownership verification -> the target's first-party subdomain serves attacker content; DELTA of the unverified-custom-domain-binding class (§17.310/§17.320) with a NEW ENTRY POINT (a page-update parameter on a third-party service), NO §17 slot. VALID-BUT-CHAIN-PRIMITIVE-ONLY (held as a lead, no entry): HackerOne #495497 (GitLab, "Know whether private project name exists or not within a group using link comments", reporter ashish_r_padelkar, Low) — a markdown link to an EXISTING private project is rewritten to the current project's URL + `/click` while a link to a NON-existent project is left as-is, so hovering the link is an existence oracle for private projects; an info-disclosure/existence oracle with NO documented chain, so per the CHAIN-ONLY rule it is not an entry (the ledger's §17.144 counter oracle and §17.159 landing-page oracle both carried PII/monetary impact; this one is Low with no chain). INVALID / BELOW THE BAR (no entry, nothing invented): HackerOne #632808 (Starbucks, "Information disclosure on sim.starbucks.com", reporter johnstone, Low 0.1 ~ 3.9) — two known Jira CVEs (CVE-2019-3403 user-picker enumeration, CVE-2019-8442 pom.xml read) with one-line PoCs and no method, bypass or chain; the assigned class INTERNAL-FILE-READ is a known-CVE fingerprint, not a technique. HackerOne #222692 (WordPress, "plugins.trac.wordpress.org likely vulnerable to Cross Site Tracing (xst), TRACE HTTP method should be disabled", reporter geeknik, 2017, "No rating", bounty) — the TRACE/XST technique is a hardening/misconfiguration finding with no demonstrated impact chain, and modern browsers block TRACE from script; the assigned class BAC is a mis-classification (XST is not BAC). 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3580511  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary, the five code-level sections, the Attack Chain, the five Exploitation Scenarios, the PoC, the remediation options, the impact table and the staff thread; no fallback route needed). Cross-checked against the linked advisory GHSA-9xrj-h377-fr87 / CVE-2026-33195 (read in full this pass).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Rails app or ActiveStorage endpoint was touched this pass; every code line and quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: path traversal / arbitrary file write-read-delete in a framework's file-storage service, reached through a DOCUMENTED model parameter (`key:`) that bypasses the model's secure-token generation -> RCE via cron / SSH keys / restart-watched files (CWE-22 / CWE-73).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "ActiveStorage's `DiskService#path_for` does not validate or sanitize blob keys before constructing file paths. Combined with the Hash attachable interface — which passes user-supplied `key:` values directly to `Blob.build_after_unfurling` without filtering — an attacker who can influence the Hash passed to `.attach()` can achieve **arbitrary file write, read, and delete** on the server's filesystem." The `key:` parameter is a documented feature (the Rails guide's "Attaching File/IO Objects" page) intended for S3 folder organization, "making it likely that developers will incorporate user input into key construction."
  2. THE SPLAT, verbatim (`activestorage/lib/active_storage/attached/changes/create_one.rb:82-88`): `when Hash` / `ActiveStorage::Blob.build_after_unfurling(**attachable.reverse_merge(record: record, service_name: attachment_service_name).symbolize_keys)` — "When a Hash is passed to `.attach()`, **every key-value pair** — including `key:` — is forwarded to `Blob.build_after_unfurling` via `**` splat."
  3. THE MODEL ACCEPTS IT, verbatim (`activestorage/app/models/active_storage/blob.rb:86-89`): `def build_after_unfurling(key: nil, io:, filename:, content_type: nil, metadata: nil, service_name: nil, identify: true, record: nil)` / `new(key: key, filename: filename, content_type: content_type, metadata: metadata, service_name: service_name)` — "A non-nil `key:` value is passed directly to `ActiveStorage::Blob.new`."
  4. THE SECURE-TOKEN CALLBACK IS A BLANK-CHECK, NOT A VALIDATOR, verbatim (`activerecord/lib/active_record/secure_token.rb:73-77`): `set_callback on, on == :initialize? ? :after : :before do` / `if new_record? && !query_attribute(attribute)` / `send("#{attribute}=", generate_token.call)` — "The `has_secure_token :key` callback on `ActiveStorage::Blob` only generates a secure random key when the attribute is **blank**. If a key is already set via the constructor, the callback is a no-op." The custom `key` getter (`blob.rb:176-179`) also preserves existing values via `self[:key] ||= ...`.
  5. THE PATH BUILDER HAS NO CONTAINMENT CHECK, verbatim (`activestorage/lib/active_storage/service/disk_service.rb:101-103, 155-157`): `def path_for(key)` / `File.join root, folder_for(key), key` / `end` / `def folder_for(key)` / `[ key[0..1], key[2..3] ].join("/")` — "`File.join` does not sanitize `../` sequences. There is no validation that the resolved path remains within `root`. A key like `../../etc/cron.d/evil` produces a path that escapes the storage directory."
  6. THERE IS NO KEY FORMAT VALIDATION, verbatim: "`ActiveStorage::Blob` has **zero validation** on the `key` attribute: `validates :service_name, presence: true` / `validates :checksum, presence: true, unless: :composed` / `# No validates :key, format: ...` — "The only constraint is the database-level `NOT NULL` and `UNIQUE` index, neither of which prevent path traversal characters."
  7. THE PRIMITIVE TABLE, verbatim: `upload` -> `IO.copy_stream(io, make_path_for(key))` -> **Arbitrary file write** + directory creation via `mkdir_p`; `download` -> `File.binread path_for(key)` -> **Arbitrary file read**; `delete` -> `File.delete path_for(key)` -> **Arbitrary file delete**; `delete_prefixed` -> `Dir.glob(path_for("#{prefix}*"))` + `rm_rf` -> **Glob-based mass delete**; `exist?` -> `File.exist? path_for(key)` -> **File existence oracle**.
  8. THE ATTACK REQUESTS, verbatim: Scenario A `POST /api/v1/documents` with body `{"file_data":"KiBldmlsIGNyb250YWIgZW50cnkK","filename":"notes.txt","content_type":"text/plain","path":"../../../../etc/cron.d/backdoor"}` -> "The uploaded content is written to `/etc/cron.d/backdoor` (if the process has permissions), achieving **Remote Code Execution** via cron." Scenario B `POST /assets` with `avatar[filename]=photo.jpg&avatar[content_type]=image/jpeg&avatar[key]=../../../../../../tmp/malicious_payload&file=@payload.jpg` -> "Arbitrary file write to `/tmp/malicious_payload`."
  9. THE FIX, verbatim: Option A `validates :key, format: { with: /\A[a-zA-Z0-9\-_]+\z/, message: "contains invalid characters" }`; Option B in `path_for`: `path = File.join(root, folder_for(key), key)` / `full_path = File.expand_path(path)` / `raise ArgumentError, "key escapes storage root" unless full_path.start_with?(File.expand_path(root))`; Option C `attachable.except(:key, "key")`. The advisory (GHSA-9xrj-h377-fr87, CVE-2026-33195) confirms: "Active Storage's `DiskService` does not validate that the resolved filesystem path remains within the storage root directory. If a blob key containing path traversal sequences (e.g. `../`) is used, it could allow reading, writing, or deleting arbitrary files on the server. Blob keys are expected to be trusted strings, but some applications could be passing user input as keys and would be affected."
